Quasiperiodic solutions of the generalized SQG equation
Javier Gómez-Serrano, Alexandru D. Ionescu, Jaemin Park

Abstract
Our goal in this monograph is twofold. First, we would like to develop a robust method to construct global time-quasiperiodic solutions of large families of quasilinear evolution equations. The method we present here expands on earlier work of many people and combines elements from KAM theory, normal forms, pseudo-differential calculus and a Nash-Moser scheme. One of our main objectives is to avoid the use of any external parameters, and exploit instead the genericity of the initial data to generate large families of quasiperiodic solutions. As a concrete application we prove the existence of linearly stable, quasiperiodic patch solutions of the generalized SQG equation for all parameters α ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha\in(1,2) , and for almost all initial data in a neighborhood of the disk solution.



1
Introduction

The generalized surface quasi-geostrophic (gSQG) equations describing the evolution of the potential temperature
\omega
read as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\omega_{t}+u\cdot\nabla\omega=0,&\text{ for }(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\\
u=-\nabla^{\perp}(-\Delta)^{-1+\alpha/2}\omega,&\alpha\in[0,2]\\
\omega(0,\cdot)=\omega_{0}(\cdot).\end{cases}

(1.1)

Formally, these equations interpolate between the case of the Euler equation
(\alpha=0)
and the case of stationary solutions
(\alpha=2)
. The case
(\alpha=1)
is known as the SQG equation.

The SQG equation models the evolution of the temperature from a general quasi-geostrophic system for atmospheric and oceanic flows (see
[
35
,
79
,
111
,
102
]
for more details).
The first rigorous mathematical study of the SQG equation was done by Constantin–Majda–Tabak
[
35
]
where its mathematical importance due to its analogy with the incompressible 3D Euler equations was highlighted and the first numerical and analytical study of the equation was carried out.
Córdoba–Fontelos–Mancho–Rodrigo in
[
39
]
proposed the gSQG or (SQG)
α
model (
1.1
) as an interpolation between the Euler and surface quasi–geostrophic equations. Nonetheless, very little is known for this family of equations and specifically the question of global existence versus finite time singularities is still open, for all
\alpha>0
. In this monograph we aim to prove the existence of a large class of initial data for which there is time quasi-periodic behaviour and thus global existence in the more singular case
\alpha\in(1,2)
.

1.0.1
Patch problems

In this monograph we will work in the
patch
setting, where
\omega(\cdot,t)=1_{D(t)}
is an indicator function of a simply-connected, bounded set that moves with the fluid. In such a situation, we parametrize
\partial D(t)
as
z(\theta,t),\,\theta\in[0,2\pi]
and the evolution equations read:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}z(\theta,t)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{\partial_{\theta}z(\theta,t)-\partial_{\theta}z(\theta-\eta,t)}{|z(\theta,t)-z(\theta-\eta,t)|^{\alpha}}d\eta+c(\theta,t)\partial_{\theta}z(\theta,t),

(1.2)

where
c(\theta,t)
accounts for the reparametrization freedom of the curve.

Concerning well-posedness results for patch solutions, Rodrigo (in a
C^{\infty}
space)
[
115
]
and Gancedo
[
59
]
and Chae–Constantin–Córdoba–Gancedo–Wu
[
30
]
(in a Sobolev space) proved local existence for the case
0<\alpha\leq 1
and
1<\alpha
respectively. See also
[
96
,
60
,
1
]
.

1.0.2
Steady solutions and global existence of the gSQG equation

The construction of nontrivial global solutions for the generalized SQG equations is a very challenging open problem for all parameters
\alpha\in(0,2)
, both in the smooth case and in the patch case. For
\alpha=0
(the 2D Euler equations), global regularity of solutions was well-understood a long time ago, both in the smooth case and in the patch case. See for example the classical papers of Wolibner
[
125
]
, Yudovich
[
126
]
, Burbea
[
21
]
, Chemin
[
31
]
, and Bertozzi-Constantin
[
17
]
. However, the construction of global solutions in the case of
\alpha\in(0,2)
is much more challenging than when
\alpha=0
, since the velocity is more singular, and only partial results have been obtained in recent years. We review some of these results below.

Most of the results around global existence of the gSQG equation have revolved around solutions that exhibited some rigid character (steady, uniformly rotating –V-states–, traveling). In the case where
0<\alpha<1
, Hassainia–Hmidi
[
74
]
proved the existence of V-states with
C^{k}
boundary regularity. Castro–Córdoba–Gómez-Serrano then expanded upon this result in
[
26
]
by showing that V-states also exist with
C^{\infty}
boundary regularity in the remaining open cases of
\alpha\in[1,2)
for existence and
\alpha\in(0,2)
for regularity. This boundary regularity was later refined to be analytic in
[
27
]
. Other notable works on rotating solutions include
[
44
,
63
,
80
,
77
]
, which discuss other families of rotating solutions or even more steady states,
[
45
,
113
]
which address the doubly connected case, and
[
28
]
which presents a construction in the smooth setting.

In
[
45
]
, de la Hoz–Hassainia–Hmidi showed that there exist non-radial patches bifurcating from annuli at negative angular velocities and Gómez-Serrano
[
68
]
constructed non-radial, doubly connected stationary patches. García
[
62
]
proved the existence of a Kármán vortex street structure by desingularizing an infinite array of point vortices in the case
\alpha\in[0,1)
. In
[
29
]
it was ruled out by Castro–Córdoba–Gómez-Serrano–Martín Zamora that ellipses could be rotating solutions for
\alpha>0
, as opposed to the case
\alpha=0
. Gravejat–Smets
[
70
]
, in the case
\alpha=1
, constructed smooth translating solutions. Ao–Dávila–del Pino–Musso–Wei
[
2
]
, expanded the range to
\alpha\in(0,2)
as well as to rotating solutions. See also
[
66
,
67
]
and
[
22
,
24
,
23
]
for alternative constructions. In
[
69
]
, Gómez-Serrano–Park–Shi–Yao proved that any smooth, non-negative rotating solution with simply-connected superlevel sets can only rotate with positive angular velocity, and in the case of a patch of fixed area derived moreover a sharp upper bound on the angular velocity.

The drawback of the aforementioned solutions is that they are
special
in the sense that general solutions will not have such behavior. Concerning results for general solutions, Córdoba–Gómez-Serrano–Ionescu
[
40
]
proved global existence for small patch data close to a halfplane in the case
\alpha\in(1,2)
, using a different mechanism based on dispersion and decay. This was extended in
[
84
,
83
]
. The main idea was to show that general initial data that are small perturbations of the halfplane stationary patch solution lead to global solutions that decay in time (at an optimal rate of
t^{-1/2}
), thus converging back to the halfplane stationary patch. Unfortunately, the mechanism of dispersion and decay seems to require unbounded domains and, in particular, infinite energy solutions.

In a different direction, one could hope to use the mechanism of inviscid damping to construct families of global-in-time solutions around explicit stationary solutions of finite energy, such as smooth shear flows or vortices. This has been successfully implemented in recent years in the 2D Euler case
\alpha=0
, for perturbations of the Couette flow (by Bedrossian–Masmoudi
[
11
]
and Ionescu–Jia
[
85
]
) and then general monotonic shear flows
[
86
,
105
]
. It is tempting to try to adapt the mechanism of inviscid damping to construct families of nontrivial global solutions of the gSQG equations, at least for some parameters
\alpha>0
small. The easiest would be to perturb around the Couette flow corresponding to
\theta(t,x,y)=-1
on the bounded channel
\mathcal{D}=\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
. Unfortunately and surprisingly, recent work of Gómez-Serrano–Ionescu–Jia (discussed in
[
87
]
) shows that this fails to produce global solutions for any parameters
\alpha>0
, due to a forward cascade that leads to loss of regularity in finite time.

1.0.3
Quasiperiodic solutions in PDE

Our main goal in this monograph is to demonstrate the existence of large families of global solutions of the generalized SQG equations. We do this using KAM theory, by constructing quasiperiodic solutions for almost all initial data in a neighborhood of the unit disk (the simplest stationary patch solution with finite energy).

The first application of KAM theory
[
97
,
3
,
108
]
was to prove the existence of invariant tori that were small perturbations of finite dimensional nearly integrable Hamiltonian systems. In order to upgrade it to the infinite dimensional (PDE) case, the first results are due to Kuksin
[
98
]
, Wayne
[
122
]
, Pöschel
[
112
]
for 1-d semilinear wave and Schrödinger equations with Dirichlet boundary conditions and Craig–Wayne

[
42
]
, Bourgain

[
19
]
, Grébert–Kappeler
[
71
]
and Chierchia–You

[
33
]
with periodic boundary conditions. See also
[
99
]
. In the semilinear multidimensional case, we refer to the works of Bourgain
[
18
]
, Eliasson–Kuksin
[
49
]
, Grébert–Paturel
[
72
]
, Wang
[
121
]
and Berti–Bolle

[
13
]
and references therein. See also De la Llave–Sire

[
46
]
. Note that all the previous results only were able to deal with semilinear problems.

In the last decade there has been an emergence of results of quasiperiodic solutions for quasilinear PDE, motivated by applications to the dynamics of confined fluids, building up and polishing the techniques and the methods and culminating with excellent theorems. Baldi–Berti–Montalto constructed quasiperiodic solutions to the Airy equation
[
6
]
and KdV and mKdV
[
7
,
8
,
9
]
. See also the results of Giuliani for gKdV
[
65
]
, and

[
51
,
56
,
106
,
55
]
and references therein for other relevant models. In the context of water waves, Baldi–Berti–Haus–Montalto
[
5
]
(gravity case), Berti–Montalto
[
16
]
(gravity-capillary case), Feola–Giuliani
[
52
,
53
]
(infinite depth) and Berti–Franzoi–Maspero
[
14
]
(constant non-zero vorticity) constructed quasiperiodic solutions. Numerically, Wilkening–Zhao
[
123
,
124
]
computed quasiperiodic gravity-capillary water waves in the infinite depth case.

Berti–Hassainia–Masmoudi
[
15
]
constructed quasiperiodic solutions close to elliptical vortex patches, introducing the angular momentum as a symplectic variable. Hassainia–Roulley
[
76
]
constructed quasiperiodic solutions of the 2D Euler equations in a bounded domain, Roulley
[
116
]
proved its existence for the Euler-
\alpha
equation and Hmidi–Roulley
[
81
]
for the QGSW equations.

Other examples of quasiperiodic solutions in the context of the incompressible Euler and Navier-Stokes equations, even in high dimensions, were obtained by Crouseilles–Faou, Elgindi–Jeong, Enciso–Peralta-Salas–Torres de Lizaur
[
43
,
48
,
50
]
for Euler, using non-KAM constructions, Baldi–Montalto
[
10
]
for forced Euler, using a KAM construction and Franzoi–Montalto, Montalto
[
57
,
107
]
for forced Navier-Stokes, using a KAM construction. Finally, we would like to draw the attention to the recent results by Hassainia–Hmidi–Masmoudi
[
75
]
who proved the existence of global quasiperiodic solutions for the generalized SQG equations, for a set of parameters
\alpha\in\left(0,1/2\right)
. The set of acceptable parameters
\alpha
is unknown, but of full measure in
(0,1/2)
.

We emphasize that most of these recent results in the quasilinear case (with the notable exception of the papers
[
52
]
,
[
65
]
and
[
8
]
) rely on the use of
external parameters
. Quasiperiodic solutions are then constructed for all initial data, but for an unknown set of parameters, usually generic of full measure. The point is that the presence of external parameters improves significantly the structure of the resonances of the system, which plays a key role in the analysis.

The drawback is that the family of acceptable parameters is not explicit, and one cannot guarantee that quasiperiodic solutions exist for a specific given equation. Our broad goal in this monograph is to develop a robust and flexible method to construct quasiperiodic solutions for certain fluid models, without requiring the presence of external parameters. The basic idea is to replace the genericity of the external parameters with genericity of the initial data. This leads however to very significant difficulties at the implementation level; see below for a more detailed discussion.

1.0.4
Weak solutions and finite time singularities

The generalized SQG equations have been studied extensively, by many authors. In this subsection we discuss two other areas of active research, and provide some references.

In his thesis
[
114
]
, Resnick demonstrated the global existence of weak solutions in
L^{2}
through the use of the oddness of the Riesz transform to achieve additional cancellation. Marchand
[
103
]
later extended this result to include initial data belonging to
L^{p}
with
p
greater than
\frac{4}{3}
. See also
[
109
]
for other existence results concerning weak solutions. Non-uniqueness of weak solutions of SQG remains a difficult problem, with progress being made through works such as Azzam–Bedrossian
[
4
]
or Isett–Vicol
[
89
]
, and most importantly, Buckmaster–Shkoller–Vicol
[
20
]
, as well as alternative proofs by Isett–Ma
[
88
]
and the investigation of the stationary problem by Cheng–Kwon–Li
[
32
]
.

One of the most significant questions in mathematical fluid mechanics is whether the SQG and gSQG system exhibits finite time singularities or has global existence. Kiselev–Nazarov
[
94
]
created solutions that exhibited norm inflation, and Friedlander–Shvydkoy
[
58
]
demonstrated the presence of unstable eigenvalues in the spectrum. He–Kiselev
[
78
]
proved an exponential in time growth of the
C^{2}
-norm. See also the construction of singular solutions with infinite energy by Castro–Córdoba
[
25
]
and ill-posedness results by Córdoba–Martínez-Zoroa and Jeong–Kim
[
41
,
91
]
.

In order to understand the possibility of a finite time blow-up scenario, numerical studies have been conducted. Constantin–Majda–Tabak
[
35
]
suggested that a singularity in the form of a hyperbolic saddle may occur, closing in a finite amount of time. However, Ohkitani–Yamada
[
110
]
and Constantin–Nie–Schörghofer
[
36
]
proposed that the growth was actually double exponential. Córdoba
[
37
]
bounded the growth at quadruple exponential, and later Córdoba and Fefferman
[
38
]
proposed a double exponential bound, which was supported by numerical simulations from Deng–Hou–Li–Yu
[
47
]
. Constantin–Lai–Sharma–Tseng–Wu
[
34
]
later reexamined the hyperbolic saddle scenario using improved algorithms and found no evidence of blowup. Scott
[
118
]
proposed a scenario in which filamentation occurs and blowup of
\nabla\theta
occurs after several cascades, starting from elliptical configurations. This is currently the only scenario that remains valid in the smooth setting. In
[
64
]
, very recently, García–Gómez-Serrano constructed a big class of non-trivial self-similar spiral solutions close to radial ones with a mild singularity at the origin.

Even though the finite time singularity problem seems elusive, there exist several numerical scenarios suggesting such a singularity. The first one, proposed by Córdoba–Fontelos–Mancho–Rodrigo
[
39
]
initially starts as two patches rolling onto each other and finally collapsing. At the intersection point the curvature blows up (the curve should lose regularity due to the results by Gancedo and Strain
[
61
]
, see also
[
93
,
90
]
) and the collapse is suggested to be asymptotically self-similar. The second scenario was proposed by Scott–Dritschel
[
119
]
, taking ellipses as initial condition; starting with an aspect ratio of
0.16
, they report a self-similar cascade of filamentation. In
[
120
]
, again taking ellipses as initial condition and combining numerical analysis with asymptotic calculations, they conjecture a scenario where the patch develops a corner in finite time, together with a self-similar spiral. Finally, Kiselev–Ryzhik–Yao–Zlatoš
[
95
]
(for
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{12}
) and later Gancedo–Patel
[
60
]
(for
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{3}
) construct finite time singularities in the presence of a boundary.

1.1
Main result

Before we state the main result, let us first recall the definition of a quasiperiodic function:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space and
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
be a fixed natural number. A function
f:\mathbb{R}\mapsto X
is said to be quasiperiodic with frequency
{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
, if there exists
i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto X
such that
f(t)=i({\omega}t).

In this monograph, we consider a patch solution to (
1.2
) of the form:

\displaystyle z(x,t):=\sqrt{1+f(x,t)}(\cos x,\sin x),\text{ for some $f(\cdot,t):\mathbb{T}\mapsto(-1,\infty)$.}

(1.3)

Note that one of the advantages of the use of the variable
f
, instead of a more natural parametrization
z(x,t)=R(x,t)(\cos x,\sin x)
relies on the conservation of the area of the patch in the dynamics in (
1.1
); if the patch initially has area
|D(0)|=\pi
, then
|D(t)|=\pi
for all
t\geq 0
, therefore

\displaystyle\pi=|D(t)|=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}R(x,t)^{2}dx=\pi+\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x,t)dx.

(1.4)

Thus, we can assume that
f
has zero average in the variable
x
.

Plugging (
1.3
) into (
1.2
), one can find that the evolution of
f
can be expressed as (we refer to Subsection
3.1
for more detailed computations)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(x,t)=\frac{2}{2-\alpha}\partial_{\theta}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(z(x,t)-z(y,t))\cdot\partial_{x}z(y,t)^{\perp}}{|z(x,t)-z(y,t)|^{\alpha}}dy\right)=:X_{\text{gSQG}}(f(x,t)).

(1.5)

As noted in
[
74
,
114
,
104
,
117
]
, the equation (
1.5
) can be seen as a Hamiltonian system with the associated Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(f):=\int_{D}1_{D}*\frac{1}{|\cdot|^{\alpha}}(x)dx,

(1.6)

where
D
is the patch determined by the parametrization
f
as in (
1.3
) (see Section
3
). More precisely, the vector field
X_{\text{gSQG}}(f)
is given by

\displaystyle X_{\text{gSQG}}(f)=\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}(f)\right),

(1.7)

where
\nabla_{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}(f)
denotes the gradient vector field of
\mathcal{H}
at
f
in the space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.

The linearized equation of (
1.5
) at the unit disk (
f=0
) can be written as (see Proposition
3.2
)

\displaystyle f_{t}=\frac{d}{dt}X_{\text{gSQG}}(tf)\bigg|_{t=0}=\partial_{x}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}f+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}f\right),

(1.8)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\alpha-1}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(f(x)-f(y))dy,\text{ and }T_{\alpha}:=\frac{2\pi\Gamma(3-\alpha)}{\Gamma(2-\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(2-\frac{\alpha}{2})}.

(1.9)

One can also rewrite the linearized equation (
1.8
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f=Op(\text{i}W(j))[f](x,t),

(1.10)

where
Op(W(j))
denotes the pseudo differential operator associated to the the Fourier multiplier
W(j)
, defined as

\displaystyle W(j):=j\left(-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\Gamma(|j|+\frac{\alpha}{2})}{\Gamma(1+|j|-\frac{\alpha}{2})}-\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}{\Gamma(1-\frac{\alpha}{2})}\right)+\frac{\pi(-1)^{j}\Gamma(3-\alpha)}{2\Gamma(2-\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(2-\frac{\alpha}{2})}\right),

(1.11)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}:=-\frac{2\pi\Gamma(1-\alpha)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}
.
A classical asymptotic analysis for the Gamma function tells us that
W(j)
exhibits an asymptotic behavior like
j|j|^{\alpha-1}
(e.g.
[
100
, Theorem 2.1]
), more precisely,

W(j)=C(\alpha)j|j|^{\alpha-1}+O(1),\text{ for some constant $C(\alpha)\in\mathbb{R}$ for $\alpha\in(0,2)\backslash\left\{1\right\}$}.

Given a set of natural numbers
S^{+}:=\left\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\right\}\subset\mathbb{N}
(also denoting
S:=\left\{\pm j:j\in S^{+}\right\}
), the linear equation (
1.10
) possesses time-quasiperiodic solutions of the form

\displaystyle f(x,t)=\sum_{j_{k}\in{S}}\sqrt{|j_{k}|\zeta_{k}}e^{\text{i}(W(j_{k})t+j_{k}x)}=\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}2\sqrt{|j_{k}|\zeta_{k}}\cos(W(j_{k})t+j_{k}x),\text{ for some $\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{\nu}>0$,}

(1.12)

for which the
j
-th Fourier coefficient is oscillating in time with frequency
W(j)
. Indeed, according to Definition
1.1
, the solution (
1.12
) to the linearized equation can be expressed as

\displaystyle f(t,\cdot)=f(t)=i_{\text{linear}}(\overline{\omega}t),\text{ where }i_{\text{linear}}(\varphi):=\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}2\sqrt{\zeta_{k}}\cos(\varphi_{k}+j_{k}x)\text{ and $\overline{\omega}_{k}:=W(j_{k})$.}

(1.13)

This naturally leads to the question whether there exists such a time-quasiperiodic solution to the full nonlinear problem (
1.5
) around the steady state
f=0
.

In our analysis, we make use of several invariance properties of the equation (
1.3
). One is the so-called time-reversibility with respect to the involution
\rho:f(x)\mapsto f(-x)
, namely,

\mathcal{H}(\rho(f))=\mathcal{H}(f),\text{ where $\mathcal{H}$ is defined as in \eqref{hamiltonian_intro11}.}

We say that a solution
f(x,t)
to (
1.5
) is reversible, if
\rho(f)(x,-t)
is also a solution. Another invariance property is the rotational invariance of solutions. More precisely, given an integer
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
, if the initial data of the gSQG equation is invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-rotation, then the solution at any time is also invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-rotation. Such an
\mathtt{M}
-fold symmetric patch can be associated to a parametrization
f
in (
1.3
) being invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation of the variable
x
:

\displaystyle f(t,x+\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}})=f(t,x).

(1.14)

Those properties of the gSQG equation will be studied in detail in Section
3
.

The main theorem we prove in this monograph is the following (stated informally, we refer to Theorem
6.5
for a precise statement):

Theorem 1.2
.

[
= Theorem
6.5
]
Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
S^{+}\subset\mathbb{N}
and a symmetry class
\mathtt{M}
satisfying some non-resonance conditions (cf. Subsection
4.1
and
5.1
) be fixed. Then, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a set of amplitudes
A_{\varepsilon}\subset[1,2]^{|S^{+}|}\subset\mathbb{R}^{|S^{+}|}
such that for each
\vec{\zeta}\in A_{\varepsilon}
, there exist a frequency vector
\omega=\omega(\vec{\zeta})
and a time-quasiperiodic solution to (
1.5
) of the form

\displaystyle f(\theta,t)=2\varepsilon\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}\sqrt{|j_{k}|\vec{\zeta}_{k}}\cos(\omega_{k}t+j_{k}x)+o(\varepsilon).

(1.15)

The set
A_{\varepsilon}
is a Cantor-like set of asymptotically full measure, in the sense that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|A_{\varepsilon}|}{\left|[1,2]^{|S^{+}|}\right|}=1
. The solution (
1.15
) is in some Sobolev space
H^{s_{0}}
for some
s_{0}\gg 1
, and it is reversible and invariant under
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation in the variable
\theta
. Lastly, the solution is linearly stable under
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant perturbations.

Some remarks are in order:

Remark 1.3
.

As stated above, our proof does not make use of any external parameters (
\alpha
would be the natural candidate) as opposed to
[
74
]
and indeed this results in needing the Diophantine constant
\gamma
to be
\gamma=o(\varepsilon^{2})
, which in turn requires Normal Form expansions (cf. Section
4
), and also the computation of the explicit terms of size
O(\varepsilon)
and
O(\varepsilon^{2})
. Relaxing this constraint would significantly shorten the length and the complexity of this monograph.

Remark 1.4
.

It is conceivable that our proof of Theorem
6.5
would also work in the case
\alpha<1
, changing the relevant sections and estimates. In the case of the SQG equation
(\alpha=1)
, the analysis in Subsection
10.3
breaks down since the sum of pseudo differential symbols is not finite anymore and the regularity losses coming from the Egorov method are not finite. Most of the other parts of the reduction also hold for all cases of
\alpha
, possibly with minimal changes.

Remark 1.5
.

The closer
\alpha
is to
1
, the more conjugations are required in the reduction of the linearized operator to a constant coefficients operator. In the adaptation of the Egorov method, inspired by
[
16
]
, we use a slightly more general flow, compared to the ones in
[
16
,
74
,
52
]
, to avoid a large number of iterations that might cause potential complexity. See Remark
1.7
.

Remark 1.6
.

All the amplitudes in the set of
A_{\varepsilon}
in Theorem
1.2
, which takes asymptotically full measure, can possess quasi-periodic solutions, if the choice of tangential sites
S^{+}
can be made properly so that some non-resonance conditions are satisfied. The precise conditions are stated in Subsection
5.1
. Then a natural question is whether such a set
S^{+}=\left\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\right\}\subset\mathbb{N}
is generic or not. The non-resonance conditions that we require can be roughly expressed as

P(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{\nu})\neq 0,\text{ for some function $P:\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{R}$}.

Compared to previous works (e.g.
[
65
,
8
,
9
]
), our
P
involves Gamma functions and the verification of the non-resonance condition is much more complicated. While we expect that such conditions can be satisfied by
“generic”
choices of
S
with small
\mathtt{M}
, we will give a rigorous proof only for the case where the frequencies are supported on multiples of sufficiently large
\mathtt{M}
. This allows us to focus on the asymptotic behavior of the Gamma function in the analysis.

1.2
Strategy of the proof and the structure of the monograph

We first outline the main ideas of the proof of Theorem
1.2
and link them with the sections of this monograph afterwards.

In order to describe the strategy of the proof, let us fix

\displaystyle\nu\in\mathbb{N},\quad S^{+}:=\left\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{\nu}\right\}\subset\mathbb{N},

(1.16)

and denote

\displaystyle S:=\left\{\pm j:j\in S^{+}\right\},\quad S^{\perp}:=\mathbb{Z}\backslash(S\cup\left\{0\right\}).

(1.17)

In view of (
1.12
), one can think of
S
as a set of Fourier modes of the solution at the linear level, and
S^{\perp}
as the support of the orthogonal correction term for the solution to the nonlinear problem, while the
0
-th mode is excluded, since we look for a solution with zero average (see (
1.4
)).
We also denote the linear frequency by

\displaystyle{\overline{\omega}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu},\quad(\overline{\omega})_{k}=W(j_{k}),\text{ for $k=1,\ldots,\nu$.}

(1.18)

Using the notations in (
1.5
) and (
1.10
), we can rewrite (
1.5
) as

\displaystyle f_{t}=X_{\text{gSQG}}(f)=Op(\text{i}W(j))[f]+P_{\text{gSQG}}(f),

(1.19)

where
P_{\text{gSQG}}(f)
collects the nonlinear contribution of the vector field
X_{\text{gSQG}}(f)
. Since we are interested in the solutions near
f=0
, replacing
f
by
\varepsilon f
for small
\varepsilon>0
, we are led to study the equation of the form:

\displaystyle f_{t}=Op(\text{i}W(j))[f]+P_{\varepsilon,\text{gSQG}}(f),\text{ where $P_{\varepsilon,\text{gSQG}}(f):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}P_{\text{gSQG}}(\varepsilon f)$}.

(1.20)

As we observed in Subsection
1.1
, we have an embedding
i_{\text{linear}}
(see (
1.13
)) for which
f_{\text{lin}}(t,\theta):=i_{\text{linear}}(\overline{\omega}t)
solves the linear equation
\partial_{t}f_{\text{lin}}=Op(\text{i}W(j))[f_{\text{lin}}]
. The question is whether such an embedding can persist under the nonlinear perturbation as in (
1.19
). Perhaps, one of the most naive attempts could be plugging the ansatz,

f(t,x)=i(\overline{\omega}t,x),\text{ for some $i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{T}\mapsto\mathbb{R}$},

into (
1.19
), which leads us to find
i
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i):=\overline{\omega}\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-Op(\text{i}W(j))[i(\varphi)]-P_{\varepsilon,\text{gSQG}}(i(\varphi))=0,\text{ for $\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}$.}

(1.21)

We can think of
\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}
as a map between spaces of functions of
(\varphi,\theta)
. Having the explicit solution
i_{\text{linear}}
at the linear level given in (
1.13
) and noting
that
P_{\varepsilon,\text{gSQG}}
is
“small”
depending on
\varepsilon>0
, we might expect the sequence of embeddings
i_{n}
, formally defined in the spirit of Newton’s method,

\displaystyle i_{0}=i_{\text{linear}},\quad i_{n+1}:=i_{n}-\left(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i_{n})\right)^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i_{n})],\text{ for $n\geq 0$},

(1.22)

where

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]:=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\bigg|_{t=0}\overset{\eqref{functional_intro_1}}{=}\overline{\omega}\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}-Op(\text{i}W(j))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]-d_{i}P_{\varepsilon,\text{gSQG}}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

(1.23)

to converge to a solution for (
1.21
). Clearly, the above argument is far less rigorous, and we will investigate how to modify the strategy.

1.2.1
Sketch of the proof, Part 1: Internal parameter and the weak Birkhoff normal form

1.2.1.1
Loss of derivatives and the Nash-Moser scheme.

As a rule of thumb in usual perturbative problems, the invertibility of the linearized operator
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\text{gSQG}}(i)
in (
1.22
) would rely on the invertibility of the linear part:

\displaystyle L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]:=\overline{\omega}\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}-Op(\text{i}W(j))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}],

(1.24)

assuming that the contribution of the perturbative part is negligible. While looking for an embedding
i
in Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})
(for large
s\gg 1
), it is not trivial whether the operator
L
can be invertible between two fixed Sobolev spaces. However, the classical KAM theory tells us that the invertibility of
L
can be achieved depending on the frequency vector
\overline{\omega}
. Indeed, for the frequency vectors that satisfy the so-called
“Melnikov condition”
with some
\gamma,\tau>0
:

\displaystyle\left\{\omega:|\text{i}\omega\cdot l-\text{i}W(j)|\geq\gamma|l|^{\tau}\text{ for all $(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{Z}$}\right\},

(1.25)

one can formally invert
L
using the Fourier series, that is,

\displaystyle L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}](\varphi,x)=g(\varphi,x)\overset{\eqref{linearpart_1}}{\iff}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}(\varphi,x)=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{Z}}\frac{\widehat{g}(l,j)}{\text{i}(\overline{\omega}\cdot l-W(j))}e^{\text{i}(\varphi\cdot l+jx)},

(1.26)

where
\hat{g}(l,j):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu+1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1}}g(\varphi,x)e^{\text{i}(\varphi\cdot l+jx)}d\varphi dx
.
Note that the expression of the inverse in (
1.26
) tells us that there is a regularity mismatch between the image and the domain spaces. If
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\in H^{s}
for some
s>0
, we see that there is a loss of derivatives due to the differential operators
\partial_{\varphi}
and
Op(W(j))
, while inverting
L
as in (
1.26
) does not gain the same amount of the regularity, and it actually causes another loss of derivatives by
\tau
; the best estimate one can expect under the condition (
1.25
) is that

\displaystyle\rVert L^{-1}[g]\rVert_{H^{s-\tau}}\lesssim\gamma^{-1}\rVert g\rVert_{H^{s}}\text{ for $g\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})$}.

(1.27)

Therefore, the formal sequence of
i_{n}
in (
1.22
) does not seem to be closed, since
i_{n+1}
must be less regular than
i_{n}
at each iteration. Hence, the crude iteration procedure in (
1.22
) needs to be replaced by the Nash-Moser scheme, projecting each approximate solution
i_{n}
into a finite dimensional space so that
i_{n}
remains in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})
for each
n\geq 0
.

1.2.1.2
Internal parameter.

We have observed above that the condition on the frequency vector in (
1.25
) is one of the necessary conditions to perform the iteration (
1.22
). Then a very natural question is how to check whether the linear frequency
\overline{\omega}
satisfies such a condition. In general (for fixed
\gamma,\tau>0
), it is very hard to determine whether a given vector
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
satisfies even a more relaxed condition (so-called Diophantine condition):

\displaystyle|\omega\cdot l|>\gamma|l|^{\tau},\text{ for all $l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}$.}

(1.28)

However, it is well-known that given an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
,
“almost all”

\omega\in\Omega
satisfy (
1.28
), more precisely, such non-resonance frequency vectors take asymptotically full measure in
\Omega
as
\gamma\to 0
. Indeed, the KAM theory does not tell us exactly which frequency vector can possess a quasiperiodic solution, but rather it tells us that the set of frequency vectors that possess a quasiperiodic solution has non-zero measure in a given set of frequencies. This is why we need parameter-dependent equations to perform the KAM theory; if our equation depends on a parameter, and the parameter can
“properly”
modulate the linear frequency
\overline{\omega}
, then for almost all parameter values, we might expect to invert the operator
L
. The gSQG equation (
1.1
) certainly involves a parameter
\alpha\in(0,2)
, therefore one might be tempted to use
\alpha
to modulate the linear frequency by looking at
\overline{\omega}
in (
1.18
) and (
1.11
) as a function of
\alpha
, that is,
\overline{\omega}=\overline{\omega}(\alpha)
. This attempt would enable us to obtain quasiperiodic solutions for
“almost every”

\alpha
(without knowing precisely which
\alpha
satisfies the condition), while such a result cannot be, in principle, obtained for
every

\alpha
. For this reason, we follow the strategy in
[
8
,
9
,
52
]
and derive a weak Birkhoff normal form of the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, from which we can see a modulation effect of the linear frequency by the amplitude. In this regard, a bit more precise explanation will follow.

1.2.1.3
Action-angle variables.

According to the decomposition in (
1.17
), we denote

\displaystyle H_{S}:=\left\{f:f_{j}=0,\text{ if $j\notin S$}\right\},\quad H_{S^{\perp}}:=\left\{f:f_{j}=0,\text{ if $j\notin S^{\perp}$}\right\},\quad f_{j}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}e^{\text{i}jx}dx,

(1.29)

and we will refer to
H_{S}
and
H_{S^{\perp}}
as
tangential space
and
normal space
, respectively.
We introduce the amplitude variable
{\zeta}
:

\displaystyle\zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}\subset\left(\mathbb{R}^{+}\right)^{\nu},

(1.30)

and consider a
\zeta
-dependent change of variables,
U_{\zeta}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon\left(\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k})}e^{\text{i}(\theta_{k}+j_{k}x)}+\varepsilon^{(b-1)}z\right),\text{ for some $b\in(1,1+1/12)$},

(1.31)

\displaystyle\text{ with }\zeta_{-k}:=\zeta_{k},\quad\theta_{-k}:=-\theta_{k},\quad y_{-k}:=y_{k}.

The variables
(\theta,y)
are the so-called action-angle variables and the above change of variables can be thought of as a reparametrization of functions in
H_{S}
. Also, in order to see the motivation of the constant
b
in (
1.31
), we note that in view of (
1.13
), the map

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\ni\varphi\mapsto U_{\zeta}(\varphi,0,0)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})

(1.32)

corresponds to
i_{\text{linear}}
up to the rescaling factor
\varepsilon
, therefore the terms
\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y
and
\varepsilon^{b-1}z
in (
1.31
) can be thought of as correction terms to solve the nonlinear problem, which justifies the requirement
b>1
.

Now we define a
\zeta
-dependent Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{H}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z).

(1.33)

We note that the factor
\varepsilon^{-2b}
in (
1.33
) naturally arises in the rescaling of the change of variables to describe the Hamiltonian equation in the new phase space
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
. Indeed, the gSQG dynamics in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
in (
1.5
) can be easily rewritten as an evolution equation in the new phase space by pulling back the vector field by
U_{\zeta}
, and one can obtain the equivalent equation is given by (one can follow the same computations given in Section
5
after the proof of Proposition
5.1
. Note that the factor
\frac{1}{2\pi}
is due to our definition for the symplectic form
\sigma
in (
2.127
) and the gradient in (
2.125
), but does not play a crucial role throughout the proof)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\theta(t)\\
y(t)\\
z(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{z}\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\right)\end{pmatrix},

(1.34)

where
\nabla_{z}\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
is the gradient vector field of
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
restricted to the subspace
H_{S^{\perp}}
. Indeed, one can show that if
(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))
is a solution to (
1.34
), then
f(t):=U_{\zeta}(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))
is a solution to the gSQG equation (
1.6
) (again, see Section
5
).

\bullet

A toy model 1: A perturbed Airy equation.

Recall that our goal at this moment is to see whether we can extract a dependence of the linear frequency on
\zeta
. Therefore the question becomes whether the new system (
1.34
) has a linear frequency that can be modulated by
\zeta
. Of course, the answer depends on the structure of
\mathcal{H}
. To this end, let us consider a simpler example, where we can see that the modulation of the linear frequency relies on the quartic homogeneous term of the Hamiltonian.

As a toy model, let us consider a perturbed Airy equation (see
[
6
,
8
,
9
,
65
]
for the results of relevant but more complicated models). We define a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(f):=\mathcal{G}_{2}(f)+\mathcal{G}_{4}(f),\text{ where }

(1.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}(f):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}f)^{2}(x)dx,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{4}(f):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}\end{subarray}}G({j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}})f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}\text{ for some $G:\mathbb{Z}^{4}\mapsto\mathbb{C}$}.

The associated nonlinear Hamiltonian equation to
\mathcal{G}
is written as (see the comparison with (
1.7
))

\displaystyle f_{t}=\partial_{x}(\nabla_{L^{2}}\mathcal{G}(f))=-\partial_{xxx}f+\partial_{x}(\nabla_{L^{2}}\mathcal{G}_{4}(f)).

(1.36)

The linearized Hamiltonian equation associated to
\mathcal{G}
depends on only the quadratic term
\mathcal{G}_{2}
and it corresponds to the Airy equation,

\displaystyle f_{t}=-\partial_{xxx}f.

(1.37)

It is trivial to see that the Airy equation possesses quasiperiodic solutions with the linear frequency
\omega^{\text{Airy}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
given by

\displaystyle(\overline{\omega}^{\text{Airy}})_{k}:=j_{k}^{3},\text{ for $S^{+}:=\left\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{\nu}\right\}$.}

(1.38)

At this point, the linearized equation does not reveal the modulation of the linear frequency by the amplitude. To make the computations easier, let us make the following assumptions on the quartic term
\mathcal{G}_{4}
:

(A1)

{G}(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})
is invariant under any permutation on
\left\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\right\}
. This assumption is simply to make the computations easier and can be assumed for general quartic Hamiltonian, since we can take the average of the summation in (
1.35
) over all the permutations.

(A2)

{G}
is supported only on modes
S
, that is,
{G}(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})=0
, if
j_{i}\notin S
for some
=1,2,3,4
. This is to focus on the contribution of
\zeta
through this example, since
\zeta
presents only in the modes in the set
S
(see (
1.31
)).

(A3)

If
G(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\neq 0
, then
j_{a}=-j_{b}
and
j_{c}=-j_{d}
for a permutation
(a,b,c,d)
of
(1,2,3,4)
. In other words, there is no nontrivial resonance in
\mathcal{G}_{4}
. Therefore, using the assumption
(A1)
and
(A2)
, we can define a
\nu\times\nu
matrix
\mathbb{G}
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}:=G(j_{i},-j_{i},j_{k},-j_{k})\text{ for $i,k=1,\ldots,\nu$ and $j_{i},j_{k}\in S^{+}$.}

(1.39)

To see the modulation of the frequency by
\zeta
, we compute the composition with the change of variables
U_{\zeta}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{G}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z).

(1.40)

For the quadratic term, we see that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{G}_{2}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{intro_change_val}}{=}\varepsilon^{-2b}\left(2\pi\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}j_{k}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}(j_{k}\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}j_{k}y_{k})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\varepsilon^{2b}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx\right)

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}(j_{k})^{3}y_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{airy_intro}}{=}C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\left(\overline{\omega}^{\text{Airy}}\cdot y\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx.

(1.41)

for some
C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}
that does not depend on
(\theta,y,z)
. For the quartic term, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{4,0}(f)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{g4_intro_1},\ref{Assumpp1_intro2}}{=}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S\end{subarray}}G(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{Assumpp1_intro1},\ref{Assumpp1_intro3}}{=}6\sum_{j_{1}=j_{2}\in S^{+}}G(j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2})|f_{j_{1}}|^{2}|f_{j_{2}}|^{2}+12\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+},\ j_{1}\neq j_{2}}G(j_{1},j_{2},-j_{1},-j_{2})|f_{j_{1}}|^{2}|f_{j_{2}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{G_symple}}{=}6\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{i}|f_{j_{i}}|^{4}+12\sum_{i,k=1,\ i\neq k}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}|f_{j_{i}}|^{2}|f_{j_{k}}|^{2}.

Therefore, using (
1.31
), we can see that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{G}_{4}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{intro_change_val}}{=}6\varepsilon^{-2b}\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{i}\varepsilon^{4}(j_{i}\zeta_{i}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}j_{i}y_{i})^{2}

\displaystyle+12\varepsilon^{-2b}\sum_{i,k=1,\ i\neq k}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}\varepsilon^{4}(j_{i}\zeta_{i}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}j_{i}y_{i})(j_{k}\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}j_{k}y_{k})

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+12\varepsilon^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{i}j_{i}^{2}\zeta_{i}y_{i}+2\sum_{i,k=1,\ i\neq k}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}j_{i}j_{k}\zeta_{i}y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\ +6\varepsilon^{2b}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{i}j_{i}^{2}y_{i}^{2}+2\sum_{i,k=1,\ i\neq k}^{\nu}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}j_{i}j_{k}y_{i}y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+12\varepsilon^{2}G^{\text{mod}}\zeta\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}G^{\text{mod}}y\cdot y,

(1.42)

where
G^{\text{mod}}
is a
\nu\times\nu
symmetric matrix defined as

\displaystyle(G^{\text{mod}})^{i}_{k}:=\begin{cases}j_{i}^{2}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{i}&\text{ if $i=k$}\\
2j_{i}j_{k}\mathbb{G}^{i}_{k}&\text{ if $i\neq k$}\end{cases},\text{ for $j_{i},j_{k}\in S^{+}=\left\{j_{1}<\cdots<j_{\nu}\right\}$.}

(1.43)

Plugging (

∙

‣
1.2.1.3
) and (
1.41
) into (
1.40
), we see a normal form of the nonlinear Hamiltonian
\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+\left(2\pi\overline{\omega}^{\text{Airy}}+12\varepsilon^{2}G^{\text{mod}}\zeta\right)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}G^{\text{mod}}y\cdot y+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}G^{\text{mod}}y\cdot y+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx,

(1.44)

where

\displaystyle\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta):=\overline{\omega}^{\text{Airy}}+\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}G^{\text{mod}}\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}.

(1.45)

Recall that we can write the Hamiltonian equation (
1.36
) in the phase space
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
as another Hamiltonian equation in the new phase space
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
by pulling back the vector field by
U_{\zeta}
, which should be written as (compare to (
1.34
))

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\theta(t)\\
y(t)\\
z(t)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{z}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\right)\end{pmatrix}\overset{\eqref{normal_form_42}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2b}G^{\text{mod}}y(t)\\
0\\
-\partial_{xxx}(z(t))\end{pmatrix}.

(1.46)

From the above equation, we can easily see that the trivial embedding
i_{triv}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
, defined as

\displaystyle i_{triv}(\varphi):=(\varphi,0,0).

(1.47)

is invariant under the vector field in (
1.46
), and the solution to (
1.46
) can be written as

\displaystyle(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))=i_{triv}(\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)t)=(\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)t,0,0).

(1.48)

Clearly, the solution (
1.48
) is time-quasiperiodic because each
“angular component”

\theta_{i}(t)
oscillates with frequency
(\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta))_{i}
for each
i=1,\ldots,\nu
and the frequency vector
\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)
is modulated by the amplitude
\zeta
through the relation in (
1.45
), which cannot be observed by just looking at the linear Airy equation (
1.37
). Therefore, a quasiperiodic solution to (
1.36
) can be obtained as
f(t):=U_{\zeta}(i_{triv}(\omega^{\text{Airy}}(\zeta)t))
. As shown in this example, our strategy to study the equation (
1.34
) is to derive a
“normal form”
where we can see a modulation of the frequency by the amplitude
\zeta
at the linear level of the equation (that is, quadratic level of the Hamiltonian).

1.2.1.4
Weak Birkhoff normal form.

Our Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in (
1.6
) does not possess a simple structure as in the toy-model example, therefore it is hopeless to expect
\mathcal{H}_{\zeta}
in (
1.33
) to have a simple form as in (

∙

‣
1.2.1.3
). However, we will construct a symplectic transformation
\Phi^{WB}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
so that defining another Hamiltonian
H
as

\displaystyle H(f):=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}(f),

(1.49)

we can rewrite
H
, under the composition with
U_{\zeta}
, as (compare below with (

∙

‣
1.2.1.3
))

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon^{-2b}H\circ(U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z))=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+P(\theta,y,z),

(1.50)

where

\displaystyle\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)=\overline{\omega}+\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{A}\zeta,\text{ (see \eqref{linear_fre_intro2} for the definition of $\overline{\omega}$)},

(1.51)

for some
\nu\times\nu
symmetric matrix
\mathbb{A}
, for some
\theta
-dependent bilinear form
\mathcal{N}(\theta)
on
H_{S^{\perp}}
, and for some perturbation
P
satisfying some smallness condition. Note that in (
1.50
), the bilinear form
\mathcal{N}
and the perturbation
P
depend on
\zeta
, while we do not denote its dependence to avoid notational complication.

Certainly, the following concerns need to be taken into account:

(C1)

What structure of
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
do we need? More precisely, what do we require on the matrix
\mathbb{A}
?.

(C2)

How to construct
\Phi^{WB}
so that
H
defined by (
1.49
) has the structure in (
1.50
) with sufficiently small
P
.

We postpone more detailed comments on the above concerns to the next paragraph but for now focus on how to transform the functional equation (
1.21
), according to the new Hamiltonian (
1.50
). By requiring
\Phi^{WB}
to be symplectic, we have that the Hamiltonian equation associated to
\mathcal{H}
is equivalent to the Hamiltonian equation associated to
H
, therefore, again pulling back the Hamiltonian vector field of
H
by
U_{\zeta}
. Thus we are led to study the equation:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\theta(t)\\
y(t)\\
z(t)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{z}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\right)\end{pmatrix}

(1.52)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{newHamiltonian_int11ro}}{=}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)\\
\partial_{x}((\mathcal{N}(\theta))^{T}[z])\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}P(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}P(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{z}P(\theta,y,z)\right)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=:X_{H_{\zeta}}(\theta(t),y(t),z(t)),

where
z\mapsto\mathcal{N}(\theta)^{T}[z]
is the linear map on
H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{N}(\theta)^{T}[z](x)h(x)dx=\nabla_{z}(\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z))[h],\text{ for all $h\in S^{\perp}$.}

If
t\mapsto i_{\infty}(\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)t)\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
is a quasiperiodic solution to (
1.52
), then the quasiperiodic solution to the gSQG equation (
1.5
) will be recovered by

f(t)=\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\infty}(\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)t))).

Assuming
P\equiv 0
, the equation (
1.52
) indeed possesses the trivial embedding (
1.47
) as a quasiperiodic solution with the frequency vector
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
. Therefore, our main goal of this monograph becomes to study whether such an embedding can persist under a perturbation
P
in the system (
1.52
). Note that taking into account the dependence of the frequency on
\zeta
, more precise statements to be proved are that
“for almost every
\zeta
”
in (
1.30
), the quasiperiodic solution with frequency
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
can survive under the perturbation. Making an ansatz,

\displaystyle\text{$t\mapsto i(\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)t)$ solves \eqref{Hamil_final_intro1}},\text{ for some $i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}$, $i(\varphi)=:(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))$},

(1.53)

we are led to find
i
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(i):=\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))=0.

(1.54)

Now, let us discuss the concerns
(C1)
-
(C2)
.

\bullet

Item
(C1)
: Frequency vector
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
and use of
\omega
as a parameter.

Let us first make it clear why we need to care about the structure of
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
. We recall from (
1.30
) that our parameter
\zeta
lies in a fixed subset
[1,2]^{\nu}
, and we want to perform the iteration (
1.61
) for sufficiently many
\zeta
in
[1,2]^{\nu}
. Denoting

\displaystyle\Omega:=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:\omega=\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta),\ \zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}\right\},

(1.55)

we can only hope that
“almost every”

\omega
in
\Omega
satisfy all the necessary non-resonance conditions, such as (
1.28
). This does not necessarily imply that for
“almost every”

\zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}
,
\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
satisfies the necessary non-resonance conditions, especially in case
\zeta\mapsto\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
is not one-to-one. Thus, we require that the matrix
\mathbb{A}
in (
1.51
) is invertible. The explicit form of
\mathbb{A}
is not important at this point, but it is important to note that
\mathbb{A}
is completely determined by the choice of the set
S^{+}
in (
1.16
). The invertibility of
\mathbb{A}
is one of the
“non-resonance conditions”
described in the statement of Theorem
1.2
. In the proof, this condition will be verified (See Subsection
5.1
and Proposition
5.5
).

Once we have the invertibility of the map
\zeta\mapsto\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)
, we will use the frequency
\omega
as a parameter of the system (
1.52
) and think of
\zeta
as a quantity determined by
\omega
. More precisely, defining

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:\omega=\overline{\omega}+\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{A}\zeta,\text{ for }\zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}\right\},

(1.56)

we think of the amplitude
\zeta
to be a function of
\omega
for
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. With a slight abuse of notation, we will still denote
\zeta
as if it is an independent variable, but it is actually a function of
\omega
, determined by

\displaystyle\zeta=\zeta(\omega):=\left(\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{A}\right)^{-1}(\omega-\overline{\omega}),\text{ for $\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}$.}

(1.57)

Then the Hamiltonian functional (
1.50
), the system (
1.52
) and the functional equation (
1.54
) become

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\omega\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+P(\theta,y,z),

(1.58)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\theta(t)\\
y(t)\\
z(t)\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)\\
\partial_{x}((\mathcal{N}(\theta))^{T}[z])\end{pmatrix}}+{\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}P(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}P(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{z}P(\theta,y,z)\right)\end{pmatrix}}=:X_{H_{\zeta}}(\theta(t),y(t),z(t)),

(1.59)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)

\displaystyle:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))=\begin{pmatrix}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\theta(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}y(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}z(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))=0.

(1.60)

Here
X_{H_{\zeta}}=X_{H_{\zeta(\omega)}}
is now thought of as an
\omega
-dependent vector field (instead of a
\zeta
-dependent vector field) and the Hamiltonian
H_{\zeta}
, as well as
\mathcal{N}
and
P
, depends on
\omega
implicitly through (
1.57
). Clearly, if an embedding
i_{\infty}
solves (
1.60
) with some
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, then
i_{\infty}
solves (
1.54
) with
\zeta(\omega)
. The reason why we use
\omega
as a parameter is that by doing so, it is easier to check the non-resonance conditions such as (
1.25
) or (
1.28
) throughout the proof.

Now, we transform the initial iteration scheme given in (
1.22
), since our new system (
1.59
) has a slightly different form, compared to (
1.20
). The main scheme is quite same as described in (
1.22
) based on Newton’s method (more precisely, Nash-Moser scheme as we discussed before): Noting that the trivial embedding (
1.47
) solves (
1.59
) without the perturbation
P
, we set up a formal sequence of approximate solutions
i_{n}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}i_{0}:=i_{triv},\quad i_{n+1}:=i_{n}-(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{n}))^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{n})],\text{ for $n\geq 0$},\\
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]:=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\bigg|_{t=0}\overset{\eqref{actual_functioanleq}}{=}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}-d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}],\end{cases}

(1.61)

and study the convergence of
i_{n}
to a solution to (
1.60
). In the iteration scheme (
1.61
), we do not expect the inverse of the linearized operator
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
to be obtained for every
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, but we select
\omega
such that
\omega
satisfies all the necessary non-resonance conditions to obtain an inverse of the linearized operator.

Before we close our discussion concerning the use of
\omega
as a parameter, we emphasize that the constant
\gamma
arising in the Diophantine condition (
1.28
) needs to be small depending on
\varepsilon
. We wish to select non-resonant frequencies from the set
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, that is, we wish the set (for some fixed
\tau>0
)

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}:=\left\{\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}:|\omega\cdot l|>\gamma|l|^{\tau},\text{ for all $l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}$}\right\}

(1.62)

to take asymptotically full measure in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
.
However,
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.56
) is an
\varepsilon^{2}
-neighborhood of
\overline{\omega}
. Therefore, in case
\overline{\omega}
is resonant, that is,
\overline{\omega}\cdot l_{*}=0
for some
l_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}
, we have

|\omega\cdot l_{*}|\leq|(\omega-\overline{\omega})\cdot l_{*}|+|\overline{\omega}\cdot l_{*}|\leq|\omega-\overline{\omega}||l_{*}|\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}|l_{*}|,\text{ for all $\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}$}.

Thus, it is not, in general, possible for
\Omega_{0}
to obtain asymptotically full measure in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, unless
\gamma=o(\varepsilon^{2})
. For this reason, we will fix
\gamma
to be

\displaystyle\gamma:=\varepsilon^{2b},\text{ where $b>1$ is chosen as in \eqref{intro_change_val}.}

(1.63)

\bullet

Item
(C2)
: Construction of
\Phi^{WB}
.
Now, we discuss how to construct the transformation
\Phi^{WB}
so that we can obtain (
1.50
) through (
1.49
).

Before we start, we first fix some notation. In view of (
1.29
), we denote by
v
and
z
the variables in spaces
H_{S}
and
H_{S^{\perp}}
respectively, so that a given
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
can be written as

\displaystyle f=v+z,\text{ for some $v\in H_{S}$ and $z\in H_{S^{\perp}}$ in a unique way.}

(1.64)

The variables
v
and
z
will be called a
“tangential variable”
and a
“normal variable”
.
We define
v_{\zeta}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\mapsto H_{S}
by

\displaystyle v_{\zeta}(\theta,y):=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+y_{k})}e^{\text{i}(\theta_{k}+j_{k}x)},

(1.65)

so that
U_{\zeta}
in (
1.31
) can be written as

\displaystyle U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)=\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y)+\varepsilon^{b}z=:\varepsilon{v_{\varepsilon}}(\theta,y)+\varepsilon^{b}z.

(1.66)

Let us consider a homogeneous expansion of
H
defined by (
1.49
):

\displaystyle H(f)=H_{2}(f)+H_{3}(f)+H_{4}(f)+H_{5}(f)+H_{\geq 6}(f),

(1.67)

where
H_{i}
is homogeneous of degree
i
for
i=1,\ldots 5
, and
H_{\geq 6}
collects all the terms that are homogenous of degree at least
6
. Also, for each
H_{i}
and
0\leq k\leq i
, we denote by
H_{i,k}
the term in
H_{i}
that is homogeneous of degree
k
in the variable
z
. For example, recalling the Airy equation in (
1.35
), we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2}(f)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{g4_intro_1}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}f)^{2}(x)dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}v)^{2}(x)dx+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}(x)dx=\mathcal{G}_{2,0}(f)+\mathcal{G}_{2,2}(f).

The reason of introducing the above notation is to see the contribution of
v
and
z
in each homogeneous term
H_{i}
separately.

Using the above notation, we can rearrange the expansion in (
1.67
) as

\displaystyle H(f)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{(H_{2,0}+H_{4,0})}_{=:Z_{0}(f)}+\underbrace{(H_{2,1}+H_{3,0}+H_{3,1}+H_{4,1}+H_{5,0}+H_{5,1})}_{=:Z_{1}(f)}+\underbrace{(H_{2,2}+H_{3,2}+H_{4,2})}_{=:Z_{2}(f)}

(1.68)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{(H_{3,3}+H_{4,3}+H_{4,4}+\sum_{k=2}^{5}H_{5,k}+H_{\geq 6})}_{=:Z_{3}(f)}.

Using the change of variables from (
1.64
) and (
1.65
), and splitting
Z_{2}(f)=Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)
into
Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,0)+z)+(Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)-Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,0)+z))
, we can rewrite the above as

\displaystyle H(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)

\displaystyle=Z_{0}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y))+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+\tilde{P}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z),

(1.69)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)

\displaystyle:=Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,0)+z),

(1.70)

\displaystyle\tilde{P}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)

\displaystyle:=Z_{1}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)+(Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z)-Z_{2}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,0)+z))+Z_{3}(v_{\zeta}(\theta,y)+z).

Therefore, using (
1.66
), and the homogeneity of each
H_{i,k}
in (
1.68
), we obtain

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{newHamiltonian_int11ro}}{=}\varepsilon^{-2b}H(U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z))

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Uzeta_rewrite_intro}}{=}\varepsilon^{-2b}H(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y)+\varepsilon^{b}z)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{invandz_intro1}}{=}\varepsilon^{-2b}Z_{0}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y))+\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(\varepsilon^{b}z,\varepsilon^{b}z)+\varepsilon^{-2b}\tilde{P}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y)+\varepsilon^{b}z)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-2b}Z_{0}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y))+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+\varepsilon^{-2b}\tilde{P}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y)+\varepsilon^{b}z),

(1.71)

where the last equality follows from the homogeneity of
z\mapsto\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)
, which can be seen from its definition in (
1.70
) and the definition of
Z_{2}
in (
1.68
) noticing that
H_{2,2},H_{3,2}
and
H_{4,2}
are homogeneous of degree
2
in the normal variable
z
. Comparing the above formula to (
1.50
), which we aim to obtain, we now see that in order to have a normal form as in (
1.50
), we need to choose
\Phi^{WB}
in (
1.49
) so that

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{-2b}Z_{0}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y))

\displaystyle=C_{\varepsilon,\zeta}+2\pi\omega^{\text{gSQG}}(\zeta)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y.

(1.72)

Once
\Phi^{WB}
is chosen, the perturbation
P
in (
1.50
) is given by

\displaystyle P(\theta,y,z)=\varepsilon^{-2b}\tilde{P}(\varepsilon v_{\zeta}(\theta,\varepsilon^{2(b-1)})+\varepsilon^{b}z),

(1.73)

which can be seen from the comparison between (

∙

‣
1.2.1.4
) and (
1.50
).
In our proof, we will construct
\Phi^{WB}
so that (see Proposition
4.4
):

(WB1)

(
1.72
) holds. Note that
Z_{0}
, that is
H_{2,0}+H_{4,0}
(see (
1.68
)), is restricted to the tangential space
H_{S}
. As we saw in the example of the Airy equation (
1.35
), whether (
1.72
) holds or not depends on the non-existence of nontrivial resonances (see assumption
(A3)
above the equation (
1.39
)).

(WB2)

Z_{1}(f)\equiv 0
in (
1.68
), therefore, (
1.68
) reduces to

\displaystyle H=(H_{2,0}+H_{4,0})+(H_{2,2}+H_{3,2}+H_{4,2})+(H_{3,3}+H_{4,3}+H_{4,4}+\sum_{k=2}^{5}H_{5,k}+H_{\geq 6}).

(1.74)

We do not write the exact formula here, but compare the structure above to the formulae in Proposition
4.4
. From (
1.73
), we see that the higher the degree of homogeneity of
\tilde{P}
is, the smaller
P
becomes, thanks to the rescaling factor
\varepsilon
. Compared to
Z_{3}
,
Z_{1}
has the low homogeneous contribution to
\tilde{P}
in (
1.70
) and the elimination of such low homogeneous contribution yields a sufficient smallness of
P_{\zeta}
to perform the iteration (
1.61
) (See Lemma
6.2
).

In regards to
(WB2)
, indeed, one can formally check whether the perturbative term
P
, defined in (
1.73
) and (
1.70
) assuming
Z_{1}\equiv 0
, is small enough. To see this,
counting the degree of homogeneity of each term in (
1.68
), one can find formally (see (
6.16
) for the precise estimates) that:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}(i_{triv})|

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon^{6-2b}),

(1.75)

\displaystyle|d_{i}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{triv})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|

\displaystyle=O(\varepsilon|\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}|^{2}).

(1.76)

Furthermore, for
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
in (
1.62
), the best possible estimate we can expect for
(D_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i))^{-1}
would be (similar to (
1.27
))

\displaystyle|d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i))^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|=O(\gamma^{-1}|\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}|).

(1.77)

Therefore, the first iteration in (
1.61
) formally gives us that

\displaystyle|i_{1}-i_{0}|\overset{\eqref{refined_sequence_intro2}}{=}|d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{triv}))^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{triv})]|\overset{\eqref{formal_inverse_1intro}}{\lesssim}\gamma^{-1}|\mathcal{F}(i_{triv})|\overset{\eqref{formal_estimate_1intro1}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1},

(1.78)

while

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{1})-\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})[i_{1}-i_{0}]|+|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})+d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})[i_{1}-i_{0}]|

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{refined_sequence_intro2}}{=}|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{1})-\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})[i_{1}-i_{0}]|+\underbrace{|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})[(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0}))^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})]]}_{=0}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{formal_estimate_1intro2}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon|i_{1}-i_{0}|^{2}\overset{\eqref{first_intro_large11}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon(\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1})^{2}\overset{\eqref{gamma_impose_intro1}}{=}\varepsilon^{13-8b}.

(1.79)

Since
\varepsilon^{13-8b}\leq\varepsilon^{6-2b}
for
b\in(1,1+1/6)
(see (
1.31
) for the range of
b
), we see that the bound of
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{1})
in (

∙

‣
1.2.1.4
) has indeed improved compared to
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{triv})
in (
1.75
). This formal computation can be thought of as evidence that
P
in (
1.73
) is small enough for the iteration to close. Furthermore, from the usual super-exponential convergence rate of the Nash-Moser scheme, we expect that each
i_{n}
stays close to the trivial embedding,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{I}_{n}|=O(\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1})\text{ and }|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{n})|=O(\varepsilon^{6-2b}),\text{ for each $n\geq 0$},\text{ where }\mathfrak{I}_{n}:=i_{n}-i_{triv}.

(1.80)

Naturally, the above computations are not rigorous at all. However in our proof, we will see that (
1.80
) actually holds true (see (
11.59
)). Therefore, in the further analysis of the invertibility of the linearized operator
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto d_{i}\mathcal{F}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
, which we will discuss in more details later, we will focus on the embeddings
i
which are close enough to the trivial embedding
i_{triv}
.

Lastly, we note that such a transformation
\Phi^{WB}
will be constructed (in Section
4
) by means of time-
1
flow maps of auxiliary Hamiltonian equations, which guarantee that the resulting transformation is symplectic. We also note that such a procedure only modifies a finite number of Fourier modes, which can be deduced from the fact that
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
in (
1.68
) involve at most one normal variable. Therefore, the flow maps that will be used to construct
\Phi^{WB}
can be constructed safely in terms of the well-posedness of the auxiliary Hamiltonian equations.

1.2.1.5
Summary of part 1.

In the above discussion, we explored how to extract an internal parameter yielding a parameter-dependent Hamiltonian
H_{\zeta}
in (
1.58
) with sufficiently small perturbative term
P
. Afterwards, we transformed the search for a quasiperiodic solution to the search for a zero of the functional equation in (
1.60
), which can be solved by means of the iterative scheme in (
1.61
).

1.2.2
Sketch of the proof, Part 2: Analysis of the linearized operator

In the second part of the sketch of the proof, we discuss in more details how to
“invert”
the linearized operator arising in the iterative scheme (
1.61
):

\displaystyle\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}],

(1.81)

for a fixed embedding
i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
. Denoting the embedding
i
as

\displaystyle i:\varphi\mapsto(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi)),

(1.82)

we think of
\theta,y
as smooth functions on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
and
z
as a smooth function on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{T}
, such that
z(\varphi)
for each
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
is restricted to the normal space
H_{S^{\perp}}
(see (
1.29
) for
H_{S^{\perp}}
). Such functions will be measured in the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu})\times H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})
. In the analysis of the linearized operator
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
, we will assume on
i
that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu}\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1},\text{ for some $\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}$, $s_{0},\mu,\mathtt{C}>0$},\text{ where $\mathfrak{I}:=i-i_{triv}$.}

(1.83)

Before we discuss the linearized operator, some comments to understand the above assumption regarding the norm
\rVert\cdot\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu}
, the set
\Omega_{1}
, the constants
s_{0},\mu,\mathtt{C}>0
and some motivation of such an assumption will follow:

\bullet

Constants arising in the proof
. We first clarify some constants arising in the proof. Let us denote

\displaystyle\mathtt{p}:=\left\{(\alpha,\nu,s_{0}):\alpha\in(1,2),\ 2\leq\nu\in\mathbb{N},\ s_{0}\geq\frac{\nu+2}{2}\right\}.

(1.84)

The
3
-tuple
\mathtt{p}
consists of the quantities that we can freely choose. For instance, if we want to find a quasiperiodic solution to the gSQG equation for
\alpha=3/2
that can be represented by a
3
-dimensional embedded torus
i_{\infty}
(see Definition
1.1
) satisfying the Sobolev regularity

\rVert i_{\infty}\rVert_{H^{10}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu})\times H^{10}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu})\times H^{10}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})}<\infty,

then we can just choose
\mathtt{p}=(3/2,3,10)
. The requirement of
s_{0}\geq\frac{\nu+2}{2}
is to guarantee that the corresponding Sobolev space becomes an algebra. Once
\mathtt{p}
is fixed, then our previous discussion can be summarized as follows: We choose the tangential sites
S^{+}
as in (
1.16
) (thus the sets of modes
S
and
S^{\perp}
are fixed accordingly) so that we can derive a weak Birkhoff normal form as in (
1.50
), and we obtain an
\omega
-dependent functional equation (
1.60
) for
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.56
). Therefore, we are led to study the convergence of the iteration (
1.61
). We will also consider the following constants fixed throughout the proof:

\displaystyle b\in(1,1+1/12),\quad\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N},\quad\tau:=\frac{2}{\alpha-1}+\nu+2.

(1.85)

\circ

Constant
b\in(1,1+1/12)
: This appears in the introduction of the action-angle variables in (
1.31
), where we already discussed why
b
needs to be larger than
1
. For some technical reasons, we will consider
b
strictly less than
1+1/12
. Note that the constant
b
also appears in the Diophantine condition to measure the non-resonance of frequency vectors (see (
1.62
) and (
1.63
)).

\circ

Constant
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
: As stated in our main theorem (Theorem
1.2
), we will find solutions that are invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation, as in (
1.14
) (at the patch level, this corresponds to the invariance under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
- rotation). For this purpose, we will choose
S^{+}
in (
1.16
) to be multiples of
\mathtt{M}
(see Remark
1.6
and Proposition
5.5
). We note that
\mathtt{M}
does play a crucial role in the convergence of the iteration scheme (its role mainly lies in the rigorous analysis of the non-resonance conditions, see Appendix
B
).

\circ

Constant
\tau
: The constant
\tau
arises in non-resonance conditions, for example in (
1.62
). The motivation is that
\tau
needs to be large enough to obtain the measure estimate for the non-resonance frequencies (See Proposition
11.7
).

\bullet

Size assumption (
1.83
) on the embedding
i
.
Firstly, for the definition of the norm
\rVert\cdot\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}
, we refer to (
2.4
) and (
2.7
). To understand the assumption (
1.83
), note that even though the iteration (
1.61
) starts with
i_{triv}
, which is independent of the parameter
\omega
, all the other approximate solutions
i_{n}
for
n>0
will implicitly depend on
\omega
since the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
and the linearized operator
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto D_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
depend on
\omega
. Hence, the dependence of the embeddings on
\omega
must be considered as well, and we will measure it in a Lipschitz way (
2.7
). Also, as we noted, we do not expect that the iteration (
1.61
) runs for every
\omega
. Therefore, at each iteration, we will exclude some resonant frequencies for which the next approximate solution may not be well-defined. Indeed, in our proof, we will have a sequence of nested sets
\mathcal{G}_{n+1}\subset\mathcal{G}_{n}\subset\Omega_{0}
such that
n
-th approximate solution
i_{n}
is well-defined for
\omega\in\mathcal{G}_{n}
(see item
(2)
of Proposition
11.1
). Therefore, to analyze the linearized operator at a given embedding
i
, we assume that
i
is defined for
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
for some set
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}
.

Another motivation that we need to keep in mind is that the approximate solutions in our iteration are expected to stay close to the trivial embedding, in view of our formal computations in (
1.80
). For this reason, we introduce the constant
\mathtt{C}
in the assumption (
1.83
), and presumably, we expect such a constant to depend on only
\mathtt{p},{S^{+}}
and the constant
b
(in (
1.84
), (
1.16
) and (
1.85
) respectively), since these quantities completely determine the functional equation (
1.60
) (see (
11.59
)).

Lastly,
\mu
in (
1.83
) denotes an additional necessary regularity to obtain numerous estimates arising in the analysis of the linearized operator. Roughly speaking, in order to obtain the invertibility or desired estimates in the analysis of the linearized operator at
i
, we need the embedding
i
to be more regular than
H^{s_{0}}
, in which we wish to see a convergence of the approximate solutions. We emphasize that as long as such an additional regularity
\mu
depends on only
\mathtt{p}
in (
1.84
), which is fixed from the beginning, the usual Nash-Moser scheme tells us that the sequence of approximate solutions in the iteration can be constructed in such a way that they converge in
H^{s_{0}}
, diverge in a finer space (
H^{\mathtt{S}}
for some very large
\mathtt{S}\gg s_{0}
), while stay small in the intermediate space
H^{s_{0}+\mu}
(interpolating the low-high norms. See item
(2)
of Proposition
11.1
). Thus our analysis (especially Section
7
-
10
) should read as
“there exists
\mu(\mathtt{p})>0
such that if (
1.83
) holds for
\mu=\mu(\mathtt{p})
, then all the necessary properties of the linearized operator hold”
. Afterwards, we will see every approximate solution satisfies such an assumption. See (
11.59
). The upper bound of
\mu
is computable in principle, but we do not do so to avoid additional complexity of the proof. Instead, we will only focus on the non-dependence of
\mu
on the parameters other than
\mathtt{p}
. We will always assume
\mu
to be as large as we want, as long as it depends only on
\mathtt{p}
.

1.2.2.1
Approximate inverse.

In practice, we will not look for the actual inverse,
(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i))^{-1}
. Instead, we will look for an
approximate inverse
,
T(i)
, such that

\displaystyle|(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)\circ T(i)-I)[g]|=O(|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)|),\text{ where $I$ is the identity operator}.

(1.86)

(See (
7.3
) for the actual estimate we obtain in our proof). The estimate in (
7.3
) is more complicated due to the consideration of the dependence on
\omega
and the contribution of the high modes when
s\gg s_{0}
, which is split into the ones from from
i_{0},Z:=\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})
and
g
).
Although the operator
T(i)
above is not the actual inverse, we expect that as the approximate solutions in (
1.61
) approach to the solution
i_{\infty}
(that is
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{n})
gets smaller in the iteration), the error becomes smaller and smaller, therefore eventually,
T(i_{\infty})
is indeed equal to the actual inverse. Thus, our iteration scheme (
1.61
) is now replaced by

\displaystyle i_{0}:=i_{triv},\quad i_{n+1}:=i_{n}-T(i_{n})[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{n})].

(1.87)

The error of size
O(|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)|)
in (
1.86
) can be managed in the usual Nash-Moser scheme. We also note that the operator
T(i)
depends on
\omega
as well, since so do
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
and
i
. However, we omit the notation of such dependence to avoid notational complexity.

Now the question is how we can find such an approximate inverse
T(i)
to run the iteration (
1.87
). To this end, we follow the theory of Berti and Bolle developed in
[
12
]
, which tells us that an approximate inverse of
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
can be obtained by an approximate inverse in the normal direction. In order to see this sentence in more detail, let us suppose
i_{\infty}
is a solution,
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})=0
, and investigate what the linearized operator
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})
looks like, because we can expect that the different nature between
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)
and
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})
can be measured quantitatively by
|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)|
, which is admissible in view of (
1.86
).

The key implication in
[
12
]
is the derivation of a normal form near invariant tori. Indeed
[
12
, Theorem 1]
tells us that (roughly speaking) if

\displaystyle\text{$i_{\infty}$ solves $\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})=0$},

(1.88)

there exists a symplectic diffeomorphism
G:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
such that
G(i_{triv}(\varphi))=i_{\infty}(\varphi)
. Moreover the Hamiltonian
K_{\infty}
, defined as

\displaystyle K_{\infty}(\psi,\eta,w):=H_{\zeta}\circ G(\psi,\eta,w),\text{ for $(\psi,\eta,w)\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}$},

(1.89)

possesses a normal form such that

\displaystyle K_{\infty}(\psi,\eta,w)

\displaystyle=C+2\pi\omega\cdot\eta

(1.90)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\eta}^{2}(K_{\infty})(\psi,0,0)[\eta,\eta]+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{w}^{2}(K_{\infty})(\psi,0,0)[w,w]+\partial_{\eta}\nabla_{w}(K_{\infty})(\psi,0,0)[\eta,w]

\displaystyle+O_{3}(\eta,w),

where
\nabla_{w}^{2}
denotes the Hessian operator in the variable
w
and
O_{3}(\eta,w)
collects all the terms that are homogeneous of degree at least
3
. This result implies that the linearized Hamiltonian vector field of
K_{\infty}
on the torus

\left\{(\psi,0,0)\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}:\psi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\right\}

can be easily computed from (
1.90
) (since such a linearized vector field does not depend on the cubic contribution of
\eta,w
). With simple computations, one can obtain that (in the direction
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}:=(\hat{\psi},\hat{\eta},\hat{w})
)

\displaystyle d_{i}X_{K_{\infty}}(i_{triv}(\psi))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\eta}K_{\infty}(i_{triv}(\psi)+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\psi}K_{\infty}(i_{triv}(\psi)+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\\
\partial_{x}\left(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty}(i_{triv}(\psi)+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\right)\end{pmatrix}\bigg|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\partial_{\eta}^{2}(K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi))[\hat{\eta}]+(\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi)))^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)\\
0\\
\partial_{x}(\nabla_{w}^{2}(K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi))[\hat{w}])+\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi))[\hat{\eta}]\end{pmatrix},

(1.91)

where
(\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi)))^{T}
denotes the linear map
H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
such that for all
