\hat{w}\in H_{S^{\perp}},\hat{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
,

(\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\psi)))^{T}[\hat{w}]\cdot\hat{\eta}=\frac{d}{dt}(\hat{w},\nabla_{w}K_{\infty}(\psi,t\hat{\eta},0))_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}|_{t=0}.

Therefore, the linearization of
i\mapsto\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i-X_{K_{\infty}}(i)
at
i_{triv}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{\infty}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]:=\begin{pmatrix}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\psi}(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\eta}(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{w}(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\partial_{\eta}^{2}(K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}]+(\partial_{\eta}\nabla(K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\varphi)))^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)\\
0\\
\partial_{x}(\nabla_{w}^{2}(K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{w}])+\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K_{\infty})(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}]\end{pmatrix}.

(1.92)

As one might have already noticed from (
1.89
), the linearized operator,
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto\mathbb{D}_{\infty}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
, is indeed the
“pullback”
of the linearized operator of
i\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)
at
i_{\infty}
. More precisely, one can obtain (we omit the computations here but one can easily follow the proof of Proposition
7.3
)

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=DG(i_{triv})\circ\mathbb{D}_{\infty}\circ(DG(i_{triv}))^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}].

(1.93)

Therefore, the invertibility of
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\infty})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
reduces to the invertibility of
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto\mathbb{D}_{\infty}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
.

From (
1.87
), recall that we need to find an approximate inverse at each
i_{n}
, which is not necessarily a solution as in (
1.88
). However the consequence of
[
12
]
even tells us that given
i
, which is not necessarily a solution, there exists a symplectic diffeomorphism
G_{\delta}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
such that defining

\displaystyle K(\psi,\eta,w)

\displaystyle:=H_{\zeta}\circ G_{\delta}(\psi,\eta,w),

(1.94)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\psi}(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\eta}(\varphi)\\
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{w}(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\partial_{\eta}^{2}(K)(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}]+(\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K)(i_{triv}(\varphi)))^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)\\
0\\
\partial_{x}(\nabla_{w}^{2}(K)(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{w}])+\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K)(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}]\end{pmatrix},

(1.95)

it holds that (compare below with (
1.93
))

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)=DG_{\delta}(i_{triv})\circ\mathbb{D}\circ(DG_{\delta}(i_{triv}))^{-1}+O(|\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)|).

(1.96)

(See (
7.58
) and (
7
) for the precise result of (
1.96
) in our proof).
Hence, if
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\mapsto\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
is invertible, then it is expected to be an approximate inverse, since the difference from the actual inverse can be quantitatively estimated by the size of
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)
. This leads us to study the invertibility of
\mathbb{D}
.

Now, let us see how to achieve the invertibility of the linear operator
\mathbb{D}
defined in (
1.95
). To simplify the notation, we denote
K_{ij}(\varphi):=(\partial_{\eta})^{i}(\nabla_{w})^{j}K(\varphi,0,0)
, that is,

\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))=:K_{20}(\varphi),\quad\partial_{\eta}(\nabla_{w}K)(i_{triv}(\varphi))=:K_{11}(\varphi),\quad\nabla_{w}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))=:K_{02}(\varphi).

(1.97)

Recalling that
K
maps
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
to
\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
, we can think of
K_{20}(\varphi)
as a
\varphi
-dependent
\nu\times\nu
symmetric matrix,
K_{11}(\varphi)
as a
\varphi
-dependent linear operator from
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
to
H_{S^{\perp}}
, and
K_{02}(\varphi)
as a
\varphi
-dependent linear operator from
H_{S^{\perp}}
to
H_{S^{\perp}}
. Therefore, given
g
, denoted as

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\ni\varphi\mapsto g(\varphi)=(g_{1}(\varphi),g_{2}(\varphi),g_{3}(\varphi))\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}},

we aim to find
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}(\varphi)=(\hat{\psi}(\varphi),\hat{\eta}(\varphi),\hat{w}(\varphi))
such that
\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=g
, that is,

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\psi}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]+K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)

\displaystyle=g_{1},

(1.98a)

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\eta}

\displaystyle=g_{2},

(1.98b)

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{w}-\partial_{x}(K_{02}[\hat{w}])-K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]

\displaystyle=g_{3}.

(1.98c)

We start with the simplest equation, the second one. One necessary condition for (
1.98b
) to have a solution
\hat{\eta}
is that
g_{2}
has zero-average in
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, which can be easily seen from the elementary Stokes theorem. In order to overcome this issue, the authors in
[
8
,
12
]
introduced an extra factor in the Hamiltonian. In our proof, we restrict the search for an invariant embedding to the search for an invariant
reversible embedding
which ensures that we only need to solve (
1.98b
) for
g_{2}
such that
g_{2}(\varphi)=-g_{2}(\varphi)
(In the statement of Proposition
7.3
, we consider the approximate inverse
T
as a map restricted to the space
Y_{i}
, which is defined in (
2.138
)). In such a case, the average of
g_{2}
is always zero. Therefore (
1.98b
) has a solution when
\omega
is Diophantine. More precisely, if
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
(see (
1.62
)) and
g_{2}=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\widehat{g_{2}}(l)e^{\text{i}l\cdot\varphi}
, then
\hat{\eta}
is determined by

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}(\varphi)=\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle+\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\frac{\widehat{g_{2}}(l)}{\omega\cdot l}e^{\text{i}l\cdot\varphi}=:\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle+(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi})^{-1}g_{2},

(1.99)

where
\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle
is a constant that does not depend on
\varphi
, which can be freely chosen in view of (
1.98b
).
Now we move to the third equation (
1.98c
) and let us denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\partial_{x}K_{02},

(1.100)

so that we are led to solve
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}[\hat{w}]=g_{3}+K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]
. Unlike the first two equations (
1.98a
) and (
1.98b
), the invertibility of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is not simply guaranteed by imposing the Diophantine condition on
\omega
, and its solvability is much more complicated, since
H_{S^{\perp}}
is an infinite dimensional space. The invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is our main goal in Section
8
-
10
and we will assume for the moment that (see Proposition
7.14
for the precise statement with necessary estimates)

\star

Invertibility in the normal direction: There exists a set of frequencies
\Omega_{\infty}(i)\subset\Omega_{0}
such that for
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}(i)
, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is invertible.

With the above assumption, we can write a solution
\hat{w}
in (
1.98c
) as

\displaystyle\hat{w}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}(g_{3}+K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]).

(1.101)

Lastly, having
\hat{\eta}
and
\hat{w}
as in (
1.99
) and (
1.101
), the first equation (
1.98a
) can be solved as

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}=(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi})^{-1}\left(g_{1}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]+K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)\right),

(1.102)

where
\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle
in (
1.99
) must be chosen to guarantee that the
g_{1}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]+K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}]\right)
has zero-average in
\varphi
. Such a choice of
(\hat{\psi},\hat{\eta},\hat{w})
is a solution to (
1.98
).

The summary of the above discussion is that the search for an approximate inverse leads us to the investigation of the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
1.100
), which is restricted to the normal direction. In view of the invertibility assumption, we will find for each
i_{n}
in the sequence (
1.87
), a nested sequence of frequency sets
\mathcal{G}_{n+1}\subset\mathcal{G}_{n}\subset\Omega_{\infty}(i_{n})\subset\Omega_{0}
such that for
\omega\in\mathcal{G}_{n+1}
, the operator
T(i_{n})
is well-defined, therefore so is
i_{n+1}
(over
\omega\in\mathcal{G}_{n+1}
). This implies that we can run the iteration for
\omega\in\cap_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}_{n}
towards the solution
i_{\infty}
, which will be rigorously proved in Proposition
11.1
. In the rest, we will focus on how to invert
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
.

1.2.2.2
Structure of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
at a fixed embedding
i_{0}
.

Let us fix an embedding
i_{0}
so that

\displaystyle i_{0}(\varphi):=(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{0}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi)),\text{ satisfies the assumption \eqref{size_assumption_intro1} with $\mathfrak{I}_{0}:=i_{0}-i_{triv}$,}

(1.103)

and investigate the invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
determined by
i_{0}
(we use a slight abuse of notation that from now on,
i_{0}
is not necessarily the trivial embedding). A very natural question at this point is what the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
does really look like. We started from the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in (
1.6
), which is already complicated but we have composed it with multiple changes of variables.
To understand the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
, let us trace back the compositions. From (
1.49
), (
1.50
) and (
1.94
), we see that the Hamiltonian
K
is given by

\displaystyle K(\psi,\eta,w)=\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}\circ U_{\zeta}\circ G_{\delta}(\psi,\eta,w),\quad(\psi,\eta,w)\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}},

(1.104)

where
\Phi^{WB}
is a transformation designed to eliminate some homogeneous contributions of the tangential variables (see
(WB1)
and
(WB2)
in Part 1),
U_{\zeta}
is the introduction of the action-angle variables as well as the amplitude variable (see (
1.31
)), and
G_{\delta}
is introduced in the study of the approximate inverse of the linearized operator. The linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}[\hat{w}]:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{w}-\partial_{x}(\nabla_{w}^{2}K(\varphi,0,0))[\hat{w}]),\text{ for $\hat{w}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{T})$ such that $\hat{w}(\varphi)\in H_{S^{\perp}}$ for each $\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}$,}

(1.105)

which follows from (
1.100
) and (
1.97
). Notice that the operator
\hat{w}\mapsto\nabla_{w}^{2}K(\varphi,0,0))[\hat{w}]
also depends on the frequency
\omega
since
K
in (
1.104
) depends on
\zeta
, while we have the one-to-one correspondence between
\omega
and
\zeta
through (
1.57
).

The earlier discussion tells us that the existence of an approximate inverse at
i_{0}
,
T(i_{0})
, is determined by the invertibility of the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
. Then, where is the information of
i_{0}
encoded in the expression (
1.105
)? Indeed, the symplectic diffeomorphism
G_{\delta}
in (
1.104
) is determined by
i_{0}
, while we do not explicitly write it in this sketch level discussion. We only note that the image of
i_{triv}
under
G_{\delta}
corresponds to an
isotropic embedding

i_{\delta}
induced from
i_{0}
(see (
7.5
), (
7.17
) and Lemma
7.2
), for which only the
“action”
coordinate differs from
i_{0}
, that is,

\displaystyle i_{\delta}(\varphi):=G_{\delta}(i_{triv}(\varphi))\overset{\eqref{iso_tropic}}{=}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi)),

(1.106)

and
i_{\delta}
is also close to the trivial embedding (see (
7.16
));

\displaystyle i_{\delta}(\varphi)\text{ satisfies the assumption \eqref{size_assumption_intro1} with $\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}:=i_{\delta}-i_{triv}$,}

(1.107)

In view of (
1.104
) and (
1.105
), let us take a simple example to see what a Hessian operator looks like on the embedded torus
G_{\delta}(i_{triv})=i_{\delta}
.

\bullet

A toy model 2: A perturbed Airy equation.
Let us denote the orthogonal projections to
H_{S}
and
H_{S^{\perp}}
by (see (
1.29
) and (
1.17
) for the notations)

\displaystyle\Pi_{S}f=\sum_{j\in S}f_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\quad\Pi_{S^{\perp}}f:=\sum_{j\in S^{\perp}}f_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\text{ for $f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})$ with $\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)dx=0$.}

(1.108)

We also denote the variables in
H_{S}
and
H_{S^{\perp}}
by
v
and
z
respectively, so that given
f
, we have a unique decomposition as

\displaystyle f=v+z,\text{ where $v\in H_{S}$ and $z\in H_{S^{\perp}}$ for $f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})$ with $\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)dx=0$.}

(1.109)

Let us consider a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{G}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(f):=\mathcal{G}_{2,2}(f)+\mathcal{G}_{3,2}(f)+\mathcal{G}_{4,2}(f)+\mathcal{G}_{5,2}(f),

(1.110)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{2,2}(f):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}z)^{2}dx,\quad\mathcal{G}_{3,2}(f):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}vz^{2}dx,

(1.111)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{4,2}(f):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}vz\Pi_{S}(vz)+v^{2}z^{2}dx,\quad\mathcal{G}_{5,2}(f):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v^{3}z^{2}dx.

Notice that
\mathcal{G}_{i,k}
is homogeneous of degree
i
in the variable
f
and homogeneous of degree
k
in the variable
z
, for a fixed
v
. The motivation for the above example is that in our case, we constructed the symplectic transformation
\Phi^{WB}
in (
1.49
) so that
H=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}
reduces to the form of (
1.74
). We designed our toy model (
1.110
) to include quadratic contributions of the normal variable
z
. The Hessian operator in the normal direction of
\mathcal{G}
at
f=v+z
can be easily computed as

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}(f)[\hat{z}]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{xx}\hat{z}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v\hat{z})+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v\Pi_{S}(v\hat{z})+v^{2}\hat{z})+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v^{3}\hat{z})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(-\partial_{xx}\hat{z}+(v+v^{2}+v^{3})\hat{z}\right)+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v\Pi_{S}(v\hat{z})).

Therefore, defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{G}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z),

(1.112)

and using the rescaled variables in (
1.66
), we obtain the Hessian operator of
\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)
in the normal direction,

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)[\hat{z}]=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(-\partial_{xx}\hat{z}+(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+\varepsilon^{2}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)^{2}+\varepsilon^{3}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)^{3})\hat{z}\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)\Pi_{S}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)\hat{z}))

(1.113)

On the embedded torus
i_{\delta}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu})\subset\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
, we obtain a
\varphi
-dependent operator in
H_{S^{\perp}}
:

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}(\varphi))[\hat{z}]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{idlsotrophi_intro_3}}{=}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi))[\hat{z}]

(1.114)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{normal_driec1nont1}}{=}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(a^{\text{Airy}}_{2}(\varphi,x)\partial_{xx}\hat{z}+a^{\text{Airy}}_{0}(\varphi,x)\hat{z}\right)+R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]=:\Pi_{S^{\perp}}M^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)+R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}],

where the coefficients
a_{2},a_{0}
are given by

\displaystyle a^{\text{Airy}}_{2}(\varphi,x):=-1,\quad a^{\text{Airy}}_{0}(\varphi,x):=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)))^{i},

(1.115)

and the operator
R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)
is defined as

\displaystyle R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]:=\varepsilon^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi))\Pi_{S}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi))\hat{z})).

(1.116)

Note that for each fixed
\varphi
, the operator
\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}(\varphi))
consists of two types of operators; the operator
\hat{z}\mapsto M^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]
is a pseudo differential operator of order
2
, and
\hat{z}\mapsto R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]
is a smoothing operator. Indeed, since
v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi))
is supported on a finite number of Fourier modes,
S
(see (
1.65
) and (
1.66
)), only a finite number of modes of
\hat{z}(\varphi)
is involved in (
1.116
), therefore
R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
is a smoothing operator.

We first analyze the coefficients
a_{2}^{\text{Airy}},a_{0}^{\text{Airy}}
, and then study the operator
R^{\text{Airy}}
. From the definition of
v_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.66
) and (
1.65
), we have

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi))(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}(y_{\delta}(\varphi))_{k})}e^{\text{i}(\theta_{0}(\varphi)_{k}+j_{k}x)},

(1.117)

while each summand can be split as

\displaystyle\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}(y_{\delta}(\varphi))_{k})}e^{\text{i}(\theta_{0}(\varphi)_{k}+j_{k}x)}=\underbrace{\sqrt{|j_{k}|\zeta_{k}}e^{\text{i}(\varphi\cdot\mathtt{l}(j_{k})+j_{k}x)}}_{=:\overline{v}_{j_{k}}(\varphi,x)}+O(|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|).

(1.118)

where
\mathtt{l}(j_{k})
is defind as

\displaystyle\mathtt{l}(j_{k}):=\mathtt{e}_{k},\text{ and }\mathtt{l}(-j_{k}):=-\mathtt{l}(j_{k})\text{ for $j_{k}\in S^{+}=\left\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\right\}$}.

(1.119)

Writing

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}:=v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)),\quad\overline{v}:=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\overline{v}_{j_{k}}(\varphi,x),

(1.120)

for simplicity, the decomposition (
1.118
) shows that the tangential variable
v_{\varepsilon}
can be decomposed into a sum of the
i_{\delta}
-independent monomial (
\overline{v}_{j_{k}}
) and a small contribution of the size of the embedding
O(|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|)\overset{\eqref{isotropic_123intro231},\eqref{size_assumption_intro1}}{=}O(\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1})
. Hence, from (
1.115
), we see that the coefficient
a_{0}^{\text{Airy}}
consists of at most cubic interactions of the monomials
\overline{v}_{j_{k}}
and the small contribution of size
O(\varepsilon|\mathfrak{I}_{0}|)
, that is,

\displaystyle a_{0}^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi,x)=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}\left(\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}\overline{v}_{j_{k_{1}}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\overline{v}_{j_{k_{i}}}(\varphi,x)\right)+O(\varepsilon|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|).

(1.121)

Now, we consider the operator
R^{\text{Airy}}
. As above, we can decompose it into

\displaystyle R^{\text{Airy}}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(\overline{v}\Pi_{S}(\overline{v}\hat{z})\right)+R_{2}^{\text{Airy}},

(1.122)

\displaystyle R^{\text{Airy}}_{2}(\varphi)[\hat{z}]

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon^{2}\left(\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v_{\varepsilon}-\overline{v})\Pi_{S}(v_{\varepsilon}\hat{z})\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\left(\overline{v}\Pi_{S}((v_{\varepsilon}-\overline{v})\hat{z})\right).

This shows that
R^{\text{Airy}}
can be also decomposed into a sum of an operator that does not depend on the embedding
i_{\delta}
and a operator
R^{\text{Airy}}_{2}
whose size is
O(\varepsilon^{2}|v_{\varepsilon}-\overline{v}|)=O(\varepsilon^{2}|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|)
, which is much smaller than
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

The summary of this example is that while the Hessian operator of
\mathcal{G}
at
f=0
is simply

\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}(0)[\hat{z}]=-\partial_{xx}\hat{z},

the Hessian operator of rescaled Hamiltonian
\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}
on the embedded torus
i_{\delta}
,
\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}(\varphi))[\hat{z}]
in (
1.114
) reveals perturbations in the coefficients and a smoothing operator
R^{\text{Airy}}
. Moreover, the deviation between these two Hessian operators is composed of the contribution of simple monomials
\overline{v}
in (
1.120
), and smaller contributions from the embedding
i_{\delta}
.

Comparing
K
in (
1.104
) and
\mathcal{G}_{\zeta}
in (
1.112
), we can deduce from the above example that in the case of gSQG the Hessian operator,
\nabla_{w}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))
, will be expressed as a perturbation from the
\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(0)
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(0)[\hat{z}]\overset{\eqref{linear_intro_operator_language1}}{=}-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}\hat{z}+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\hat{z}.

(1.123)

Indeed, in Section
8
, we will find that
\nabla_{w}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))
has the form (compare below to (
1.114
)):

\displaystyle\nabla_{w}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))[\hat{w}]=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}M(\varphi)[\hat{w}]+R(\varphi)[\hat{w}],

(1.124)

where
M(\varphi)
is an operator with perturbed coefficients and and
R(\varphi)
is a smoothing operator. The operator
M(\varphi)
is of the form:

\displaystyle M(\varphi)[\hat{w}]:=\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}(\varphi)}\hat{w}+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}(\varphi)\right)\hat{w}+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{3}(\varphi)}\hat{w},

(1.125)

where

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{a}h:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}a(x,y)(h(x)-h(y))dy,

(1.126)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}h:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}a(x,y)h(y)dy.

The
\varphi
-dependent
“coefficients”

a_{1}(\varphi)=a_{1}(\varphi,x,y),a_{2}(\varphi)=a_{2}(\varphi,x),a_{3}(\varphi)=a_{3}(\varphi,x,y)
and the operator
R(\varphi)
are small;

\displaystyle a_{i}=O(\varepsilon)\text{ for $i=1,2,3$},\quad R=O(\varepsilon).

(1.127)

With (
1.124
), the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
1.105
) tells us that the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is of the form :

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M(\varphi)+\mathcal{R}(\varphi),\text{ where $\mathcal{R}:=-\partial_{x}R$.}

(1.128)

The derivation of the from of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is the main result of Section
8
and Proposition
8.18
.

Actually, Proposition
8.18
tells us something more. As we observed in the toy example (see (
1.121
) and (
1.122
)), we can decompose the contribution to the coefficients and
R
into the ones from the monomials
\overline{v}
and the ones from
i_{\delta}
. This is the motivation for the definition of the class of coefficients
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
in Definition
8.2
and the class of a
“finite dimensional operator”

\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
in Definition
8.11
. In Proposition
8.18
, we prove that

\displaystyle a_{1},a_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0}),\quad a_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0}),\quad\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0}).

(1.129)

Indeed, we will see that the coefficients/operators of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
are not perturbative (see (
1.141
)). Therefore, in the reduction procedure, which we will discuss later, we need more precise information about coefficients/operators of sizes
O(\varepsilon),O(\varepsilon^{2})
. The inclusions (
1.129
) tell us that the eigenvalues of the Hessian
\nabla_{w}^{2}K(i_{triv}(\varphi))
are independent of
i_{0},i_{\delta}
up to
o(\varepsilon^{2})
, since the contributions of size
O(\varepsilon),O(\varepsilon^{2})
are solely determined by the monomials
\overline{v}
in (
1.120
).

1.2.2.3
KAM reducibility and homological equation

The invertibility of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is achieved by diagonalizing the operator. Roughly speaking, we aim to find
\varphi
-dependent invertible transformations
\Phi_{1,\infty}(\varphi),\Phi_{2,\infty}(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
and Fourier multipliers
j\mapsto d_{\infty}(j)
such that

\displaystyle\Phi_{1,\infty}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\circ\Phi_{2,\infty}=\mathcal{L}_{\infty}:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\text{diag}_{j\in S^{\perp}}d_{\infty}(j).

(1.130)

Note that since the multipliers
d_{\infty}(j)
are the eigenvalues of
\hat{w}\mapsto\partial_{x}(\nabla_{w}^{2}K(\varphi,0,0))[\hat{w}])
(we remove the dependence on
\varphi
by reparametrizing in the variable
\varphi
) and
K
depends on
\omega
, so does
d_{\infty}
as well, therefore, we denote

\displaystyle d_{\infty}(j)=d_{\infty}(\omega,j),\text{ for $j\in S^{\perp}$}.

(1.131)

Then, for the frequencies satisfying the so-called first order Melnikov condition (as we saw in (
1.26
)):

\displaystyle\left\{\omega:|\text{i}\omega\cdot l-d_{\infty}(\omega,j)|\geq\gamma|l|^{-\tau}|j|^{\alpha},\text{ for all $(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\times S^{\perp}$}\right\},

(1.132)

we can invert the operator in (
1.130
).

In order to achieve (
1.130
), let us suppose we have an operator
L
of the form:

\displaystyle L:=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\text{diag}_{j\in S^{\perp}}d(\omega,j)+\mathcal{R}(\varphi)=:\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D+\mathcal{R}(\varphi),

(1.133)

for some Fourier multipliers
j\mapsto d(\omega,j)
and for some
\varphi
-dependent operator
\mathcal{R}(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
and investigate how to obtain, from
L
, a fully diagonalized operator such as (
1.130
). We consider a transformation
\phi(\varphi)
such that

\displaystyle\phi(\varphi)=I+\psi(\varphi),\text{ for some small operator $\psi(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}$, so that $\phi(\varphi)^{-1}=:I+\tilde{\psi}(\varphi)$ exists.}

(1.134)

We compute the conjugation of
L
with
\phi
, that is
\phi^{-1}\circ L\circ\phi
. First, we compute

\displaystyle L\circ\phi=L\circ(I+\psi)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{l_0inftronotext123}}{=}L+(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\circ\psi-D\circ\psi+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi)

\displaystyle=L+\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\psi)+\psi\circ(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi})-D\circ\psi+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi

\displaystyle=(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D)+\mathcal{R}+\psi\circ(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi})-\psi\circ D+\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\psi)+(\psi\circ D-D\circ\psi)+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi

\displaystyle=(I+\psi)\circ(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D)+(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\psi)+[\psi,D]+\mathcal{R})+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi.

(1.135)

Denoting

\displaystyle r(j):=\mathcal{R}^{j}_{j}(0),\quad D_{r}:=\text{diag}_{j\in S^{\perp}}r(j),

(1.136)

where
\mathcal{R}^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l)
is the matrix representation of the operator
\mathcal{R}(\varphi)
such that

\mathcal{R}(\varphi)[f]=\sum_{(l,j_{1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{R}^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l)f_{j_{1}}e^{\text{i}(\varphi\cdot l+j_{2}x)},

we solve a homological equation for the operator
\psi
:

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\psi)+[\psi,D]+\mathcal{R}=\text{diag}_{j\in S^{\perp}}r(j)=:D_{r}.

(1.137)

For the solution
\psi
, it follows from (
1.2.2.3
) that

L\circ\phi=\phi\circ(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D)+D_{r}+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi=\phi\circ(\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-(D+D_{r}))-\psi\circ D_{r}+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi.

Therefore, we see that

\displaystyle\phi^{-1}\circ L\circ\phi=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\underbrace{(D-D_{r})}_{=:D_{\text{new}}}+\underbrace{\phi^{-1}\circ\left(-\psi\circ D_{r}+\mathcal{R}\circ\psi\right)}_{=:\mathcal{R}_{\text{new}}}.

(1.138)

In the above formal computations, we observe that given a linear operator of the form (
1.133
), a solution to the homological equation (
1.137
) leads us to an updated linear operator with a new diagonal part and a new remainder part. If the size of the new remainder
\mathcal{R}_{new}
is shown to be significantly smaller than the previous remainder
\mathcal{R}
, we can deduce that an infinite number of such conjugations converge to a fully diagonalized operator (
1.130
). The first thing we can observe from the homological equation (
1.137
) is that the size of
\psi
will depend on
\mathcal{R}
, while we do not expect the solution to exist for every frequency
\omega
and we need to select
\omega
that satisfies sufficient non-resonance conditions. From the Diophantine condition (
1.28
), heuristically we can deduce that the size of
\psi
must be

\displaystyle\psi=O(\gamma^{-1}|\mathcal{R}|).

(1.139)

Therefore, the new remainder (
1.138
) can be inferred to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\text{new}}=O(\gamma^{-1}|\mathcal{R}|^{2}).

(1.140)

In order for this new remainder to be smaller than the previous one,
|\mathcal{R}|\gtrsim\gamma^{-1}|\mathcal{R}|^{2}
, we must have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=o(\gamma^{-1})\overset{\eqref{gamma_impose_intro1}}{=}o(\varepsilon^{2b}).

(1.141)

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{R}_{\text{new}}
is quadratic in
\mathcal{R}
, we need
\mathcal{R}
to be bounded in the sense that the composition
\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{R}
does not accumulate the derivatives (for example, compositions of an operator such as
\varepsilon^{3}\partial_{x}
do not get smaller after the compositions, since its contribution to high modes are increasing because of the accumulation of the derivatives).

In our proof, we will measure the size of the remainder using the notion of
“modulo-tame constant, Lip-modulo-tame constant”
(see Definition
2.20
,
2.22
). Such notions were introduced in
[
16
]
. In regard to the above discussion, compositions of
0
-modulo-tame operators remain as a
0
-modulo-tame operator, which implies that compositions of such operators do not accumulate the derivatives (Lemma
2.23
). We also note that in order to rigorously construct the infinite sequence of conjugations (
1.138
) and (
1.134
), we need to regularize the remainder
\mathcal{R}(\varphi)
at each step (regularize with respect to the variable
\varphi
), which requires the estimates of not only the remainder
\mathcal{R}
but also the derivatives of it with respect to
\varphi
, that is
\partial_{\varphi}^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}(\mathcal{R}_{0})
for sufficiently large
\mathtt{b}_{0}>0
. A question how large
\mathtt{b}_{0}
needs to be could be answered by solving (
1.137
) rigorously (in our paper, our choice is given in (
10.70
)).

Our operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
1.141
) does not satisfy such conditions. Therefore, inspired by
[
6
]
, we will conjugate
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
a finite number of times into the form with a sufficiently small remainder. That is, we will construct invertible transformations
\Phi_{1-6,1},\Phi_{1-6,2}
such that the conjugated operator has the form:

\displaystyle L_{0}:=\Phi_{1-6,1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\circ\Phi_{1-6,2}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{0},

(1.142)

with

(R1)

D_{0}
is a Fourier multiplier, that is,
D_{0}
does not depend on
\varphi,x
.

(R2)

\partial_{\varphi}^{\mathtt{b}_{0}}\mathcal{R}_{0}(\varphi),\ \mathcal{R}_{0}
satisfies the smallness condition
\mathcal{R}_{0}=o(\varepsilon^{2b})
(as a
0
-Lip-modulo-tame operator) for sufficiently large
\mathtt{b}_{0}>0
.

This is the main task in Subsections
10.1
-
10.5
yielding Proposition
10.76
. After achieving (
1.142
), the rigorous iteration to construct
\phi
in (
1.138
) towards the full diagonal will be proved rigorously throughout Subsection
10.6
.

1.2.2.4
Reduction to a constant coefficient operator: Subsections
10.1
-
10.5

In view of the above discussion, we are left to study how to construct transformations
\Phi_{1-6,1},\Phi_{1-6,2}
with which the conjugation of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is reduced to the form of (
1.142
) satisfying
(R1)
and
(R2)
.

Recall from (
1.128
) and (
1.125
) that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
has the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}(\varphi)}+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}(\varphi)\right)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{3}(\varphi)}\right)+\mathcal{R}(\varphi),

(1.143)

for some
a_{1}(\varphi)=a_{1}(\varphi,x,y),\ a_{2}(\varphi)=a_{2}(\varphi,x)
and
a_{3}(\varphi)=a_{3}(\varphi,x,y)
such that
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}=O(\varepsilon)
and for some smoothing operator
\mathcal{R}
of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.
We reduce the coefficients to constants (remove the dependence on
x
and
\varphi
) from the highest order term to the lower ones, using the conjugation techniques motivated from
[
6
,
8
,
16
]
.

\bullet

Change of the space variables (Subsection
10.1
): We conjugate
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
with a
\varphi
-dependent time-
1
flow map
\Phi_{1}
determined by the PDE,

\partial_{t}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\frac{\beta(\varphi,x)}{1+t\beta_{x}(\varphi,x)}u(t,\varphi,x)\right),\text{ for some function $\beta(\varphi,x)$}
.
The function
\beta
is chosen so that the conjugated operator is of the form:

\mathcal{L}^{1}:=(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\circ\Phi_{1}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(b_{1}(\varphi)\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+b_{2}(\varphi,x)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{b_{3}(\varphi)}\right)+R_{1}(\varphi),

for some
b_{1}(\varphi),b_{2}(\varphi,x),b_{3}(\varphi,x,y)
, where
b_{1}
, does not depend on
x
anymore and
b_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4},b_{3}=O(\varepsilon)
.
{R}_{1}(\varphi)
is a smoothing operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
. A more precise statement can be found in Proposition
10.1
.

\bullet

Reparametrization of time (Subsection
10.2
): We conjugate
\mathcal{L}^{1}
with a transformation
\Phi_{2}:[h](\varphi,x):=h(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi),x)
for some function
p_{1}(\varphi)
. The function
p_{1}
is chosen so that the conjugated operator is of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}:=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}^{1}\circ\Phi_{2}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+d_{2}(\varphi,x)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}(\varphi)}\right)+R_{2}(\varphi),

(1.144)

for some functions
\rho(\varphi),d_{2}(\varphi,x),d_{3}(\varphi,x,y)
and for a constant
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
that does not depend on any of
\varphi,x
, and
d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4},d_{3}=O(\varepsilon)
.
R_{2}(\varphi)
is a smoothing operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
. A more precise statement can be found in Proposition
10.10
.

\bullet

Symbolic representation of the operator (Subsection
10.3.1
): We rewrite the operator
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+d_{2}(\varphi,x)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}(\varphi)}
in (
1.144
) as a pseudo differential operator with associated symbols. Using the Fourier multiplier
m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)
associated to the operator
\Lambda^{\alpha-1}
, that is,
Op^{W}(m_{1,\alpha}(\xi))=\Lambda^{\alpha-1}
(see Proposition
2.34
), we rewrite
\mathcal{L}^{2}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{d}_{0}(\varphi)\right)+R_{2}(\varphi),

(1.145)

for some
\varphi
-dependent classical symbol
\mathfrak{d}_{0}(\varphi,x,\xi)
of order
0
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{0}=O(\varepsilon)
, where
Op^{W}
denotes the Weyl quantization of the symbol (
2.13
). A more detailed statement can be found in (
10.28
).

\bullet

Egorov method (step 1) (Subsection
10.3.2
): We conjugate
\mathcal{L}^{2}
with
\varphi
-dependent time-
1
flow map
\Phi_{3}
determined by the PDE,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}(\varphi))[u],

(1.146)

for some
\varphi
-dependent symbol function
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{a}(\varphi,x,\xi)
of order
1-\alpha
.
The symbol
\mathfrak{a}
chosen so that the conjugated operator is of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{3}

\displaystyle:=(\Phi_{3})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}^{2}\circ\Phi_{3}

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}M_{x}(\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi))+\mathfrak{r}_{-2}(\varphi)\right)+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{0}(\varphi)+R_{3}(\varphi).

where

\circ

\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi,x,\xi)
are
\varphi
-dependent symbols of order
k(1-\alpha)
for
k=1\ldots,\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
.
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
is a constant that depends on only
\mathtt{p}
(see (
1.84
) and (
10.70
)). The motivation of
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
is that this constant is arising in the Taylor expansion of the flow map of (
1.146
) in the time variable
t
to obtain necessary estimates.

\circ

M_{x}(\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi))
is the average of the symbol
\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi,x,\xi)
, therefore the symbol
\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}M_{x}(\mathfrak{d}_{k}(\varphi))
in the expression of
\mathcal{L}^{3}
does not depend on the variable
x
.

\circ

\mathfrak{r}_{-2}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{r}_{-2}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is a
\varphi
-dependent symbol of order
-2
such that
\mathfrak{r}_{-2}=O(\varepsilon)
.

\circ

\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{0}(\varphi)
is a remainder satisfying the requirement
(R2)
for (
1.142
).

\circ

R_{3}
is a smoothing operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

A more detailed statement can be found in Proposition
10.30
.

Remark 1.7
.

As opposed to the earlier works
[
16
,
75
]
, we choose a slightly more general symbol
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)
in (
1.146
) to eliminate the dependence of the lower order symbols on the variable
x
. This is why we use the Weyl quantization in the reduction procedure. The PDE in (
1.146
) may not be well-posed in general, unless the operator
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}(\varphi))
is a symmetric operator. The use of the Weyl quantization makes it easier to verify the symmetry of the operator, since
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}(\varphi))
is symmetric if and only if
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)
is a real-valued symbol. Furthermore, compared to
[
16
,
75
]
, the choice of the symbol
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)
is more delicate, since the highest order of our equation is
“weak”
. We overcome this issue by writing
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)
as a finite sum of lower order symbols,
\mathfrak{a}(\varphi)=\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\mathfrak{a}_{k}(\varphi)
for some
\mathfrak{a}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{(k+1)(1-\alpha)}
and search for
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
to obtain the desired conjugated operator of the form (
1.146
).

\bullet

Egorov method (step 2) (Subsection
10.3.3
): We conjugate
\mathcal{L}^{3}
with a
\varphi
-dependent time-
1
flow map
\Phi_{4}
determined by the PDEs
\partial_{t}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\varphi))[u],
for some
\varphi
-dependent Fourier multiplier
\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{p}_{2}(\varphi,\xi)
of order
1-\alpha
that does not depend on
x
.
The function
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
is chosen so that the conjugated operator is of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{4}

\displaystyle:=(\Phi_{4})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}^{3}\circ\Phi_{4}

(1.147)

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{m}_{\leq 0}(\xi)+\mathfrak{r}_{-2,1}(\varphi)\right)+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{1}(\varphi)+R_{4}(\varphi),

where

\circ

\mathfrak{m}_{\leq 0}(\xi)
is a Fourier multiplier of order
0
that is independent of
\varphi,x
.

\circ

\mathfrak{r}_{-2,1}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{r}_{-2,1}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is a
\varphi
-dependent symbol of order
-2
such that
\mathfrak{r}_{-2,1}=O(\varepsilon)
.

\circ

\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{1}(\varphi)
is a remainder satisfying the requirement
(R2)
for (
1.142
).

\circ

R_{4}
is a smoothing operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

A more detailed statement can be found in Proposition
10.41
.

\bullet

Linear Birkhoff normal form (step 1) (Subsection
10.4.1
): Comparing (
1.147
) to (
1.142
) and recalling the requirement for the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{0}=o(\varepsilon^{2b})
from
(R2)
, the operators
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{r}_{-2,1}(\varphi))
and
R_{4}(\varphi)
are not perturbative, that is, their size is not
o(\varepsilon^{2b})
since
b>1
(see (
1.85
)). In order to remove terms of size
O(\varepsilon)
, we conjugate
\mathcal{L}^{4}
with a
\varphi
-dependent time-
1
flow map
\Phi_{5}
determined by the PDEs
\frac{d}{dt}u=\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\varepsilon Op^{W}(\rho_{1}(\varphi))[u]
for some
\varphi
-dependent symbol
\rho_{1}(\varphi)=\rho_{1}(\varphi,x,\xi)
of order
-1-\alpha
. The function
\rho_{1}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is chosen so that the conjugated operator has the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{5}

\displaystyle=(\Phi_{5})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}^{4}\circ\Phi_{5}

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{m}_{\leq 0}(\xi)\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}(\varphi)+{\mathfrak{r}}_{-2,\leq 3,*}(\varphi)\right)+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{2}(\varphi)+R_{5}(\varphi),

where

\circ

\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}(\varphi)=\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is a
\varphi
-dependent symbol of order
-2
such that
\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}=O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

\circ

\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,*}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,*}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is a
\varphi
-dependent symbol of order
-2
such that
\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,*}=o(\varepsilon^{2b})
.

\circ

\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{2}(\varphi)
is a remainder satisfying the requirement
(R2)
for (
1.142
).

\circ

R_{5}
is a smoothing operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

A more detailed statement can be found in Proposition
10.51
.

\bullet

Linear Birkhoff normal form (step 2) (Subsection
10.4.3
): Comparing (
1.147
) to (
1.142
) and recalling the requirement for the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{0}=o(\varepsilon^{2b})
from
(R2)
, the operators
Op^{W}(\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}(\varphi))
and
R_{5}(\varphi)
are not perturbative, that is, their size is not
o(\varepsilon^{2b})
since
b>1
(see (
1.85
)). To eliminate the terms of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
, we conjugate
\mathcal{L}^{5}
with a
\varphi
-dependent time-
1
flow map
\Phi_{6}
determined by the PDEs
\frac{d}{dt}u=\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\varepsilon Op^{W}(\rho_{2}(\varphi))[u]
for some
\varphi
-dependent symbol
\rho_{2}(\varphi)=\rho_{2}(\varphi,x,\xi)
of order
-1-\alpha
. The function
\rho_{2}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is chosen so that the conjugated operator has the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{6}

\displaystyle:=(\Phi_{6})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\circ\Phi_{6}

(1.148)

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{m}_{\leq 0}(\xi)+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\xi)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,\sharp}(\varphi))+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{3}(\varphi)+R_{6}(\varphi),

where

\circ

\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\xi)
is a Fourier multiplier of order
-2
that is independent of
\varphi,x
.

\circ

\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,\sharp}(\varphi)=\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,\sharp}(\varphi,x,\xi)
is a
\varphi
-dependent symbol of order
-2
such that
\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,\sharp}=o(\varepsilon^{2b})
.

\circ

\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{3}(\varphi)
is a remainder satisfying the requirement
(R2)
for (
1.142
).

\circ

R_{6}
is a smoothing operator of size
o(\varepsilon^{2b})
.

A more detailed statement can be found in Proposition
10.66
.

Finally, denoting

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{r}_{-2,\leq 3,\sharp}(\varphi))+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{3}(\varphi)+R_{6}(\varphi),

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle:=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{m}_{\leq 0}(\xi)+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\xi)\right)\right),

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}^{6}\overset{\eqref{ubtr_ju_fn11}}{=}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}-D_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{0},

we prove in Subsection
10.5
that
\mathcal{R}_{0}
indeed satisfies the smallness condition
(R2)
and thus achieve the desired reduction to (
1.142
).

Lastly, we note that all transformations, constructed in the reduction procedure from
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
to
\mathcal{L}^{6}
, are required to be 1) reversibility preserving, 2)
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving and 3) real (mapping a real-valued function to a real-valued function), to guarantee that the quasiperiodic solution
f(t,x)
in Theorem
1.2
is a reversible,
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant and real-valued solution.

1.2.3
Summary of the sketch and the structure of the monograph

In summary, we derive a weak Birkhoff normal form from the Hamiltonian of the gSQG equation with two purposes: 1) extraction of an internal parameter and 2) reduction of the size of the perturbative term, inspired by
[
8
,
9
,
52
]
. Once the weak Birkhoff normal from is derived, we aim to perform a Nash-Moser scheme, which leads us to study an approximate inverse of the linearized operator. The search for an approximate inverse reduces to the invertibility of the linearized operator restricted to the normal space in the spirit of the derivation of the normal form derivation near an invariant torus in
[
12
]
. The invertibility of the linearized operator in the normal direction will be achieved by reduction of the operator to a constant coefficients operator (a diagonalized operator) by means of symplectic transformations studied in
[
6
,
16
]
. Once the reduction is complete, then we finally prove that the approximate solutions constructed by the Nash-Moser iteration converge to the desired quasiperiodic solution. Meanwhile, necessary non-resonance conditions on the frequencies will be taken into account following the strategy in
[
65
]
.

In this monograph, the above scheme has been structured as follows:

Chapter
2
is devoted to the basic definitions of the spaces and operators, and also to the spaces we will work in. In particular, we also analyze the different multipliers that will appear throughout the proof and the properties of the special functions involved.

Chapter
3
explains the Hamiltonian character of the gSQG equation and recasts it in a way which is compatible with its associated Poisson bracket. We also perform expansions of the Hamiltonian that will later prove useful in the upcoming chapters. Some useful invariance properties of the gSQG equation will be considered as well.

Chapter
4
performs the calculation of the weak Birkhoff normal form, reducing the nonlinear interactions in a way that for homogeneous terms of degree
n
,
n\leq 5
, which ensures the sufficient smallness condition of the perturbative term. The nonexistence of non-trivial resonances in the quartic Hamiltonian will be investigated, which serves as a key ingredient to derive a desired normal form.

Chapters
5
and
6
set the problem up into action-angle coordinates and split the nonlinear operator into three different components. We summarize the necessary conditions on the choice of the tangential sites
S
, which will be mainly used in the measure estimate of frequency set. We also state our main theorem there in terms of finding a zero of a nonlinear operator
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
.

Chapter
7
reduces the problem of understanding
the linearization of
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
at a given embedding into a linearization of a normal form around the trivial embedding and proving it is invertible as an operator between the corresponding spaces. Furthermore, the system is diagonal and the condition can be further reduced to prove the invertibility of the
normal part
of the operator.

Chapter
8
computes a more explicit representation of the aforementioned operator, emphasizing that the calculation is not restricted to the
O(1)
terms but also to the
O(\varepsilon)
and
O(\varepsilon^{2})
as well, since these terms are not perturbative. For this purpose, we will classify the coefficients/smoothing operators arising from the linearized operator as special classes
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}/\mathfrak{R}
, which reveals that the non-perturbative terms (terms of size
O(\varepsilon),O(\varepsilon^{2})
) are independent of the approximate solutions.

Chapter
9
collects some useful properties of symplectic transformations that will be used in the reduction process towards a constant coefficients operator.

In Chapter
10
, we will conjugate the operator via reversible transformations in order to reduce it to a constant-coefficient operator, modulo semilinear and
O(\varepsilon^{3})
parts. This is the most important piece of the manuscript and the most demanding one. These transformations are discussed in Chapter
9
, along with their corresponding spaces.

Finally, in Chapters
11
and
12
we finalize the proof of the Theorem by using a Nash-Moser argument and computing the estimates on the measure of set of the non-resonant frequencies.

Appendix
A
contains technical lemmas related to the computations of the different pseudo-differential operators.

Appendix
B
contains a rigorous construction of tangential sites satisfying all the non-resonance conditions described in Section
5.1
.

The relevant connections between sections/propositions in the proof are summarized in Figure
1
.

Connections between relevant sections and propositions

2
Preliminaries and notations

In this section, we recall some classical concepts and specify notations that are used throughout the paper.

2.0.0.1
Basic notations

We denote by
\mathbb{N},\mathbb{Z}
, the sets of natural numbers and integers, respectively, and denote
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{0\right\}
.

For an object
O
, which possibly is a number, operator or a set, and two real numbers
A,B
, we denote
A\leq_{O}B,
if there exists a constant
C
that depends on
O
such that
A\leq CB
. Furthermore, we denote by
C_{O},c_{O}
or
C(O),c(o)
, positive constants that depend on
O
, which might vary from line to line in the computations.

For a linear operator
A
acting on a vector space
X
, we denote the action of
A
on
h\in X
by
Ah
or
A[h]
, depending on whether a a clarification is necessary. In case where
X
is a Hilbert space, we denote by
A^{T}
be the adjoint operator of
A
.

2.1
Function spaces and norms

2.1.0.1
Usual Sobolev spaces

We denote by
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
the space of square-integrable
2\pi
-periodic real-valued functions. We denote for
s\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle L^{2}_{0}:=\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)dx=0\right\},\quad H^{s}_{0}:=\left\{u\in H^{s}(\mathbb{T}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)dx=0\right\},\quad C^{\infty}_{0}:=\cap_{s\geq 0}H^{s}_{0}.

(2.1)

For
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, we think of a
\varphi
-dependent function
f(\varphi)\in C^{\infty}_{0}
as a function
f=f(\varphi,x)
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1}
.
We denote the Sobolev spaces:

\displaystyle H^{s}_{\varphi,x}:=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1}),\quad H^{s}_{\varphi}:=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu}),\quad H^{s}_{x}:=H^{s}(\mathbb{T}),

(2.2)

\displaystyle L^{2}_{\varphi,x}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1}),\quad L^{2}_{\varphi}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu}),\quad L^{2}_{x}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{T}),

\displaystyle C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}:=\cap_{s\geq 0}H^{s}_{\varphi,x},\quad C^{\infty}_{\varphi}:=\cap_{s\geq 0}H^{s}_{\varphi},\quad C^{\infty}_{x}:=\cap_{s\geq 0}H^{s}_{x}.

For
u\in C^{\infty}_{0}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, we denote its Fourier modes by

\displaystyle\widehat{u}_{j}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)e^{-\text{i}jx}dx,\quad\text{ and }\quad\widehat{f}_{j}(l):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu+1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(\varphi,x)e^{-\text{i}(l\cdot\varphi+jx)}dxd\varphi.

so that the Fourier inversion formula reads

\displaystyle u(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{u}_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\quad\text{ and }\quad f(\varphi,x)=\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu},j\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{f}_{j}(l)e^{\text{i}(l\cdot\varphi+jx)}.

(2.3)

We use the norm in
H^{s}_{\varphi,x}
, defined as

\displaystyle\rVert f\rVert_{s}:=\rVert f\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}:=\sqrt{\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu+1}}\langle l,j\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{f}_{i}(l)|^{2}},\text{ where $\langle l,j\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|l|^{2}+|j|^{2})}$.}

(2.4)

Throughout the paper, we consider
H^{s}_{\varphi,x}
, where the regularity index
s
is assumed to be
s\geq s_{0}
, unless specified, and
s_{0}
is fixed so that

\displaystyle s\geq s_{0}>\frac{\nu+2}{2}.

(2.5)

For such
s,s_{0}
, the usual Sobolev embedding theorem gives us that

\displaystyle\rVert fg\rVert_{s}\leq_{s}\rVert f\rVert_{s}\rVert g\rVert_{s_{0}}+\rVert f\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert g\rVert_{s},\text{ for all $f,g\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}$}.

(2.6)

Lemma 2.1
.

[
16
, Lemma 2.2]

Let
a_{0},b_{0}\geq 0
and
p,q>0
. For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon,p,q)>0
such that

\rVert u\rVert_{{a_{0}+p}}\rVert v\rVert_{{b_{0}+q}}\leq\varepsilon\rVert u\rVert_{{a_{0}+p+q}}\rVert v\rVert_{{b_{0}}}+C\rVert u\rVert_{a_{0}}\rVert v\rVert_{{b_{0}+p+q}},\text{ for all $u,v\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}$.}

2.1.0.2
\omega
-dependent functions

For a set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
and a Hilbert space
(E,\rVert\cdot\rVert_{E})
, we consider
E
-valued functions
u=u(\omega)
that depend on
\omega\in\Omega
. For such functions, we define the norms

\displaystyle\rVert u\rVert_{E,\Omega}^{\sup}:=\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\rVert u(\omega)\rVert_{E},\quad\text{ and }\quad\rVert u\rVert_{E,\Omega}^{\text{lip}}:=\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega,\omega_{1}\neq\omega_{2}}\frac{\rVert u(\omega_{1})-u(\omega_{2})\rVert_{E}}{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|}.

Given
0<\gamma<1
, we denote

\displaystyle\rVert u\rVert_{E}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}:=\rVert u\rVert_{E,\Omega}^{\sup}+\gamma\rVert u\rVert_{E,\Omega}^{\text{lip}},\quad\text{Lip}(\Omega,E):=\left\{f:\Omega\mapsto E\ |\ \rVert f\rVert_{E}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}<\infty\right\}.

(2.7)

In case
E=\mathbb{R}^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we simply denote

\displaystyle|u|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}:=\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}|u(\omega)|+\gamma\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega}\frac{|u(\omega_{1})-u(\omega_{2})|}{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|}.

(2.8)

When
E=H^{s}_{\varphi,x}
, we simply denote

\rVert u\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}=:\rVert u\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}.

We have useful estimates for such
\omega
-dependent functions:

Lemma 2.2

(Multiplication)
.

[
6
, Lemma 2.3]

\rVert uv\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\leq_{s}\rVert u\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\rVert v\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}+\rVert u\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\rVert v\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)},\text{ for all $f,g\in\text{Lip}(\Omega,H^{s}_{\varphi,x})$}.

Lemma 2.3

(Interpolation)
.

Let
p\geq q\geq 0
. Then, there exists a constant
C=C(s_{0},q,p)
such that

\displaystyle\rVert u\rVert_{s_{0}+q}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\leq C\left(\rVert u\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s_{0}}\right)^{1-\frac{q}{p}}\left(\rVert u\rVert_{s_{0}+p}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\right)^{\frac{q}{p}}.

Proof.

Apply the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality to
u(\omega)
and
\gamma\frac{u(\omega_{1})-u(\omega_{2})}{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|}
for
\omega,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
and take the supremum over
\omega,\omega_{1},\omega_{2}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4

(Change of variables)
.

[
8
, Lemma 2.4]

Let
\beta:\mathbb{T}\mapsto\mathbb{T}
be such that
\rVert\beta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})}<\frac{1}{2}
. Then,

(1)

f_{\beta}:x\mapsto x+\beta(x)
is invertible, and there exists
\tilde{\beta}=\tilde{\beta}(x)
such that
f_{\beta}^{-1}(x)=x+\tilde{\beta}(x)
.

(2)

If
\beta
depends on
\omega\in\Omega
and
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, it holds that
\rVert\tilde{\beta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})}\leq_{s}\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{W^{s+1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})}
.

(3)

Given
u\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, it holds that denoting
(u\circ f_{\beta})(\varphi,x):=u(\varphi,x+\beta(\varphi,x))
,

\rVert u\circ f_{\beta}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\leq_{s}\left(\rVert u\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s+1}+\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{W^{s,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})}\rVert u\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\right).

2.2
Linear operators

Let
\mathcal{A}:C^{\infty}_{x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{x}
be a linear operator.
We denote
\mathcal{A}^{T}
to be its adjoint operator, that is,

(\mathcal{A}[u],v)_{L^{2}_{x}}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{A}u(x)\overline{v}(x)dx=\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\overline{\mathcal{A}^{T}v}(x)dx=(u,\mathcal{A}^{T}[v])_{L^{2}_{x}},

where
\overline{u}(x)
is the complex conjugation of
u(x)
. We also consider linear operators
\mathcal{A}:C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
. Clearly, we can think of
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\varphi)
as a
\varphi
-dependent linear operator in
C^{\infty}_{x}
. Thanks to the following elementary relation:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}[f],g)_{L^{2}_{\varphi,x}}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathcal{A}(\varphi)\left(f(\varphi,x)\right)\overline{g}(\varphi,x)dxd\varphi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(\varphi,x)\overline{(\mathcal{A}(\varphi))^{T}g(\varphi,x)}dxd\varphi=(f,\mathcal{A}^{T}[g])_{L^{2}_{\varphi,x}},

we have that
\mathcal{A}^{T}(\varphi)=\mathcal{A}(\varphi)^{T}
, we simply use the notation
\mathcal{A}^{T}
to denote the adjoint of
\mathcal{A}
as a linear operator in
C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
or
C^{\infty}_{x}
, without any confusion.

For a linear operator
\mathcal{A}:C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, we have its matrix representation as (recalling our convention of the Fourier series expansion in (
2.3
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}f(\varphi,x)=\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in\mathbb{Z},\ l,l^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}}\mathcal{A}^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l-l^{\prime})\widehat{f}_{j_{1}}(l^{\prime})e^{\text{i}(l\cdot\varphi+j_{2}x)}.

(2.9)

Definition 2.5
.

We say that an operator (not necessarily linear)
\mathcal{A}
is real if
\mathcal{A}
maps a real-valued function to a real-valued function.

2.2.1
Pseudo differential operators

We consider symbols
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
. We say
\mathfrak{a}
is a classical symbol of order
m
and denote
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{R}
, if

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{s}\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)|\langle\xi\rangle^{-(m-\eta)}<\infty,\text{ for all $s,\eta\geq 0$},\text{ where $\langle\xi\rangle:=\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}$.}

(2.10)

We denote

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{s}_{x},\eta_{0}}:=\max_{0\leq\eta\leq\eta_{0}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\rVert\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}(\cdot,\xi)\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}\langle\xi\rangle^{\eta-m}.

(2.11)

2.2.1.1
Standard quantization and Weyl quantization

For a given symbol
\mathfrak{a}
, we denote its standard quantization by

\displaystyle Op(\mathfrak{a})[u]=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{a}(x,j)\widehat{u}_{j}e^{\text{i}jx}.

(2.12)

We also define the Weyl quantization of
\mathfrak{a}
by

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[u]:=\sum_{{k,j}\in\mathbb{Z}}\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(k-j,\frac{k+j}{2}\right)\widehat{u}_{j}e^{\text{i}kx},\text{ where }\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(k,\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)e^{-\text{i}kx}dx.

(2.13)

Compared to the usual quantization in (
2.12
), one can easily check that

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=Op(\mathfrak{b})\iff\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(k,\frac{k}{2}+\xi\right)=\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\left(k,\xi\right)\text{ for all $k,\xi\in\mathbb{Z}$}.

(2.14)

For a linear operator
A
, we say a linear operator
A\in OPS^{m}
, if there exists a classical symbol
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
such that
A=Op(\mathfrak{a})
or
A=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
. Note that thanks to Lemma
2.6
, the statement
A\in OPS^{m}
is well-defined, whether we use the standard quantization or the Weyl quantization.

With the above definition of
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
, one can easily check, using (
2.13
), that

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})^{T}=Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{a}}),

(2.15)

therefore,

\displaystyle\text{$Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})$ is a symmetric operator }\iff\text{ $\mathfrak{a}$ is a real-valued symbol.}

(2.16)

The following results are classical:

Lemma 2.6
.

We have that:

(1)

(Equivalence of the quantizations) For
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
such that
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=Op(\mathfrak{b})
, there exists
\mu=\mu(m,\eta)
such that for all
s\geq 0
and
\eta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s}_{x},\eta}\leq_{\eta,s}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},\eta},\quad|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{s}_{x},\eta}\leq_{\eta,s}|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},\eta}.

(2)

(Action as a linear operator) Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
. There exists
\mu=\mu(m)
such that for all
s\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}\leq|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},0}\rVert h\rVert_{H^{m}_{x}}+|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{\mu}_{x},0}\rVert h\rVert_{H^{s+m}_{x}}.

(2.17)

Proof.

Proof of
(1)
.
We prove only

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s}_{x},\eta}\leq_{\eta,s,m}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},\eta},

(2.18)

since the other estimate follows in the same way.
By definition of the norm in (
2.11
), we have that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z},\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|\widehat{\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}}(k,\xi)|^{2}\langle k\rangle^{2s}\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|\widehat{\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}}(j,\xi)|^{2}\langle j\rangle^{2s}\leq_{s,\eta}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta}^{2}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}.

(2.19)

Therefore, using (
2.14
), we compute

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{b}(\cdot,\xi)\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{2s}|\widehat{\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{b}}(k,\xi)|^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{2s}\left|\widehat{\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}}(k,\xi+\frac{k}{2})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{2s+\mu}\left|\widehat{\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}}(k,\xi+\frac{k}{2})\right|^{2}\langle k\rangle^{-\mu}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{jsd2sdszzz}}{\leq_{s,\eta}}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s+\mu,\eta}^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle\xi+\frac{k}{2}\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\langle k\rangle^{-\mu},

(2.20)

for any
\mu\geq 0
. By choosing
\mu:=4|m-\eta|+2
, it is clear that

\langle\xi+\frac{k}{2}\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\langle k\rangle^{-\mu}\leq_{m,\eta}\begin{cases}\langle k\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\langle k\rangle^{-\mu}\leq_{m,\eta}\langle k\rangle^{-2|m-\eta|-2},&\text{ if $\frac{2}{3}|k|\geq|\xi|$,}\\
\langle\xi\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\langle k\rangle^{-\mu}\leq_{m,\eta}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\langle k\rangle^{-2},&\text{ if $\frac{2}{3}|k|\leq|\xi|$}.\end{cases}

Hence, (
2.20
) yields that

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{b}(\cdot,\xi)\rVert^{2}_{H^{s}_{x}}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,m,\eta}|\mathfrak{a}|^{2}_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},\eta}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},\ \frac{2}{3}|k|\geq|\xi|}\langle k\rangle^{-2|m-\eta|-2}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2(m-\eta)}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z},\ \frac{2}{3}|k|\leq 4|\xi|}\langle k\rangle^{-2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq_{m,\eta}|\mathfrak{a}|^{2}_{m,H^{s+\mu}_{x},\eta}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(m-\eta)},

which proves (
2.18
).

Proof of
(2)
.
Let us choose
\mathfrak{b}
so that
Op(\mathfrak{b})=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
using (
2.14
). We compute

\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}=\rVert Op(\mathfrak{b})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k\rangle^{s}\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k-j,j)h_{j}\right)^{2}.

Using
\langle k\rangle^{s}\leq_{s}\langle k-j\rangle^{s}+\langle j\rangle^{s}
, we have

\displaystyle\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}\leq_{s}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k-j\rangle^{s}|\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k-j,j)||h_{j}|\right)^{2}+\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}|\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k-j,j)|\langle j\rangle^{s}|h_{j}|\right)^{2}\right)

(2.21)

Using (
2.19
) with
\eta=0
, we have

|\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k-j,j)|\leq|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s+2}_{x},0}\langle k-j\rangle^{-s-2}\langle j\rangle^{m},\text{ and }|\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k-j,j)|\leq|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{2}_{x},0}\langle k-j\rangle^{-2}\langle j\rangle^{m}.

Plugging this into (
2.21
), we get

\displaystyle\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}\leq_{s}|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s+2}_{x},0}^{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k-j\rangle^{-2}\langle j\rangle^{m}|h_{j}|\right)^{2}+|\mathfrak{b}|^{2}_{m,H^{2}_{x},0}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\langle k-j\rangle^{-2}\langle j\rangle^{s+m}|h_{j}|\right)^{2}

(2.22)

Let
\underline{g},\underline{h}_{m},\underline{h}_{s+m}
, be such that

\underline{g}(x):=\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{-2}e^{\text{i}kx},\quad\underline{h}_{m}(x):=\sum_{j}\langle j\rangle^{m}|h_{j}|e^{\text{i}jx},\quad\underline{h}_{s+m}(x):=\sum_{j}\langle j\rangle^{s+m}|h_{j}|e^{\text{i}jx}.

Clearly,

\rVert\underline{g}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C,\quad\rVert\underline{h}_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq\rVert h\rVert_{H^{m}_{x}},\quad\rVert\underline{h}_{s+m}\rVert_{L^{2}_{x}}\leq\rVert h\rVert_{H^{s+m}_{x}},

for some
C>0
.
Hence, it follows from (
2.22
) that

\displaystyle\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s+2}_{x},0}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\underline{g}(x)\underline{h}_{m}(x))^{2}dx+|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{2}_{x},0}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\underline{g}(x)\underline{h}_{s+m}(x))^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq C\left(|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{s+2}_{x},0}^{2}\rVert h\rVert_{H^{m}_{x}}^{2}+|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,H^{2}_{x},0}^{2}\rVert h\rVert_{H^{s+m}_{x}}^{2}\right).

Using
(1)
, we obtain (
2.17
).
∎

2.2.1.2
Compositions:

For two symbols
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}}
, it follows straightforwardly from the definitions in (
2.12
) and (
2.13
) that

\displaystyle Op(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op(\mathfrak{b})=Op(\mathfrak{c}),\quad\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}},\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}(k,\xi)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(j_{1},\xi+j_{2})\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(j_{2},\xi),

(2.23)

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{c}),\quad\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}},\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}(k,\xi)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(j_{1},\xi+\frac{j_{2}}{2}\right)\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\left(j_{2},\xi-\frac{j_{1}}{2}\right).

Lemma 2.7
.

For two symbols
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}}
, the composition of
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}),Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})

\displaystyle=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{c}),

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{c}}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}+\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}+\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}^{1}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}},

where
\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}:=\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}-\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{b}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}
, and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}^{1}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}-2},\ \sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}-3}
with

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}^{1}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(-\partial_{xx}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{b}+2\partial_{x\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x\xi}\mathfrak{b}-\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{xx}\mathfrak{b}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}}(k,\xi)

\displaystyle=(a_{1}b_{4}+a_{2}(b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{3}(b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{4}(b_{1}+b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})),\text{ where $a_{i},b_{i}$ are given in \eqref{fsdsdnotsds}.}

Proof.

Recall the Fourier series representation of the composed symbol from (
2.23
):

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}(k,\xi)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(j_{1},\xi+\frac{j_{2}}{2}\right)\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\left(j_{2},\xi-\frac{j_{1}}{2}\right).

(2.25)

Using the Taylor expansion of smooth functions,

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}f^{(n)}(0)\frac{x^{n}}{n!}+x^{N+1}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(N+1)}(x\tau)\frac{(1-\tau)^{N}}{N!}d\tau\text{ for $x\in\mathbb{R}$},

(2.26)

we consider the expansion of
\widehat{\mathfrak{a}},\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}
in the variable
\xi
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(j_{1},\xi+\frac{j_{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(j_{1},\xi)+\widehat{\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{a}}(j_{1},\xi)\frac{\text{i}j_{2}}{2\text{i}}+\frac{1}{2!}\widehat{\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{a}}(j_{1},\xi)\left(\frac{\text{i}j_{2}}{2\text{i}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{j_{2}}{2}\right)^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{\partial_{\xi\xi\xi}\mathfrak{a}}(j_{1},\xi+\tau\frac{j_{2}}{2})\frac{(1-\tau)^{2}}{2!}d\tau

(2.27)

\displaystyle=:a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{4},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\left(j_{2},\xi-\frac{j_{1}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(j_{2},\xi)-\widehat{\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{b}}(j_{2},\xi)\frac{\text{i}j_{1}}{2\text{i}}+\frac{1}{2!}\widehat{\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{b}}(j_{2},\xi)\left(\frac{\text{i}j_{1}}{2\text{i}}\right)^{2}-\left(\frac{j_{1}}{2}\right)^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\widehat{\partial_{\xi\xi\xi}\mathfrak{b}}(j_{2},\xi-\tau\frac{j_{1}}{2})\frac{(1-\tau)^{2}}{2!}d\tau

\displaystyle=:b_{1}+b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4}.

Then, we can collect the terms as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}\left(j_{1},\xi+\frac{j_{2}}{2}\right)\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}\left(j_{2},\xi-\frac{j_{1}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=(a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{3}+a_{4})(b_{1}+b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})

\displaystyle=a_{1}b_{1}+(a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1})+(a_{1}b_{3}+a_{2}b_{2}+a_{3}b_{1})

\displaystyle\ +(a_{1}b_{4}+a_{2}(b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{3}(b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{4}(b_{1}+b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})).

Summing up all
j_{1},j_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}a_{1}b_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b},

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}(a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\left(\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{b}-\partial_{\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}\right)

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}(a_{1}b_{3}+a_{2}b_{2}+a_{3}b_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{b}+\partial_{x\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x\xi}\mathfrak{b}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\xi\xi}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{xx}\mathfrak{b}\right)=:\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}.

Denoting the rest by

\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}:=\sum_{k}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=k}(a_{1}b_{4}+a_{2}(b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{3}(b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})+a_{4}(b_{1}+b_{2}+b_{3}+b_{4})),

we obtain the expression for
\mathfrak{c}
in (
2.24
). Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{m}
in (
2.10
) and noting that
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}
are smooth in the variable
x
, it follows straightforwardly that
\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}-2},\ \sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}-3}
(we do not prove quantitative estimates for
\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}},\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}
here, but we postpone them to Lemma
2.14
).
∎

Remark 2.8
.

In case
\mathfrak{a}
or
\mathfrak{b}
is independent of
x
, we have cleaner expression for the composition. Indeed, if
\mathfrak{a}(\xi)
is independent of
x
, then it immediately follows from (
2.23
) that

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})=\mathfrak{a}\left(\xi+\frac{k}{2}\right)\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k,\xi),\quad Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})\circ Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}(k,\xi)\mathfrak{a}\left(\xi-\frac{k}{2}\right).

(2.28)

This immediately implies that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=Op^{W}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}+i\xi\mathfrak{a}\right),\quad Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\partial_{x}=Op^{W}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}+\text{i}\xi\mathfrak{a}\right).

(2.29)

2.2.1.3
Symmetric commutator

The usual commutator of two linear operators is defined as

\displaystyle[A,B]:=AB-BA.

(2.30)

We also consider a slight variation:

\displaystyle[A,B]_{x}:=A\partial_{x}B-B\partial_{x}A.

(2.31)

Note that if
A,B
are symmetric operators, then
[A,B]_{x}
is also a symmetric operator, since
\partial_{x}
is asymmetric.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
A=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
,
B=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})
for some
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}}
. Then
[A,B]_{x}\in OPS^{m+m^{\prime}}
. That is, there exists a symbol
\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}}
such that

\displaystyle[A,B]_{x}=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}).

(2.32)

The symbol
\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}
admits an expansion

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b})(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}_{x}+r_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}},

(2.33)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}_{x}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}-\mathfrak{b}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}+\xi\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}=\partial_{\xi}(\xi\mathfrak{a})\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}-\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{\xi}(\xi\mathfrak{b})\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}},

\displaystyle r_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\xi\mathfrak{a}}\right)\in\mathcal{S}^{m+m^{\prime}-2},

where
\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}^{i}
for
i=1,2
are as in Lemma
2.7
.

Proof.

From Lemma
2.7
and (
2.29
), we have that

\displaystyle A\partial_{x}B

\displaystyle=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}+\text{i}\xi\mathfrak{b})=\frac{1}{2}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b})+\text{i}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ Op^{W}(\mathfrak{\xi\mathfrak{b}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}Op^{W}\left(\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}+\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\left\{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}\right\}+(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}})\right)+\text{i}Op^{W}\left(\left(\mathfrak{a}\xi\mathfrak{b}+\frac{1}{2\text{i}}\left\{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}\right\}+\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}\right)+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}\right)

\displaystyle=Op^{W}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{a}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}+\frac{1}{4\text{i}}\left\{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}\right\}+\text{i}\mathfrak{a}\xi\mathfrak{b}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}\right\}+\text{i}\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}^{1}\right)+Op^{W}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}\right).

Similarly, we have

B\partial_{x}A=Op^{W}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{b}\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}+\frac{1}{4\text{i}}\left\{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}\right\}+\text{i}\mathfrak{b}\xi\mathfrak{a}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{\mathfrak{b},\xi\mathfrak{a}\right\}+\text{i}\sigma_{\mathfrak{b},\xi\mathfrak{a}}^{1}\right)+Op^{W}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\xi\mathfrak{a}}\right)

Using the definitions of
\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}
and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}}^{1}
in Lemma
2.7
, we have

\displaystyle A\partial_{x}B-B\partial_{x}A

\displaystyle=Op^{W}\left(\left\{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\right\}_{x}\right)+Op^{W}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{b}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{a},\xi\mathfrak{b}}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}}+\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\partial_{x}\mathfrak{a}})+\text{i}\sigma^{2}_{\mathfrak{b},\xi\mathfrak{a}}\right)\right),

which gives us (
2.33
).
∎

2.2.1.4
Norms of symbols

As in Subsection
2.1
, we will consider symbols that depend on
\omega\in\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
and
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, that is,
\mathfrak{a}(\omega)=\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
. We define the norms:

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}(\omega)|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\max_{0\leq\eta\leq\eta_{0}}\sup_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}\rVert\partial_{\xi}^{\eta}\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\cdot,\cdot,\xi)\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}\langle\xi\rangle^{\eta-m},

(2.34)

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}^{\sup}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}|\mathfrak{a}(\omega)|_{m,s,\eta_{0}},

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}^{\text{lip}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega}\frac{|\mathfrak{a}(\omega_{1})-\mathfrak{a}(\omega_{2})|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}}{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|},

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}

\displaystyle:=|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}^{\sup}+\gamma|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}^{\text{lip}},\text{ for some $\gamma\in(0,1)$.}

The following lemma can be easily proved by following the proof of Lemma
2.6
, word by word. For simplicity, we omit the proof:

Lemma 2.10
.

We have that:

(1)

(Equivalence of the quantizations) For
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
such that
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=Op(\mathfrak{b})
, there exists
\mu=\mu(m,\eta)
such that for all
s\geq s_{0}
and
\eta\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,s,\eta}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\leq_{\eta,s}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,{s+\mu},\eta}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)},\quad|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s,\eta}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\leq_{\eta,s}|\mathfrak{b}|_{m,{s+\mu},\eta}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}.

(2)

(Action as a linear operator) Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
. There exists
\mu=\mu(m)
such that for all
s\geq s_{0}
,

\displaystyle\rVert Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}\leq_{s}|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,{s+\mu},0}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s_{0}+m}+|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,{s_{0}+\mu},0}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{{s+m}}.

(2.35)

In what follows, we collect useful properties of the norms for the symbols depending on
\omega,\varphi
. We denote the Fourier modes of a symbol
\mathfrak{a}
in
\varphi,x
by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}(\omega,\varphi,j,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)e^{-\text{i}jx}dx,\quad\widehat{\mathfrak{a}}^{\varphi,x}(\omega,l,j,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu+1}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{T}}\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)e^{-\text{i}(l\cdot\varphi+jx)}d\varphi dx.

(2.36)

We first prove the estimates for a product of two symbols:

Lemma 2.11
.

Given
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
,
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}}
for some
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}|_{m+m^{\prime},s,\eta}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq_{m,m^{\prime},s,\eta}|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s,\eta}|\mathfrak{b}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m^{\prime},s_{0},\eta}+|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s_{0},\eta}|\mathfrak{b}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s,\eta}\text{ for $\gamma\in(0,1)$.}

(2.37)

Proof.

We first prove

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b}|_{m+m^{\prime},s,\eta_{0}}^{\sup}

\displaystyle\leq_{m,m^{\prime},s,\eta}|\mathfrak{a}|^{\sup}_{m,s,\eta}|\mathfrak{b}|^{\sup}_{m^{\prime},s_{0},\eta}+|\mathfrak{a}|^{\sup}_{m,s_{0},\eta}|\mathfrak{b}|^{\sup}_{m^{\prime},s,\eta}.

(2.38)

Let
\omega
be fixed. For simplicity, we omit the dependence on
\varphi,x,\xi
in the notation. For a fixed
0\leq\eta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it is clear that

\left(\partial_{\xi}\right)^{\eta}(\mathfrak{a}(\omega)\mathfrak{b}(\omega))=\sum_{n=0}^{\eta}\binom{\eta}{n}((\partial_{\xi})^{\eta-n}\mathfrak{a}(\omega))((\partial_{\xi})^{n}\mathfrak{b}(\omega)).

Therefore using (
2.6
) and splitting
\langle\xi\rangle^{\eta-(m+m^{\prime})}\leq_{\eta,m,m^{\prime}}\langle\xi\rangle^{\eta-n-m}\langle\xi\rangle^{n-m^{\prime}}
, we get
