-Hessian of
H
at
f
,
\nabla^{2}H(f)
, as the unique linear operator such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}^{2}H(f)[h](x)g(x)dx=\frac{d^{2}}{dsdt}H(f+th+sg)\bigg|_{s=t=0}.

(2.126)

Clearly, the gradient and the Hessian of
H
above are well-defined thanks to the classical Riesz representation theorem.

We consider the symplectic form
\sigma
on
L^{2}_{0}
, given by

\displaystyle\sigma(f,g):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\partial_{x}^{-1}f\right)(x)g(x)dx.

(2.127)

We define the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
as the unique vector field such that

\displaystyle(\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}H(f),h)_{L^{2}}=\sigma(X_{H}(f),h),

Therefore, from (
2.127
), we see that the Hamiltonian vector field can be written as

X_{H}(f)=\partial_{x}\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}H(f).

Let
\Phi:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
be a symplectic transformation, that is,

\sigma(\Phi(f),\Phi(g))=\sigma(f,g),\quad\text{ for all $f,g\in L^{2}_{0}$.}

Using the definition of
\sigma
in (
2.127
), it is clear that a linear operator
\Phi
is a symplectic transformation if and only if

\displaystyle\Phi^{T}\partial_{x}^{-1}\Phi=\partial_{x}^{-1}.

(2.128)

2.4.0.1
Hamiltonian operators

We define a class of linear operators on
C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, generated by a Hamiltonian.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
M=M(\varphi):L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L_{0}^{2}
be a
\varphi
-dependent linear operator. We say that a linear operator
\mathcal{L}:C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}[h]=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h-\partial_{x}M[h],

(2.129)

is Hamiltonian, if
M(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
is symmetric, that is
M(\varphi)=M(\varphi)^{T}
for each
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
.

Let
\Phi=\Phi(\varphi)
be a symplectic transformation for each
\varphi
. The conjugation of the linear operator of the form in (
2.129
) with
\Phi
is also Hamiltonian, that is,

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{L}\Phi=\omega\partial_{\varphi}-\partial_{x}{N},\quad N=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\Phi^{T})\partial_{x}^{-1}\Phi+\Phi^{T}M\Phi.

Indeed,
N
is symmetric, since differentiating (
2.128
), we have
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\Phi^{T})\partial_{x}^{-1}\Phi+\Phi^{T}\partial_{x}^{-1}\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}(\Phi)=0
. Therefore we have the following:

Lemma 2.41
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a Hamiltonian operator and
\Phi
is a symplectic transformation, then
\Phi^{-1}\mathcal{L}\Phi
is also a Hamiltonian operator.

2.4.0.2
Homogeneous Hamiltonians

Typically, a Hamiltonian
H
in
L^{2}_{0}
is not well-defined everywhere in
L^{2}_{0}
but its
L^{2}_{0}
-gradient,
\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}H:f\mapsto\nabla H(f)
, can be defined as a nonlinear operator between two Sobolev spaces. We specify an elementary notion arising from the
L^{2}_{0}
-gradient of a Hamiltonian.

Definition 2.42
.

We say a
k
-linear map
A:(C^{\infty}_{x})^{k}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{x}
is
\mu
-regular, if

\displaystyle\sup_{f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})\in(C^{\infty}_{x})^{k}}\frac{\rVert A(f)\rVert_{H^{s}_{x}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\rVert f_{i}\rVert_{H^{s+\mu}_{x}}\Pi_{j=1,\ j\neq i}^{k}\rVert f_{j}\rVert_{H^{\mu}_{x}}\right)}<\infty,\text{ for all $s\geq 0$.}

For example, a trilinear map
A(f):=\partial_{x}^{2}(f)f^{2}
, is
\mu
-regular, for some
\mu\geq 0
, which can be seen from (
2.6
).
We also specify the notion of a homogeneous Hamiltonian in
L^{2}_{0}
:

Definition 2.43
.

We say a Hamiltonian is
\mu
-regular if
H
admits a homogeneous expansion,

H:=\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}H_{k},

where
H_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
k
such that each
\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}H_{k}
, which is a
(k-1)
-linear map, is
\mu
-regular.

2.5
Time-reversible Hamiltonians

In this subsection, we briefly recall some basic properties of a reversible Hamiltonian system.

Definition 2.44
.

Let
X
be a Hilbert space and
\rho:X\mapsto X
be an involution, that is,
\rho\circ\rho(f)=f,
for all
f\in X
. We say that a Hamiltonian
H
on
X
is reversible (or time-reversible) with respect to
\rho
, if
H\circ\rho=H
.
We also say that
H
is reversibility preserving if
H\circ\rho=-H
. Furthermore, we say that a transformation
\Phi:X\mapsto X
is reversibility preserving if
\rho\circ\Phi\circ\rho=\Phi
.

2.5.0.1
Reversible Hamiltonian on
L^{2}_{0}
.

We denote by
\rho:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
, the involution given as

\displaystyle\rho(f)(x):=f(-x).

(2.130)

Lemma 2.45
.

Let
G
be a reversibility preserving Hamiltonian in
L^{2}_{0}
with respect to an involution
\rho
, and let
\Phi_{G}^{t}
be the unique flow map determined by the Hamiltonian PDE at time
t
:

\displaystyle f_{t}=X_{G}(f).

(2.131)

Then,
\Phi^{t}_{G}
is a reversibility preserving map.

Proof.

Since
G\circ\rho=-G
, differentiating
G(\rho(f))=-G(f)
in
f
, we have
\rho\left(\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}G(\rho(f))\right)=-\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}G(f).

Hence, taking
\partial_{x}
on both sides, we see that

\displaystyle\rho X_{G}(\rho f)=X_{G}(f),\text{ for all $f\in L^{2}_{0}$}.

(2.132)

In order to show that
\Phi_{G}^{t}
is reversibility preserving, we need to show that

\displaystyle\rho\Phi^{t}_{G}(\rho(f))=\Phi^{t}_{G}(f).

(2.133)

By its definition,
\Phi_{G}^{t}
solves
\partial_{t}\Phi^{t}_{G}(f)=X_{G}(\Phi^{t}_{G}(f))
with
\Phi_{G}^{0}(f)=f
. Furthermore,
\rho\Phi_{G}^{t}(\rho f)
also solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left(\rho\Phi_{G}^{t}(\rho f)\right)=\rho X_{G}(\Phi_{G}^{t}(\rho f))\overset{\eqref{Hamitlon_rev}}{=}X_{G}(\rho\Phi_{G}^{t}(\rho f)),\text{ with }\rho\Phi^{t}_{G}(\rho f)|_{t=0}=\rho\circ\rho(f)=f.

Since the flow map
\Phi^{t}_{G}
is unique, we have
\rho\Phi_{G}^{t}(\rho f)=f
and this implies (
2.133
).∎

Definition 2.46
.

[
6
, Definition 2.2]

Let

\displaystyle X:=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}:f(-\varphi,-x)=f(\varphi,x)\right\},\quad Y:=\left\{f\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}:f(-\varphi,-x)=-f(\varphi,x)\right\}

(2.134)

We say that a linear operator
\mathcal{A}:C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
is

1.

reversible if
X\mapsto Y
and
Y\mapsto X
.

2.

reversibility preserving
X\mapsto X,\ Y\mapsto Y
.

Definition 2.47
.

We say a symbol
\mathfrak{a}
is a reversible symbol, if
\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
is a reversible operator. We say
\mathfrak{a}
is a reversibility preserving symbol, if
\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
is a reversibility preserving operator.

In view of the real operators (Definition
2.5
), symmetric operators, and reversible/reversibility preserving operators (Definition
2.47
), one can easily show from the definition of Weyl quantization in (
2.13
) that

\displaystyle\text{$\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})$ is a real operator if $\mathfrak{a}(\varphi,x,\xi)=\overline{a}(\varphi,x,-\xi)$},

(2.135)

\displaystyle\text{$Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})$ is a symmetric operator if $\mathfrak{a}(\varphi,x,\xi)=\overline{\mathfrak{a}}(\varphi,x,\xi)$},

\displaystyle\text{$\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})$ is a reversible operator if $\mathfrak{a}(\varphi,x,\xi)=\mathfrak{a}(-\varphi,-x,-\xi)$}.

For a general linear operator
\mathcal{A}
, we have, using the notation in (
2.9
),

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{A}$ is a real operator if $\mathcal{A}^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l)=\overline{\mathcal{A}^{-j_{1}}_{-j_{2}}(-l)}$},

(2.136)

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{A}$ is a symmetric operator if $(\mathcal{A})^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l)=(\overline{\mathcal{A})^{j_{2}}_{j_{1}}(l)}$},

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{A}$ is a reversible operator if $(\mathcal{A})^{j_{1}}_{j_{2}}(l)=\mathcal{A}^{-j_{1}}_{-j_{2}}(-l)$}.

Lemma 2.48
.

If
\mathfrak{a}
is a reversible symbol, and
\mathfrak{b}
is a reversibility preserving symbol, then
\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}
is a reversible symbol.

Proof.

Recalling the definition of
\star
from (
2.32
), we have that

\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b})=\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\circ\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})-\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})\circ\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}),

where each term on the right-hand side is a composition of a reversible operator and a reversibility preserving operator. Therefore,
\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b})
is a reversible operator.
∎

2.5.0.2
Reversible Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
.

Another important phase space that we will work on is
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
, where
H_{S^{\perp}}
, a subspace of
L^{2}_{0}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle H_{S^{\perp}}:=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{0}:f_{j}=0\text{ if $j\in S$},\right\},

\displaystyle S^{+}=\left\{0<j_{1},\ldots,<j_{\nu}\in\mathbb{N}\right\},\ S:=S\cup(-S),\quad S^{\perp}:=\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left(S\cup\left\{0\right\}\right),

for a finite subset
S\subset\mathbb{Z}
. Let
H:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
be a Hamiltonian, and let a symplectic two form
\mathcal{W}
be such that

\mathcal{W}:=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}d_{\theta_{i}}\wedge d_{y_{i}}\oplus\sigma_{S^{\perp}},

where
\sigma_{S^{\perp}}
is the restriction of
\sigma
to
H_{S^{\perp}}
, that is,

\sigma_{S^{\perp}}(z,z)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\partial_{x}^{-1}z\right)(x)z(x)dx
. Furthermore, let us assume that
H
is reversible with respect to
\rho_{*}
, defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{*}(\theta,y,z):=(-\theta,y,\rho(z)),

(2.137)

where
\rho
is as in (
2.130
). In the rest of this section, we recall some basic properties of a dynamical system induced by a reversible Hamiltonian
H
with respect to the symplectic form
\mathcal{W}
and the involution
\rho_{*}
.

Definition 2.49
.

We say a smooth embedding
i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
,
i(\varphi):=(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))
is reversible if
i(-\varphi)=\rho_{*}i(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, that is,

\theta(-\varphi)=-\theta(\varphi),\ y(-\varphi)=y(\varphi),\ z(-\varphi)=\rho z(\varphi).

We denote

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle:=\left\{i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}:\rho_{*}i(\varphi)=i(-\varphi))\right\},\quad Y_{i}:=\left\{i:\mathbb{T}^{\varphi}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}:\rho_{*}i(\varphi)=-i(-\varphi))\right\},

(2.138)

\displaystyle X_{e}

\displaystyle:=\left\{f:\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:f(\varphi)=f(-\varphi)\right\},\quad Y_{o}:=\left\{f:\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:f(\varphi)=-f(-\varphi)\right\},

\displaystyle X_{\perp}

\displaystyle:=\left\{z\in H_{S^{\perp}}:z(\varphi,x)=z(-\varphi,-x)\right\},\quad Y_{\perp}:=\left\{z\in H_{S^{\perp}}:z(\varphi,x)=-z(-\varphi,-x)\right\}.

It is clear that if
i=(\theta,y,z)\in X_{i}
then
\theta\in Y_{o},y\in X_{e}
and
z\in X_{\perp}
.

In view of (
2.125
) and (
2.126
), we define
\nabla_{z}H(i),\ \nabla_{z}^{2}H(i)[z_{0}]\in H_{S^{\perp}}
for
z_{0}\in H_{S^{\perp}}
be such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\nabla_{z}H(i)(x)z(x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}H(i+t(0,0,z(x)))\bigg|_{t=0},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(i)[z_{0}](x)z(x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}}{dtds}H(i+(0,0,tz_{0}+sz))\bigg|_{t=s=0},

for all
z\in H_{S^{\perp}}
.

Lemma 2.50
.

Let
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
and let
H
be a reversible Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
. Then, the linear map
L:h\mapsto\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h-\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(\varphi))[h]
is reversible.

Proof.

In view of Definition
2.46
, we need to show that
L
maps
X
to
Y
, and
Y
to
X
. We will show that
L
maps
X
to
Y
only, because the other case can be done in the same way.
Since
H
is reversible, we have that for all
\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu},t\in\mathbb{R}
and
h\in X
,

H(\varphi,0,th(\varphi))=H(\rho_{*}(\varphi,0,th(\varphi)))=H(-\varphi,0,t\rho(h)(-\varphi)).

Differentiating in
t
at
t=0
, we have

\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\varphi,0,0)[h(\varphi)]=\rho(\nabla_{z}^{2}H(-\varphi,0,0)[\rho(h)(-\varphi)]).

Using
\rho(h)(\varphi)=h(-\varphi)
, and
\partial_{x}\rho(h)(\varphi)=-\rho(\partial_{x}h)(\varphi)
, we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(\varphi))[h(\varphi)]=\partial_{x}\rho(\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(-\varphi))[h(-\varphi)])=-\rho(\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(-\varphi))[h(-\varphi)]).

(2.139)

Furthermore, for
h\in X
, that is,
h(\varphi,x)=h(-\varphi,-x)
, we have that
\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h(-\varphi,-x)=-\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h(\varphi,x)
. Thus, it follows that

\displaystyle Lh(-\varphi,-x)

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h(-\varphi,-x)-\rho(\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(-\varphi))[h(-\varphi)])

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{revsd22323s}}{=}-\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}h(\varphi,x)+\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\bar{i}(\varphi))[h(\varphi)]

\displaystyle=-Lh(\varphi,x).

Hence,
Lh\in Y
, which proves
L
maps
X
to
Y
.
∎

2.6
Translation invariance

In thus subsection, we collect properties of a Hamiltonian in
L^{2}_{0}
that is invariant under
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation for some
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
.

For
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
, let us denote

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)(x):=f(x+\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}),\text{ for $f\in C^{\infty}_{x}$,}\quad X_{\mathtt{M}}:=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{0}:\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)=f\right\}.

(2.140)

It is clear that

\displaystyle f\in X_{\mathtt{M}}\iff f(x)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left\{0\right\}}\widehat{f}_{\mathtt{M}j}e^{\text{i}\mathtt{M}jx},

(2.141)

that is, the Fourier coefficients of
f
are supported only in the modes of multiples of
\mathtt{M}
.

Definition 2.51
.

For
\mathtt{M}\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that a Hamiltonian
H
in
L^{2}_{0}
is invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation if
H(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))=H(f).
Furthermore, we say that a transformation
\Phi:C^{\infty}_{x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{x}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving, if
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)=\Phi(f)
.

Lemma 2.52
.

If a linear map
\mathcal{A}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving, then
\mathcal{A}:X_{\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{\mathtt{M}}
.

Proof.

For
f\in X_{\mathtt{M}}
, we have
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}\mathcal{A}(f)=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{2}\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\mathcal{A}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}f)=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{2}\mathcal{A}(f)
. Therefore, we have
\mathcal{A}=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}\mathcal{A}(f)
, which implies
\mathcal{A}f\in X_{\mathtt{M}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.53
.

Let
G
be a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant Hamiltonian, and let
\Phi^{t}_{G}
be the unique flow map determined by the Hamiltonian PDE at time
t
:

\displaystyle f_{t}=X_{G}(f).

(2.142)

Then,
\Phi^{t}_{G}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving.

Proof.

Since
G
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, it follows that
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\nabla G(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))
, therefore,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}X_{G}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))=X_{G}(f).

(2.143)

Hence, we have that

\frac{d}{dt}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}X_{G}(\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}X_{G}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f))\overset{\eqref{wearenoty}}{=}X_{G}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)),

which solves (
2.142
). Since
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)\bigg|_{t=0}=f
, we have
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}^{-1}\circ\Phi_{G}^{t}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)=f
for all
t\geq 0
.
∎

2.6.0.1
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant Hamiltonian on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
.

We denote

\displaystyle X_{i,\mathtt{M}}:=\left\{i=(\theta,y,z):\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}:\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(z(\varphi))=z(\varphi)\right\},\quad\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(i):=(\theta,y,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(z)).

(2.144)

Definition 2.54
.

We say that a Hamiltonian
H
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant if
H(\theta,y,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}z)=H(\theta,y,z)
. We say an embedding
i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, if
\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(i)=i
. Lastly, we say that a symbol
\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant if
x\mapsto\mathfrak{a}(x,\xi)
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.

3
Hamiltonian structure of the gSQG equations

3.1
Hamiltonian equation in the patch setting

We consider the Hamiltonian structure of the generalized SQG equations in the patch setting. We recall the symplectic structure
(L^{2}_{0},\sigma)
, where the symplectic
2
-form
\sigma
is defined as in (
2.127
).

Throughout the paper, we consider a star-shaped domain
D
defined as

\displaystyle D:=\left\{r(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0\leq r<R(\theta)\right\}.

We denote by
z(\theta)
and
\Psi
the boundary of
D
and stream function determined by
1_{D}
respectively, that is,

\displaystyle z(\theta):=R(\theta)(\cos(\theta),\sin(\theta)),\quad\text{ and }\quad\Psi(x):=1_{D}*\frac{1}{|x|^{\alpha}}.

(3.1)

Recall that the patch solutions for the gSQG equations in (
1.1
) can be written in terms of
R(t,\theta)
as

\displaystyle R(t,\theta)\partial_{t}R(t,\theta)=\nabla\Psi(z(t,\theta))\cdot\partial_{\theta}z(t,\theta).

(3.2)

In order to rewrite (
3.2
) in terms of the Hamiltonian vector field, we define
{H}:L^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
as

\displaystyle H(R)

\displaystyle:=\int_{D}\Psi(x)dx

\displaystyle=\int_{D}\int_{D}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{0}^{R(\theta)}\int_{0}^{R(\eta)}\frac{1}{|r(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)-\rho(\cos\eta,\sin\eta)|^{\alpha}}r\rho drd\rho d\theta d\eta.

(3.3)

Computing
\frac{d}{ds}H(R+sh)\bigg|_{s=0}
, one can immediately find that

\frac{d}{ds}H(R+sh)\bigg|_{s=0}=2\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Psi(z(\theta))R(\theta)h(\theta)d\theta.

In other words, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{L^{2}}H(R)(\theta)=2R(\theta)\Psi(z(\theta)),

(3.4)

where
\nabla_{L^{2}}
denotes the gradient in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
Now, we define a Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(f):=H(\sqrt{1+f}).

(3.5)

Using this and the chain rule, one can easily see from (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) that

\displaystyle\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}\mathcal{H}(f)(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1+f(\theta)}}\nabla_{L^{2}}H(\sqrt{1+f})(\theta)=\Psi(z(\theta)).

(3.6)

Also, for
f(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}_{0}
such that
\sqrt{1+f(t,\theta)}=R(t,\theta)
, it follows immediately that
R(t,\theta)\partial_{t}R(t,\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}f(t,\theta)
. Therefore, we obtain from (
3.6
) and (
3.2
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,\theta)=2\partial_{\theta}\left(\Psi(z(t,\theta))\right)=2\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}\mathcal{H}(f).

(3.7)

By reparametrizing the time as
g(t,\theta):=f\left(\frac{1}{2}t,\theta\right)
, we obtain from (
3.7
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g=2\partial_{\theta}\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}\mathcal{H}(g)=X_{\mathcal{H}}(g).

We summarize the above considerations in the following proposition:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the Hamiltonian given as in (
3.5
) and (
3.1
) and
\sigma
be the symplectic
2
-form given in (
2.127
). Let
f(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}_{0}
be a solution to the Hamiltonian equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,\theta)=X_{\mathcal{H}}(f(t,\cdot)).

(3.8)

Then
\omega(t,x):=1_{D_{t}}(x)
, where
D_{t}:=\left\{r(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:0\leq r<\sqrt{1+f(2t,\theta)}\right\}
is a weak solution to (
1.1
).

In the rest of the paper, we denote the
L^{2}_{0}
-gradient by
\nabla
, instead of
\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}
.

3.2
Expansion of
\mathcal{H}

The main result of this subsection can be summarized in the following proposition:

Proposition 3.2
.

\mathcal{H}(f)
, defined in (
3.5
) and (
3.1
), admits the following homogeneous expansion:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{2}(f)+\mathcal{H}_{3}(f)+\mathcal{H}_{4}(f)+\mathcal{H}_{\geq 5}(f),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2}(f)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta+\frac{1}{8}T_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{2}d\theta,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\alpha}{16}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta-\frac{\alpha}{48}T_{\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{3}d\theta,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4}(f)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\alpha(\alpha+4)}{192}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{3}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{256}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(f^{2})d\theta+\frac{\alpha}{384}\int_{\mathbb{T}}M_{4}(f)(\theta)d\theta

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha(2+\alpha)T_{\alpha}}{384}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(\theta)^{4}d\theta,

where

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}=m_{2,\alpha}(0)\text{ (see \eqref{multiplier_2})},\quad M_{4}(f)(\theta):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(f(\theta)-f(\eta))^{4}d\eta,\quad\Lambda^{\alpha-1}\text{ is as in \eqref{fractional_1}.}

(3.10)

\mathcal{H}_{\geq 5}
collects all the terms homogeneous of degree at least
5
in
f
and its gradient is of the form:

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{\geq 5}(f)

\displaystyle=\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F_{1,\geq 3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),J(\theta,\eta))(f(\theta)-f(\eta))d\eta

(3.11)

\displaystyle+\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F_{2,\geq 4}(f(\theta),f(\eta))d\eta,

where
J(\theta,\eta):=\frac{(f(\theta)-f(\eta))^{2}}{2-2\cos(\theta-\eta)}
for some functions
F_{1,\geq 3}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and
F_{2,\geq 4}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
that are analytic functions near the origin and homogeneous of degree at least
3
and
4
respectively.

Proof.

We split the proof into three steps. In the first step, we rewrite the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in terms of
f
. In the second step, we specify the linear, quadratic and cubic terms of
\nabla\mathcal{H}(f)
. Afterwards, we integrate them to recover the quadratic, cubic and quartic terms of
\mathcal{H}
. In the last step, we show that the terms in
\nabla\mathcal{H}
that are homogeneous of degree at least
4
in
f
are of the form in (
3.11
).

Expression for
\nabla\mathcal{H}
in terms of
f
.

We first rewrite the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
more explicitly. Given
f\in L^{2}_{0}
, we set

\displaystyle R(\theta):=\sqrt{1+f(\theta)},\quad\text{ and }z(\theta):=R(\theta)(\cos\theta,\sin\theta),

(3.12)

so that
z(\theta)
represents a parametrization of
\partial D
, determined by
f
. Using the identity:

\int_{D}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}dy=-\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int_{\partial_{D}}\frac{(x-y)}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}\cdot\vec{n}(y)d\sigma=\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(x-z(\eta))\cdot\partial_{\eta}z(\eta)^{\perp}}{|x-z(\eta)|^{\alpha}}d\eta,

which simply follows from integration by parts, we find that the corresponding stream function
\Psi(z(\theta))
in (
3.1
) can be written as

\displaystyle\Psi(z(\theta))=\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\left(R(\theta)R(\eta)+\frac{(R(\theta)-R(\eta))^{2}}{2-2\cos(\theta-\eta)}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3})d\eta,

(3.13)

where

A_{1}:=R(\theta)R^{\prime}(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta),\quad A_{2}=R(\eta)R(\theta)(1-\cos(\theta-\eta)),\quad A_{3}=-R(\eta)(R(\theta)-R(\eta)).

Recalling (
3.6
) and replacing
R(\theta)
in (
3.13
) by
\sqrt{1+f(\theta)}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}(f)=\Psi(z(\theta))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{1}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})f^{\prime}(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

(3.14)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2(2-\alpha)}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{2}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})d\eta

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})(f(\theta)-f(\eta))d\eta,

where

\displaystyle{\displaystyle J}=J(\theta,\eta):=\frac{(f(\theta)-f(\eta))^{2}}{2-2\cos(\theta-\eta)},

(3.15)

\displaystyle G_{1}(x,y,z)=\left(\sqrt{1+x}\sqrt{1+y}+z\frac{\left(\sqrt{1+x}-\sqrt{1+y}\right)^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{1+x}}{2\sqrt{1+y}}

\displaystyle G_{2}(x,y,z)=\left(\sqrt{1+x}\sqrt{1+y}+z\frac{\left(\sqrt{1+x}-\sqrt{1+y}\right)^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sqrt{1+x}\sqrt{1+y}

\displaystyle G_{3}(x,y,z)=\left(\sqrt{1+x}\sqrt{1+y}+z\frac{\left(\sqrt{1+x}-\sqrt{1+y}\right)^{2}}{(x-y)^{2}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{1+y}(\sqrt{1+x}-\sqrt{1+y})}{x-y}\right).

Explicit formulae for
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{2}
,
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{3}
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{4}
.
We note that the functions
G_{1},G_{2},G_{3}
are analytic near the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(more precisely, they can be extended so that they are analytic near the origin). In order to find
\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{3},\mathcal{H}_{4}
, which will correspond to the quadratic, cubic and quartic parts of the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, we will compute the Taylor series coefficients of
G_{i}
near the origin. For
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, we find that

G_{1}(0,0,0)=\frac{1}{2},\quad\partial_{x}G_{2}(0,0,0)=\partial_{y}G_{2}(0,0,0)=\frac{2-\alpha}{4},\quad G_{3}(0,0,0)=\frac{1}{2}.

This gives the linear term of
\nabla\mathcal{H}
, that is, from (
3.14
), it follows that

\nabla\mathcal{H}_{2}(f)=-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}f(\theta)+\frac{1}{4}T_{\alpha}f(\theta).

Integrating this with respect to
f
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2}=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{8}f^{2}d\theta.

(3.16)

Similarly, we compute
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{3},\nabla\mathcal{H}_{4}
and find that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{3}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{16}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(3f(\theta)^{2}-2f(\theta)f(\eta)-f(\eta)^{2})d\eta-\frac{\alpha}{16}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}d\eta f(\theta)^{2}.

and

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{4}(f)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha}{192}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}((7\alpha+16)f(\theta)^{3}-3(\alpha+4)f(\theta)^{2}f(\eta)-3\alpha f(\theta)f(\eta)^{2}-(\alpha+4)f(\eta)^{3})d\eta

\displaystyle\ +\frac{\alpha}{48}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(f(\theta)-f(\eta))^{3}d\eta+\frac{\alpha}{96}(2+\alpha)T_{\alpha}f(\theta)^{3}.

Integrating them with respect to
f
, one can find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}(f)=\frac{\alpha}{16}\int f^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta-\frac{\alpha}{48}T_{\alpha}\int f^{3}d\theta

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4}(f)=-\frac{\alpha(\alpha+4)}{192}\int f^{3}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}fd\theta-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{256}\int f^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(f^{2})d\theta+\frac{\alpha}{384}\int M_{4}(f)d\theta+\frac{\alpha}{384}(2+\alpha)T_{\alpha}\int f(\theta)^{4}d\theta,

where
T_{\alpha}
and
M_{4}
are as in (
3.10
). With (
3.16
), we obtain (
3.9
).

Expression for
\mathcal{H}_{5}
.

Let
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{5}:=\nabla\mathcal{H}-\sum_{i=2}^{4}\nabla\mathcal{H}_{i}
. Since all linear, quadratic and cubic contributions of
f
on
\nabla\mathcal{H}
are contained in
\sum_{i=2}^{4}\nabla\mathcal{H}_{i}
, we see from (
3.14
) that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{5}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{1,\geq 3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})f^{\prime}(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

(3.17)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2(2-\alpha)}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{2,\geq 4}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})d\eta

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2-\alpha}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{3,\geq 3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})(f(\theta)-f(\eta))d\eta,

where
G_{1,\geq 3}
and
G_{2,\geq 3}
collect all the terms from
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
that are homogeneous of degree at least
3
and
G_{2\geq 4}
collects the terms from
G_{2}
that are homogeneous of degree at least
4
. We claim that there exist
F_{1,\geq 3}(x,y,z),F_{2,\geq 4}(x,y,z)
, which are analytic near
(0,0,0)
, homogeneous of degree at least
3
and
4
respectively, and

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{5}(f)

\displaystyle=\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F_{1,\geq 3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})(f(\theta)-f(\eta))d\eta

\displaystyle\ +\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F_{2,\geq 4}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})d\eta.

(3.18)

Clearly, the second and the third integrals in (
3.17
) are already of the form in (
3.2
), thus once we prove that the first integral in (
3.17
) is of the form (
3.2
), we will finish the proof of the proposition.

For the first integral in (
3.17
), let us write the integral, using the Taylor expansion of
G_{1,\geq 3}
, as

\displaystyle I_{1}:=\int

\displaystyle(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}G_{1,\geq 3}(f(\theta),f(\eta),{J})f^{\prime}(\eta)\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=4}^{\infty}\sum_{k+l+2m=n}C_{k,l,m}f(\theta)^{k}\underbrace{\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(\eta)^{l}f^{\prime}(\eta){J}^{m}\sin(\theta-\eta)d\eta}_{=:a_{l,m}}.

(3.19)

Using integration by parts, it follows immediately that

\displaystyle a_{l,m}=f(\theta)d_{l,m}a_{l-1,m}+c_{l,m},

(3.20)

\displaystyle a_{0,m}=\frac{2-\alpha-2m}{4(2m+1)}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(f(\theta)-f(\eta)){J}^{m}d\eta,

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2m+\alpha-1}{2m+1}\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(f(\theta)-f(\eta)){J}^{m}d\eta,

(3.21)

where

\displaystyle d_{l,m}:=\frac{l}{2m+1+l}

\displaystyle c_{l,m}:=\frac{2-\alpha-2m}{4(2m+1+l)}\left(\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(\eta)^{l}(f(\theta)-f(\eta)){J}^{m}d\eta\right),

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2m+\alpha-1}{2m+1+l}(\int(2-2\cos(\theta-\eta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}f(\eta)^{l}(f(\theta)-f(\eta)){J}^{m}d\eta).

Hence, it follows from (
3.20
) that

a_{l,m}=f(\theta)^{l}\left(\Pi_{j=1}^{l}d_{l,m}\right)a_{0,m}+\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}\left(\Pi_{j=l-i}^{l}d_{j,m}\right)f(\theta)^{i+1}c_{l-i-1,m}+c_{l,m}.

Plugging this into (
3.19
), it is clear that
I_{1}
in (
3.19
) is of the form in (
3.2
). The convergence of the series is guaranteed from the fact that
G_{1}
is analytic near
(0,0,0)
. Therefore we have (
3.2
).

Lastly, since any term in the second integral in (
3.2
) that is homogeneous of degree at least order
1
in
{J}
is of the form as the first integral in (
3.2
), we obtain (
3.11
).
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

The Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
is
\mu_{0}
-regular for some
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}(\alpha)>0
.

Proof.

The result follows straightforwardly from the expansion of
\mathcal{H}
in Proposition
3.2
and successive applications of (
2.6
). ∎

3.3
Conservation of momentum, time-reversibility and
\mathtt{M}
-fold symmetry

Before we close this section, we point out three useful properties of the gSQG equations: Conservation of momentum, time-reversibility and invariance under rotations.

3.3.1
Conservation of momentum

We recall that the gSQG dynamics preserves the angular momentum,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(t,x)|x|^{2}dx
. In the patch setting, this corresponds to (using the notations in (
3.12
)),

\displaystyle M(f):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|f(x)|^{2}dx\text{ is a preserved quantity.}

In other words, we have
\left\{\mathcal{H},M\right\}=0
, where the Poisson bracket
\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}
is defined as

\displaystyle\left\{H,F\right\}(f):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\nabla H(f)\partial_{x}\nabla F(f)dx=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j\neq 0}\text{i}j\left(\partial_{f_{-j}}H\right)(f)\left(\partial_{f_{j}}F\right)(f).

(3.22)

Note that for a homogeneous Hamiltonian
H_{n}
of degree
n
such that

H_{n}(f)=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}f_{j_{1}}\cdots f_{j_{n}},

H_{n}
preserves the momentum, that is,
\left\{H_{n},M\right\}=0
if

\displaystyle H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}=0,\text{ for }j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}\neq 0.

(3.23)

It is straightforward to see from Proposition
3.2
that each homogeneous term
\mathcal{H}_{n}
satisfies (
3.23
), thus the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
preserves the momentum.

3.3.2
Time-reversibility

From (
3.1
) and (
3.5
), one can easily check that the gSQG Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in (
3.5
) is time-reversible with respect to the involution
\rho
in (
2.130
).
Moreover, one can easily see from Proposition
3.2
that
\mathcal{H}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,4
are time-reversible as well.
Note that for a homogeneous Hamiltonian
H_{n}
of degree
n
such that
H_{n}(f)=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}f_{j_{1}}\cdots f_{j_{n}}
, the time-reversibility of
H_{n}
with respect to
\rho
implies that

H_{n,-j_{1},\ldots,-j_{n}}=H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}.

On the other hand, if
H_{n}\circ\rho=-H_{n}
, that is,
H_{n}
is reversibility preserving (see Definition
2.44
), then it holds that

\displaystyle H_{n,-j_{1},\ldots,-j_{n}}=-H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}.

(3.24)

In such case, Lemma
2.45
implies that the time-1 flow map of the Hamiltonian PDE associated to
H_{n}
is a reversibility preserving map. In the next section, our symplectic transformations to obtain the weak Birkhoff normal form will be obtained as flow maps generated by Hamiltonians of the form in (
3.24
). Thus, they are not only symplectic, but also reversibility preserving.

Remark 3.4
.

It is clear from (
3.1
) that
H
is a real-valued Hamiltonian. In general, a homogeneous Hamiltonian
H_{n}(f)\mapsto\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}f_{j_{1}},\ldots,f_{j_{n}}
is real-valued if and only if

\displaystyle\overline{H_{n,j_{1},\ldots j_{n}}}=H_{n,-j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}.

(3.25)

Since our phase space is a set of real-valued functions, it follows that its Hessian operator is a real linear operator (see Definition
2.5
).

3.3.3
\mathtt{M}
-fold symmetric patches

Another key property of the gSQG equations is that the solutions of (
1.1
) enjoy certain rotational symmetry. More precisely, let us denote by
R_{\theta}
the rotation matrix:

R_{\theta}:=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},\text{ for $\theta\in\mathbb{T}$.}

One can easily check that if
\omega(t,x)
is a solution to (
1.1
) with an initial datum
\omega_{0}(x)
, then
\omega_{\theta}(t,x):=\omega(t,R_{\theta}x)
solves (
1.1
) with the initial datum
\omega_{0}(t,R_{\theta}x)
. Especially, if the initial datum
\omega_{0}
is invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-rotation, that is,
\omega_{0}(x)=\omega_{0}(R_{2\pi/\mathtt{M}}x)
, then the solution
\omega(t,x)
is also invariant under a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-rotation. In the phase space
L^{2}_{0}
, such invariance can be rephrased as
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance. Indeed, we have the following:

Lemma 3.5
.

The Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in (
3.5
) is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.

Proof.

In view of (
3.5
), it suffices to show that
H
in (
3.1
) satisfies
H(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(R))=H(R)
, that is,

\displaystyle\int_{R_{\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}}D}\int_{R_{\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}}D}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}dxdy=\int_{D}{\int_{D}}\frac{1}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}dxdy,

(3.26)

which follows immediately from a change of variables.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

One can easily see from Proposition
3.2
that
\mathcal{H}_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,4
are
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant as well. Note that for a homogeneous Hamiltonian
H_{n}
of degree
n
such that
H_{n}(f)=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}f_{j_{1}}\cdots f_{j_{n}}
, the condition (
3.23
) implies
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance of
H_{n}
as well. Indeed,

H_{n}(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}f)=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}\widehat{\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widehat{\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(f)}_{j_{n}}=\sum_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}H_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}e^{\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}\text{i}(j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n})}\widehat{f}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widehat{f}_{j_{n}}

which is equal to
H_{n}(f)
if (
3.23
) holds.

4
Weak Birkhoff normal form

In this section, we aim to normalize the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, up to the quintic term.

4.1
Tangential sites and normal sites

Given
f\in L^{2}_{0}
, we recall the Fourier series expression (compared to (
2.3
), we replace
\widehat{f}_{j}
by
f_{j}
for simplicitiy).

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{j\neq 0}f_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\text{ where }f_{j}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}f(x)e^{-\text{i}jx}dx.

(4.1)

Plugging the series expansion in (
4.1
) into (
3.9
), one can rewrite
\mathcal{H}_{n}
, for
n=1,...4
, as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{2}(f)=\sum_{j\neq 0}\kappa_{j}|f_{j}|^{2},\quad\mathcal{H}_{3}(f)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},\quad\mathcal{H}_{4}(f)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0}H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}.

(4.2)

where

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}

\displaystyle:={\pi}\left(-\frac{1}{2}m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right),

(4.3)

\displaystyle H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\pi\alpha}{24}\left(m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{2})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{3})-T_{\alpha}\right),

\displaystyle H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{\alpha(\alpha+4)\pi}{384}(m_{1,\alpha}(j_{1})+m_{1,\alpha}(j_{2})+m_{1,\alpha}(j_{3})+m_{1,\alpha}(j_{4}))

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha^{2}\pi}{768}(m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1}+j_{2})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1}+j_{3})

\displaystyle+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1}+j_{4})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{2}+j_{3})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{2}+j_{4})+m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{3}+j_{4}))

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha\pi}{192}m^{\circ}_{4,\alpha}(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})+\frac{\alpha(\alpha+2)T_{\alpha}\pi}{192}.

and

\displaystyle m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j):=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(\theta))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(1-e^{-\text{i}j\theta})d\theta,\quad T_{\alpha}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(\theta))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}d\theta,

(4.4)

\displaystyle m^{\circ}_{4,\alpha}(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(\theta))^{-1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(1-e^{-\text{i}j_{1}\theta})(1-e^{-\text{i}j_{2}\theta})(1-e^{-\text{i}j_{3}\theta})(1-e^{-\text{i}j_{4}\theta})d\theta.

Note that
H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}
and
H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}
are expressed in such a way that they are invariant under any permutation of the sub-indices.

Now we pick the tangential sites of the Fourier modes, for which we normalize the Hamiltonian. For
\nu\in\mathbb{N}
, we pick natural numbers
0<\mathtt{j_{1}}<\cdots<\mathtt{j}_{\nu}
and set

\displaystyle S_{0}:=\left\{\pm\mathtt{j}:\mathtt{j}\in S_{0}^{+}\right\},\quad S_{0}^{+}:=\left\{0<\mathtt{j}_{1}<\cdots<\mathtt{j}_{\nu},\text{ for $i=1,\ldots,\nu$}\right\}.

(4.5)

The tangential sites
S
and the normal sites
S^{\perp}
are chosen to be

\displaystyle S^{+}:=\mathtt{M}S_{0}^{+}:=\left\{\mathtt{M}j:j\in S_{0}^{+}\right\},\quad S:=\left\{\pm j:|j|\in S^{+}\right\},\quad S^{\perp}:=\mathbb{Z}\backslash\left(S\cup\left\{0\right\}\right).

(4.6)

In order to perform derive the weak Birkhoff normal form, we impose the following conditions on the choice of
S
:

S.1

If
j_{1},j_{2}\in S
and
j_{1}\neq-j_{2}
, then
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}
.

S.2

If more than three of
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}
are in
S
and none of them is
0
, then
j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}+j_{5}\kappa_{j_{5}}\neq 0.

Let
S,S^{\perp}
be fixed as above. Before performing the weak Birkhoff normal form, we specify some useful notations:

4.1.0.1
Notations

We denote

\displaystyle H_{S}:=\left\{f\in L^{2}_{0}:f_{j}=0,\text{ for }j\in S^{\perp}\right\},\quad H_{S^{\perp}}:=(H_{S})^{\perp}\ (\text{orthogonal complement of $H_{S}$ in $L^{2}_{0}$}).

(4.7)

We denote by
\Pi_{S}
and
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
the projections onto the
subspaces
H_{S}
and
H_{S^{\perp}}
respectively. Given
f\in L^{2}_{0}
, we can write it as

\displaystyle f:=v+z=\sum_{j\in S}v_{j}e^{\text{i}jx}+\sum_{j\in S^{\perp}}z_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\text{ where }v:=\Pi_{S}f\in H_{S},\text{ and }z:=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}f\in H_{S^{\perp}}.

(4.8)

Let
E
be a finite dimensional subspace in
L^{2}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle E:=\text{span}\left\{e^{\text{i}jx}:|j|\leq C\right\},

(4.9)

for some constant
C>0
, and denote by
\Pi_{E}
the orthogonal projection to
E
.

In the weak Birkhoff normal form procedure, we do not need an explicit expression for some multilinear maps, but we only need how such multilinear maps depend on the tangential/normal component. For simplicity, we denote a
k
-linear map in
(v,z)
by

\displaystyle R(v^{k-q}z^{q})=M[\underbrace{v,\ldots,v}_{k-q\text{ times}},\underbrace{z,\ldots,z}_{q\text{ times}}],\quad M=k\text{-linear}.

(4.10)

For a homogeneous Hamiltonian
H_{n}
of degree
n
, we write it as

\displaystyle H_{n}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}H_{n,i},\text{ where each of $H_{n,i}$ is of the form $R(v^{n-i}z^{i})$.}

(4.11)

4.2
Composition with a time-1 flow

In order to find the weak Birkhoff normal form of the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, we construct a sequence of symplectic transformations to remove the trivial resonances arising from the tangential sites
S
. We first recall the following classical lemma to find an expansion of a composition of a Hamiltonian with a symplectic transformation:

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
H,F:L^{2}_{0}:\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
be Hamiltonians in
L^{2}_{0}
such that
F
is supported on a finite number of Fourier modes, more precisely,
F(\Pi_{E^{c}}u)=0
for a finite dimensional space of the form in (
4.9
). Also, let
\Phi_{t}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
be the associated flow map at time
t
, that is,
\frac{d}{dt}\Phi_{t}(u)=X_{F}(\Phi_{t}(u))
for
t\geq 0
. Then for each
t\geq 0
,
\Phi_{t}
is a symplectic transformation and admits the following Taylor expansion:

\displaystyle H\circ\Phi_{1}(u)

\displaystyle=H(u)+\left\{H,F\right\}+\left\{\left\{H,F\right\},F\right\}+\frac{1}{2!}\left\{\left\{\left\{H,F\right\},F\right\},F\right\}+\ldots

(4.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{i=0}^{n}A_{n}(u)+\frac{1}{n!}\int_{0}^{1}A_{n+1}(\Phi_{t}(u))(1-t)^{n}dt,

\displaystyle A_{0}:=H,\quad A_{n+1}:=\left\{A_{n},F\right\},\text{ for $n\geq 0$.}

where
\left\{\cdot,\cdot\right\}
denotes the Poisson Bracket given in (
3.22
).

Proof.

Since
F
is supported on a finite dimensional space
E
, the map
\Phi^{t}
is well-defined for all
t>0
which depends on
t
smoothly. That
\Phi_{t}
is symplectic transformation is a classical result (see
[
82
]
for example). To see the expansion in (
4.12
), let
f(t):=H(\Phi_{t}(u))
. From (
3.22
) and the fact that
\Phi_{t}
is the flow map of the Hamiltonian
F
, it follows that

\displaystyle f^{(n+1)}(t)=\left\{A^{n},F\right\}(\Phi_{t}(u)).

Therefore (
4.12
) follows from (
2.26
).
∎

We already observed in Subsection
3.3
that each
\mathcal{H}_{n}
in Proposition
3.2
preserves the momentum, that is, it satisfies (
3.23
). In the sequel, we will construct a symplectic transformation as a time-1 flow of a homogenous Hamiltonian of degree
3,4,5
satisfying (
3.23
). The next lemma shows that the composition with such transformation preserves the property (
3.23
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
H_{n},F_{m}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
be homogeneous Hamiltonians of degree
n
and
m
respectively, such that

\displaystyle H_{n,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}=0,\text{ if }j_{1}+\cdots j_{n}\neq 0,\quad F_{m,j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}}=0,\text{ if }j_{1}+\cdots j_{m}\neq 0.

Then,
G:=\left\{H_{n},F_{m}\right\}
is a homogeneous Hamiltonian of degree
n+m-1
such that

G_{n+m,j_{1},\ldots,j_{n+m-1}}=0,\text{ if }j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n+m-1}\neq 0.

Proof.

The lemma follows straightforwardly from the definition of the Poisson bracket (
3.22
).
∎

From Corollary
3.3
, we also know that
\mathcal{H}
is
\mu
-regular for some
\mu=\mu(\alpha)>0
. The following lemma shows that the weak Birkhoff normal form that we will obtain in the next subsection satisfies the same property:

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
H
be
\mu
-regular and
F:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\cup\left\{\infty\right\}
be a Hamiltonian supported on a finite number of Fourier modes and
\Phi:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\mapsto C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
be the time-1 flow map of its Hamiltonian PDE as in Lemma
4.1
. Then
H
is
\mu
-regular.

Proof.

Note that the Hamiltonian PDE of
F
,
f_{t}:=\partial_{x}\nabla F(f)
, is actually a finite dimensional ODE, where with a smooth vector field. This gives us a smooth flow map
\Phi_{t}
. Therefore the result follows straightforwardly from the Talyor expansion of
t\mapsto\Phi_{t}
at
t=0
.
∎

4.3
Weak Birkhoff normal form

The main result of this subsection is as follows:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
S,S^{\perp}
be fixed as in (
4.6
) satisfying
S.1
and
S.2
. Then, there exists a symplectic transformation
\Phi^{WB}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle H:=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}=H_{2}+H_{3}+H_{4}+H_{5}+H_{\geq 6},

(4.13)

where

1

(Quadratic and cubic terms) we have

\displaystyle H_{2}(f)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\frac{1}{4}v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{8}v^{2}dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}-\frac{1}{4}z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{8}z^{2}dx=:H_{2,0}+H_{2,2}

(4.14)

\displaystyle H_{3}(f)=\frac{\alpha}{16}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)dx+\frac{\alpha}{16}\int_{\mathbb{T}}z^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{3}z^{3}dx=:H_{3,2}+H_{3,3}.

2

(Quartic and quintic terms) We have
H_{4}(f)=H_{4,0}+H_{4,2}+H_{4,3}+H_{4,4}
where

\displaystyle H_{4,0}(f)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{j_{1}\in S^{+}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{1},-j_{1}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{4}+12\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+},\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{2}|v_{j_{2}}|^{2},

(4.15)

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\frac{(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}&\text{ if $j_{1}=j_{2}$,}\\
H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}+\frac{(j_{1}-j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},-j_{2},-(j_{1}-j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}-j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}-j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}\right)&\text{ if $j_{1}\neq j_{2}$,}\end{cases}

(4.16)

\displaystyle H_{4,2}(f)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{H}_{4,2}(f)+\mathfrak{H}_{1}(f)+\mathfrak{H}_{2}(f)

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{1}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)-T_{\alpha}vz\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx

(4.18)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{2}(f)

\displaystyle:=\frac{3\alpha}{16}\int\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx,\text{ where $K_{1},K_{2}$ are as in \eqref{K12def},}

\displaystyle H_{4,3}

\displaystyle=R(vz^{3}),\quad H_{4,4}=R(z^{4}),

\displaystyle H_{5}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=2}^{5}H_{5,i}=\sum_{i=2}^{5}R(v^{5-i}z^{i}),

and
H_{\geq 6}
collects all the terms homogeneous of degree at least
6
.

3

There exists a finite dimensional space
E
of the form in (
4.9
) such that
\Phi^{WB}(f)=f+\Psi(f)
where
\Psi
satisfies
\Psi(f)=\Pi_{E}\Psi(\Pi_{E}f)
.

4

\Phi^{WB}
is real, reversibility preserving and
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving.

5

H
is
\mu_{1}
-regular for some
\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}(\alpha)>0
.

The proof of the proposition will be given at the end of the section, using several lemmas.

Lemma 4.5
.

(Normalization of the cubic Hamiltonian) There exists a symplectic transformation
\Phi^{(3)}
such that

\displaystyle H^{(3)}(f):=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{(3)}(f)=H^{(3)}_{2}(f)+H^{(3)}_{3}(f)+H^{(3)}_{4}(f)+H^{(3)}_{\geq 5}(f),

where

(1)

(Quadratic and cubic terms) We have

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{2}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{2}(f),

(4.19)

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{3}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{3,\geq 2}=\frac{\alpha}{16}\int_{\mathbb{T}}v\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)dx+\frac{\alpha}{16}\int_{\mathbb{T}}z^{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{3}z^{3}dx.

(2)

(Quartic and higher degree terms) We have

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4}(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{4}\mathcal{H}^{(3)}_{4,i}(f),

(4.20)

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,0}=\mathcal{H}_{4,0}+\frac{3\alpha}{32}\int\left(\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})+2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v-T_{\alpha}v^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx,

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,2}=\mathcal{H}_{4,2}+\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)-T_{\alpha}vz\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx

\displaystyle+\frac{3\alpha}{16}\int\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx.

and
H^{(3)}_{\geq 5}(f)
collects all the terms homogeneous of degree at least
5
and
H^{(3)}_{5}
satisfies (
3.23
).

(3)

There exists a finite dimensional space
E
of the form in (
4.9
) such that
\Phi^{(3)}(f)=f+\Psi^{(3)}(f)
where
\Psi^{(3)}
satisfies
\Psi^{(3)}(f)=\Pi_{E}\Psi^{(3)}(\Pi_{E}f)
.

(4)

\Phi^{(3)}
is real and reversibility preserving.

Proof.

Proof of (1).
Let us consider a cubic Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle F^{(3)}(f):=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

(4.21)

which will be determined later, but we assume that
F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}
is invariant under any permutation of the sub-indices (see (
4.28
) for the choice of
F^{(3)}
). We denote its time-
1
flow map by
\Phi^{(3)}
. We compute using Lemma
4.1
,

\displaystyle{H}^{(3)}:=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{F^{(3)}}_{1}=\underbrace{\mathcal{H}_{2}}_{=:{H}^{(3)}_{2}}+\underbrace{\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(3)}\right\}+\mathcal{H}_{3}}_{=:{H}^{(3)}_{3}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{H}_{4}+\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3},F^{(3)}\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(3)}\right\},F^{(3)}\right\}}_{=:{H}^{(3)}_{4}}+{H}^{(3)}_{\geq 5}.

(4.22)

Using (
4.3
), we have
\left(\partial_{f_{-j}}\mathcal{H}_{2}\right)(f)=2\kappa_{j}f_{j}
,
\left(\partial_{f_{j}}F^{(3)}\right)(f)=3\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=-j}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}
. Thus, using (
3.22
), we obtain (relabeling
j
by
j_{3}
),

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{6\text{i}}{2\pi}\sum_{j_{3}\neq 0}\left(j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}f_{j_{3}}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}\right)=-\frac{6\text{i}}{2\pi}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\right)F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

where the last equality follows from the assumption that
F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}
is invariant under a permutation on the sub-indices
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}
.
Therefore, it follows from (
4.2
) and (
4.22
) that

\displaystyle{H}^{(3)}_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0}\left(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\right)F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}\end{subarray}}\left(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\right)F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}

(4.23)

\displaystyle\ +\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}^{c}\end{subarray}}\left(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\right)F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

(4.24)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{3}:=\left\{(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}:\ \text{at least two of $\left\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\right\}$ are in $S$}\right\}.

(4.25)

We claim that

\displaystyle j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\neq 0,\quad\text{if $j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0$.}

(4.26)

To see this, let us suppose to the contrary that there exist
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}
such that

j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\quad j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}=0,\quad j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\neq 0.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
j_{1}>0
,
j_{2}>0
, hence
|j_{3}|>|j_{1}|,|j_{2}|
. From
\kappa_{j}
in (
4.3
) and
j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0
, it holds that

j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}=0\implies j_{1}m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1})+j_{2}m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{2})+j_{3}m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{3})=0.

Therefore, we have
j_{1}\left(m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{1})-m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{3})\right)+j_{2}\left(m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{2})-m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j_{3})\right)=0
. Thanks to
(2)
of Proposition
2.34
, we have that

\displaystyle j\mapsto m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(j)\text{ is monotone increasing and even,}

(4.27)

which yields a contradiction. This proves the claim (
4.26
). Therefore, we can define
F^{(3)}
to be

\displaystyle F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}}{2\text{i}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}\right)}&\text{ if $(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}$},\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(4.28)

From (
4.22
), (
4.24
) and (
4.28
), we obtain (
4.19
). Indeed, the modified Hamiltonian does not change the terms involving more than
2
components in the normal site, that is

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{3}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{3,2}+\mathcal{H}_{3,3},

(4.29)

which gives (
4.19
).

Proof of (2).
From (
4.19
), and
\mathcal{H}^{(3)}_{3}
in (
4.22
), we have

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(3)}\right\}=-\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}:=-(\mathcal{H}_{3,0}+\mathcal{H}_{3,1}),

(4.30)

and

\displaystyle{H}^{(3)}_{4}=\mathcal{H}_{4}+\frac{1}{2}\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1},F^{(3)}\right\}+\left\{{H}^{(3)}_{3},F^{(3)}\right\}.

(4.31)

To compute the Poisson bracket, we write
F^{(3)}
as

\displaystyle F^{(3)}(f)=3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S\\
j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

(4.32)

which follows from (
4.28
) and that there is no
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\in S
such that
j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0
(see
S.1
). Therefore, we have that for
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
,

\displaystyle\left(\nabla F^{(3)}(f),h\right)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\pi\cdot 6\sum_{j_{1}\in S}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{2}+j_{3}=j_{1}\\
j_{2}\in S,\\
j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}F^{(3)}_{-j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}\right)\overline{h}_{j_{1}}+2\pi\cdot 3\sum_{j_{3}\in S^{\perp}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=j_{3}\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S\end{subarray}}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},-j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}\right)\overline{h}_{j_{3}}

\displaystyle=:6\int\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)hdx+3\int\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)hdx,

(4.33)

where

\displaystyle K_{1}(v,z)=\sum_{j_{2}+j_{3}=j}F^{(3)}_{-j,j_{2},j_{3}}v_{j_{2}}z_{j_{3}}e^{\text{i}jx},\quad\text{ and }\quad K_{2}(v,v)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}=j}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},-j}v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}e^{\text{i}jx}.

(4.34)

From (
4.34
), one can easily see that

\displaystyle\int v\Pi_{S}K_{1}(w,z)dx=\int z\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(w,v)dx,\quad\text{ for $v,w\in H_{S}$ and $z\in H_{S^{\perp}}$}.

(4.35)

Hence (
4.3
) tells us that

\displaystyle\nabla F^{(3)}(f)=6\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)+3\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v).

(4.36)

Furthermore, from
\mathcal{H}_{3}
and
\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}
in (
3.9
) and (
4.30
), we see that

\displaystyle\nabla\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{16}\Pi_{S}\left(2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})+2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+2\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)\right)-\frac{\alpha}{16}T_{\alpha}\Pi_{S}\left(v^{2}+2vz\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha}{16}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})+2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v\right)-\frac{\alpha}{16}T_{\alpha}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(v^{2}).

(4.37)

and from (
4.19
), we have

\displaystyle\nabla{H}^{(3)}_{3}(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{16}\Pi_{S}\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})\right)-\frac{\alpha}{16}T_{\alpha}\Pi_{S}z^{2}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{\alpha}{16}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+2\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)+2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})\right)-\frac{\alpha}{16}T_{\alpha}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(2vz+z^{2}\right).

(4.38)

Using (
4.36
), (
4.3
) and the definition of the Poisson bracket in (
3.22
), we have

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\alpha}{16}\int\left(\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})+2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v-T_{\alpha}v^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})-T_{\alpha}v^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx}_{=0\text{ thanks to \ref{tangent_2}}}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3\alpha}{4}\int\left(v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)-T_{\alpha}vz\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx.

(4.39)

Similarly, using (
4.3
) and (
4.36
), we have

\displaystyle\left\{{H}^{(3)}_{3},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)+z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v-T_{\alpha}vz\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3\alpha}{16}\int\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx

(4.40)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx

Collecting the terms involving only tangential sites or two normal sites, we find that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1},F^{(3)}\right\}+\left\{{H}^{(3)}_{3},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{3\alpha}{32}\int\left(\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(v^{2})+2v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v-T_{\alpha}v^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3\alpha}{8}\int\left(v\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}v+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(vz)-T_{\alpha}vz\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)dx

\displaystyle\ +\frac{3\alpha}{16}\int\left(2z\Lambda^{\alpha-1}z+\Lambda^{\alpha-1}(z^{2})-T_{\alpha}z^{2}\right)\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)dx

\displaystyle\ +R(v^{3}z)+R(vz^{3})+R(z^{4}),

for some multilinear map
R
. With (
4.31
), this proves (
4.20
). Also, Lemma
4.2
implies that
H^{(3)}_{5}
satisfies (
3.23
).

Proof of (3).

\mathcal{A}_{3}
is a finite set since it is contained in
[-2K,2K]^{3}
, where
K=\max_{i=1,\ldots\nu,\hskip 8.19447ptj_{i}\in S}|j_{i}|

Proof of (4).
Since
\mathcal{H}
is real-valued Hamiltonian,
H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}
satisfies (
3.25
). Using that
j\mapsto\kappa_{j}
is even, we see that
F^{(3)}
in (
4.28
) also satisfies (
3.25
), thus
F^{(3)}
is a real-valued Hamiltonian as well. Since
\Phi^{(3)}
is the time-
1
flow map of Hamiltonian PDE, generated by
F^{(3)}
,
\Phi^{(3)}
maps a real-valued function to a real-valued function. For the reversibility preserving property, it follows immediately from the definition of
F^{(3)}
in (
4.28
), that
F^{(3)}\circ\rho=-F^{(3)}
with the involution
\rho
in (
2.130
) (see Subsection
3.3
and the definition of
\kappa_{j}
in (
4.2
)). Then the result follows from Lemma
2.45
.
∎

We specify the Hamiltonian
H^{(3)}
in terms of the Fourier modes:

Lemma 4.6
.

We have

\displaystyle H_{3}^{(3)}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}^{c}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

(4.41)

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,0}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S\end{subarray}}H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S,\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}(j_{1}+j_{2})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{3}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{3}+j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

(4.42)

\displaystyle H_{4,2}^{(3)}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
\text{exactly two of $j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}$ are in $S^{\perp}$}\end{subarray}}H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -9\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{2}+j_{3}\in S\end{subarray}}(j_{2}+j_{3})\frac{H_{-(j_{2}+j_{3}),j_{2},j_{3}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{4},-(j_{1}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{1}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -\frac{9}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}(j_{3}+j_{4})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}-(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

(4.43)

Proof.

The expression (
4.41
) follows immediately from (
4.23
), (
4.24
) and (
4.28
).

Now, let us derive (
4.42
) and (
4.43
). In view of (
4.31
), we compute
\nabla\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}
and
\nabla{H}^{(3)}_{3}
in terms of the Fourier modes. For

, it follows from (
4.30
) and (

) that (recalling

in the condition of the tangential sites)

\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}=3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{f_{-j}}\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1}(f)=\begin{cases}6\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{2}+j_{3}=j,\\
j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,-j,j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}&\text{ if $j\in S$,}\\
3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=j,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-j}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}&\text{ if $j\in S^{\perp}$}.\end{cases}

(4.44)

Similarly, we have for
H^{(3)}_{3}
(see (
4.41
)),

H^{(3)}_{3}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})\in\mathcal{A}_{3}^{c}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}=3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
j_{1}\in S,j_{2},j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

therefore

\displaystyle\partial_{f_{-j}}H_{3}^{(3)}(f)=\begin{cases}3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{2}+j_{3}=j,\\
j_{2},j_{3}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,-j,j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}&\text{ if $j\in S$,}\\
6\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=j,\\
j_{1}\in S,\ j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-j}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}+3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=j,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-j}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}&\text{ if $j\in S^{\perp}$}.\end{cases}

(4.45)

Also, from (
4.21
) and (
4.28
), it follows that

F^{(3)}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\

thus (again using
S.1
in the tangential site condition),

\displaystyle\partial_{f_{j}}F^{(3)}(f)=\begin{cases}6\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=-j,\\

3\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}=-j,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S\end{subarray}}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}&\text{ if $j\in S^{\perp}$}.\end{cases}

(4.46)

Therefore, using (
3.22
), (
4.44
) and (
4.46
), we can find

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S,\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\text{i}(j_{1}+j_{2})H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}F^{(3)}_{j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -\frac{36}{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{2}+j_{3}\in S\end{subarray}}\text{i}(j_{2}+j_{3})H_{-(j_{2}+j_{3}),j_{2},j_{3}}F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{4},-(j_{1}+j_{4})}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}},

and using (
4.45
) and (
4.46
),

\displaystyle\left\{H_{3}^{(3)},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\text{i}(j_{3}+j_{4})F^{(3)}_{j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}+R(vz^{3})+R(v^{3}z).

Therefore, it follows from the above expressions and (
4.28
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\{\mathcal{H}_{3,\leq 1},F^{(3)}\right\}+\left\{H^{(3)}_{3},F^{(3)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S,\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}(j_{1}+j_{2})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{3}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{3}+j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -9\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{2}+j_{3}\in S\end{subarray}}(j_{2}+j_{3})\frac{H_{-(j_{2}+j_{3}),j_{2},j_{3}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{4},-(j_{1}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{1}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ -\frac{9}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}(j_{3}+j_{4})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}-(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle+R(vz^{3})+R(v^{3}z).

Plugging this into (
4.31
), we obtain (

) and (

).
∎

Now we turn to the quartic Hamiltonian. The normalization of the quartic Hamiltonian relies on the non-existence of nontrivial resonances, which we will prove in Lemma
4.8
.

Lemma 4.7
.

(Normalization of the quartic Hamiltonian) There exists a symplectic transformation
\Phi^{(4)}
such that

\displaystyle H^{(4)}(f):=H^{(3)}\circ\Phi^{(4)}(f)=H^{(4)}_{2}(f)+H^{(4)}_{3}(f)+H^{(4)}_{4}(f)+H^{(4)}_{\geq 5}(f),

where

(1)

(Quadratic and cubic terms) We have

\displaystyle H^{(4)}_{2}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{2}(f),\quad H^{(4)}_{3}(f)={H}^{(3)}_{3}(f)

(4.47)

(2)

(Quartic and higher degree terms) Using the Fourier series expansion in (
4.1
), let us rewrite
H^{(3)}_{4,0}
in (
4.20
) as

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,0}=:\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S\\
j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}v_{j_{4}},

(4.48)

where
H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}
is invariant under any permutation on
\left\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\right\}
. Then we have

\displaystyle H^{(4)}_{4}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{4}H^{(4)}_{4,i}(f),\text{ where }

(4.49)

\displaystyle H^{(4)}_{4,0}

\displaystyle=6\sum_{j_{1}\in S^{+}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{1},-j_{1}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{4}+12\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+},\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{2}|v_{j_{2}}|^{2},

\displaystyle\quad H^{(4)}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=0,\quad H^{(4)}_{4,i}=H^{(3)}_{4,i},\text{ for }i=2,3,4,

and
H^{(4)}_{\geq 5}(f)
collects all the terms homogeneous of degree at least

and

).

(3)

of the form in (

) such that

.

(4)

\Phi^{(4)}
is real and reversibility preserving.

Proof.

We will prove

first and then prove

.

Proof of
(2)
.
Let us consider a Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle F^{(4)}(f):=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0}F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}},

(4.50)

which will be determined later and let us denote its time-1 flow map by
\Phi^{(4)}
. We compute using Lemma
4.1
,

\displaystyle{H}^{(4)}:={H}^{(3)}\circ\Phi^{(4)}=H^{(3)}_{2}+{H}^{(3)}_{3}+{H}^{(4)}_{4}+{H}^{(4)}_{\geq 5},\quad\text{ where }\quad{H}^{(4)}_{4}={H}^{(3)}_{4}+\left\{{H}_{2}^{(3)},F^{(4)}\right\}.

(4.51)

From (
4.19
), we have
H^{(3)}_{2}=\mathcal{H}_{2}
. Also, it follows from (
3.22
), (
4.50
) and (
4.2
) that

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(4)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0}\frac{8\text{i}}{2\pi}j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\text{i}}{2\pi}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}\right)F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle{H}^{(4)}_{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0}\left(H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}-\frac{2\text{i}}{2\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}\right)F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\in\mathcal{A}_{4}\end{subarray}}\left(H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}-\frac{2\text{i}}{2\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}\right)F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\ +\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0\\

(4.52)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{4}:=\left\{(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}:j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}\neq 0\text{ and at least three of $\left\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\right\}$ are in $S$ }\right\}.

Hence we can define
F^{(4)}
to be

\displaystyle F^{(4)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}}{2\text{i}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}\right)}&\text{ if $(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\in\mathcal{A}_{4}$},\\
0&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(4.53)

so that (
4.52

\displaystyle{H}^{(4)}_{4}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\notin\mathcal{A}_{4}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}.

(4.54)

Recalling the notation in (

{H}^{(4)}_{4,i}={H}^{(3)}_{4,i}\quad\text{ for }i=2,3,4,

thanks to (

, which show that if at least two of

are in

\displaystyle{H}^{(4)}_{4,1}=0.

(4.55)

\displaystyle{H}^{(4)}_{4,0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\

\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}v_{j_{4}}

(4.56)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}=0,\\

\displaystyle=6\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}=0,\\

j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}v_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle=6\sum_{j_{1}\in S^{+}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{1},-j_{1}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{4}+12\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+},\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2},\\
j_{1}=-j_{3},\ j_{2}=-j_{4}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}v_{j_{1}}v_{j_{2}}v_{j_{3}}v_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle=6\sum_{j_{1}\in S^{+}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{1},-j_{1}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{4}+12\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+},\ j_{1}\neq j_{2}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}|v_{j_{1}}|^{2}|v_{j_{2}}|^{2}.

This proves (

). Also, Lemma
4.2
implies that

).
