Lemma 4.8
.

Let
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
,
j_{i}\neq 0
be solutions of

\displaystyle j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\quad j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}=0

Then the only solutions are given by
j_{a}=-j_{b},j_{c}=-j_{d}
where
(a,b,c,d)
is a permutation of
(1,2,3,4)
.

Proof.

It is enough to consider the case when two of the
j
’s are positive and two are negative since if sign
(j_{1})
= sign
(j_{2})
= sign
(j_{3})
we can rewrite the equation as

\displaystyle 0=j_{1}(\kappa_{j_{1}}-\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}})+j_{2}(\kappa_{j_{2}}-\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}})+j_{3}(\kappa_{j_{3}}-\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}})

and we get a contradiction by the monotonicity of
\kappa_{j}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
0<j_{1},j_{4}
and
0>j_{2},j_{3}
. We claim that the following equation (in
j_{1}
) has exactly two solutions (
j_{1}=-j_{2}
,
j_{1}=-j_{3}
):

\displaystyle F(j_{1}):=j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}-(j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}}=0

(4.57)

To do so, we will prove that for
\alpha\geq 1
,
F
is increasing for
j_{1}\geq-j_{2}-j_{3}
and that
F
is convex for
0\leq j_{1}\leq-j_{2}-j_{3}
and for
\alpha<1
,
F
is decreasing for
j_{1}\geq-j_{2}-j_{3}
and
F
is concave for
0\leq j_{1}\leq-j_{2}-j_{3}
. Together with
\lim_{\pm\infty}F(j)=+\infty
for
\alpha\geq 1
and
\lim_{\pm\infty}F(j)=-\infty
for
\alpha<1
this ensures that there are at most 2 solutions, which are the ones given above.

Under the hypotheses
j_{1}>0,j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\geq 0
and using the definition of
\kappa_{j}
in (
4.2
) and (
2.69
), we obtain, for a positive constant
C
:

\displaystyle F(j_{1}+1)-F(j_{1})=C\alpha\left(\left(j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}-\left(j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\right)}\right),

and the monotonicity result follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\left(j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}-\left(j_{1}+1+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+1\right)}=-(\alpha-1)(1+j_{1})\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(3-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}.

To prove the convexity part of the statement, we calculate, for
j_{1}\geq 1,j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\leq-1
:

\displaystyle(F(j_{1}+1)

\displaystyle-F(j_{1}))-(F(j_{1})-F(j_{1}-1))

\displaystyle=C\alpha(\alpha-1)\left(j_{1}\frac{\Gamma\left(-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}-(j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3})\frac{\Gamma\left(-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\right)}\right),

and the convexity result follows from

\displaystyle j_{1}\frac{\Gamma\left(-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}-(j_{1}+1)\frac{\Gamma\left(-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(2-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}+1\right)}=(2-\alpha)\left(j_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{\Gamma\left(-1+\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}{\Gamma\left(3-\frac{\alpha}{2}+j_{1}\right)}.

∎

Before normalizing the quintic Hamiltonian, we need to have a symmetrized expression of
H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}
and compute
H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}
for
j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+}
in view of
H^{(4)}_{4,0}
in (
4.49
).

Lemma 4.9
.

For
j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+}
,
H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}
in (
4.48
) and (
4.49
) can be written as

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}=\begin{cases}H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\frac{(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}&\text{ if $j_{1}=j_{2}$,}\\
H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}-\frac{3}{4}\left(\frac{(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}+\frac{(j_{1}-j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},-j_{2},-(j_{1}-j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}-j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}-j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}\right)&\text{ if $j_{1}\neq j_{2}$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

From (
4.42
) we see that

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}_{1}-\frac{9}{4}\mathbb{H}_{2},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S\end{subarray}}H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S,\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{1_{\left(j_{1}+j_{2}\neq 0\right\}}(j_{1}+j_{2})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{3}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{3}+j_{4}}}}_{=:\mathbb{H}_{2,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}

(4.58)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}\in S\end{subarray}}\mathbb{H}_{2,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}.

Note that in the very last equality, we removed the restriction on the modes
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}
, which is allowed thanks to the condition on the tangential sites in
S.1
.
Hence, we can write
H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}
for
j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+}
in (
4.48
), in case where
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})=(j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2})
, as (noting that
H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}
in (
4.3
) is already invariant under permutations on the sub-indices)

\displaystyle H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}

\displaystyle=H_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}-\frac{9}{4}\times\frac{1}{24}\times 4\underbrace{\left(\mathbb{H}_{2,j_{1},j_{2},-j_{1},-j_{2}}+\mathbb{H}_{2,j_{1},-j_{2},-j_{1},j_{2}}+\mathbb{H}_{2,-j_{1},j_{2},j_{1},-j_{2}}+\mathbb{H}_{2,-j_{1},-j_{2},j_{1},j_{2}}\right)}_{=:K_{j_{1},j_{2}}}.

Using the definition of
\mathbb{H}_{2,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}
in (
4.3
), we have that for
j_{1},j_{2}\in S^{+}
,

K_{j_{1},j_{2}}=\begin{cases}\frac{2(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}&\text{ if $j_{1}=j_{2}$,}\\
\frac{2(j_{1}+j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}-j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}+\frac{2(j_{1}-j_{2})(H_{3,j_{1},-j_{2},-(j_{1}-j_{2})})^{2}}{(j_{1}-j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}-j_{2}}-j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}}&\text{ if $j_{1}\neq j_{2}$}.\end{cases}

Plugging this into the above expression for
H^{(3)}_{4,j_{1},-j_{1},j_{2},-j_{2}}
, the desired result follows.
∎

Lastly, we deal with the quintic Hamiltonian.

Lemma 4.10
.

(Normalization of the quintic Hamiltonian) There exists a symplectic transformation
\Phi^{(4)}
such that

\displaystyle H^{(5)}(f):=H^{(4)}\circ\Phi^{(5)}(f)=H^{(5)}_{2}(f)+H^{(5)}_{3}(f)+H^{(5)}_{4}(f)+H^{(5)}_{5}(f)+H^{(5)}_{\geq 6}(f)

where

(1)

(Quadratic, cubic and quartic terms) We have

\displaystyle H^{(5)}_{2}(f)=\mathcal{H}_{2}(f),\quad H^{(5)}_{3}(f)={H}^{(3)}_{3}(f),\quad H^{(5)}_{4}(f)=H^{(4)}_{4}(f),\quad H^{(5)}_{5}=\sum_{i=2}^{5}R(v^{5-i}z^{i}),

(4.59)

and
H^{(5)}_{\geq 6}
collects all the terms homogeneous of degree at least
6
.

(2)

There exists a finite dimensional space
E
of the form in (
4.9
) such that
\Phi^{(5)}(f)=f+\Psi^{(4)}(f)
where
\Psi^{(5)}
satisfies
\Psi^{(5)}(f)=\Pi_{E}\Psi^{(5)}(\Pi_{E}f)
.

(3)

\Phi^{(5)}
is real and reversibility preserving.

Proof.

Proof of (1).

Let us write
\mathcal{H}^{(4)}_{5}
obtained in Lemma
4.7
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{(4)}_{5}(f)=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0}H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}},

(4.60)

where
H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}
is assumed to be invariant under any permutations on
\left\{j_{1},j_{2}.j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}\right\}
without loss of generality. Indeed, we already prove in
(2)
of Lemma
4.7
that
H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}=0
, if
j_{1}+\cdots,j_{5}\neq 0
, hence we have the expression in (
4.60
). As before, we consider a Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle F^{(5)}(f):=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0}F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}},

(4.61)

which will be determined later and let us denote its time-1 flow map by
\Phi^{(5)}
. We compute using Lemma
4.1
,

\displaystyle{H}^{(5)}:={H}^{(4)}\circ\Phi^{F^{(5)}}_{1}={H}^{(4)}_{2}+{H}^{(4)}_{3}+{H}^{(4)}_{4}+\mathcal{H}^{(5)}_{5}+\mathcal{H}^{(5)}_{\geq 6},\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathcal{H}^{(4)}_{5}+\left\{{H}^{(4)}_{2},F^{(5)}\right\}.

(4.62)

Again, it follows (
4.47
) that
H^{(4)}_{2}=\mathcal{H}_{2}
, thus using (
3.22
), we have

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{H}_{2},F^{(5)}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\text{i}}{2\pi}\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}+j_{5}\kappa_{j_{5}}\right)F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}}.

With
(2)
in Lemma
4.7
, which implies that
H^{(4)}_{5}
satisfies (
3.23
), we have

\displaystyle{H}^{(5)}_{5}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0}\left(H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}+j_{5}\kappa_{j_{5}}\right)F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5})\in\mathcal{A}_{5}\end{subarray}}\left(H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}+j_{5}\kappa_{j_{5}}\right)F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}}

\displaystyle\ +\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5})\notin\mathcal{A}_{5}\end{subarray}}\left(H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}-\frac{\text{i}}{\pi}\left(j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}+j_{3}\kappa_{j_{3}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}+j_{5}\kappa_{j_{5}}\right)F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}\right)f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}},

(4.63)

where
\mathcal{A}_{5}:=\left\{(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5})\in\mathbb{Z}^{5}:\sum_{i=1}^{5}j_{i}\kappa_{j_{i}}\neq 0\text{ and at least four of $\left\{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}\right\}$ are in $S$ }\right\}
.
Hence we can define
F^{(5)}
to be

\displaystyle F^{(5)}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}}{\text{i}\sum_{i=1}^{5}j_{i}\kappa_{j_{i}}}&\text{ if $(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5})\in\mathcal{A}_{5}$ },\\
0&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

(4.64)

so that (
4.63
) yields that

{H}^{(5)}_{5}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}+j_{5}=0\\
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5})\notin\mathcal{A}_{5}\end{subarray}}H^{(4)}_{5,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4},j_{5}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}f_{j_{5}}.

Indeed,
S.2
tells us that (
4.64
) is well-defined.

Now, let us write
{H}^{(5)}_{5}=\sum_{i=0}^{5}{H}^{(5)}_{5,i}
, where
{H}^{(5)}_{5,i}
is of the form
R(v^{i}z^{5-i})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{5}
, it holds that

\displaystyle{H}^{(5)}_{5,0}={H}^{(5)}_{5,1}=0.

(4.65)

This gives (
4.59
).

Proof of (2)

\mathcal{A}_{5}
is a finite set since it is contained in
[-4K,4K]^{5}
, where
K=\max_{i=1,\ldots\nu,\hskip 8.19447ptj_{i}\in S}|j_{i}|

Proof of (3).
The proof is identical to (4) in Lemma
4.5
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
4.4
.

Proof of Proposition
4.4
:

Let

\displaystyle\Phi^{WB}:=\Phi^{(3)}\circ\Phi^{(4)}\circ\Phi^{(5)},

(4.66)

where
\Phi^{(3)},\Phi^{(4)},\Phi^{(5)}
are the symplectic transformations obtained in Lemmas
4.5
,
4.7
and

. Then it follows from the lemmas that

H:=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}=H^{(5)}_{2}(f)+H^{(5)}_{3}(f)+H^{(5)}_{4}(f)+H^{(5)}_{5}(f)+H^{(5)}_{\geq 6}(f).

Then, (
4.14
) follows from (

) and (
4.19
). For the quartic and quintic terms, (
4.15

), (

3
follows from Lemma
4.5
,

and

as well.
\Phi^{WB}
is real and reversibility preserving because it is a composition of real, reversibility preserving transformations. Furthermore, from (

), (

) and (

), it follows that

is a composition of
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant preserving transformations (see Remark
3.6
and Lemma

), there for
\Phi^{WB}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant preserving as well.
5
follows immediately from Lemma

since

are supported on a finite number of Fourier modes.

\Box

We specify
H_{3,2},H_{4,2}
in the following lemma:

Lemma 4.11
.

H_{3,2}
in (
4.14
) and
H_{4,2}=\mathcal{H}_{4,2}+\mathfrak{H}_{1}+\mathfrak{H}_{2}(f)
in (
4.17
) has the following formula in terms of the Fourier modes:

\displaystyle H_{3,2}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=0,\\
\text{exactly two of ${}_{j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}$ are in $S^{\perp}$}\end{subarray}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}},

(4.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{4,2}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
\text{exactly two of $j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}$ are in $S^{\perp}$}\end{subarray}}H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}},

(4.68)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{1}(f)

\displaystyle=-9\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{2}+j_{3}\in S\end{subarray}}(j_{2}+j_{3})\frac{H_{3,-(j_{2}+j_{3}),j_{2},j_{3}}H_{3,j_{1},j_{4},-(j_{1}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{4}\kappa_{j_{4}}-(j_{1}+j_{4})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{4}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}},

(4.69)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{2}(f)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}+j_{4}=0,\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in S,\ j_{3},j_{4}\in S^{\perp},\\
j_{1}+j_{2}\in S^{\perp}\end{subarray}}(j_{3}+j_{4})\frac{H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},-(j_{1}+j_{2})}H_{3,j_{3},j_{4},-(j_{3}+j_{4})}}{j_{1}\kappa_{j_{1}}+j_{2}\kappa_{j_{2}}-(j_{1}+j_{2})\kappa_{j_{1}+j_{2}}}f_{j_{1}}f_{j_{2}}f_{j_{3}}f_{j_{4}}.

(4.70)

Proof.

For
H_{3,2}
, the expansion follows from (
4.14
). From Lemma
4.7
and Lemma
4.10
, it follows that
H_{4,2}=H^{(3)}_{4,2}
. Therefore, the result for
H_{4,2}
follows from (
4.43
) in Lemma
4.6
.
∎

Before we close this section, we prove the expansion of
\Phi^{WB}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For
f\in L^{2}_{0}
, we have the expansion,

\Phi^{WB}(f)=f+6\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v,z)+3\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v,v)+\Phi^{WB}_{\geq 3}(f),

where
K_{1},K_{2}
are as in (
4.34
) and
\Phi^{WB}_{\geq 3}:L^{2}_{0}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
collects all the homogeneous terms of degree at least
3
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
\Phi^{WB}
(
4.66
) and that each
\Phi^{(i)},\ i=3,4,5
are time-1 flow map of Hamiltonians
F^{(i)}
. Since
F^{(4)},F^{(5)}
are quartic and quintic Hamiltonian respectively, the linear and the quadratic terms of
\Phi^{WB}
coincide with those of
\Phi^{(3)}
. Denoting
\Phi^{(3)}_{t}
be the Hamiltonian flow of
F^{(3)}
in the proof of Lemma
4.5
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\Phi^{(3)}_{t}=\partial_{x}\nabla F^{(3)}(\Phi_{t}^{(3)}(f)),

hence, the Taylor expansion as in (
2.26
) of
t\mapsto\Phi_{t}^{(3)}
gives us that

\Phi^{(3)}(f)=\Phi_{1}^{(3)}(f)=f+\partial_{x}\nabla F^{(3)}(f)+\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{x}\nabla^{2}F^{(3)}(\Phi_{t}^{(3)}(f))[\partial_{x}\nabla F^{(3)}(\Phi_{t}^{(3)}(f))]dt.

Since
F^{(3)}
is cubic, the last term in integral is homogeneous of degree
\geq 3
.
Recalling (
4.36
) for
\nabla F^{(3)}(f)
, the desired result follows.
∎

5
Action-angle variables

In this section, we introduce action-angle variables and rewrite the Hamiltonian
H
that was obtained in Proposition
4.4
in terms of those variables. First, we denote the amplitude vector by
\zeta
:

\displaystyle\zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\nu},

(5.1)

For each
\zeta
, we define a change of variables,
U_{\zeta}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto L^{2}_{0}
, as follows

\displaystyle U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon{\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k})}e^{\text{i}\theta_{k}}}e^{\text{i}j_{k}x}+\varepsilon^{b}z{=:\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)}+\varepsilon^{b}z,

(5.2)

\displaystyle\zeta_{-k}:=\zeta_{k},\quad y_{-k}=y_{k},\quad\theta_{-k}=-\theta_{k},\quad k=1,\ldots,\nu,\quad\varepsilon>0\quad b-1\in(0,\frac{1}{12}).

Note that
v_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\zeta
as well, but we omit it in the notations for simplicity. Clearly, we have
\overline{v}_{j}=v_{-j}
hence
v_{\varepsilon}
is real-valued and its Fourier modes are supported on the tangential sites. For sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
, we can easily see that
\zeta
determines the size of the amplitude of the function determined by
(\theta,y,z)
. Using the new variables
(\theta,y,z)
, we define a new
\zeta
-dependent Hamiltonian in
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
:

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2b}}H(U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)),\text{ where $H$ is as in Proposition~\ref{normal_form_prop11231}.}

(5.3)

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
H
be the Hamiltonian obtained in Proposition
4.4
and let
v_{\varepsilon},U_{\zeta}
and
H_{\zeta}
be as in (
5.2
) and (
5.3
). Then we have that for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)
,

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)=C_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)+2\pi{\omega(\zeta)}\cdot y+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+P(\theta,y,z),

(5.4)

where

\displaystyle\omega(\zeta):=\left(\overline{\omega}+\frac{6\varepsilon^{2}}{\pi}\mathbb{A}\zeta\right),\text{ where $\overline{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}$ and $\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu\times\nu}$ are in \eqref{linear_frequency_aa} and \eqref{amplitude_modulation1}},

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z):=H_{2,2}(z)+\varepsilon H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)+\varepsilon^{2}H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z),

(5.6)

\displaystyle P(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon\left(H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right)+\varepsilon^{2}\left(H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\ +6\varepsilon^{2b}y^{T}\mathbb{A}y+\varepsilon^{-2b}\left(H_{3,3}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}+H_{\geq 6}\right)\circ U_{\zeta}.

(5.7)

Proof.

We recall
H
in Proposition
4.4
and see that

\displaystyle H=\left(H_{2,0}+H_{4,0}\right)+\left(H_{2,2}+H_{3,2}+H_{4,2}\right)+\left(H_{3,3}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}+H_{\geq 6}\right).

(5.8)

Let us compute the terms in each parenthesis separately:

Computation for
H_{2,0}+H_{4,0}
.

Using
H_{2,0}
in (
4.14
), we compuate

\displaystyle H_{2,0}(U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z))

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{expansion_2},\eqref{normal_formsf}}{=}\mathcal{H}_{2}(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y))

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{fourier_hamiltonian}}{=}2\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}j_{k}\kappa_{j_{k}}\left(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}C_{1}(\zeta)+2\varepsilon^{2b}\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}j_{k}\kappa_{j_{k}}y_{k}

\displaystyle=:\varepsilon^{2}C_{1}(\zeta)+2\pi\varepsilon^{2b}\overline{\omega}\cdot y,

where
C_{1}(\zeta)
is a constant that does not depend on
(\theta,y,z)
and
\overline{\omega}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
is defined as

\displaystyle(\overline{\omega})_{k}:=\frac{1}{\pi}\kappa_{j_{k}}\overset{\eqref{moreexplicits2}}{=}j_{k}\left(-\frac{1}{2}m_{1,\alpha}(j_{k})+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right)\quad\text{ for $j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\in S^{+}$}.

(5.9)

Using
H_{4,0}
in (
4.15
), we have

\displaystyle H_{4,0}(U_{\zeta})(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle=6\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{k},-j_{k},j_{k},-j_{k}}\varepsilon^{4}j_{k}^{2}(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k})^{2}

\displaystyle\ +12\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}\in S^{+},\\
j_{k_{1}}\neq j_{k_{2}}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{k_{1}},-j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},-j_{k_{2}}}\varepsilon^{4}j_{k_{1}}j_{k_{2}}(\zeta_{k_{1}}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k_{1}})(\zeta_{k_{2}}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}y_{k_{2}})

\displaystyle=C_{2}(\zeta)+12\varepsilon^{2b+2}\left(\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}H_{4,j_{k},-j_{k},j_{k},-j_{k}}^{(3)}j_{k}^{2}\zeta_{k}y_{k}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}\in S^{+},\\
j_{k_{1}}\neq j_{k_{2}}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{j_{k_{1}},-j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},-j_{k_{2}}}j_{k_{1}}j_{k_{2}}\zeta_{k_{1}}y_{k_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\ +6\varepsilon^{4b}\left(\sum_{j_{k}\in S^{+}}H_{4,j_{k},-j_{k},j_{k},-j_{k}}^{(3)}j_{k}^{2}y_{k}^{2}+2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}\in S^{+},\\
j_{k_{1}}\neq j_{k_{2}}\end{subarray}}H^{(3)}_{j_{k_{1}},-j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},-j_{k_{2}}}j_{k_{1}}j_{k_{2}}y_{k_{1}}y_{k_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=C_{2}(\zeta)+12\varepsilon^{2b+2}\mathbb{A}\zeta\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{4b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y,

where
C_{2}(\zeta)
is a constant that does not depend on
(\theta,y,z)
and
\mathbb{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu\times\nu}
is given by

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{A}^{k_{1}}_{k_{2}}:=\begin{cases}j_{k_{1}}^{2}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{k_{1}},-j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},-j_{k_{2}}}&\text{ if $k_{1}=k_{2}$,}\\
2j_{k_{1}}j_{k_{2}}H^{(3)}_{4,j_{k_{1}},-j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},-j_{k_{2}}}&\text{ if $k_{1}\neq k_{2}$},\end{cases}\quad\text{ where }k_{1},k_{2}=1,\ldots,\nu,\text{ and $j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}\in S^{+}$.}

(5.10)

Therefore, we have that for some constant
C_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)
,

\displaystyle H_{2,0}(U_{\zeta})+H_{4,0}(U_{\zeta})=C_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)+2\pi\varepsilon^{2b}\left(\overline{\omega}+\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{A}\zeta\right)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{4b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y.

(5.11)

Computation for
H_{2,2}+H_{3,2}+H_{4,2}
.

For
H_{2,2}
, we have

\displaystyle H_{2,2}(U_{\zeta})=\varepsilon^{2b}H_{2,2}(z).

(5.12)

For
H_{3,2}+H_{4,2}
, recall from (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) that they are of the form
R(vz^{2})
and
R(v^{2}z^{2})
respectively, therefore we have (recalling
U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)=\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+\varepsilon^{b}z
as in (
5.2
)),

\displaystyle H_{3,2}(U_{\zeta})+H_{4,2}(U_{\zeta})

\displaystyle=H_{3,2}(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+\varepsilon^{b}z)+H_{4,2}(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+\varepsilon^{b}z)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{1+2b}H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)+\varepsilon^{2+2b}H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{1+2b}H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)+\varepsilon^{2+2b}H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)+\varepsilon^{1+2b}P_{1}(\theta,y,z),

where

\displaystyle P_{1}(\theta,y,z)=\left(H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right)+\varepsilon\left(H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right).

(5.13)

With (
5.12
), we denote by
\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)
the bilinear form in
z
, that is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z):=H_{2,2}(z)+\varepsilon H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)+\varepsilon^{2}H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z),

(5.14)

then, we obtain

\displaystyle H_{2,2}(U_{\zeta})+H_{3,2}(U_{\zeta})+H_{4,2}(U_{\zeta})=\varepsilon^{2b}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+\varepsilon^{1+2b}P_{1}(\theta,y,z).

(5.15)

Computation for
H_{3,3}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}+H_{\geq 6}
.
Since all of these terms are small enough, we simply denote

\displaystyle P_{2}(\theta,y,z):=\varepsilon^{-2b}\left(H_{3,3}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}+H_{\geq 6}\right),

(5.16)

so that

\displaystyle H_{3,3}(U_{\zeta})+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}(U_{\zeta})+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}(U_{\zeta})+H_{\geq 6}(U_{\zeta})=:\varepsilon^{2b}P_{2}(\theta,y,z).

(5.17)

Therefore, plugging (
5.11
), (
5.15
) and (
5.17
) into (
5.8
) and (
5.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)=\frac{C_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)}{\varepsilon^{-2b}}+2\pi\left(\overline{\omega}+\frac{6}{\pi}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{A}\zeta\right)\cdot y+6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}y\cdot y+\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)+\varepsilon P_{1}(\theta,y,z)+P_{2}(\theta,y,z).

(5.18)

This is equivalent to (
5.4
).
∎

Now, we consider the corresponding symplectic
2
-form
\mathcal{W}
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
, which is obtained as the pullback of
\sigma
in (
2.127
) by the transformations that we have performed. From Proposition
4.4
(
\Phi^{WB}
is symplectic), and (
5.2
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{W}}:=(\Phi^{WB}\circ U_{\zeta})^{*}\sigma=U_{\zeta}^{*}\sigma=\varepsilon^{2b}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{\nu}d\theta_{k}\wedge dy_{k}\right)\oplus\varepsilon^{2b}\sigma_{S^{\perp}}=:\varepsilon^{2b}{\mathcal{W}},

(5.19)

where
\sigma_{S^{\perp}}
is the restriction of
\sigma
to
H_{S^{\perp}}
, that is,

\sigma_{S^{\perp}}(z,z)=\int\left(\partial_{x}^{-1}z\right)zdx
. From (
5.3
), it follows that the gSQG dynamics are equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\theta}\\
\dot{y}\\
\dot{z}\end{pmatrix}=\widehat{X}_{H\circ U_{\zeta}}(\theta,y,z)=\varepsilon^{2b}\widehat{X}_{H_{\zeta}}(\theta,y,z),

(5.20)

where
\widehat{X}_{H\circ U_{\zeta}},\widehat{X}_{\zeta}
are the Hamiltonian vector fields induced by the symplectic form
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
. From (
5.3
), we have that for any vector field
h
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\nabla_{\theta,y,z}H_{\zeta}[h]=\varepsilon^{-2b}(\nabla_{\theta,y,z}(H\circ U_{\zeta}),h)=\varepsilon^{-2b}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(\widehat{X}_{H\circ U_{\zeta}},h)=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(\widehat{X}_{H_{\zeta}},h)=\varepsilon^{2b}\mathcal{W}(\widehat{X}_{H_{\zeta}},h),

where the last equality follows from (
5.19
).
This implies (using the definition of
\mathcal{W}
in (
5.19
)),

\varepsilon^{2b}\widehat{X}_{H_{\zeta}}(\theta,y,z)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore, (
5.20
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\theta}\\
\dot{y}\\
\dot{z}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{y}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\\
\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)\end{pmatrix}=:X_{H_{\zeta}}.

(5.21)

Note that
X_{H_{\zeta}}
is the Hamiltonian vector field of
H_{\zeta}
induced by
\mathcal{W}
. We summarize these considerations in the following proposition:

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
H_{\zeta}
be a
\zeta
-dependent Hamiltonians given in Proposition
5.1
. Then a quasi-periodic solution
t\mapsto(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))
to (
5.21
) determines a quasi-periodic solution for the gSQG, that is,
f(t):=\Phi^{WB}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))
solves (
3.8
).

Our new Hamiltonian
H_{\zeta}
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
satisfies the time-reversibility and the
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance:

Lemma 5.3
.

H_{\zeta}
is time-reversible with respect to
\rho_{*}
, defined in (
2.137
).

Proof.

It is easy to see from (
5.2
) that
\rho(f)=\rho_{*}(U_{\zeta})
, where
\rho
is defined in (
2.130
). Since
H
is reversible (see
4
in Proposition
4.4
) with respect to
\rho
, we have

H_{\zeta}\circ\rho_{*}=\varepsilon^{-2b}H(\rho_{*}(U_{\zeta}))=\varepsilon^{-2b}H(\rho(f))=\varepsilon^{-2b}H(f)=\varepsilon^{-2b}H(U_{\zeta})=H_{\zeta},

which implies that
H_{\zeta}
is a time-reversible Hamiltonian with respect to the involution
\rho_{*}
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

H_{\zeta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.

Proof.

Recalling the property of
S
in (
4.6
), we see that

\displaystyle U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}z)=\rho_{\mathtt{M}}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z).

(5.22)

Since
H
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, which follows from
4
of Proposition
4.4
, the definition of
H_{\zeta}
in (
5.3
) tells us that

\displaystyle H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}z)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2b}}H(U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}z))=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2b}}H(\rho_{\mathtt{M}}\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z))=H_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z).

(5.23)

This tells us that
H_{\zeta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant (see Definition
2.54
).
∎

5.1
Hypotheses on the tangential sites

In this subsection, we will specify precise conditions on the choice of our tangential sites
S
in (
4.6
). As seen in Section
4
, the conditions,
S.1
and
S.2
are imposed to derive the weak Birkhoff normal from in Proposition
4.4
. In order to estimate the measure of frequencies, which will be explained below, we require more conditions on the choice of
S
. Those conditions are mainly due to two different reasons, 1) Dependence of the Hamiltonian on a parameter to perform the KAM theory and 2) Measure estimate for the set of non-resonant frequencies. We explain more details separate in what follows:

5.1.0.1
Parameter dependent Hamiltonian

A crucial consequence of Proposition
5.2
(and Proposition
5.1
,
4.4
) is that if we choose the tangential sites
S
satisfying
S.1
and
S.2
, we obtain a
\zeta
-dependent Hamiltonian
H_{\zeta}
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
, which gives us equivalent dynamics of the gSQG equations. Compared to the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
in (
3.5
), this parameter-dependent Hamiltonians is necessary for the proof of the persistence of quasi-periodic solutions, because the KAM theory does not tell us exactly which frequency can produce a persistent quasi-periodic solutions, but rather tell us the measure of the set of frequencies which produce persistent quasi-periodic solutions. More precisely, in view of (
5.4
) and (
5.21
), we are led to find a solution
(\theta(t),y(t),z(t))
satisfying

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\theta}\\
\dot{y}\\
\dot{z}\end{pmatrix}=X_{H_{\zeta}}=\begin{pmatrix}\omega(\zeta)\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)\\
\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}(\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z))\end{pmatrix}+X_{P}(\theta,y,z).

(5.24)

Neglecting the perturbed term
X_{P}
, one can easily see that
(\theta(t),y(t),z(t)):=(\omega(\zeta)t,0,0)
solves (
5.24
), in other words, the linearized equation for (
5.24
) possesses a quasi-periodic solution with frequency
\omega(\zeta)
for each
\zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}
(see (
5.1
)). Therefore the aim of the rest of the paper is to find out the set of
\zeta
for which such quasi-periodic solutions can survive under the presence of the nonlinear perturbation
X_{P}
, which has been already made small enough in the weak Birkhoff normal form procedure (Proposition
4.4
).

Following the convention in the literature, we will use
\omega(\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
as a parameter instead of
\zeta
. That is, in view of (
5.5
) and (
5.1
), we define
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, the set of frequencies, by

\displaystyle\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}:\omega=\omega(\zeta)=\frac{6\varepsilon^{2}}{\pi}\mathbb{A}\zeta+\overline{\omega}\ \text{ for some }\ \zeta\in[1,2]^{\nu}\right\}.

(5.25)

We will investigate for which
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, the following
\omega
-dependent equation has a quasi-periodic solution:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\dot{\theta}\\
\dot{y}\\
\dot{z}\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\omega\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\partial_{\theta}\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z)\\
\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}(\mathcal{N}(\theta)(z,z))\end{pmatrix}-X_{P}(\theta,y,z)=0.

(5.26)

We note that
\mathcal{N}
and
P
in (
5.24
) depend on
\zeta
(this follows from their definitions in (
5.6
) and (
5.7
), while
v_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\zeta
as in (
5.2
)). Therefore,
\mathcal{N}
and
P
in (
5.26
) must depend on
\omega
(however, we omit their dependence in the notation for simplicity).

A necessary requirement for the use of
\omega
as a parameter as described above is a one-to-one correspondence between
\omega
and
\zeta
, that is, the invertibility of the matrix
\mathbb{A}
in (
5.25
). Recalling the definition of
\mathbb{A}
from (
5.10
), we see that the invertibility of
\mathbb{A}
completely depends on the choice of
S^{+}
in (
4.6
). Thus, we are led to make the following hypothesis on
S^{+}
:

(\mathtt{H}1)

\mathbb{A}
defined in (
5.10
) is invertible, thus, it satisfies

\displaystyle|\vec{w}|\leq_{\alpha,\nu,S}|\mathbb{A}\vec{w}|\leq_{\alpha,\nu,S}|\vec{w}|,\text{ for all $\vec{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}$}.

(5.27)

5.1.0.2
Non-resonant frequencies

Other requirements for the choice of
S
, that is the choice of
S^{+}
, arise in the measure estimate of the non-resonant frequency set in Subsection
11.1
. In order to state the conditions, we recall
S^{+}=\left\{0<j_{1}<\cdots<j_{\nu}\right\}
from (
4.6
) and define
\vec{D}(\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
for each
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash S
by

\displaystyle(\vec{D}(\xi))_{k}

\displaystyle:=j_{k}\left(\frac{12}{\pi}H_{4,j_{k},-j_{k},\xi,-\xi}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\ +\frac{18}{\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{(\xi-j_{k})H_{3,-j_{k},\xi,-(\xi-j_{k})}H_{3,j_{k},\xi-j_{k},-\xi}}{\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(j_{k})+\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(\xi-j_{k})-\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(\xi)}+\frac{(\xi+j_{k})H_{3,j_{k},\xi,-(\xi+j_{k})}H_{3,-j_{k},\xi+j_{k},-\xi}}{-\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(j_{k})+\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(\xi+j_{k})-\lambda^{\circ}_{\alpha}(\xi)}\right)\right),

(5.28)

if
\xi\neq 0
and

\displaystyle(\vec{D}(\xi))_{k}=0,\text{ if $\xi=0$.}

(5.29)

Note that the explicit expressions for
H_{4,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4}},H_{3,j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}}
and
\lambda_{\alpha}^{\circ}(j_{1})
for integers
j_{1},\ldots,j_{4}
can be find in (
4.3
) and (
2.78
), therefore
\vec{D}(\xi)
is also completely determined by the choice of
S^{+}
. The motivation of the definition of
\vec{D}(\xi)
is that
\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{\zeta}(\theta,0,0)
, the Hessian of
H_{\zeta}
in the normal direction at
(\theta,0,0)
, can be conjugated by a symplectic transformation to a diagonal operator with the Fourier multiplier
-\frac{1}{2}m^{\circ}_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\vec{D}(\xi)\cdot\zeta+o(\varepsilon^{2})
(see (
11.86
) and (
11.67
)). We observe that the Fourier multiplier acts on the normal sites
S^{\perp}
, while we can infer from our choice of
S
in (
4.6
) and Lemma
5.4
that we can even restrict the action of the multiplier to the
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant functions. For this reason, we denote

\displaystyle S^{\perp}_{\mathtt{M}}:=\left\{\mathtt{M}j\in\mathbb{Z}:j\in S^{\perp}\right\}.

(5.30)

With
S
in (
4.6
), we see that
S\cup S^{\perp}_{\mathtt{M}}
is the set of all
\mathtt{M}
multiples of non-zero integers. With
\vec{D}(\xi)
in (
5.1.0.2
) and (
5.29
), and the set
S^{\perp}_{\mathtt{M}}
in (
5.30
), we state the conditions on the choice of
S
as follows: Denoting

\displaystyle W(j):=\frac{1}{\pi}j\kappa_{k}\overset{\eqref{moreexplicits2},\eqref{lambdadeffsd}}{=}-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\alpha}(j)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}j,

(5.31)

(\mathtt{H}2)

There exists a constant
C_{\mathtt{H}2}(\alpha,\nu,S)>0
such that for
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in S_{\mathtt{M}}^{\perp}
satisfying
\max\left\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right\}\geq C_{\mathtt{H2}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|(|\xi_{1}|^{\alpha-1}+|\xi_{2}|^{\alpha-1})\leq_{\alpha,\nu,S}\left|\left(W(\xi_{1})-\frac{\pi}{6}\xi_{1}\vec{D}(\xi_{1})\cdot\mathbb{A}^{-1}\overline{\omega}\right)-\left(W(\xi_{2})-\frac{\pi}{6}\xi_{2}\vec{D}(\xi_{2})\cdot\mathbb{A}^{-1}\overline{\omega}\right)\right|,

(5.32)

where
\kappa_{\xi}
and
\overline{\omega}
are as in (
4.3
) and (
5.9
), respectively.

(\mathtt{H}3)

Define
\mathbb{C}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{1}},\mathbb{B}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu\times\nu}
by

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{B}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}\right)^{i}_{k}:=\frac{(\xi_{1}\vec{D}(\xi_{1})-\xi_{2}\vec{D}(\xi_{2}))_{i}}{W(\xi_{1})-W(\xi_{2})}(\overline{\omega})_{k},\quad\mathbb{C}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}:=\mathbb{A}^{T}-\frac{\pi}{6}\mathbb{B}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}}.

(5.33)

Then,

\displaystyle\text{det}(\mathbb{C}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}})\neq 0,\text{ for each $\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in S_{\mathtt{M}}^{\perp}\cup\left\{0\right\}$ such that $\xi_{1}\neq\xi_{2}$.}

(5.34)

The hypotheses

(

⁢
H
2

)

and

(

⁢
H
3

)

are sufficient conditions for us to prove that the set of
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
that produces persistent quasi-periodic solutions have positive measure. We will not state the precise motivation for the above hypotheses here (see Lemma
11.6
), however, we emphasize that

(

⁢
H
2

)

and

(

⁢
H
3

)

solely depend on the choice of
S
, that is, the choice of
S_{0}
and
\mathtt{M}
in (
4.6
).

Now the question is whether one can pick the tangential sites
S
so that
S
satisfies
S.1
-
S.2
as well as

(

⁢
H
1

)

-

(

⁢
H
3

)

. The following proposition ensures that there are infinitely many choices of
S
for which all the properties are satisfied. To avoid the complexity of the paper, we postpone the proof to Appendix
B
. See Proposition
B.16
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
2\leq\nu\in\mathbb{N}
. There exist infinitely many
S_{0}^{+}
and
\mathtt{M}
such that
S
constructed in (
4.6
) satisfies
S.1
,
S.2
and the hypotheses

(

⁢
H
1

)

-

(

⁢
H
3

)

.

6
The nonlinear functional setting

We look for
\omega
and an embedding
i:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\ni\varphi\mapsto(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)(\varphi):=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))=:\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i(\varphi)-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))=0.

(6.1)

Clearly, a solution
(\omega,i)
to the above equation determines a quasi-periodic solution to the Hamiltonian equation (
5.21
) as

t\mapsto i(\omega t).

As described in Subsection
5.1
, we will use
\omega
as a parameter, instead of
\zeta
, more precisely,
\zeta
in (
6.1
) will be thought of as a function of
\omega
in such a way that (see
\omega(\zeta)
in (
5.5
))

\displaystyle\zeta(\omega):=\frac{\pi}{6\varepsilon^{2}}\mathbb{A}^{-1}(\omega-\overline{\omega}).

(6.2)

On the frequencies in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(see (
5.25
)), we impose the Diophantine condition:

\displaystyle\Omega_{0}:=\left\{\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}:|\omega\cdot l|>\gamma|l|^{\tau},\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}\backslash\left\{0\right\}\right\},\quad\gamma:=\varepsilon^{2b}\text{ (recall $b>1$ from \eqref{actionssx})},\quad\tau:=\frac{2}{\alpha-1}+\nu+2.

(6.3)

Now we consider the functional spaces for the problem (
6.1
). Recall the function spaces in Subsection
2.1
with
s>s_{0}
, where
s_{0}
is fixed so that

\displaystyle s_{0}>\frac{\nu+2}{2}.

(6.4)

Given an embedding
i(\varphi):=(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))
, we think of
i
as an element in
H^{s}_{\varphi}\times H^{s}_{\varphi}\times H^{s}_{\varphi,x}
.
We note that all the embeddings that will be considered throughout the paper are allowed to depend on the parameter
\omega\in\Omega_{\varepsilon}
. For a function on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
,

F:\Omega_{\varepsilon}\ni\omega\mapsto(F_{1}(\omega),F_{2}(\omega),F_{3}(\omega))\in H^{s}_{\varphi}\times H^{s}_{\varphi}\times H^{s}_{\varphi,x},

we denote its norm by (for a set
\Omega\subset\Omega_{\varepsilon}
),

\displaystyle\rVert F(\omega)\rVert_{s}:=\rVert F_{1}(\omega)\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}}+\rVert F_{2}(\omega)\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}}+\rVert F_{3}(\omega)\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}},\quad\text{ (see \eqref{norms22} for $\rVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}},\ \rVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}$)},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\rVert F\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}:=\rVert F_{1}\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}+\rVert F_{2}\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}+\rVert F_{3}\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)},\quad\text{ (see \eqref{omega_dep_norm1} for $\rVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)},\ \rVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}_{\varphi,x}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}$)},

and we denote

\text{Lip}(\Omega,C^{\infty}):=\cap_{s\geq 0}\text{Lip}\left(\Omega,H_{\varphi}^{s}\times H^{s}_{\varphi}\times H^{s}_{\varphi,x}\right),\text{ see \eqref{omega_dep_norm1} for the definition of $\text{Lip}(\Omega,E)$}.

Remark 6.1
.

v_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\zeta
smoothly for small enough
y
and
\varepsilon
, with a Lipschitz constant
O(1)
(see (
5.2
)). This yields that
v_{\varepsilon}
depends on
\omega
smoothly with a Lipschitz constant
O(\varepsilon^{-2})
, because of (
6.2
).

Regarding the Diophantine condition in (
6.3
), for
\omega\in\Omega_{0}
and a function
u
with zero-average in
\varphi
, that is,
\int u(\varphi)d\varphi=0
, one can easily check (see
[
8
, eq. (5.21)]
)

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}u\rVert_{s}\leq_{s,\tau}\gamma^{-1}\rVert u\rVert_{s+\tau}\quad\text{ for $u\in C_{\varphi,x}^{\infty}$}

(6.6)

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}u\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{s,\tau}\gamma^{-1}\rVert u\rVert_{s+2\tau+1}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\quad\text{ for $u\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{0},C^{\infty})$}.

(6.7)

6.1
Regularity of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}

In this subsection, we study the regularity of the domain/image spaces of the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
. Throughout the paper, we will focus on the embeddings that are close to the trivial embedding. More precisely, we consider the embeddings
i
such that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu}\leq C\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1}=C\varepsilon^{6-4b},\text{ for some $\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}$, $\mu,C>0$},\text{ where }\mathfrak{I}(\varphi):=i(\varphi)-i_{triv}(\varphi).

(6.8)

Recalling the norms in (
6.5
) and the smallness assumption in (
6.8
), we give the estimates on the functional
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
:

Lemma 6.2
.

There exists
\mu_{2}=\mu_{2}(\alpha,\nu)>0
such that if an embedding
i\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})
satisfies (
6.8
) for
\mu=\mu_{2}
and
C>0
, then (denoting
\rVert\cdot\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}
by
\rVert\cdot\rVert^{\text{Lip}}
and
\leq_{s,C}
by
\leq
for simplicity),

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}P(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}},\quad\quad\rVert\partial_{\theta}P(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{6-2b}+\varepsilon^{11-6b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}

(6.9)

\displaystyle\rVert\nabla_{z}P(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{5-b}+\varepsilon^{6-3b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}},\quad\quad\rVert X_{P}(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{6-2b}+\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}

(6.10)

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{\theta}\partial_{y}P(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}},\quad\quad\rVert\partial_{y}\nabla_{z}P(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{3+b}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}

(6.11)

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{yy}P(i)-6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{2+2b}+\varepsilon^{3}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}},\quad\text{ where $\mathbb{A}$ is as in \eqref{amplitude_modulation1}}.

(6.12)

Furthermore, for
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}(\omega)\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon^{2b-1}(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}})

(6.13)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]-(0,0,(-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}\partial_{x}+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\partial_{x})\hat{z})\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}})

(6.14)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}^{2}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\right)^{2}),

(6.15)

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{triv})\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s}\leq\varepsilon^{6-2b},\quad\rVert d_{i}^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}\right)^{2}).

(6.16)

Especially, it holds that for
i,i_{1},i_{2}\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})
satisfying (
6.8
),

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{1})-\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{2})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{2})[i_{1}-i_{2}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\varepsilon\left(\rVert i_{1}-i_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s+\mu_{2}}\rVert i_{1}-i_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}+(\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{1}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s+\mu_{2}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s+\mu_{2}})\left(\rVert i_{1}-i_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s_{0}+\mu_{2}}\right)^{2}\right),

(6.17)

where
\mathfrak{I}_{2}:=i_{2}-i_{triv}
.

Proof.

We give a proof for
\partial_{y}P
in (
6.9
) only, since all the other estimates can be obtained in a similar manner.

Recall the definition of
P
from Proposition
5.7
:

\displaystyle P(\theta,y,z)

\displaystyle:=\underbrace{\varepsilon\left(H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right)}_{=:P_{1}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}\left(H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{4,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)\right)}_{=:P_{2}}

\displaystyle\ +6\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2b}y^{T}\mathbb{A}y}_{=:P_{3}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{-2b}\left(H_{3,3}+\sum_{i=3}^{4}H_{4,i}+\sum_{i=2}^{5}{H_{5,i}}+H_{\geq 6}\right)\circ U_{\zeta}(\theta,y,z)}_{=:P_{4}}.

(6.18)

We prove the estimate for
P_{1}
only, that is,

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}P_{1}(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}},\text{ for some $\mu_{2}\geq 0$,}

(6.19)

since the other terms can be treated in the same way. For
P_{1}
,
we recall from
5
in Proposition
4.4
that
H
is
\mu_{1}
-regular for some
\mu_{1}(\alpha)>0
. From its definition in Definition
2.43
, we can easily see that there exists a bilinear map
R:(H^{s+\mu_{1}}(\mathbb{T}))^{2}\mapsto H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
such that
\nabla_{v}H_{3,2}(f)=\Pi_{S}R(z,z)
(denoting
f=v+z
and
\nabla_{v}:=\Pi_{S}\nabla_{L^{2}_{0}}
) and

\displaystyle\rVert R(z,z)\rVert_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,C}\rVert z\rVert_{H^{s+\mu_{1}}(\mathbb{T})}\rVert z\rVert_{H^{s_{0}+\mu_{1}}(\mathbb{T})},

which implies (denoting
i(\varphi)=(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{0}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi))
),

\displaystyle\rVert R(z_{0}(\cdot),z_{0}(\cdot))\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq_{s}\rVert z_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s+\mu_{1}}\rVert z_{0}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{1}}^{\text{Lip}}\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon^{6-4b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s+\mu_{1}},

(6.20)

where the last inequality follows from (
6.8
).
Note that the elementary chain rule gives us that

\displaystyle\partial_{y}P_{1}(\theta,y,z)=\varepsilon(\nabla_{v}(H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y)+z)-H_{3,2}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,0)+z)),\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y))_{L_{x}^{2}}=\varepsilon(R(z,z),\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta,y))_{L^{2}_{x}}.

(6.21)

Therefore,
\partial_{y}P_{1}(i)(\varphi)=\varepsilon(R(z_{0}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi)),\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{0}(\varphi)))_{L^{2}_{x}}
, while it follows from (
5.2
) that

\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{0})=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\frac{\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}|j_{k}|}{2\sqrt{|j_{k}|(\zeta_{k}+\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}(y_{0})_{k})}}e^{\text{i}(\theta_{0})_{k}}e^{\text{i}j_{k}x}.

Since
\zeta_{k}\in[1,2]
(see (
5.25
)), the denominator is strictly positive for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore (see Remark
6.1
)

\rVert\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\cdot),y_{0}(\cdot))\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon^{2(b-1)}(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s}).

Combining this with (
6.20
) and applying Lemma
2.2
to (
6.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}P_{1}(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon\left(\rVert\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert R(z_{0},z_{0})\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s_{0}}+\rVert\partial_{y}v_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}}\rVert R(z_{0},z_{0})\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon^{2b-1}\left(\varepsilon^{6-4b}(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s})\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{1}}^{\text{Lip}}+\varepsilon^{6-4b}(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s_{0}})\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{1}}\right).

Assuming (
6.8
) for some
\mu=\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{2}
is large enough depending in
\mu_{1}
, the above inequality implies

\rVert\partial_{y}P_{1}(i)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}}\leq_{s,C}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}\rVert_{s+\mu_{2}}^{\text{Lip}}.

Since
b-1\in(0,1/12)
as fixed in (
5.2
), we have
\varepsilon^{5-2b}<\varepsilon^{2b}
, thus the estimate (
6.19
) follows.

We point out that
\varepsilon^{4}
in the estimate for
\rVert\partial_{y}P\rVert^{\text{Lip}}_{s}
in (
6.9
) is due to the term
P_{4}
in (
6.18
), especially
H_{\geq 6}
.
∎

6.2
Reversible and
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant solutions

6.2.0.1
Reversible solutions

From Lemma
5.3
, we already know that
H_{\zeta}
is reversible with respect to
\rho_{*}
in (
2.137
). Therefore we are led to look for a reversible embedding
i
such that

\rho_{*}(i(\varphi))=i(-\varphi),\text{ that is, }(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))=(-\theta(-\varphi),y(-\varphi),\rho(z(-\varphi))).

Recalling the function spaces in (
2.138
), we have the following lemma:

Lemma 6.3
.

\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(\cdot):X_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}
.

Proof.

It is clear that,
\partial_{\varphi}:X_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}
. For
X_{H_{\zeta}}
, with a simple computation, the reversibility of
H_{\zeta}
implies that for each
i=(\theta,y,z)
,

-\rho_{*}(X_{H_{\zeta}}\circ\rho_{*}(i))=X_{H_{\zeta}}(i).

Note that for
i\in X_{i}
, it holds that
\rho_{*}(i(-\varphi))=i(\varphi)
, therefore,

X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(-\varphi))=-\rho_{*}(X_{H_{\zeta}}(\rho_{*}i(-\varphi)))=-\rho_{*}(X_{H_{\zeta}}(i(\varphi))),

which implies
X_{H_{\zeta}}(i)\in Y_{i}
.
∎

6.2.0.2
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant solutions

From Lemma
5.4
, we already know that
H_{\zeta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant. Recalling the space
X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
from (
2.144
), we have the following lemma:

Lemma 6.4
.

\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(\cdot):X_{i,\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{i,\mathtt{M}}.

Proof.

Recalling
\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}
in (
2.144
), Lemma
5.4
tells us that
H_{\zeta}\circ\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}=H_{\zeta}
, which implies

X_{H_{\zeta}}(\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(i))=\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(X_{H_{\zeta}}(i)).

Since
i\in X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
implies
\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(i)=i
, the above equality gives us that

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(X_{H_{\zeta}}(i))=X_{H_{\zeta}(i)}.

(6.22)

Furthermore, it is trivial that
\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(i)\right)=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(i))=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(i)
. Combining this with (
6.22
), we see that

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i))=\rho_{\mathtt{M},*}(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(i)-X_{H_{\zeta}}(i))=\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i),

(6.23)

hence,
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)\in X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
.
∎

6.3
Statement of the main theorem

We fix

\displaystyle\mathtt{p}

\displaystyle:=\left\{(\alpha,\nu,s_{0}):\ \alpha\in(1,2),\ 2\leq\nu\in\mathbb{N},\ s_{0}\geq\frac{\nu+2}{2}\right\},\quad b\in(1,1+1/12).

(6.24)

We are ready to state our main theorem of this paper:

Theorem 6.5
.

Given
\mathtt{p}
in (
6.24
), let us fix the tangential sites
S
as in (
4.5
), that is, fix
S_{0}^{+}
and
\mathtt{M}
so that
S.1
,
S.2
and the hypotheses

(

⁢
H
1

)

-

(

⁢
H
3

)

hold. Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\mathtt{p},S^{+}_{0},\mathtt{M},b)>0
such that
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, there exist a Cantor-like set
\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}\subset\Omega_{\varepsilon}
(see (
5.25
)) such that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\mapsto 0}\frac{\left|\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\varepsilon}\right|}=1,

(6.25)

and for each
\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{\varepsilon}
, there exists a reversible,
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant embedding
i_{\infty}(\omega)\in H_{\varphi}^{s_{0}}\times H_{\varphi}^{s_{0}}\times H_{\varphi,x}^{s_{0}}
which solves (
6.1
). Furthermore,
i_{\infty}(\omega)
is linearly stable under
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant perturbations.

Remark 6.6
.

By the linear stability under
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant perturbations, we mean the following: If
I(0)=(\theta(0),y(0),z(0))
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, and
I(t)
solves the linearized Hamiltonian equation,

\dot{I}(t)=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{\infty}(\omega)(\omega t))[I(t)],

then the norm,
\sup_{t\geq 0}\left(|\theta(t)|+|y(t)|+\rVert z(t)\rVert_{H^{s_{0}}_{x}}\right)<C
, for some constant
C>0
.

7
Approximate inverse

In this section, we will study the linearized operator of
i\mapsto\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i)
in (
6.1
), in order to implement the Nash-Moser scheme to find a solution to (
6.1
). For each fixed
\omega
, the linearized operator of
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
at
(i_{0}(\omega)
is given by

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=\omega\cdot\partial_{\varphi}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}-d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}].

(7.1)

As mentioned in the previous section, we will focus on the reversible,
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant embeddings that are close to the trivial embedding. More precisely, we assume the following:
The embedding
i_{0}
depends on
\omega
for some
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}
and denoting

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{0}:=i_{0}-i_{triv},\quad Z:=\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0}),

(7.2)

we assume that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu}\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1},\quad\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq\mathtt{C}\varepsilon^{6-2b},\quad\text{ for some $\mu,\mathtt{C}>0$},

(7.3)

i_{0}(\omega)
is a reversible and
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.

(7.4)

We will look for an approximate inverse of the linear operator
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})
.
Towards the construction of an approximate inverse, we first construct an isotropic embedding. Given
i_{0}=i_{0}(\varphi)=(\theta(\varphi),y(\varphi),z(\varphi))
, we define

\displaystyle i_{\delta}(\varphi)=(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi),z_{0}(\varphi)),

(7.5)

where

\displaystyle y_{\delta}(\varphi):=y_{0}(\varphi)+(\partial_{\varphi}\theta_{0}(\varphi))^{-T}[\rho(\varphi)],\quad\rho_{j}(\varphi):=\Delta_{\varphi}^{-1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\nu}\partial_{\varphi_{j}}A_{k,j}(\varphi)\right),

(7.6)

\displaystyle A_{k,j}(\varphi):=\partial_{\varphi_{k}}a_{j}(\varphi)-\partial_{\varphi_{j}}a_{k}(\varphi),\quad a_{k}(\varphi):=-((\partial_{\varphi}\theta_{0}(\varphi))^{T}[y_{0}(\varphi)])_{k}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\varphi_{k}}z_{0}(\varphi),\partial_{x}^{-1}z_{0}(\varphi))_{L^{2}}.

(7.7)

Lemma 7.1
.

i_{\delta}
is a reversible embedding, that is,
\rho_{*}\circ i_{\delta}(\varphi)=i_{\delta}(\varphi)
, where
\rho_{*}
is the involution given in (
2.137
). Furthermore,
i_{\delta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.

Proof.

For the reversibility, it is sufficient to prove that
y_{\delta}(-\varphi)=y_{\delta}(\varphi)
. Using that
i_{0}
is reversible (
\rho_{*}\circ i_{0}=i_{0}
), it is straightforward that
\varphi\mapsto a_{k}(\varphi)
in (
7.7
) is even. Using (
7.6
), one can easily show that
y_{\delta}
is even as well. Since
i_{\delta}
does not change
z_{0}
, it is trivial that
i_{\delta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

[
8
, Lemma 6.2, Lemma 6.3]

i_{\delta}
is isotropic, that is,
i_{\delta}(\omega)^{*}\mathcal{W}=0
on
\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
, where
i_{\delta}(\omega)^{*}\mathcal{W}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{W}
by
i_{\delta}(\omega)
and
\mathcal{W}
is as in (
5.19
). Furthermore, there exists
\mu_{3}(\tau)>0
such that for all
s\geq s_{0}
,

\displaystyle\rVert y_{\delta}-y_{0}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

(7.8)

\displaystyle\rVert y_{\delta}-y_{0}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega^{1})}\right)

(7.9)

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

(7.10)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}i_{\delta}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{3}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},\text{ for $\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})$.}

(7.11)

In view of (
7.5
), the estimate (
7.8
) tells us that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\varepsilon})}_{s}\leq_{s}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\varepsilon})}_{s+\mu_{3}}

(7.12)

The goal of this section is summarized in the next proposition:

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\mathtt{S}\gg s_{0}
,
\mathtt{C}>0
be fixed. There exist
\mu_{\mathtt{p}}=\mu_{\mathtt{p}}(\mathtt{p})>0
and
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{M},S_{0},\mathtt{S},b,\mathtt{C})>0
such that if a reversible,
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant embedding
i_{0}
satisfies (
7.3
) for
\mu=\mu_{\mathtt{p}}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
for some
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega_{0}
, then the following holds:
There exist a set of frequencies
\Omega_{\infty}=\Omega_{\infty}(i_{0})\subset\Omega_{1}
and a linear map
T(i_{0})(\omega):Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
for
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}
such that
T(i_{0})(\omega)
is an approximate inverse of
d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega))
. More precisely, for all
g(\omega)\in Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
, it holds that
T(i_{0})(\omega)[g(\omega)]\in X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
and (denoting
\leq_{\mathtt{p},b,\mathtt{M},S_{0},\mathtt{S},\mathtt{C}}
by
\leq
)

\displaystyle\rVert

\displaystyle\left(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})\circ T(i_{0})-I\right)[g]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon^{2b-1}\gamma^{-2}\left(\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert g\rVert_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})\rVert g\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\right),

(7.13)

and

\displaystyle\rVert T(i_{0})[g]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g\rVert_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert g\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p}}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\right),

(7.14)

for all
s\in[s_{0},\mathtt{S}]
.

Remark 7.4
.

In Section
7
-Section
10
, the loss of derivatives will be denoted by
\mu_{0}
, which might vary from line to line but depend on only the fixed parameters
\mathtt{p}
in (
6.24
). The loss of derivatives
\mu_{\mathtt{p}}
in Proposition
7.3
will be chosen large enough so that all the estimates in what follows hold. Especially
\mu_{\mathtt{p}}
is assumed to be larger than
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\mu_{3}
that appear in
5
of Proposition
4.13
, Proposition
6.2
and Lemma
7.2
.

Also, in view of the dependence of
\varepsilon_{0}
, let us denote

\displaystyle\mathtt{pe}:=\left\{\mathtt{p},\mathtt{M},S_{0},b,\mathtt{C}\right\}.

(7.15)

Here,
\mathtt{p}
and
b
denote the parameters that we fixed in (
6.24
),
\mathtt{M}
and
S_{0}
correspond to the choice of the tangential sites
S
as in (
4.6
), and
\mathtt{C}
is a constant that appears in the size assumption of the embedding in (
7.3
). Note that
\varepsilon
needs to be small enough depending on
\mathtt{pe}
and the range of
s
, that is
[s_{0},\mathtt{S}]
.
\mathtt{S}
will be fixed in the Nash-Moser iteration in Section
11
(see (
11.6
)). We remark that
\mathtt{S}
implicitly depends on
\mathtt{p}
, however we will not explicitly trace its size, for simplicity of the proof. Instead, we will assume that
\varepsilon
is always small enough, depending on
s
as well as
\mathtt{pe}
.

Remark 7.5
.

It is an immediate consequence of (
7.12
) that
given
\mu_{0}\geq 0
, we can choose
\mu=\mu(\mu_{0},\mathtt{p})
in (
7.3
) so that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\varepsilon})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{6-2b}\gamma^{-1}.

(7.16)

We will frequently use the smallness of
i_{\delta}
in (
7.16
), instead of the smallness of
i_{0}
, assuming that
\mu_{0}
in (
7.16
) is large enough depending on
\mathtt{p}
.

The proof of Proposition
7.3
is identical to
[
8
, Theorem 6.10]
, since the estimates in Lemma
6.2
are same as
[
8
, Lemma 5.3]
. We will describe the general idea of the proof for the sake of completeness. The proof of Proposition
7.3
will be given at the end of this section by using Proposition
7.14
, which will be proved in Section
8
-
10
.

We define a change of variables
G_{\delta}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
as

\displaystyle G_{\delta}\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\eta\\
w\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\theta\\
y\\
z\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{0}(\psi)\\
y_{\delta}(\psi)+(\partial_{\psi}\theta_{0}(\psi))^{-T}\eta+((\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0})(\theta_{0}(\psi)))^{T}\partial_{x}w\\
z_{0}(\psi)+w\end{pmatrix},

(7.17)

where
\tilde{z}_{0}(\theta):=z_{0}(\theta_{0}^{-1}(\theta))
for
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
.
Clearly, the image of the trivial embedding
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi,0,0)
is mapped to the embedded torus
i_{\delta}
by the map
G_{\delta}
. We also list some properties of
G
in the following lemmas.

Lemma 7.6
.

G_{\delta}
is reversibility preserving, that is
G_{\delta}:X_{i}\mapsto X_{i}
and
G_{\delta}:Y_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}
. Furthermore, for the
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invarance, we have that,
G_{\delta}:X_{i,\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
.

Proof.

We first prove the reversibility preserving of
G_{\delta}
. It suffices to show that
\rho_{*}\circ G_{\delta}\circ\rho_{*}=G_{\delta}
, where
\rho_{*}
is given in (
2.137
). Since
i_{\delta}
is a reversible embedding (see Lemma
7.1
), we have that
\theta_{0}
is odd,
y_{\delta}
is even and
z_{0}
satisfies
\rho z_{0}(\varphi)=z_{0}(-\varphi)
. Especially it holds that
\rho\tilde{z}_{0}(\theta)=\tilde{z}_{0}(-\theta)
.
This implies that
\rho\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(\theta)=-\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(-\theta)
. Therefore, using
\rho^{T}=\rho
and
\partial_{x}(\rho w)=-\rho(\partial_{x}w)
, which easily follows from its definition in (
2.130
), we see that

(\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(-\theta))^{T}\partial_{x}(\rho w)=-(\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(-\theta))^{T}\rho(\partial_{x}w)=-(\rho\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(-\theta))^{T}\partial_{x}w=(\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}_{0}(\theta))^{T}\partial_{x}w,

for all
\theta\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu},\ w\in H_{S^{\perp}}
.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle G_{\delta}\circ\rho_{*}\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\eta\\
w\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{0}(-\psi)\\
y_{\delta}(-\psi)+(\partial_{\psi}\theta_{0}(-\psi))^{-T}\eta+(\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}(\theta_{0}(-\psi)))^{T}\partial_{x}(\rho w),\\
z_{0}(-\psi)+\rho w\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-\theta_{0}(\psi)\\
y_{\delta}(\psi)+(\partial_{\psi}\theta_{0}(\psi))^{T}\eta+(\partial_{\theta}\tilde{z}(\theta_{0}(\psi)))^{T}\partial_{x}w\\
\rho(z_{0}(\psi)+w)\end{pmatrix},

which gives
\rho_{*}(G_{\delta}\circ\rho_{*})=G_{\delta}
.

For the preservation of the
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance, the result follows immediately from the definition of
X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
in (
2.144
) and the assumption on
i_{0}
in (
7.4
).
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

[
12
, Lemma 2]

G_{\delta}
is symplectic.

Lemma 7.8
.

[
8
, Lemma 6.7]

We have that

\displaystyle\rVert DG_{\delta}(\varphi,0,0)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert(DG_{\delta}(\varphi,0,0))^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

and

\displaystyle\rVert D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{2}])\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{2}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{2}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

for
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{1},\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}_{2}\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})
.

Composing
H_{\zeta}
in (
5.4
) with the transformation
G_{\delta}
, we define a Hamiltonian
K
to be

\displaystyle K(\psi,\eta,w):=H_{\zeta}(G_{\delta}(\psi,\eta,w)).

(7.18)

For each
\psi
, we consider the Taylor expansion of
K
in
\eta,w
(in the space
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times H_{S^{\perp}}
) at
(\psi,0,0)
:

\displaystyle K(\psi,\eta,w)

\displaystyle=K_{00}(\psi)+K_{10}(\psi)[\eta]+K_{01}(\psi)[w]+\frac{1}{2}K_{20}(\psi)[\eta,\eta]+\frac{1}{2}K_{02}(\psi)[w,w]+K_{11}(\psi)[\eta,w]

(7.19)

\displaystyle+K_{\geq 3}(\psi,\eta,w),

where

\displaystyle K_{ij}(\psi):=\partial_{\eta}^{i}\nabla_{w}^{j}K(\psi,0,0),

(7.20)

and
K_{\geq 3}
collects all the terms homogeneous in
(\eta,w)
of order at least
3
. Note that the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{K}
generated by
K
with the symplectic form
\mathcal{W}
in (
5.19
) at
(\psi,0,0)
is

X_{K}(\psi,0,0)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{10}(\psi)\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\psi}K_{00}(\psi)\\
\partial_{x}(K_{01}(\psi))\end{pmatrix}.

Lemma 7.9
.

X_{K}
is reversible, that is,
X_{K}:X_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to Lemma
6.3
. Indeed,
K
is also reversible with respect to the involution
\rho_{*}
, thanks to Lemma
5.3
, Lemma
7.6
and (
7.18
).
∎

Let us denote the trivial embedding by

\displaystyle\bar{i}(\varphi):=i_{triv}(\varphi)=(\varphi,0,0).

(7.21)

Then the linearization of
X_{K}
at
\bar{i}
in the direction
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}(\varphi)=(\hat{\psi}(\varphi),\hat{\eta}(\varphi),\hat{w}(\varphi))
is given by (using (
7.19
)),

\displaystyle d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}](\varphi)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\eta}K(\bar{i}+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\psi}K(\bar{i}+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\\
\partial_{x}\nabla_{w}K(\bar{i}+t\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})\end{pmatrix}\bigg|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\partial_{\psi}K_{10}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi}(\varphi)]+K_{20}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{w}(\varphi)]\right)\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(-\partial_{\psi\psi}K_{00}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi}(\varphi)]-\partial_{\psi}K_{10}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]-\partial_{\psi}K_{01}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{w}(\varphi)]\right)\\
\partial_{x}(\partial_{\psi}K_{01}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi(\varphi)}]+K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)])\end{pmatrix}.

(7.22)

Now we consider the linear operator
L
:

\displaystyle L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}](\varphi):=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}(\varphi)-d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}](\varphi),

(7.23)

which is obtained by linearizing
i\mapsto\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i-X_{K}(i)
at
\bar{i}
. In order to find an approximate inverse of
L
, we decompose it as

\displaystyle L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\psi}(\varphi)\\
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\eta}(\varphi)\\
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{w}(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(K_{20}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{w}(\varphi)]\right)\\
0\\
\partial_{x}(K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)])\end{pmatrix}}_{=:\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]}

(7.24)

\displaystyle-\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\psi}K_{10}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi}(\varphi)]\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(-\partial_{\psi\psi}K_{00}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi}(\varphi)]-\partial_{\psi}K_{10}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]-\partial_{\psi}K_{01}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{w}(\varphi)]\right)\\
\partial_{x}(\partial_{\psi}K_{01}(\varphi)[\hat{\psi(\varphi)}])\end{pmatrix}.

We check that
\mathbb{D}
is reversible, that is,
\mathbb{D}:X_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}
:

Lemma 7.10
.

For

K_{20}(\varphi):=\partial_{yy}K(\bar{i}(\varphi)),\quad K_{11}(\varphi):=\partial_{y}\nabla_{z}K(\bar{i}(\varphi)),\quad K_{02}(\varphi):=\nabla_{z}^{2}K(\bar{i}(\varphi)),

it holds that if
(\hat{\psi},\hat{\eta},\hat{w})\in Y_{o}\times X_{e}\times X_{\perp}=X_{i}
,

K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]\in X_{e},\quad K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\in Y_{\perp},\quad K_{02}[\hat{w}]\in X_{\perp},\quad K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}]\in X_{e}.

Therefore,
\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\in Y_{i}
.

Proof.

We prove
K_{20}
only since the other operators can be managed in the same way.

Since
K
is reversible (see Lemma
7.6
and (
7.18
) and Lemma
5.3
, which say that
H_{\zeta}
is reversible and
G_{\delta}
is reversibility preserving) and
\hat{\eta}
is even, we have that for all
\varphi,t
,

K(\varphi,t\hat{\eta}(\varphi),0)=K(-\varphi,t\hat{\eta}(\varphi),0).

Differentiating in
t
at
t=0
, we have
\partial_{yy}K(\bar{i})[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]=\partial_{yy}K(\bar{i}(-\varphi))[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]
. Therefore,

K_{20}(-\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(-\varphi)]\overset{\eqref{rksksd1kpsxx}}{=}\partial_{yy}K(-\bar{i}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}(-\varphi)]\overset{\hat{y}\in X_{e}}{=}\partial_{yy}K(-\bar{i}(\varphi))[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]=\partial_{yy}K(\bar{i})[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]\overset{\eqref{rksksd1kpsxx}}{=}K_{20}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)].

Hene,
\varphi\mapsto K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]
is even.
∎

We check
\mathbb{D}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, that is,
\mathbb{D}:X_{i,\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For
(\hat{\psi},\hat{\eta},\hat{w})\in X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
, we have that

K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}],\ K_{11}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}]\in X_{\mathtt{M}}.

Proof.

We prove
K_{02}
only since the other operator can be managed in the same way.

From Lemma
5.4
, and Lemma
7.6
, we have that
K
defined in (
7.18
) is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant.
Therefore, for
\hat{w}\in X_{\mathtt{M}}
, we have that for real numbers
t,s
close to
0
and
h\in H_{S^{\perp}}
, we have

K(\varphi,0,t\hat{w}(\varphi)+sh)=K(\varphi,0,\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(t\hat{w}(\varphi)+sh)=K({\varphi,0,t\hat{w}(\varphi)+s\rho_{\mathtt{M}}h}).

Differentiating in
t,s
at
t=s=0
, we have
(K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}],h)_{L^{2}_{x}}=(K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}],\rho_{\mathtt{M}}h)_{L^{2}_{x}}
.
This implies that
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}]=K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}]
, that is,
K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}]
is invariant under the
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation. Therefore,
K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}]\in X_{\mathtt{M}}
.
∎

The next lemma shows that the approximate inverse of
L
can be obtained by inverting
\mathbb{D}
.

Lemma 7.12
.

[
8
, Lemma 6.4]

If
i_{0}
is an invariant torus, then
L=\mathbb{D}
. More precisely, we have

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{\psi}K_{00}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert K_{10}-\omega\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\partial_{\psi}K_{01}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

(7.25)

Therefore, we have that,

\displaystyle\rVert

\displaystyle(L-\mathbb{D})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega^{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})})\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega^{1})}.

(7.26)

In order to prove the invertibility of
\mathbb{D}
, we recall the estimates for
K_{20}
and
K_{11}
:

Lemma 7.13
.

[
8
, Lemma 6.6]

For
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{1},C^{\infty})
, we have

\displaystyle\rVert K_{20}-6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b+2}+\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

(7.27)

\displaystyle\rVert K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\rVert K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega^{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\hat{w}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{w}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

In particular,
\rVert K_{20}-6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{6-2b}
and

\rVert K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\quad\text{ and }\quad\rVert K_{11}[\hat{w}]\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\hat{w}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

Now, we study how to invert
\mathbb{D}
. Given
g:=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\in Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
, we will find
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}=(\widehat{\psi},\widehat{\eta},\widehat{w})\in X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
such that (recalling
\mathbb{D}
from (
7.24
))

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\psi}(\varphi)\\
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\eta}(\varphi)\\
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{w}(\varphi)\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(K_{20}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)^{T}[\hat{w}(\varphi)]\right)\\
0\\
\partial_{x}(K_{02}(\varphi)[\hat{w}(\varphi)]+K_{11}(\varphi)[\hat{\eta}(\varphi)])\end{pmatrix}=g

(7.28)

Let us begin with the second equation,
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\eta}=g_{2}
. Since the right-hand side has zero mean (
g\in Y_{i}
, thus
\varphi\mapsto g_{2}(\varphi)
is odd), we can invert
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
, thus,

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}:=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}g_{2}+\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle\in X_{e},

(7.29)

where
\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle
is the average of
\hat{\eta}
in
\varphi
, which can be freely chosen. This will be fixed later soon (see (
7.45
)). Therefore, we have (using (
6.7
) and recalling
|\cdot|^{\text{Lip}}
from (
2.8
)),

\displaystyle\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\rVert g_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+|\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

(7.30)

Now we turn to the third equation from (
7.28
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\hat{w}:=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{w}-\partial_{x}(K_{02}[\hat{w}])=g_{3}+\partial_{x}(K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]).

(7.31)

Note that
g\in Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
and Lemma
7.11
,
7.10
imply that

\displaystyle g_{3}+\partial_{x}(K_{11}[\hat{\eta}])\in Y_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}.

(7.32)

The proof of the following proposition will be given through Section
8
-
10
.

Proposition 7.14
.

Given
\mathtt{S}\gg s_{0}
, there exist constants
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}=\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}(\mathtt{p})\geq 0
,
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\mathtt{pe},\mathtt{S})>0
and a set
\Omega_{\infty}=\Omega_{\infty}(i_{0})\subset\Omega_{1}
such that if (
7.3
) holds for some
\mu=\mu_{\mathtt{p},0},\mathtt{C}>0
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, then for all
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}:X_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}\mapsto Y_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}
is invertible. More precisely, given
f(\omega)\in Y_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}
, there exists
w(\omega)\in X_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}[w(\omega)]=f(\omega)
for each
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}
and

\displaystyle\rVert w\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}=\rVert\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}[g]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},\mathtt{S}}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert f\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}}\rVert f\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}}\right),

(7.33)

for all
s\in[s_{0},\mathtt{S}]
.

Since
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}
in the above proposition depends only on
\mathtt{p}
, we still denote it by
\mu_{0}
, which always varies from line to line.

Using the above proposition, we can solve (
7.31
) by

\displaystyle\hat{w}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}[g_{3}+\partial_{x}(K_{11}[\hat{\eta}])]\in X_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}},

(7.34)

and obtain the estimates,
