\displaystyle\rVert\hat{w}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g_{3}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g_{3}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)

(7.35)

\displaystyle+\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

while Lemma
7.13
implies that

\displaystyle\rVert K_{11}

\displaystyle[\hat{\eta}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert K_{11}[\hat{\eta}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{5-2b}\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{sizeofeta}}{\leq}_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\left(\varepsilon^{5-2b}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right)|\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\rVert\hat{w}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)

(7.36)

\displaystyle+\gamma^{-1}\left(\varepsilon^{5-2b}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right)|\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

Lastly, the first equation in (
7.28
) can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\psi}=g_{1}+\frac{1}{2\pi}(K_{20}[\hat{\eta}]+K_{11}^{T}[\hat{w}])\overset{Lemma~\ref{ress2}}{\in}X_{e}.

(7.37)

Using (
7.29
) and (
7.34
), this is equivalent to

\displaystyle 2\pi\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\hat{\psi}=2\pi g_{1}+\underbrace{(K_{20}+K_{11}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}\partial_{x}(K_{11}))[\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle]}_{=:M_{1}(\varphi)[\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle]}+\underbrace{(K_{20}+K_{11}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}\partial_{x}(K_{11}))[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}g_{2}]}_{=M_{1}(\varphi)[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}g_{2}]}+\underbrace{K_{11}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}[g_{3}]}_{=:M_{2}(\varphi)[g_{3}]}.

(7.38)

To invert
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
, we will choose
\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle
so that the right-hand side has zero average. To do so, let us write
M_{1}
in (
7.38
) as

\displaystyle M_{1}(\varphi)[\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle]=(6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}+\underbrace{(K_{20}-6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}+K_{11}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}\partial_{x}(K_{11})}_{=:M_{3}(\varphi)}))[\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle].

(7.39)

Using Lemma
7.13
, Proposition
7.14
and (
7.3
), we have that

\displaystyle\rVert K_{20}-6\varepsilon^{2b}\mathbb{A}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b+2}+\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\rVert K_{11}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{-1}\partial_{x}(K_{11})\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{10-6b}+\varepsilon^{4-2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})},

hence
M_{3}
in (
7.39
) can be estimated as

\displaystyle\rVert M_{3}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{10-6b}+\varepsilon^{4-2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}.

(7.40)

Plugging
s=s_{0}
, we have
\rVert M_{3}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq\varepsilon^{10-6b}
(see (
7.3
), (
7.16
)).
Hence, (
5.27
) tells us that
M_{1}=6\varepsilon^{2b}\left(\mathbb{A}+\frac{M_{3}}{\varepsilon^{2b}}\right),
is invertible and satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert M_{1}h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b}\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\varepsilon^{4-2b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

(7.41)

\displaystyle\rVert M_{1}^{-1}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{-2b}=\gamma^{-1}.

Especially,
\langle M_{1}\rangle
, the average of
M_{1}
in
\varphi
has an inverse and

\displaystyle|\langle M_{1}\rangle^{-1}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{-2b}.

(7.42)

Again using Lemma
7.13
, and Proposition
7.14
, we have for
M_{2}
in (
7.38
) that

\displaystyle\rVert M_{2}[g_{3}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{5-4b}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

(7.43)

especially when
s=s_{0}
(thanks to (
7.3
)),

\displaystyle\rVert M_{2}[g_{3}]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{5-4b}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}.

(7.44)

Hence, we choose
\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle
so that the right-hand side of (
7.38
) is zero, in other words,

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle:=-\langle M_{1}\rangle^{-1}\left(\langle 2\pi g_{1}\rangle+\langle M_{1}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}g_{2}]\rangle+\langle M_{2}[g_{3}]\rangle\right).

(7.45)

Then, it follows from (
7.41
), (
7.42
), (
7.44
) and (
6.7
) that

\displaystyle|\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\gamma^{-1}\rVert g\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}.

(7.46)

Plugging this into (
7.36
) and (
7.30
), we have

\displaystyle\rVert\hat{w}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

(7.47)

\displaystyle\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\rVert g_{2}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}.

(7.48)

Finally, we can choose (see (
7.38
))

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}:=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}\left(g_{1}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(M_{1}[\langle\hat{\eta}\rangle]+M_{1}(\varphi)[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}g_{2}]+M_{2}[g_{3}]\right)\right),

(7.49)

then it follows from (
7.41
), (
7.43
) and (
6.7
) that

\displaystyle\rVert\hat{\psi}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

(7.50)

Thus the inverse image of
g
in (
7.28
) can be chosen as in (
7.29
), (
7.45
), (
7.34
) and (
7.49
). The estimates for
\mathbb{D}^{-1}
is given in the next lemma.

Lemma 7.15
.

[
8
, Proposition 6.9]

For all
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}
where
\Omega_{\infty}
is as defined in Proposition
7.14
,
\mathbb{D}(\omega)^{-1}:Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}\mapsto X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
is well-defined. More precisely, given
g\in\text{Lip}(\Omega_{\infty},Y_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}})
, it holds that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathbb{D}^{-1}[g]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}(\rVert g\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert g\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}).

Proof.

The estimates for
\rVert\hat{\psi}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}+\rVert\hat{\eta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}+\rVert\hat{w}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}
follow from (
7.47
), (
7.48
) and (
7.50
). To show that
\mathbb{D}^{-1}[g]\in X_{i}
, we see from (
7.29
), (
7.34
) and (
7.37
), which give us that
\hat{\psi}
is odd,
\hat{\eta}
is even, and
\hat{w}\in X_{\perp}\cap X_{\mathtt{M}}
. This implies
(\hat{\psi},\hat{\eta},\hat{w})\in X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}.

∎

Now, we are ready to prove Proposition
7.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
:

For
\omega\in\Omega_{\infty}
, which is given in Proposition
7.14
, let us define an
\omega
-dependent linear map
T(i_{0})=T(i_{0})(\omega)
,

\displaystyle T(i_{0})[g]:=D{G}_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ\mathbb{D}^{-1}\circ DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}[g],\quad g\in Y_{i}.

(7.51)

Thanks to Lemma
7.6
and Lemma
7.15
, we have
T(i_{0})[g]\in X_{i}\cap X_{i,\mathtt{M}}
.

Therefore it suffices to prove the estimates (
7.3
) and (
7.14
).
Let us first derive the estimate
7.14
. From Lemma
7.8
and
7.15
, direct computations give us that

\displaystyle\rVert T(i_{0})[g]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{7}\gamma^{-4}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert g\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),

(7.52)

which gives (
7.14
).

In order to prove (
7.3
), we write

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})=d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})+\underbrace{d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})}_{=:\mathcal{E}_{1}}.

(7.53)

We further decompose the first term
d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})
. Note that for an embedding
i
, using (
6.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(G_{\delta}(i))=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(G_{\delta}(i))-X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(i))=DG_{\delta}(i)[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i]-X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(i))=DG_{\delta}(i)[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i-DG_{\delta}(i)^{-1}[X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(i))]].

Since
K(i)=H_{\zeta}(G_{\delta}(i))
(see(
7.18
)), and
G_{\delta}
is symplectic, we have

\displaystyle X_{K}(i)=DG_{\delta}(i)^{-1}[X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(i))].

(7.54)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(G_{\delta}(i))=DG_{\delta}(i)[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i-X_{K}(i)].

(7.55)

Differentiating the above equation at
(\bar{i})
in the direction
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\in X_{i}
(see (
2.138
) for
X_{i}
), we obtain

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(G_{\delta}(\bar{i}))[DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]]

\displaystyle=D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\bar{i}-X_{K}(\bar{i}),\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[DG_{\delta}^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})],\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ L[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}],

where
L
is as in (
7.23
) and the last equality follows from (
7.55
) with
G_{\delta}(\bar{i})=i_{\delta}
(see (
7.17
)). Replacing
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}
by
DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}(\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}})
, we obtain

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[DG_{\delta}^{-1}[\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta})],DG_{\delta}^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]]}_{=:\mathcal{E}_{2}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]}+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ L[DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]]

(7.56)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{2}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ L\circ D{G}_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}].

(7.57)

Therefore, it follows from (
7.53
) and (
7.57
) that

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})=\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}+\underbrace{DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ(L-\mathbb{D})\circ D{G}_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}}_{=:\mathcal{E}_{3}}+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ\mathbb{D}\circ D{G}_{\delta}(\bar{i})^{-1}.

(7.58)

Therefore, it follows from the definition of
T(i_{0})
in (
7.51
) that

\displaystyle d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})\circ T(i_{0})-I=(\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{3})\circ T(i_{0}).

(7.59)

Now we estimate the size of
\mathcal{E}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
.
For
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, it follows from the definitions in
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}
,
H_{\zeta}
and
i_{\delta}
in (
6.1
), (
5.4
) and (
7.5
) that

\displaystyle(d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})-d_{i}\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{\delta}))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=(d_{i}X_{P}(i_{0})-d_{i}X_{P}(i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{dt}(d_{i}X_{P}(t(i_{0}-i_{\delta})+i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}])dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(t(i_{0}-i_{\delta})+i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\cdot(y-y_{\delta})dt.

Therefore, using (
2.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{E}_{1}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\rVert\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(t(i_{0}-i_{\delta})+i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert y-y_{\delta}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\ +\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\rVert\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(t(i_{0}-i_{\delta})+i_{\delta})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert y-y_{\delta}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}.

(7.60)

Note that

\displaystyle t(i_{0}-i_{\delta})+i_{\delta}=\bar{i}+\mathfrak{I}_{0}+(t-1)(i_{0}-i_{\delta})=:\bar{i}+\mathfrak{I}_{t}.

(7.61)

For
\mathfrak{I}_{t}
as defined above, it follows from (
7.9
) that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{t}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\gamma^{-1}(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\gamma^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})},

where the last inequality follows from (
7.3
).
Thus, it follows from (
6.13
) and (
7.14
) that

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(\bar{i}+\mathfrak{I}_{t})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b-1}(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{t}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b-1}(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\left(\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\gamma^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\right)\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}),

and

\displaystyle\rVert\partial_{y}d_{i}X_{P}(\bar{i}+\mathfrak{I}_{t})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})},

where we used (
7.3
) in the last inequality. Hence, plugging this and (
7.9
) with (
7.61
) into (
7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b-1}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\right).

(7.62)

For
\mathcal{E}_{2}
in (
7.56
), it is straightforward from Lemma
7.8
that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{E}_{2}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}.

(7.63)

Hence, it follows that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
satisfies (
7
) (even without the coefficient
\varepsilon^{2b-1}\gamma^{-1}\gg 1
). Similarly,
\mathcal{E}_{3}
in (
7.58
) can be estimated, using Lemma
7.8
and (
7.12
), as

\rVert\mathcal{E}_{3}[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})},

which satisfies the same estimate as in (
7
). Hence,
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}+\mathcal{E}_{3}
satisfies the estimate in (
7
), that is,

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2b-1}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+(\rVert Z\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}+\rVert Z\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{0}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})})\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{\infty})}\right).

(7.64)

Hence, (
7.3
) follows from (
7.14
) and (
7.59
).

\Box

Remark 7.16
.

As mentioned, the proof of Proposition
7.14
will be achieved throughout Sections
8
-
10
. The proof of Proposition
7.3
in this subsection tells us that if Proposition
7.14
holds for some
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}\geq 0
, then Proposition
7.3
holds for some
\mu_{\mathtt{p}}>0
, which is possibly larger than
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}
. In other words, once we fix
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}
, then
\mu_{\mathtt{p}}
can be fixed, depending on
\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}
and
\mathtt{p}
. Since both of
\mu_{\mathtt{p}},\mu_{\mathtt{p,0}}
depend only on
\mathtt{p}
, we see that there exists a constant
\mu_{\mathtt{p},2}=\mu_{\mathtt{p},2}(\mu_{\mathtt{p},0},\mathtt{p})>0
such that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}<\mu_{\mathtt{p}}<\mu_{\mathtt{p},0}+\mu_{\mathtt{p},2}.

(7.65)

7.0.0.1
Linearized system at an invariant torus

Before we close this section, we digress briefly to study the linearized Hamiltonian system at
i_{0}
, assuming that
i_{0}
is a solution, that is
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0}(\varphi))=0
.
If
i_{0}
is an invariant torus, that is,
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})=0
, then the linearized Hamiltonian system at
i_{0}(\omega t)
can be conjugated to the linear system with the vector field
d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i})
. To see this more precisely, we see from (
7.54
) that

\displaystyle DG_{\delta}(i)X_{K}(i)=X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(i)).

(7.66)

Therefore, differentiating it in the direction
\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}
at the trivial embedding
\bar{i}
, we have

\displaystyle D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[X_{K}(\bar{i}),\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(\bar{i}))\circ DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}].

(7.67)

Furthermore, if
i_{0}
is invariant, then using
i_{\delta}\overset{\eqref{y_estimate}}{=}i_{0}
and
G_{\delta}(\bar{i})=i_{\delta}
, which follows from (
7.17
), we see that

\displaystyle d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0})\circ DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]

\displaystyle=DG_{\delta}(\bar{i})\circ d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]+D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i})[X_{K}(\bar{i}),\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}].

(7.68)

Also, differentiating
G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\varphi))=i_{0}(\varphi)
in
\varphi
, we see that

\displaystyle DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\varphi))[(\omega,0,0)]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i_{0}(\varphi),\text{ for all $\varphi\in\mathbb{T}^{\nu}$.}

(7.69)

Now, consider the linearized system

\displaystyle\dot{I}(t):=(\dot{\theta}(t),\dot{y}(t),\dot{z}(t))=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))[{\theta}(t),{y}(t),z(t)]=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))[I(t)],

(7.70)

which is the linearized Hamiltonian system of
H_{\zeta}
at the quasi-periodic solution
i_{0}(\omega t)
.
Then, defining
A(t):=DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))^{-1}[I(t)]=(DG_{\delta}(\omega t,0,0))^{-1}[I(t)]
, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[{A}(t)]\right)

\displaystyle=D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[(\omega,0,0),A(t)]+DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[\dot{A}(t)]=\dot{I}(t)=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))[I(t)]

\displaystyle=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))\circ DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[A(t)],

which shows that

\displaystyle DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[\dot{A}(t)]=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))\circ DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[A(t)]-D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[(\omega,0,0),A(t)].

(7.71)

Plugging (
7.69
) into (
7.71
), we see that

\displaystyle DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[\dot{A}(t)]

\displaystyle=DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))\circ d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[A(t)]

\displaystyle\ +D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t)),A(t)]-D^{2}G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[(\omega,0,0),A(t)],

while we have

\displaystyle X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t))-(\omega,0,0)

\displaystyle=(DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t)))^{-1}\circ(DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))\circ X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t))-DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[\omega,0,0])

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{rjjsdsdww2},\eqref{wlfkfahsxh1}}{=}X_{H_{\zeta}}(G_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t)))-D_{\omega}i_{0}(\omega t)

\displaystyle\overset{G_{\delta}(\bar{i})=i_{0}}{=}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))-\mathcal{D}_{\omega}i_{0}(\omega t)

\displaystyle=0.

Hence, combining this with (
7.71
), we see that
DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[\dot{A}(t)]=DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))\circ d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[A(t)]
, that is,
\dot{A}(t)=d_{i}X_{K}(\bar{i}(\omega t))[A(t)]
. Therefore, using the expression of
d_{i}X_{K}
in (
7
) and using (
7.25
), we obtain the following:

Lemma 7.17
.

Suppose
i_{0}
satisfies
\mathcal{F}_{\omega}(i_{0})=0
and consider the linear equation
I(t)
given by
\dot{I}(t)=d_{i}X_{H_{\zeta}}(i_{0}(\omega t))[I(t)].

Then
A(t):=DG_{\delta}(\bar{i}(\omega t))^{-1}[I(t)]
satisfies

\displaystyle\dot{A}(t)=\begin{pmatrix}0&K_{20}(\omega t)&K_{11}(\omega t)^{T}\\
0&0&0\\
0&K_{11}(\omega t)&\partial_{x}\circ K_{02}(\omega t)\end{pmatrix}A(t).

(7.72)

8
Linearized operator in the normal directions

In Sections
8
and
10
, we aim to prove Proposition
7.14
. In this section, we will derive more explicit formula for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\partial_{x}K_{02}(\psi)
in (
7.31
). More precisely, in view of (
7.20
), we need to find a linear operator
K_{02}(\psi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

\displaystyle(K_{02}(\psi)[w],w)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}=\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}K(\psi,0,tw)|_{t=s=0},\text{ for all }w\in H_{S^{\perp}}.

(8.1)

From now on, we use
\varphi
, instead of
\psi
, for the
“time”
variable.
From (
7.18
), (
5.3
) and Proposition
4.4
, we can easily see that for an embedding
i(\varphi)
,

\displaystyle K(i(\varphi))=H_{\zeta}(G_{\delta}(i(\varphi)))=\varepsilon^{-2b}H\circ U_{\zeta}\circ G_{\delta}(i(\varphi))=\varepsilon^{-2b}\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}\circ U_{\zeta}\circ G_{\delta}(i(\varphi)).

(8.2)

We split this section into several subsections. In Subsection
8.1
, we study some useful properties of the coefficients generated by
\Phi^{WB}\circ U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})
, at which we linearize the gSQG equation. From
3
in Proposition
4.4
, we already know that
\Phi^{WB}
changes only a finite number of Fourier modes. This will generate smoothing operators in the expression of
\nabla_{z}^{2}K
, which will be studied in Subsection
8.2
. In Subsection
8.3
, we derive the explicit form of the Hessian
\nabla_{z}^{2}K
.

8.1
Homogeneous expansion

In this subsection, we investigate the structure of the coefficients and symbols arising in the linearized operator.

To begin with, let us denote (recall
U_{\zeta}
from (
5.2
)),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle:=u(\varphi):=\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}(\varphi))),

(8.3)

\displaystyle\bar{v}

\displaystyle:=\bar{v}(\varphi,x):=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\sqrt{j_{k}\zeta_{k}}e^{\text{i}\mathtt{l}(j_{k})\cdot\varphi}e^{\text{i}j_{k}x}=:\sum_{j_{k}\in S}\bar{v}_{j_{k}}(\varphi,x),

(8.4)

where
\mathtt{l}:S\mapsto\mathbb{Z}^{\nu}
such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{l}(j_{k}):=\mathtt{e}_{k},\text{ and }\mathtt{l}(-j_{k}):=-\mathtt{l}(j_{k})\text{ for $j_{k}\in S^{+}=\left\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\nu}\right\}$},

(8.5)

and
\mathtt{e}_{k}:=\left(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0\right)
denotes the
k
-th vector of the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}
.
We also denote the average operators:

\displaystyle M_{x}(q):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}q(\omega,\varphi,x)dx,\quad M_{\varphi,x}(q):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}M_{x}(q)(\omega,\varphi)d\varphi.

(8.6)

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
u
be as in (
8.3
). Then
u
has the expansion:

\displaystyle u=\varepsilon\bar{v}+3\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(\bar{v},\bar{v})+q,

(8.7)

where
K_{2}
is as in (
4.34
). And we have that

\displaystyle\rVert q\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},\quad\rVert d_{i}q(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(8.8)

Furthermore, it holds that
M_{x}(q)=0
.

Proof.

From Lemma
4.12
, (
8.3
), (
5.2
) and (
7.5
) , we have that

u=\Phi^{WB}(\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta})+\varepsilon^{b}z_{0})=\varepsilon v_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{b}z_{0}+6\varepsilon^{1+b}\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v_{\varepsilon},z_{0})+3\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(v_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})+\Phi^{WB}_{\geq 3}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})).

Let
q=q(\varphi,x)
be

\displaystyle q:=\varepsilon\left(v_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}\right)+3\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(K_{2}(v_{\varepsilon},v_{\varepsilon})-K_{2}(\bar{v},\bar{v})\right)+\varepsilon^{b}z_{0}+6\varepsilon^{1+b}\partial_{x}\Pi_{S}K_{1}(v_{\varepsilon},z_{0})+\Phi^{WB}_{\geq 3}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})).

(8.9)

Clearly, we have the expression in (
8.7
). Noticing that
|v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta})-\bar{v}|=O(\mathfrak{I}_{\delta})
, more precisely (using (
5.2
)),

\displaystyle\rVert v_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s},

(8.10)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}(v_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v})(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

and using (
7.16
), the estimate (
8.8
) follows straightforwardly (for example, in the estimate for
q
in (
8.8
),
\varepsilon^{3}
comes from
\Phi^{WB}_{\geq 3}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))
in (
8.9
) and
\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}
comes from
\varepsilon\left(v_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}\right)
. All the other terms in (
8.9
) have smaller contribution in the estimates). Since
\Phi^{WB}(f)\in L^{2}_{0}
, for
f\in L^{2}_{0}
, we have that
M_{x}(u)=0
. Thus (
8.7
) implies
M_{x}(q)=0
, since
\overline{v}
and
\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}K_{2}(\bar{v},\bar{v})
have zero average.
∎

8.1.1
Homogeneous expansion of the coefficients: Class
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}

Now, we will define some classes of functions depending on the embedding
i_{0}
. The motivation of such classes is that Lemma
8.1
tells us that
u(x),u(x)-u(y)
, where
u
are in (
5.2
), is contained in those classes (see Lemma
8.3
).

Definition 8.2
.

We say that a function
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x)\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, if
a
admits an expansion:

\displaystyle a(\omega,\varphi,x)=\varepsilon a_{1}(\omega,\varphi,x)+\varepsilon^{2}a_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x)+\varepsilon^{3}a_{3}(\omega,\varphi,x)+q(\omega,\varphi,x),

(8.11)

where

(1)

Each
a_{i}
is of the form:

\displaystyle a_{i}(\omega,\varphi,x)=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}\bar{v}_{j_{1}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\bar{v}_{j_{i}}(\varphi,x),\text{ for $i=1,2,3$},

(8.12)

for some constants
C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}
, which do not depend on any of
i_{0},\omega,\varphi,x,\varepsilon
(note that
\bar{v}
depends on
\zeta
(see (
8.4
)) and thus
\omega
by (
6.2
)).

(2)

q
in (
8.11
) satisfies that, assuming (
7.16
) for some
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}(\mathtt{p})
,

\displaystyle\rVert q\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},\quad\rVert d_{i}q(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(8.13)

Furthermore,
M_{x}(q)-M_{\varphi,x}(q)
satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert M_{x}(q)-M_{\varphi,x}(q)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

(8.14)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}(M_{x}(q)-M_{\varphi,x}(q)(i_{0}))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

We also say that a function
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
, if
a
admits an expansion:

\displaystyle a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)=\varepsilon a_{1}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)+\varepsilon^{2}a_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)+\varepsilon^{3}a_{3}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)+q(\omega,\varphi,x,y),

(8.15)

where

(1)

Each
a_{i}
is of the form:

\displaystyle a_{i}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(x-y)\bar{v}_{j_{1}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\bar{v}_{j_{i}}(\varphi,x),

(8.16)

for
i=1,2,3
, for some smooth functions
C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(\cdot)
, which do not depend on any of
i_{0},\omega,\varphi,\varepsilon
, but
(x-y)
.

(2)

q
in (
8.15
) satisfies the same estimates as in (
8.13
) (where the norm,
\rVert\cdot\rVert_{s}
, is replaced by
\rVert\cdot\rVert_{H^{s}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+2})}
). Furthermore, denoting
Q:=q(\omega,\varphi,x,x-y)
,
M_{x}(Q)-M_{\varphi,x}(Q)
satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert M_{x}(Q)-M_{\varphi,x}(Q)\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},

(8.17)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}(M_{x}(Q)-M_{\varphi,x}(Q))(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

In the following lemma, we list some properties of the functions in
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
. The proof is straightforward from its definition, therefore we omit it for simplicity.

Lemma 8.3
.

We have:

(1)

If
a\in\mathfrak{C}_{n}(i_{0})
, for
n=1,2
, it holds that

\rVert a\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right).

(2)

u\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
where
u
is as in (
8.3
).

(3)

\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
are closed under addition, multiplication, differentiation in
x,y,\varphi
.

(4)

Denoting
u_{1}(x)=u_{1}(\omega,\varphi,x),\ u_{2}(y)=u_{2}(\omega,\varphi,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
,
u_{1}(x)u_{2}(y)
, then
u_{1}(x)+u_{2}(y),\ J(u_{1})(x,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
, where
J(u_{1})(x,y)=\frac{(u_{1}(x)-u_{1}(y))^{2}}{2-2\cos(x-y)}
.

(5)

Let
F:\mathbb{R}^{3}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be an analytic function such that
F(0,0,0)=0
. We have that
F(u(x),u(y),J(u)(x,y))\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
, .

(6)

If
u\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
and
a\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
, then

\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{a}u,\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}u\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
(see (
2.67
) for the definitions of
\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{a}
and
\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}
.).

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
a\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
. Then,
M_{x}(a)
has a decomposition:

\displaystyle M_{x}(a)(\omega,\varphi)=\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{m}(\omega)+r(\omega)+\tilde{q}(\omega,\varphi),\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\tilde{q}(\omega,\varphi)d\varphi=0,

such that
\mathtt{m}(\omega),r(\omega)
do not depend on
\varphi,x
, and
\mathtt{m}
does not even depend on
i_{0}
and

\displaystyle|\mathtt{m}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}1,

(8.18)

\displaystyle|r|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{7-4b},\quad|d_{i}r(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}|_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

(8.19)

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{q}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},\quad\rVert d_{i}\tilde{q}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(8.20)

Proof.

By definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
,
a
can be written as

a=\varepsilon a_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}a_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}a_{3}+q,

where
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
have the form as in (
8.12
) and
q
satisfies the estimates in
(2)
in Definition
8.2
. Clearly, we have

M_{x}(a)=\varepsilon M_{x}(a_{1})+\varepsilon^{2}M_{x}(a_{2})+\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(a_{3})+M_{x}(q)=\varepsilon^{2}\underbrace{M_{x}(a_{2})}_{=:\mathtt{m}}+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(a_{3})+M_{\varphi,x}(q)}_{=:r}+\underbrace{M_{x}(q)-M_{\varphi,x}(q)}_{=:\tilde{q}},

where the last equality follows from the fact that
0
is not in the tangential sites therefore
\int_{\mathbb{T}}a_{1}dx=0
(see (
4.6
)).

By its definition, we have
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\tilde{q}d\varphi=0.

Furthermore, (
8.18
) follows from the structure of
a_{2}
in (
8.12
): Recalling (
8.4
) and (
8.5
), we have that
M_{x}(a_{2})=M_{\varphi,x}(a_{2})
. (
8.19
) follows from the structure of
a_{3}
in (
8.12
) and the estimate for
q
in (
8.13
) with (
7.16
). The last estimate (
8.20
) for
\tilde{q}
is trivial from (
8.14
).
∎

8.1.2
Homogeneous expansion of the symbols: Class
\mathfrak{S}^{m}

Definition 8.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)
be a symbol that depends on
\omega,\varphi
, that is,
a(\omega,\varphi,\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
, for some
m\in\mathbb{R}
(see (
2.2.1
) for the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{m}
). We say
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}(i_{0})
if
\mathfrak{a}
admits an expansion:

\mathfrak{a}=\varepsilon\mathfrak{a}_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{a}_{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\mathfrak{a}_{3}+\mathfrak{q},

with the following properties:

(1)

Each
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is of the form,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{i}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(\xi)\bar{v}_{j_{1}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\bar{v}_{j_{i}}(\varphi,x),

(8.21)

for
i=1,2,3
, for some smooth symbols
C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(\xi)\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
, which do not depend on any of
i_{0},\omega,\varphi,x,\varepsilon
(note that
\bar{v}
depends on
\zeta
(see (
8.4
)) and thus
\omega
by (
6.2
)).

(2)

\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}(\omega,\varphi,\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
, satisfies (under the assumption that (
7.16
) holds for some
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}(\mathtt{p},\eta_{0})
,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,s,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},\quad|d_{i}\mathfrak{q}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(8.22)

Furthermore,
M_{x}(\mathfrak{q})-M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q})
satisfies

\displaystyle|M_{x}(\mathfrak{q})-M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q})|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

(8.23)

\displaystyle|d_{i}(M_{x}(\mathfrak{q})-M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q})(i_{0}))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

Remark 8.6
.

In what follows, we allow the loss of derivatives
\mu_{0}
to depend on
\eta_{0}
, for example,
(2)
of Definition
8.5
. We note that
\eta_{0}
will be bounded depending on only
\mathtt{p}
, therefore we still have
\mu_{0}
depend on only
\mathtt{p}
. For simplicity, we will not trace the explicit dependence of
\mu_{0}
on
\eta_{0}
.

The motivation of the above definition is that such symbols naturally arise from the coefficients in
\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
in the linearized operator (see Lemma
8.7
).

Lemma 8.7
.

If
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
, then there exists
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}(i_{0})
such that
\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})
.

Proof.

We first claim that

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}=Op(\mathfrak{b}),\text{ for some $\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}.$}

(8.24)

By Definition
8.2
for the class
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle a=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}a_{i}+q,

(8.25)

which is the decomposition of
a
as in (
8.16
). We aim to find a symbol
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}
such that

\displaystyle Op(\mathfrak{b})=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{i}}+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{q}.

(8.26)

For each
i=1,2,3
,
(1)
of Definition
8.2
tells us that

\displaystyle a_{i}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(x-y)\bar{v}_{j_{1}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\bar{v}_{j_{i}}(\varphi,x),

(8.27)

for some function
y\mapsto C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(y)
that does not depend on any of
i_{0},\omega,\varphi,\varepsilon
.
Recalling
S_{\Upsilon}
from (
2.108
), we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{i}:=S_{\Upsilon}(a_{i}),\quad\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{b}}=S_{\Upsilon}(q),\quad\mathfrak{b}:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}\mathfrak{b}_{i}+\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{b}}.

(8.28)

From the construction of
S_{\Upsilon}
in (
2.105
)-(
2.108
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}S_{\Upsilon}(C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}})(\xi)\bar{v}_{j_{1}}(\varphi,x)\cdots\bar{v}_{j_{i}}(\varphi,x).

Note that Since
S_{\Upsilon}(C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}})(\xi)
does not depend on any of
i_{0},\omega,\varphi,x,\varepsilon
. From Lemma
2.39
, (
8.28
) tells us that
S_{\Upsilon}(C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}})(\xi)\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha-3}
and

\displaystyle Op(\mathfrak{b})=\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}.

To finish the proof of the claim that
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}
, we need to show that
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{b}}
satisfies the estimates in (
8.22
) and (
8.23
). Thanks to the estimates for
q
in
(2)
of Definition
8.2
, the estimates (
8.22
) and (
8.23
) for
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{b}}
follows from (
2.124
).

Therefore, we have
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha-3}
satisfying (
8.24
). To find
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}
such that
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}=Op(\mathfrak{b})
, we use
(1)
of Lemma
2.10
, then the result follows immediately.
∎

As we will see later (especially in Lemma
8.17
), the linearized operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
can be thought of as a linearized gSQG at
u
, up to a smoothing operator. Thanks to Lemma
8.1
, we can see that the terms of size
O(\varepsilon),O(\varepsilon^{2})
solely depend on
\bar{v}
, but not on
i_{0}
. The following definition will be useful to analyze the terms of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
.

Definition 8.8
.

We say
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{b}=\mathfrak{b}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{2}
,
\mathfrak{c}=\mathfrak{c}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{3}
if

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k}\in S}C_{j_{k}}(\xi)\bar{v}_{j_{k}}(\varphi,x),

(8.29)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}}}(\xi)\bar{v}_{j_{k_{1}}}(\varphi,x)\bar{v}_{j_{k_{2}}}(\varphi,x),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},j_{k_{3}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},j_{k_{2}},j_{k_{3}}}(\xi)\bar{v}_{j_{k_{1}}}(\varphi,x)\bar{v}_{j_{k_{2}}}(\varphi,x)\bar{v}_{j_{k_{3}}}(\varphi,x),

for some smooth symbols
C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(\xi)\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{R}
, which do not depend on any of
i_{0},\varphi,x,\varepsilon
(note that
\zeta
depend on
\omega
by (
6.2
))

8.2
Finite dimensional operators

We collect useful properties of the finite dimensional operators that are arising in the linearized operator.

Definition 8.9
.

We say that a
\varphi
-dependent linear operator
C^{\infty}_{x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{x}
is a finite dimensional operator if

\displaystyle R(\varphi)[h]=\sum_{|j|\leq C}\int_{0}^{1}(h,g_{j}(\varphi,\tau))_{L^{2}_{x}}\chi_{j}(\varphi,\tau)d\tau,

(8.30)

for some
C>0
and
g_{j}(\varphi,\tau),\chi_{j}(\varphi,\tau)\in C^{\infty}_{x}
.

Clearly,
R
is a smoothing operator (in the variable
x
) in the sense that
R(\varphi)[h]
is as smooth as
g_{j}
and
\chi_{j}
. Its tame constants described in Subsection
2.2.2
can be estimated in terms of
g
and
\chi
:

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
R
be a finite dimensional operator, where
g=(g_{j})
and
\chi=(\chi_{j})
depend on
\omega\in\Omega_{1}
. Then, the Lip-
0
-tame estimates of
R
(see Subsection
2.2.2
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}^{\gamma}_{R}(0,s)\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert g(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\rVert\chi(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}+\rVert g(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\right).

Furthermore, if
g
and
\chi
depend on embedding
i
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}_{d_{i}R(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]}(0,s)

\displaystyle\quad\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\left(\rVert d_{i}g(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi(i)\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}g(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert\chi_{j}(i)\rVert_{s_{0}}+\rVert d_{i}\chi(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert g(i)\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}\chi(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert g(i)\rVert_{s_{0}}\right)

Proof.

The proof is straightforward from the definition of the Lipschitz tame operators and using Lemma
2.2
and (
2.6
).
∎

We define a class of finite dimensional operators, which will appear in our reduction procedure in Section
10
.

Definition 8.11
.

We say
R\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, if
R
is a linear combination of the finite dimensional operators of the form
\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}+R_{2}
, where
R_{1}
is of the form,

\displaystyle R_{1}[h]=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Pi_{S}(Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})h),

(8.31)

for some
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{1}
for some
m\geq 0
(see Definition
8.8
) and
R_{2}
is of the form,

\displaystyle R_{2}(\varphi)[h]=\sum_{|j|\leq C}\int_{0}^{1}(h,g_{j}(\varphi,\tau))_{L^{2}_{x}}\chi_{j}(\varphi,\tau)d\tau,

(8.32)

for some
g_{j},\chi_{j}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert g(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\rVert\chi(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}+\rVert g(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

(8.33)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\left(\rVert d_{i}g(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi(i_{0})\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}g(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert\chi_{j}(i_{0})\rVert_{s_{0}}+\rVert d_{i}\chi(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert g(i_{0})\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}\chi(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert g(i_{0})\rVert_{s_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}.

Note that the operator in (
8.31
) is indeed a finite dimensional operator of the form in (
8.30
), because

Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Pi_{S}(Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})h)=\sum_{j\in S}(Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}_{x}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[e^{\text{i}jx}]=\sum_{j\in S}(h,Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{b}})[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}_{x}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[e^{\text{i}jx}],

where the last equality follows from (
2.15
).

Lemma 8.12
.

Let
\Phi^{\pm}=\Phi,\Phi^{-1}
be a symplectic transformations on
C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
with the estimates:

\displaystyle\rVert(\Phi^{\pm}-I)h\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)

(8.34)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\Phi^{\pm}(i_{0})h[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}.

If
\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, then we have
\mathcal{R}\Phi,\Phi\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

We will show that if
\mathcal{R}=\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}+R_{2}
where
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are of the form in (
8.31
) and (
8.32
), then
\mathcal{R}\Phi\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
. The same results for
\Phi\mathcal{R}
and a linear combination of such operators follow in a similar way.

We have that

\displaystyle R_{1}h

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Pi_{S}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})h,\text{ with $\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{1}$ for some $m\in\mathbb{R}$},

\displaystyle R_{2}h

\displaystyle=\sum_{|j|\leq C}\int_{0}^{1}(h,g_{j}(\tau))_{L^{2}}\chi_{j}(\tau)d\tau,

as described in Definition
8.11
. Therefore it follows that

\mathcal{R}\Phi h=\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}\Phi h+R_{2}\Phi h=\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}h+\underbrace{\left(\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}(\Phi-I)h+R_{2}\Phi h\right)}_{=:\mathcal{R}_{2}}.

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{R}_{2}
can be written in the form (
8.32
) with the desired estimates in (
8.33
). Using
\Pi_{S}h=\sum_{j\in S}(h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}}e^{\text{i}jx}
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}R_{1}(\Phi-I)h

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\sum_{j\in S}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[(Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})(\Phi-I)h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}e^{\text{i}jx}]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}(h,\left(Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})(\Phi-I)\right)^{T}[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[e^{\text{i}jx}]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}(h,Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{b}})(\Phi^{T}-I)[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[e^{\text{i}jx}]

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}(h,\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{b}})(\Phi^{T}-I)[e^{\text{i}jx}]}_{=:\tilde{g}_{j}})_{L^{2}}\underbrace{Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})[e^{\text{i}jx}]}_{=:\tilde{\chi}_{j}},

where the third equality follows from (
2.16
). Note that the last expression is of the form (
8.32
). Also, using that
\Phi
is symplectic and (
2.128
), we have

\tilde{g}_{j}=\varepsilon^{2}(Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{b}})(\partial_{x}^{-1}\Phi^{-1}\partial_{x}-I))=\varepsilon^{2}(Op^{W}(\overline{\mathfrak{b}})(\partial_{x}^{-1}(\Phi^{-1}-I)\partial_{x}))

Using the definition of
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
and the estimates (
8.34
), it follows straightforwardly that
\tilde{g}_{j},\tilde{\chi}_{j}
satisfy the estimates (
8.33
).
∎

8.3
Linearized operator in the normal directions

In view of (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), we look at the contribution of each transformation to the Hessian of the Hamiltonian
K
.

Lemma 8.13
.

[
8
, Lemma 7.4]

Let
H
be as in (
4.13
). There exists a linear operator
R(\varphi)=R(i_{0})(\varphi):H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

K_{02}(\varphi)[w]=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}(\varphi)))[w]+R(\varphi)[w],

where
R(\varphi)
is a finite dimensional operator in (
8.30
) such that

g_{j}(\varphi)=g_{j}(i_{0})(\varphi)
and
\chi_{j}(\varphi)=\chi(i_{0})(\varphi)
satisfy

\displaystyle\rVert g_{j}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\rVert\chi_{j}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}+\rVert g_{j}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi_{j}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{s}\varepsilon^{b+1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}

(8.35)

and

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}g_{j}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert\chi_{j}(i_{0})\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}g_{j}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert\chi_{j}(i_{0})\rVert_{s_{0}}+\rVert d_{i}\chi_{j}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}}\rVert g_{j}(i_{0})\rVert_{s}+\rVert d_{i}\chi_{j}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\rVert g_{j}(i_{0})\rVert_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq_{s}\varepsilon^{b+1}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}.

(8.36)

Remark 8.14
.

Among the finite dimensional operators arising in
\nabla_{z}^{2}H
, the largest contribution comes form the Hessian of
\mathfrak{H}_{1}
in (
4.17
) (see Lemma
8.17
). Note that
\nabla_{z}\mathfrak{H}_{1}
is a finite dimensional operator, since
z\mapsto\mathfrak{H}_{1}(v+z)
depends on only a finite number of Fourier modes of
z
, because of the projection
\Pi_{S}
in the integral.

Now, we consider the contribution of
\Phi^{WB}
. We first recall the following lemma:

Lemma 8.15
.

[
8
, Lemma 7.1]

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a Hamiltonian of class
C^{2}(H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{R})
and consider a map
\Phi(f):=f+\Psi(f)
satisfying
\Psi(f)=\Pi_{E}\Psi(\Pi_{E}f)
, for all
f\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{T})
, for some finite dimensional subspace
E
as in (
4.9
). Then

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi)(f)[h]=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi(f))[h]+R_{\mathcal{H}}(f)[h],

where
R_{\mathcal{H}}(f)
is a finite dimensional operator in (
8.30
). For the explicit form of
R_{\mathcal{H}}(f)
, denoting

\displaystyle h(x):=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{j}e^{\text{i}jx},\quad\nabla\Psi(f)[\cdot]:=\Pi_{E}\nabla\Psi(f)[\Pi_{E}\cdot],\quad\nabla^{2}\Psi(f)[\cdot,\cdot]=\Pi_{E}\nabla^{2}\Psi(f)[\Pi_{E}\cdot,\Pi_{E}(\cdot)],

(which follow from
\Psi(f)=\Pi_{E}\Psi(\Pi_{E}f)
), we have that

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{H},0}(f)[h]=\sum_{|j|<C}h_{j}\chi_{j}(x),\quad\chi_{j}:=\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi(f))[\Pi_{E}\nabla\Psi(f)[e^{\text{i}jx}]],

(8.37)

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{H},1}(f)[h]=\sum_{|j|<C}h_{j}\chi_{j}(x),\quad\chi_{j}:=\Pi_{E}A_{j}^{T}[\Pi_{E}\nabla\mathcal{H}(\Phi(f))],\text{ where }A_{j}[\cdot]:=\nabla^{2}\Psi(f)[e^{\text{i}jx},\Pi_{E}\cdot],

(8.38)

\displaystyle R_{\mathcal{H},2}(f)[h]=\sum_{|j|<C}h_{j}\chi_{j},\quad\chi_{j}:=\Pi_{E}(\nabla\Psi(f))^{T}\Pi_{E}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi(f))[e^{\text{i}jx}].

(8.39)

Recall from Proposition
4.4
that
H
in (
4.13
) is given by
H=\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}
, where
\Phi^{WB}=f+\Psi
and
\Psi
satisfies
\Psi=\Pi_{E}\Psi\circ\Pi_{E}
for a finite dimensional space
E
of the form in (
4.9
).

Lemma 8.16
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be as in Proposition
3.2
. There exist linear operators
R_{1},R_{2}
of the form (
8.30
) such that

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\left(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}\right)(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))=\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})))+R_{1}(\varphi)+R_{2}(\varphi),

(8.40)

where

\displaystyle R_{1}(\varphi)[h]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)))[h],\text{ where $\mathfrak{H}_{1}$ is as in \eqref{sjdjsjs93jx}},

and
R_{2}
, which can be written as
R_{2}(\varphi)[h]=\sum_{|j|\leq C}(h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}}\chi_{j}(\varphi)
, satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert\chi_{j}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{7-3b}+\varepsilon^{1+b}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}

(8.41)

and

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\chi_{j}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\varepsilon^{2b-1}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}.

(8.42)

Proof.

From Lemma
8.15
, it follows that there exists a finite dimensional operator
R
such that

\nabla_{z}^{2}(\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB})(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))=\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})))+R(\varphi),

where
R
is of the form

\displaystyle R(\varphi)[h]=\sum_{|j|\leq C}(h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}}\chi_{j}(\varphi).

(8.43)

We recall from Proposition
4.4
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\circ\Phi^{WB}(f)=H(f)=H_{2}+H_{3}+H_{4}+H_{\geq 5},

(8.44)

where
H_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
k
. First (denoting
f=v+z
the tangential and normal component of
f
), we notice from (
4.14
) that
\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{2}+H_{3})(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))
does not have a finite dimensional operator. In
H_{4}
, we can find from
2
of Proposition
4.4
and (
3.9
) that
\mathcal{H}_{4,2},\mathfrak{H}_{2}
do not induce a finite dimensional operator, while
\mathfrak{H}_{1}
in (
4.18
) gives a finite dimensional operator and we have

R_{1}(\varphi):=\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))[h]=\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)))[h].

All the other homogeneous terms which can yield a nonzero Hessian in
z
are of the form (recall the notation (
4.10
)):

\displaystyle G(f)=G(v+z)=\begin{cases}R(vz^{3}),\text{ or }R(z^{4})&\text{from quartic terms,}\\
R(v^{n-k}z^{k}),\quad\text{ for $n=5$, and $k\geq 2$, or $n\geq 6$}&\text{ from higher order terms},\end{cases}

(8.45)

which follows from
2
of Proposition
4.4
.
We will show that a finite dimensional operator
\tilde{R}
of the form in (
8.43
), given as
\tilde{R}[h]=\nabla_{z}^{2}G(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))
, satisfies the estimates (
8.35
) and (
8.13
).

We will prove the case where
G(f)=R(vz^{3})
case only, since the other terms can be treated in the same way. Thanks to (
5
) in Proposition
4.4
, there exists a
\mu_{0}
-regular trilinear map
A
(see Definition
2.42
) such that (recalling (
5.2
))

\tilde{R}(\varphi)[h]=\nabla_{z}^{2}G(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))[h]=\varepsilon^{1+b}A(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)),z_{0}(\varphi),h)=A(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)),z_{0}(\varphi),\Pi_{E}h),

for some finite dimensional space
E
, where the last equality follows from the assumption that
\tilde{R}
in (
8.43
) takes only a finite number of Fourier modes. This implies that
\chi_{j}
is given by

\chi_{j}(\varphi):=\varepsilon^{1+b}A(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)),z_{0}(\varphi),e^{\text{i}jx}).

Since
A
is
\mu_{0}
-regular, recalling its definition from Definition
2.42
and using (
7.16
), the estimates (
8.35
) and (
8.13
) follow straightforwardly.
∎

Combining Lemma
8.13
and Lemma
8.16
, we have the following:

Lemma 8.17
.

We have that

\displaystyle K_{02}(\varphi)=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})))+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]+R(\varphi),

(8.46)

where a finite dimensional operator
R
in the form in (
8.32
) satisfies the estimates in (
8.33
).

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
8.13
, Lemma
8.16
and (
4.13
), we have

\displaystyle K_{02}(\varphi)[h]

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))[h]+R_{1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\Phi^{WB}(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta})))[h]+R_{1}(\varphi)[h]+R_{2}(\varphi)[h]+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)))[h],

(8.47)

for some
R_{1}
satisfying the estimates in (
8.35
) and (
8.13
), and
R_{2}
satisfying (
8.41
) and (
8.42
). Note that the estimates (
8.35
), (
8.13
), (
8.41
) and (
8.42
) are stronger than (
8.33
), therefore,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
satisfy (
8.33
).
Therefore, to finish the proof, it suffices to prove that

R_{*}(\varphi):=\varepsilon^{2}\left(\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0}(\varphi),y_{\delta}(\varphi)))-\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))\right),

satisfies the estimate (
8.33
), which will give us (
8.46
) for
R:=R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{*}
. Recalling
\mathfrak{H}_{1}
from (
4.17
), we see that there exists a
\mu_{0}
-regular (for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
. See Definition
2.42
) trilinear operator
A=A(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
and a finite dimensional space
E
of the form in (
4.9
) such that

\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(f)[h]=A(\Pi_{S}f,\Pi_{S}f,\Pi_{E}h).

Hence,

\displaystyle R_{*}[h]

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\left(A(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta}),v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta}),\Pi_{E}h)-A(\bar{v},\bar{v},\Pi_{E}h)\right)=:\sum_{|j|\leq C}(h,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}}\chi_{j},

(8.48)

where

\displaystyle\chi_{j}(\varphi,x)=\varepsilon^{2}\left(A(v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta}),v_{\varepsilon}(\theta_{0},y_{\delta}),e^{\text{i}jx})-A(\bar{v},\bar{v},e^{\text{i}jx})\right).

(8.49)

Then, from (
8.10
), which says
|v_{\varepsilon}-\bar{v}|=O(\varepsilon^{6-4b}+|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|)
, it follows that
g_{j}:=e^{\text{i}jx}
and
\chi_{j}
defined in (
8.49
) satisfy the estimates (
8.33
) (the estimate for
d_{i}\chi(i_{0})
in (
8.33
) follows straightforwardly, since
A
is a trilinear map). Hence,
R_{*}
in (
8.48
) satisfies the estimates (
8.33
).
∎

Now we state the main result of this section.

Proposition 8.18
.

The linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
7.31
) has the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}h

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M[h]+\mathcal{R},\text{ with }M[h]:=\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}\right)h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{3}}

(8.50)

where

(1)

a_{1},a_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
and
a_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
(see Definition
8.2
for the sets
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
). Furthermore,
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are real-valued and

\displaystyle a_{1}(\varphi,x,y)=a_{1}(-\varphi,-x,-y),\quad a_{3}(\varphi,x,y)=a_{3}(-\varphi,-x,-y),\ a_{2}(\varphi,x)=a_{2}(-\varphi,-x).

(8.51)

(2)

\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
. Specifically,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\varphi):=\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]+\partial_{x}R(\varphi),

(8.52)

where
R
is a finite dimensional operator satisfying (
8.33
).

(3)

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is real, reversible (see Definition
2.46
for a reversible operator) and Hamiltonian (see Definition
2.40
for a Hamiltonian operator).

(4)

\mathcal{L}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant and
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are also
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, that is,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(a_{2})(\varphi,x)=a_{2}(\varphi,x),\quad\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(a_{i})(\varphi,x,y)=a_{i}(\varphi,x,y),\text{ for $i=1,3$},

(8.53)

where
\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(a)(\varphi,x,y):=a(\varphi,x+\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}},y+\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}})
.

Proof.

We first prove the expression of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
8.50
) and then give proofs for
(1)
-
(4)
. For
u(x)=u(\varphi,x)
, defined in (
8.3
),
we compute
\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(u)[h]
. From Proposition
3.2
, it is clear that there exist
F^{1},F^{2},F^{3}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, which are real analytic near
(0,0,0)
such that
F^{i}(0,0,0)=0
for
i=1,2,3
and

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}_{z}\mathcal{H}(u)[h]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}h+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}h

(8.54)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F^{1}(u(x),u(y),J(u)(x,y))(h(x)-h(y))dy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F^{2}(u(x),u(y),J(u)(x,y))(u(x)-u(y))dyh(x)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F^{3}(u(x),u(y))h(y)dy,

where
J(u)(x,y):=\frac{(u(x)-u(y))^{2}}{2-2\cos(x-y)}
.
Let us denote

\displaystyle a_{1}(x,y):=F^{1}(u(x),u(y),J(u)(x,y)),

(8.55)

\displaystyle a_{2}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}F^{2}(u(x),u(y),J(u)(x,y))(u(x)-u(y))dy,

\displaystyle a_{3}(x,y):=F^{3}(u(x),u(y)),

so that we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}(u)[h]=\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}h+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}\right)h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{3}}h=M[h].

(8.56)

Note that
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
depend on
\omega,\varphi
and
i_{0}
as well, since so does
u
(see (
8.3
)). Then, it follows from Lemma
8.17
and (
8.3
) that

\displaystyle K_{02}=M[h]+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]+R(\varphi),

(8.57)

where
R
satisfies the estimates in (
8.33
).
From the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
7.31
), we have

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Mh+\partial_{x}\mathcal{R},\quad\mathcal{R}:=\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]+R(\varphi),

which gives the expression of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
8.50
).

We prove
(2)
first and then move on to
(1)
,
(3)
and
(4)
.

Proof of
(2)
.

The decomposition
\mathcal{R}=\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]+R(\varphi)
, where
R
satisfies (
8.33
), has just been proved above. Noticing
\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathfrak{H}_{1}(\bar{v}(\varphi))[h]
is of the form in (
8.31
) (see (
4.17
) for the definition of
\mathfrak{H}_{1}
), it implies that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

Proof of
(1)
.

It follows immediately from Lemma
8.3
that
a_{1},a_{3}\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}(i_{0})
and
a_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}(i_{0})
. Furthermore,
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are real, since
F^{1},F^{2},F^{3}
in (
8.55
) are real-valued functions. To prove the symmetry in (
8.51
), note that
u\in X
, that is,

u(-\varphi,-x)=u(\varphi,x),

which follows from (
8.3
), Lemma
7.1
,
4
of Proposition
4.4
, and (
5.2
). Hence, (
8.51
) follows straightforwardly from (
8.55
).

Proof of
(3)
.

Clearly,
K
is a real-valued Hamiltonian (see (
7.18
)) since
H_{\zeta}
is a real-valued Hamiltonian.

K
is a reversible Hamiltonian, since
H_{\zeta}
is reversible (Lemma
5.3
),
G_{\delta}
is reversibility preserving (Lemma
7.6
) and
K=H_{\zeta}\circ G_{\delta}
, which is given in (
7.18
). From the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
, and Lemma
2.50
, we see that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is reversible. Furthermore, since
K_{02}
is a symmetric operator (because it is a Hessian of a Hamiltonian, thanks to (
8.1
)),
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
(see (
7.31
)) is a Hamiltonian operator.

Proof of
(4)
.
Lemma
7.11
imples that
K_{02}
in (
8.1
) is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, and therefore so is
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in (
7.31
), since
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
does not destroy this property. Since
i_{\delta}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, it follows from
4
of Proposition
4.4
, (
5.22
) and (
8.3
) that
u
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant. Then, (
8.53
) follows immediately from (
8.55
).
∎

8.4
Structure of the operator of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})

In Proposition
8.18
, we decomposed the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
into a sum (up to
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
) of a finite dimensional operator
\mathcal{R}
and a
“non”
-finite dimensional operator
M
. Before we close this section, we specify the terms in
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
for the purpose of the analysis of the eigenvalues of
\partial_{x}M+\mathcal{R}
. The results in this subsection will be used in Subsection
10.4
.
We say that for linear operators
L_{1},L_{2}:C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}\mapsto C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, and
a>0
,

\displaystyle L_{1}=L_{2},\text{ up to $O(\varepsilon^{a})$},

(8.58)

if there exists
C,\mu_{0}>0
such that
\rVert(L_{1}-L_{2})[h]\rVert_{{s_{0}}}\leq C\varepsilon^{a}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}
, for all
h\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
.

Arguing as in the proofs of Lemma
8.16
and Lemma
8.17
, we see that the terms of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
depend only on
H_{2,2},H_{3,2}
and
H_{4,2}
in
2
of Proposition
4.4
, that is, (for each fixed
\omega
)

\nabla_{z}^{2}H(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))=\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{2,2}(\overline{v})+\varepsilon\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{3,2}(\bar{v})+\varepsilon^{2}\nabla_{z}^{2}\mathcal{H}_{4,2}(\bar{v}),\text{ up to $O(\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I_{\delta}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}})=O(\varepsilon^{7-4b}$)}

Furthermore, (
8.35
) in Lemma
8.13
says that (note that
|\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}|=O(\varepsilon^{6-4b})
thanks to Remark
7.5
),

K_{02}=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(U_{\zeta}(i_{\delta}))\text{ up to $O(\varepsilon^{7-4b})$.}

Therefore, we have that (for each fixed
\omega
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\partial_{x}K_{02}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\left(\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{2,2}(\bar{v})+\varepsilon\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{3,2}(\bar{v})+\varepsilon^{2}\partial_{x}\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{4,2}(\bar{v})\right),\text{ up to $O(\varepsilon^{7-4b})$},

(8.59)

where
\overline{v}
is defined in (
8.4
).

9
Symplectic transformations

In Section
10
, we will conjugate the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
with reversible transformations
\Phi
to reduce each coefficient of
M
to a constant.
Before we start the conjugations, we study useful properties of the transformations that we will use throughout the section.

Definition 9.1
.

(1)

We say that
\Phi[h]
is type (1) if it is a time-
1
flow map determined by a Hamiltonian PDE in
H_{S^{\perp}}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}(b(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}u),\text{ where $b(t)=b(t,\omega,\varphi,x):=\frac{\beta(\omega,\varphi,x)}{1+t\beta_{x}(\omega,\varphi,x)}$, for some $\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})$,}

(9.1)

where
\beta
is real-valued and
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
is defined in Definition
8.2
.

(2)

We say that
\Phi[h]
is type (2) if it is a time-
1
flow map determined by a Hamiltonian PDE in
H_{S^{\perp}}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Pi_{S^{\perp}}u,\text{ for some real-valued symbol $\mathfrak{a}$.}

(9.2)

(3)

We say that
\Phi[h]
is type (3) if

\displaystyle\Phi[h]=h(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi),x),\text{ for some real-valued function $p_{1}(\varphi)=p_{1}(\omega,\varphi)$, independent of $x$. }

(9.3)

(4)

We say that
\Phi[h]
is type (4) if
\Phi[h]=\sum_{j\neq 0}h_{j}(\varphi)e^{\text{i}j(x+p_{2}(\varphi,j))}
for some real-valued symbol
p_{2}(\varphi,\xi)=p_{2}(\omega,\varphi,\xi)
that depends on
i_{0}
and
\omega
but not on
x
.

Note that all of the transformations above are symplectic, more precisely, they satisfy (
2.128
), since each of them is generated by a Hamiltonian PDE. For type (3) and (4), we have more explicit expressions for the transformations. In the rest of this subsection, we will study useful properties of the transformations of types (1) and (2), which will be used in Section
10
.

9.1
Properties of the flow type (1)

We study the flow map
\Phi(t)
determined by the PDE in (
9.1
). We first recall some properties of
\Psi(t)
, which is the flow of the PDE:

\displaystyle u_{t}=\partial_{x}(b(t)u)=:\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)u,

(9.4)

where
b(t)
is as described in (
9.1
). We recall that
\Psi(t)
has an explicit form:

\displaystyle\Psi(t)h(\varphi,x)=(1+t\beta_{x})h(\varphi,x+t\beta(\varphi,x)).

(9.5)

9.1.0.1
Invertibility

Since
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, it follows from
(1)
in Lemma
8.3
that

\displaystyle\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right).

(9.6)

Specifically, Remark
7.5
implies that
\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon
. Therefore, Lemma
2.4
tells us that there exists
\tilde{\beta}(t)=\tilde{\beta}(t,\varphi,x)
such that

\displaystyle B^{-1}(\varphi,x)=x+\tilde{\beta}(t,\varphi,x),\text{ where }B(\varphi,x):=x+t\beta(\varphi,x),

(9.7)

with the estimate

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{\beta(t)}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},\text{ for some $\mu_{0}\geq 0$, possibly larger than $\mu_{0}$ in above}.

(9.8)

Using (
9.6
), we have

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{\beta}(t)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),\text{ for some $\mu_{0}\geq 0$, possibly larger than $\mu_{0}$ in above}.

(9.9)

The invertibility of
\Psi(t)
follows immediately from the following result:

Lemma 9.2
.

[
8
, Section 8.1]

If
\rVert\beta\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{\nu+1})}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, then the map
\Psi(t)
in (
9.5
) is invertible and

\Psi(\tau)^{-1}h(\varphi,x)=(1+\tilde{\beta}_{x}(t,\varphi,x))h(\varphi,x+\tilde{\beta}(t,{\varphi,x}),\text{ where $\tilde{\beta}$ is as in \eqref{ftsdsd2}.}

Lemma 9.3
.

We have that
\tilde{\beta}(t)\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, for each
t\in[0,1]
.

Proof.

Note that a fixed
t
does not play a role in the proof, therefore in what follows, we omit it in the notations.

The lemma can be proven simply expanding
\tilde{\beta}
by Taylor series. Indeed, (
9.7
) implies that

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x)=-\beta(x+\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x)),

(9.10)

hence, using (
2.26
), we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x)=-\underbrace{\beta(\varphi,x)}_{=:A_{1}}-\underbrace{\beta_{x}(\varphi,x)\tilde{\beta}({\varphi,x})}_{=:A_{2}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\beta_{xx}(\varphi,x)(\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x))^{2}}_{=:A_{3}}-\underbrace{(\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x))^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\beta_{xxx}(\varphi,x+s\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x))\frac{(1-s)^{2}}{2!}ds}_{=:A_{4}}.

(9.11)

Using that
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, let us write

\displaystyle\beta=\varepsilon a_{1,\beta}+\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\beta}+\varepsilon^{3}a_{3,\beta}+q_{\beta},

(9.12)

where
a_{i,\beta}
are of the form in (
8.12
) and
q_{\beta}
satisfies the estimates (
8.13
) and (
8.14
). We look for an expansion of
\tilde{\beta}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}=\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}+\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\tilde{\beta}}+\varepsilon^{3}a_{3,\tilde{\beta}}+q_{\tilde{\beta}},

(9.13)

such that
a_{i,\tilde{\beta}}
are of the form in (
8.12
) and
q_{\tilde{\beta}}
satisfies the estimates (
8.13
) and (
8.14
). To simplify the notations, we write for a quantity
Q
,

\displaystyle Q=O(\varepsilon^{k}),\text{ for $k\geq 0$, if $\rVert Q\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{k}(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}})$ for some $\mu_{0}>0$}.

(9.14)

Note that thanks to Lemma
2.2
and (
9.9
), the term of size
O(\varepsilon)
in (
9.11
) is from
A_{1}
, especially
a_{1,\beta}
in (
9.12
) thus

\displaystyle\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}:=-\varepsilon a_{1,\beta},

(9.15)

so that

\tilde{\beta}-\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}=O(\varepsilon^{2}).

Again, we see the terms of size
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in (
9.11
). Defining

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\tilde{\beta}}:=-\varepsilon^{2}\left(a_{2,\beta}+(a_{1,\beta})_{x}a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}\right),

(9.16)

we see that
\tilde{\beta}-\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}-\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\tilde{\beta}}=O(\varepsilon^{3})
.
Similarly, for
a_{3,\tilde{\beta}}
defined as

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{3}a_{3,\tilde{\beta}}:=-\varepsilon^{3}\left(a_{3,\beta}+((a_{2,\beta})_{x}a_{1,\tilde{\beta}})+(a_{1,\beta})_{x}(a_{2,\tilde{\beta}})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{1,\beta})_{xx}(a_{1,\tilde{\beta}})^{2}\right),

(9.17)

we get (using the fact that
q_{\beta}
satisfies the estimates (
8.13
) and (
8.14
)),

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{\beta}-\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}-\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\tilde{\beta}}-\varepsilon^{3}a_{3,\tilde{\beta}}+q_{\beta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}=O(\varepsilon^{4})

(9.18)

Therefore, writing

\displaystyle q_{\tilde{\beta}}:=-q_{\beta}+(\tilde{\beta}-\varepsilon a_{1,\tilde{\beta}}-\varepsilon^{2}a_{2,\tilde{\beta}}-\varepsilon^{3}a_{3,\tilde{\beta}}+q_{\beta}),

(9.19)

we have that (
9.15
), (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) imply that each
a_{i,\tilde{\beta}}
are of the form in (
8.12
) and (
9.18
) implies that (recalling the notation in (
9.14
))
q_{\tilde{\beta}}
satisfies

\rVert q_{\tilde{\beta}}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{3}+\varepsilon\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},\quad\rVert M_{x}(q_{\tilde{\beta}})-M_{\varphi,x}(q_{\tilde{\beta}})\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}.

To finish the proof that
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, it suffices to prove the estimates in (
8.13
) and (
8.14
) for
d_{i}q_{\tilde{\beta}}
. Since
d_{i}q_{\beta}
satisfies such estimates, and
a_{i,\tilde{\beta}}
are independent of
i_{0}
, we see from (
9.19
) that it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\tilde{\beta}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

(9.20)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}(M_{x}(\tilde{\beta})-M_{\varphi,x}(\tilde{\beta})(i_{0}))[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

Note that the fact that
a_{i,\beta}
is independent of
i_{0}
implies that
\beta
satisfies the same estimates in (
9.20
), for
\tilde{\beta}
, replaced by
\beta
.
Towards the proof, we denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}[h](\varphi,x):=h(\varphi,x+\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x)),\text{ for $h\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}$},

(9.21)

so that (
9.7
) (neglecting
t
, and denoting the dependence on
i_{0}
) implies

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})=-\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[\beta(i_{0})],\text{ and }\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[\beta_{x}(i_{0})]=-\frac{\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})}{1+\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})}.

(9.22)

Since
d_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\beta}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}])[h](\varphi,x)=d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}](\varphi,x)\mathcal{A}_{\beta}(i_{0})[h_{x}](\varphi,x)
, which can be seen easily from (
9.21
), it follows from (
9.22
) that

d_{i}\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=-d_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}])[\beta]-\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]]=-d_{i}\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[\beta_{x}(i_{0})]-\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(i_{0})[d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]].

Therefore, we get

d_{i}\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]=-\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}[d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]]}{1+\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}(i_{0})}[\beta_{x}(i_{0})]}.

Since
\frac{1}{1+\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{\beta}}(\beta_{x})}-1=O(\varepsilon^{2})
, and
d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]
satisfies (
9.20
), we have that
\tilde{\beta}
satisfies (
9.20
) as well.
∎

9.1.0.2
Adjoint operator

Now, we study the adjoint operators
\Psi(t)^{T}
and
\left(\Psi(t)^{-1}\right)^{T}
. From the explicit expressions of
\Psi(t),\Psi(t)^{-1}
in (
9.5
) and Lemma
9.2
, it follows straightforwardly from the change of variables that (see
[
8
, Section 8.1]
),

\displaystyle\Psi(t)^{T}h(\varphi,x)=h(\varphi,x+\tilde{\beta}(t,\varphi,x)),\quad\left(\Psi(t)^{-1}\right)^{T}h(\varphi,x)=h(\varphi,x+t{\beta}(\varphi,x)).

(9.23)

9.1.0.3
Expansion of
\Psi(t)

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
\psi(t)\in\left\{\Psi(t),\Psi(t)^{T},\Psi(t)^{-1},\left(\Psi(t)^{T}\right)^{-1}\right\}
. For each
\psi(t)
, there exist a symbol
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{1}
(see definition
8.8
) and an operator
R_{\psi}(t)
such that
