\displaystyle\psi(t)=I+\varepsilon\tau Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})+R_{\psi}(\tau),

(9.24)

where
\mathcal{R}(t)\in\left\{R_{\Psi}(t),R_{\Psi^{*}}(t),R_{\Psi^{-1}}(t),R_{\left(\Psi^{-1}\right)^{T}}(t)\right\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\rVert\mathcal{R}(t)h\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\right),

(9.25)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\rVert d_{i}\mathcal{R}(t)(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]h\rVert_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}.

(9.26)

Proof.

We will prove the case where
\psi(t)=\Psi(t)
only. The proof is based on the explicit expression of
\Psi(t)
in (
9.5
) and the fact that
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
. The other operators
\Psi(t)^{-1},\Psi(t)^{T}
and
\left(\Psi(t)^{T}\right)^{-1}
can be proved in the same way using the expressions in (
9.23
), Lemma
9.2
and Lemma
9.3
.

To show (
9.25
), using the Taylor expansion in (
2.26
), we write (see (
9.5
))

\displaystyle\Psi(t)h

\displaystyle=h(\varphi,x+t\beta(\varphi,x))+t\beta_{x}(\varphi,x)h(\varphi,x+t\beta(\varphi,x))

\displaystyle=h(\varphi,x)+t\beta h_{x}(\varphi,x)+(t\beta)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}h_{xx}(\varphi,x+st\beta(\varphi,x))(1-s)ds

\displaystyle\ +t\beta_{x}\left(h(\varphi,x)+t\beta\int_{0}^{1}h_{x}(\varphi,x+st\beta(\varphi,x))ds\right)

\displaystyle=:h(\varphi,x)+t\partial_{x}(\beta h)+R_{\Psi,1}(t)[h],

(9.27)

where

\displaystyle R_{\Psi,1}(t)[h]:=(t\beta)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}h_{xx}(\varphi,x+st\beta(\varphi,x))(1-s)ds+t^{2}\beta_{x}\beta\int_{0}^{1}h_{x}(\varphi,x+st\beta(\varphi,x))ds.

(9.28)

Furthermore, using that
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, we have an expansion

\beta=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}a_{i}+q,

where
a_{i},q
are as described in Definition
8.2
. Hence, we can write from (
9.27
) that

\displaystyle\Psi(t)h=h(\varphi,x)+t\varepsilon\partial_{x}(a_{1}h)+\underbrace{t\partial_{x}\left(\left(\sum_{i=2}^{3}\varepsilon^{i}a_{i}+q\right)h\right)}_{=:R_{\Psi,2}(t)[h]}+R_{\Psi,1}(t)[h].

(9.29)

Since
a_{1}
is of the form in (
8.12
) for
i=1
, we can find
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{1}
such that
\partial_{x}(a_{1}h)=:Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})h
, therefore we have

\Psi(t)=I+\varepsilon tOp^{W}(\mathfrak{b})+R_{\Psi}(t),\quad R_{\Psi}(t):=R_{\Psi,1}(t)+R_{\Psi,2}(t).

Now, it suffices to show that
R_{\Psi}
satisfies the estimates in (
9.25
) and (
9.26
).
For
R_{\Psi,1}
defined in (
9.28
), using (
2.2
) and
(3)
of Lemma
2.4
, we have that for some
\mu_{0}\geq 0
,

\rVert R_{\Psi,1}(t)[h]\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\left(\rVert\beta\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)^{2}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}.

Using (
9.6
) and (
7.3
), we obtain that
R_{\Psi,1}
satisfies (
9.25
). To show (
9.26
) for
R_{\Psi,1}
, we differentiate (
9.28
) in
i_{0}
to see that

\rVert d_{i}(R_{\Psi}(t)(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}])h\rVert_{s_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert d_{i}\beta(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert\beta\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

where the last inequality follows from
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
,
which proves (
9.26
) (with actually a higher power on
\varepsilon
).
For
R_{\Psi,2}
in (
9.29
), we use that
q
satisfies the estimates in (
8.13
) and
a_{i}
is of the form in (
8.12
), then the result follows straightforwardly.

∎

9.1.0.4
Finite dimensional operators

Lemma 9.5
.

[
52
, Lemma A.4]

Let
\Phi(t),\Psi(t)
be the flow maps determined by the PDEs in (
9.1
) and (
9.4
), respectively. Let
R(t):=\Phi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
. Then
R(t)
is a finite dimensional operator, more precisely,
R(t)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
(see Definition
8.31
).

Proof.

As stated in
[
52
, Lemma A.4]
,
R(t):=\Phi(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
is a finite dimensional operator. We aim to show that
R(t)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, following the strategy of
[
55
, Lemma C.1]
. We will show
R(1)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, since one can straightforwardly show that
R(1)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
implies
R(t)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, by reparametrizing the variable
t
.

We define a operator
U(t)
by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}U(t)=-(\Psi(\tau))^{-1}\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Pi_{S}\Psi(t)U(t),\quad U(0)=I,\text{ where $\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)$ is as in \eqref{psei_type1}}.

(9.30)

One can easily check that
\Phi(t)=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)U(t).
Indeed, the left hand side,
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)U(t)
, solves

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)U(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\Psi(t)\right)U(t)+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(\Psi(t))\left(\frac{d}{dt}U(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\left(\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Psi(t)-\Psi(t)\Psi(t)^{-1}\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Pi_{S}\Psi(t)U(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)U(t),

which solves the same equation (
9.1
) as
\Phi(t)
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}+{\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(t)(U(t)-I)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}}.

(9.31)

Therefore, it suffices to show that
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)(U(1)-I)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
. Towards the proof, let us denote
Z(t):=\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Pi_{S}\Psi(t)
, so that the equation for
U(t)
in (
9.30
) can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}U(t)=-(\Psi(t))^{-1}Z(t)U(t).

(9.32)

For the operator
Z(t)
, we have that

\displaystyle Z(t)u=\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\Pi_{S}\Psi(t)u=\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)\left(\sum_{j\in S}(\Psi(t)u,e^{\text{i}jx})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}e^{\text{i}jx}\right)=\sum_{j\in S}(u,\underbrace{\Psi(t)^{*}[e^{\text{i}jx}]}_{=:g_{j}(t)})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\underbrace{\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)[e^{\text{i}jx}]}_{=:\chi_{j}(t)}.

(9.33)

Now, we write
U(t)
as (for
u\in H_{S^{\perp}}
),

\displaystyle U(t)u-u

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Useqxxzz}}{=}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d}{d\tau}U(\tau)ud\tau=\int_{0}^{t}-(\Psi(\tau))^{-1}Z(\tau)U(\tau)ud\tau

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{psisd2sdsd2}}{=}-\sum_{j\in S}\int_{0}^{t}(U(\tau)u,g_{j}(\tau))_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}(\Psi(\tau))^{-1}[\chi_{j}(\tau)]d\tau

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j\in S}\int_{0}^{t}(u,{U(\tau)^{T}g_{j}(\tau)})_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}{(\Psi(\tau))^{-1}[\chi_{j}(\tau)]}d\tau.

Therefore, we obtain (using
g_{j}
and
\chi_{j}
in (
9.33
) and
u\in H_{S^{\perp}}
),

\displaystyle\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)(U(1)-I)u

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}\int_{0}^{1}(u,\tilde{g}_{j}(t))\tilde{\chi}_{j}(t)dt,

(9.34)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{j}(t):=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}U(t)^{T}\Psi(t)^{*}[e^{\text{i}jx}],

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{j}(t):=-\Psi(1)\Psi(t)^{-1}\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)[e^{\text{i}jx}],\quad\text{ for $j\in S$}.

To finish the proof, we need to estimate
\tilde{g}_{j},\tilde{\chi}_{j}
. We need the following lemma.

Lemma 9.6
.

\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t),U(t)^{T}
have expansions as in (
9.24
). That is, there exist symbols
\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{B}},\mathfrak{b}_{U}\in\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{1}
and operators
R_{\mathcal{B}}(t),R_{U}(t)
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{\mathcal{B}})+R_{\mathcal{B}}(t),

\displaystyle U(t)^{T}

\displaystyle=I+\varepsilon tOp^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{U})\Pi_{S}+R_{U}(t),

and
R_{\mathcal{B}}(t),R_{U}(t)
satisfy the estimates in (
9.25
) and (
9.26
).

Proof.

The results can be proved in the same way as in the proof of Lemma
9.4
, using the Taylor expansions of the operators
\mathcal{B}(t)
and
U(t)^{T}
.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
9.4
and Lemma
9.6
, we have
\mathfrak{b}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}^{1}_{1}
, operators
R_{g}(\tau),\ R_{\chi}(\tau)
and functions
f_{1}(t),f_{2}(t)
that depend on
t
only such that such that

\displaystyle U(t)^{T}\Psi(t)^{*}[e^{\text{i}jx}]

\displaystyle=e^{\text{i}jx}+\varepsilon f_{1}(t)Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})[e^{\text{i}jx}]+R_{g}(\tau)[e^{\text{i}jx}],

\displaystyle-\Psi(1)\Psi(t)^{-1}\partial_{x}\mathcal{B}(t)[e^{\text{i}jx}]

\displaystyle=e^{\text{i}jx}+\varepsilon f_{2}(t)Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{2})[e^{\text{i}jx}]+R_{\chi}(\tau)[e^{\text{i}jx}],

where
R_{g},R_{\chi}
satisfy the estimates (
9.25
) and (
9.26
). Plugging this into (
9.34
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)(U(1)-I)u

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\underbrace{\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}(u,Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{2})[e^{\text{i}jx}]\left(\int_{0}^{1}f_{1}(t)f_{2}(t)dt\right)}_{=:\mathcal{R}_{1}[u]}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}\int_{0}^{1}(u,\varepsilon f_{1}(t)Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{1})[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}R_{\chi}(t)[e^{\text{i}jx}]dt}_{\mathcal{R}_{2,1}[u]}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\sum_{j\in S}\int_{0}^{1}(u,R_{g}(\tau)[e^{\text{i}jx}])_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\left(\varepsilon f_{2}(t)Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b}_{2}))+R_{\chi}(t)\right)[e^{\text{i}jx}]dt}_{=:\mathcal{R}_{2,2}[u]}.

Using that
R_{g},R_{\chi}
satisfy the estimates (
9.25
) and (
9.26
), it follows straightforwardly that
\mathcal{R}_{2,1},\mathcal{R}_{2,2}
are of the form (
8.32
) with the estimates (
8.33
). We also have
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is of the form (
8.31
). Therefore,
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)(U(1)-I)
in (
9.31
) satisfies
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)(U(1)-I)\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

If
\beta(-\varphi,-x)=-\beta(\varphi,x)
, then
\Phi(t)
is a reversibility preserving operator.

Proof.

From
b(t)
in (
9.1
), it is clear that
b(t)(-\varphi,-x)=-b(t)(\varphi,x)
for each
t
. In the equation (
9.1
), this implies that each space
X,Y
is invariant under the vector field

u\mapsto\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}(b(t)\Pi_{S}^{\perp}u).

Therefore, the flow map is reversibility preserving.
∎

9.2
Properties of the flow type (2)

We study the flow map
\Phi(t)
determined by the PDE in (
9.2
). We first recall some properties of
\Psi(t)
, which is the flow of the PDE:

\displaystyle u_{t}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})u=:\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}u,\text{ for some real-valued symbol $\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}$, for some $m<0$,},

(9.35)

that is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Psi(\tau)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}\Psi(t),\quad\Psi(0)=I.

(9.36)

Since the symbol
\mathfrak{a}
is independent of
t
, the equation (
9.36
) is autonomous, therefore, we have

\displaystyle\Psi(t)^{-1}=\Psi(-t),\quad\Psi(t)\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}=\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}\Psi(t),\quad\Psi(t_{1}+t_{2})=\Psi(t_{1})\Psi(t_{2}).

(9.37)

Again, (
2.16
) tells us that
\mathcal{A}
is symmetric. In the following proposition, we collect tame estimates of the flow map
\Psi(t)
. The proof can be done following the same strategy in
[
16
, Appendix A]
word
by word.

Proposition 9.8
.

[
16
, Appendix A]

Let us fix the constants
\mathtt{b}_{0},\mathtt{S},k
so that

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}_{0}\geq 0,\ \mathtt{S}\in(s_{0},\infty),\ |k|\leq\mathtt{b}_{0}.

(9.38)

We denote

\Psi_{k}(\tau):=|D|^{k}\Psi(\tau)|D|^{-k}.

There exist
\mu_{0}=\mu_{0}(\mathtt{p},\mathtt{b}_{0}),\eta_{0}=\eta_{0}(\mathtt{b}_{0})
and
\delta=\delta(\mathtt{S})>0
, such that if,

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s_{0}+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\leq\delta,

(9.39)

the following hold:

(1)

For all
s,\mathtt{b}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s\in[0,\mathtt{S}]
and
0\leq\mathtt{b}\leq s_{0}+\mathtt{b}_{0}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}(\Psi_{k}(\tau))|D|^{-\mathtt{b}(1+m)}h\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}},

(9.40)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert|D|^{-\mathtt{b}(1+m)}\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}(\Psi_{k}(\tau))h\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|\mathfrak{a}|_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}}

(9.41)

(2)

Given
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
, we denote

\Delta_{12}A(\omega):=\frac{{A(\omega_{1})-A(\omega_{2})}}{|\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}|},

for an
\omega
-dependent object
A
(symbols, operators,… etc). For all
s,\mathtt{b}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s\in[0,\mathtt{S}]
and
0\leq\mathtt{b}\leq s_{0}+\mathtt{b}_{0}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\gamma\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega,\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}(\Delta_{12}\Psi_{k}(\tau))|D|^{-(1+\mathtt{b})(1+m)}h\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}},

(9.42)

\displaystyle\gamma\sup_{\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega,\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert|D|^{-(1+\mathtt{b})(1+m)}\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}(\Delta_{12}\Psi(\tau))h\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega)}_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}},

(9.43)

(3)

In case
\mathfrak{a}
depends on embeddings
i
, we have that for all
s,\mathtt{b}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
s\in[0,\mathtt{S}]
and
0\leq\mathtt{b}\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}s_{0}+\mathtt{b}_{0}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}\left(d_{i}\Psi_{k}(\tau)(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\right)|D|^{-(1+b)(1+m)}\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}|d_{i}\mathfrak{a}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s_{0}+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|d_{i}\mathfrak{a}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}}

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert|D|^{-(1+b)(1+m)}\partial_{\varphi_{j}}^{\mathtt{b}}\left(d_{i}\Psi_{k}(\tau)(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\right)\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{s,\mathtt{b}_{0},m}|d_{i}\mathfrak{a}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s_{0}+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s}+|d_{i}\mathfrak{a}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s+\mu_{0},\eta_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}}.

Lemma 9.9
.

Under the assumption (
9.39
), the operators
\Psi(\tau)^{-1},\Psi(\tau)^{T},\left(\Psi(\tau)^{-1}\right)^{T}
satisfy the same estimates as in Proposition
9.8
.

Proof.

Since the evolution equation (
9.35
) is autonomous,
\Psi(-\tau)=\Psi(\tau)^{-1}
solves the same equation (
9.35
) with
\mathcal{A}\to-\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, Propsition
9.8
applies to
\Psi(\tau)^{-1}
. For
\Psi(\tau)^{T}
, it follows from (
2.128
) that
\Psi(\tau)^{T}=\partial_{x}^{-1}\Psi(\tau)^{-1}\partial_{x}
. Therefore, using that
\Psi(\tau)^{-1}
satisfies the estimates in the proposition, it follows straightforwardly, noticing that
|D|^{-1}\partial_{x},\ |D|\partial_{x}^{-1}
are isomorphisms in between
H^{s}_{x}
.
∎

9.2.0.1
Finite dimensional operator

As in Lemma
9.5
, we will study the smoothing effect of the operator
\Phi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
.

Lemma 9.10
.

\Phi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
is a finite dimensional operator. More precisely, if the symbol
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{S}^{m}
in (
9.35
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}=\varepsilon\mathfrak{b}_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{2}+\mathfrak{q},

(9.44)

for some
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{b}_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{2}
(see Definition
8.8
) and
\mathfrak{q}
such that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,s,0}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\varepsilon^{5}+\varepsilon^{3}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),

(9.45)

\displaystyle|d_{i}\mathfrak{q}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s,0}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{3}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

Then,
\Phi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi(1)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

Note that assuming the expansion of
\mathfrak{a}
in (
9.44
), the transformation
\Psi(\tau)
admits the same expansion as in Lemma
9.4
. Then, the proof of the lemma can be proved by following the same argument word by word as in the proof of Lemma
9.5
.
∎

9.2.0.2
Approximate solutions of a Heisenberg equation

Let
m:=1-\alpha<0
(see (
9.35
) for
m
) and let us consider a real-valued symbol
\mathfrak{p}(x,\xi)=\mathfrak{p}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}}
for some
m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
P(\tau
), defined as a solution to a Heisenberg equation:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}P(\tau)=[P(\tau),\mathcal{A}]_{x},\quad P(0)=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{p}),\text{ where $[A,B]_{x}:=A\partial_{x}B-B\partial_{x}A$.}

(9.46)

In the rest of this subsection, we will aim to find an approximate solution to the equation (
9.46
), using the argument introduced in
[
16
]
. In the following proposition, we will use the operator
\star
, defined in (
2.32
).

Proposition 9.11
.

[
16
, Lemma 6.15, 6.16]

Let
P(\tau)
be a solution to the Heisenberg equation (
9.46
) and let
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Then we have an expansion
P(\tau)=Q(\tau)+W(\tau)
such that

(1)

Q(\tau)
is a pseudo differential operator with a symbol
\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau)
, that is
Q(\tau)=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau))
. The symbol
\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{n!}\tau^{n}\mathfrak{q}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{q}_{0}:=\mathfrak{p},\quad\mathfrak{q}_{n}:=\mathfrak{q}_{n-1}\star\mathfrak{a},\text{ for $n=1,\ldots,\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}$.}

(9.47)

(2)

For
W(\tau)
, we have

\displaystyle W(\tau)=\frac{1}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Psi(\tau-t)^{T}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi(\tau-t)t^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}dt,

(9.48)

Proof.

For a fixed constant
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
, we see that the sequence of symbols
\mathfrak{q}_{n}
defined in (
9.47
) satisfies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{n}\in\mathcal{S}^{m^{\prime}+n(1-\alpha)}.

(9.49)

Indeed, (
9.49
) follows from
(3)
in Lemma
2.15
.
Now we derive the expression for
W(\tau)=P(\tau)-Q(\tau)
.

We first observe that the symbol
\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau)
satisfies (from (
9.47
)),

\frac{d}{d\tau}\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau)=\sum_{n=1}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\mathfrak{q}_{n}\frac{\tau^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=\sum_{n=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}-1}\left(\mathfrak{q}_{n}\star\mathfrak{a}\right)\frac{\tau^{n}}{n!}=\mathfrak{q}_{\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}(\tau)\star\mathfrak{a}-\frac{\tau^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a}

Hence,
Q(\tau)
solves

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}Q(\tau)=[Q(\tau),Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})]_{x}-\frac{\tau^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a}),\quad Q(0)=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{p}).

(9.50)

Therefore, using (
9.46
), we see that
W(\tau):=P(\tau)-Q(\tau)
solves

\frac{d}{d\tau}W(\tau)=[W(\tau),\mathcal{A}]_{x}+\frac{\tau^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a}),\quad W(0)=0.

Now, let us denote
V(\tau):=\Psi(\tau)\partial_{x}W(\tau)\Psi(-\tau)
. Then, using (
9.35
) and (
9.37
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}V(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\Psi(\tau)\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi(-\tau),\quad V(0)=0.

Integrating in
\tau
, we obtain

\partial_{x}W(\tau)=\frac{1}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Psi(t-\tau)\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi(\tau-t)t^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}dt.

Using that
\Psi(\tau)
is symplectic and (
2.128
) holds, we obtain

W(\tau)=\frac{1}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\int_{0}^{\tau}\partial_{x}^{-1}\Psi(t-\tau)\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi(\tau-t)t^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}dt=\frac{1}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Psi(\tau-t)^{T}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi(\tau-t)t^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}dt.

Therefore, we obtain the desired result (
9.48
).
∎

10
Reduction to a constant coefficients operator

In this section, we aim to reduce the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in Proposition
8.18
to a constant coefficients operator to find its inverse, that is, we will prove Proposition
7.14
in Subsection
10.7
. Throughout Subsections
10.1
-
10.5
, we will conjugate
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
to a constant coefficients operator up to a Lip-
0
-modulo tame operator, using symplectic transformations that are reversibility preserving and
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving (See Proposition
10.76
). In Subsection
10.6
, we will restrict the operator to
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant functions (the functions whose Fourier modes are supported only on the multiples of
\mathtt{M}
), and proceed to obtain the full reduction.

10.1
Change of the space variables

The goal of this section is to make the coefficient of the highest order term
\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}
in (
8.50
) independent of the variable
x
. The result of this section is summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 10.1
.

There exists a linear transformation
\Phi_{1}:H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{1}[h]

\displaystyle:=(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}[h]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}[h]+R_{1}[h],

(10.1)

\displaystyle M_{1}[h]

\displaystyle:={b}_{1}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}h+b_{2}h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{{b}_{3}}h,

satisfies the following:

(1)

{b}_{1}
is independent of
x
and there exist constants
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}(\omega),\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}(\omega)
and
\tilde{q}_{\alpha}
, such that

\displaystyle b_{1}(\varphi)=-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}+\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}+\tilde{q}_{\alpha},\quad\text{ with }\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\tilde{q}_{\alpha}(\omega,\varphi)d\varphi=0,

(10.2)

and

\displaystyle|\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}1,

(10.3)

\displaystyle|\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{7-4b},\quad|d_{i}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}|_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{q}_{\alpha}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},\quad\rVert d_{i}\tilde{q}_{\alpha}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(2)

{b}_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
and
{b}_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
and

\displaystyle b_{1}(-\varphi)=b_{1}(\varphi),\quad b_{2}(-\varphi,-x)=b_{2}(\varphi,x),\quad b_{3}(-\varphi,-x,-y)=b_{3}(\varphi,x,y).

(10.4)

(3)

R_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

(4)

\Phi_{1}
is real, reversibility preserving and and symplectic. Therefore,
\mathcal{L}^{1}
is a reversible and Hamiltonian.

(5)

\mathcal{L}^{1}
and
\Phi_{1}
are
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving and

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(b_{2})=b_{2},\quad\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(b_{3})=b_{3}.

(10.5)

Proof.

The proof will be completed using the lemmas presented in the rest of the subsection. The expression (
10.1
) and the item
(1)
will follow from Lemma
10.2
(note that
\partial_{x}(b_{1})=0
in (
10.12
), since
b_{1}
does not depend on
x
) with the estimates for
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1},\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2},\tilde{q}_{\alpha}
given in Lemma
10.3
. The items
(2)
-
(4)
will follow from Lemma
10.3
, Lemma
10.6
, Lemma
10.7
, Lemma
10.8
and Lemma
10.9
. Lastly, if
i_{0}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, then
(4)
of Proposition
8.18
tells us that our definition of
\beta
in (
10.25
) is also
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant. Hence, it follows from Lemma
2.53
that the flow map
\Phi_{1}
defined by (
10.6
) is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving, since the PDE in (
10.6
) is a Hamiltonian PDE associated to
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant Hamiltonian:
u\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}(b(\tau,\varphi,x)u(x))dx
. Using (
8.53
), and (
10.13
), one can easily see that (
10.5
) holds true.
∎

In order to find a symplectic transformation in Proposition
10.1
, we will use the transformation of type (1), described in Definition
9.1
. Let
\Phi_{1}
be the time-
1
flow map of the PDE:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}(b(\tau,\varphi,x)u),

(10.6)

\displaystyle b(\tau,\varphi,x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\beta(\varphi,x)}{1+\tau\beta_{x}(\varphi,x)},\text{ for some $\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})$ to be chosen later (see Lemma~\ref{beta_choice}),}

and let
\Psi_{1}
be the time-
1
flow map of the PDE:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u=\partial_{x}(b(\tau,\varphi,x)u).

(10.7)

Our analysis in this section is based on the properties of
\Phi_{\tau},\Psi_{\tau}
, studied in Subsection
9.1
.

First of all, it follows from (
9.5
) that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}h(\varphi,x):=(1+\beta_{x}(\varphi,x))h(\varphi,x+\beta(\varphi,x))=:\partial_{x}B(\varphi,x)h\circ B(\varphi,x),

(10.8)

where
B(\varphi,x)=x+\beta(\varphi,x)
. Since
\Psi_{1}
is a symplectic transformation in
L^{2}_{0}
(see
[
6
, Remark 3.3]
), it holds that
\sigma(\Psi_{1}[f],\Psi_{1}[g])=\sigma(f,g)
for all
f,g\in L^{2}_{0}
(see (
2.127
) for the definition of
\sigma
), therefore,

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\Psi_{1}^{T}=\partial_{x}.

(10.9)

For
\Psi_{1}^{-1},\Psi_{1}^{T}
, we use Lemma
9.2
and (
9.23
) to see that

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{-1}[h]=(1+\tilde{\beta}_{x}(\varphi,x))h(x+\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x))=\partial_{x}\left(B^{-1}\right)h\circ B^{-1},\quad\Psi^{T}h=h\circ B^{-1},

(10.10)

where
B^{-1}:x\mapsto x+\tilde{\beta}(\varphi,x)
is the inverse of
B:x\mapsto x+\beta(\varphi,x)
.
To compute the conjugation of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
with
\Phi_{1}
, we denote, for a given
a=a(x,y)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,

\displaystyle B_{2}[a](x,y):=a(B(x),B(y)),\quad B_{2}^{-1}[a](x,y):=a(B^{-1}(x),B^{-1}(y)),\quad\tilde{a}(x):=a(x,x).

(10.11)

Now we compute the conjugation of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
with
\Phi_{1}
.

10.1.0.1
Computing the conjugation

Lemma 10.2
.

We have that

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}[h]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}[h]+R_{1}[h],

(10.12)

\displaystyle M_{1}[h]:={b}_{1}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}h+{b}_{2}h+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}({b}_{1})\partial_{x}\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{b_{3}}h,

where the new coefficients
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
are given by

\displaystyle{\displaystyle b}_{1}=\Psi_{1}^{T}\left[(\partial_{x}B)^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{2}+\tilde{a_{1}}\right)\right],

(10.13)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle b}_{2}=\Psi_{1}^{T}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}\partial_{x}B+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}\right)\partial_{x}B-\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta\right]+\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(a_{1,B}(x,y)-a_{1,B}(x,x))dy,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle b}_{3}=\left(\frac{2-2\cos(B^{-1}(x)-B^{-1}(y))}{2-2\cos(x-y)}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\times B_{2}[a_{3}](x,y)-\frac{a_{1,B}(x,y)-a_{1,B}(x,x)-\partial_{y}a_{1,B}(x,x)\sin(x-y)}{2-2\cos(x-y)},

\displaystyle a_{1,B}(x,y):=\left(\frac{2-2\cos(B^{-1}(x)-B^{-1}(y))}{2-2\cos(x-y)}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}B_{2}^{-1}[a_{1}](x,y),

with a new remainder
R_{1}
given by

\displaystyle R_{1}[h]=\Phi_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}-\mathcal{R}_{M}\right)h+\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}\Phi_{1}h,\text{ where $\mathcal{R}_{\omega},\mathcal{R}_{M}$ are defined in \eqref{conju_with_dw1} and \eqref{rmdefs1}.}

(10.14)

Proof.

From Proposition
8.18
, we have that for all
h(\varphi,x)\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, such that
h(\varphi,\cdot)\in H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}[h]=(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}[h]-(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Phi_{1}[h]+(\Phi_{1})^{-1}\mathcal{R}\Phi_{1}[h]

(10.15)

Conjugation of
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
.

Noticing that
\Phi_{1}
depends on
\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}[h]=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1})[h],
hence,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{1})+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}).

From the definition of
\Psi_{1}
in (
10.8
), it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{1})[h]=\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}h

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h+\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\left(\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Phi_{1}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1})}_{=:\mathcal{R}_{\omega}[h]}.

(10.16)

Therefore, we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h\right)+\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}[h].

(10.17)

Conjugation of
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M
.

We compute

\displaystyle\partial_{x}M\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}+\partial_{x}M\left(\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}+\partial_{x}M\left(\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)-\partial_{x}M\Pi_{S}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}.

(10.18)

Let us focus on
\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
first. From
M
in (
8.50
), we have that for
h\in H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{T}M\Psi_{1}h=\Psi_{1}^{T}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}\Psi_{1}h+\Psi_{1}^{T}\left[\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}\right)\Psi_{1}h\right]+\Psi_{1}^{T}\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a_{3}}\Psi_{1}h.

Applying Lemma
A.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}^{T}M\Psi_{1}[h]=\mathtt{a}_{1}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}h+\mathtt{a}_{2}h+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}(\mathtt{a}_{1})\partial_{x}\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{\mathtt{a}_{3}}h=:\tilde{M}[h],

(10.19)

where

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}_{1}(\varphi,x)=\Psi_{1}^{T}\left[(\partial_{x}B)^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{2}+\tilde{a_{1}}\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}_{2}(\varphi,x)=\Psi_{1}^{T}\left[\Lambda^{\alpha-1}_{-\frac{1}{2}+a_{1}}\partial_{x}B+\left(\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+a_{2}\right)\partial_{x}B\right]+\int_{\mathbb{T}}(2-2\cos(x-y))^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}(a_{1,B}(x,y)-a_{1,B}(x,x))dy,

(10.20)

\displaystyle\mathtt{a}_{3}(\varphi,x,y)=\left(\frac{2-2\cos(B^{-1}(x)-B^{-1}(y))}{2-2\cos(x-y)}\right)^{1-\frac{\alpha}{2}}B_{2}[a_{3}](x,y)-\frac{a_{1,B}(x,y)-a_{1,B}(x,x)-(\partial_{y}a_{1,B})(x,x)\sin(x-y)}{2-2\cos(x-y)},

where

a_{1,B}(x,y):=\left(\frac{2-2\cos(B^{-1}(x)-B^{-1}(y))}{2-2\cos(x-y)}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}B_{2}^{-1}[a_{1}](x,y).

Recalling (
10.9
), it follows that
\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\Psi_{1}^{T}M\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}
, where
\tilde{M}
is as in (
10.19
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}

\displaystyle\ +(\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}})\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}\tilde{M},

(10.21)

Hence, it follows from (
10.1.0.1
) that

\displaystyle\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\left(\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Pi_{S}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+(\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}})\partial_{x}\tilde{M}

\displaystyle\ +\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\left(\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Pi_{S}\Psi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=:\Phi_{1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+\mathcal{R}_{M}.

(10.22)

Then, we obtain,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M\Phi_{1}=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\tilde{M}+\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}_{M}.

(10.23)

Now, we plug the computations obtained in (
10.17
) and (
10.23
) into (
10.15
), and obtain that for
h\in H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\Phi_{1}h

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\underbrace{\left(-\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta(\varphi,\cdot)]h+\tilde{M}h\right)}_{=:M_{1}[h]}+\underbrace{\Phi_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}-\mathcal{R}_{M}\right)h+\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}\Phi_{1}h}_{=:R_{1}[h]}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}[h]+R_{1}[h].

Recalling the coefficients of
\tilde{M}
from (
10.19
), combining the coefficients
\mathtt{a}_{1},\mathtt{a}_{2},\mathtt{a_{3}}
with
-\Psi_{1}^{T}[\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\beta]
in
M_{1}
, we obtain (
10.12
).
∎

10.1.0.2
Analysis of the new coefficients
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}

We need to choose
\beta
to make
b_{1}
in (
10.12
) independent of the variable
x
(recalling
B
from (
10.8
)).

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
b_{1}
be as in Lemma
10.2
, that is,

\displaystyle b_{1}=\mathcal{A}^{T}\left[(\partial_{x}B)^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{2}+\tilde{a}_{1}\right)\right],

(10.24)

where
B,\tilde{a}_{1}
are as in (
10.8
) and (
10.11
). We have the following:

(1)

We can choose
\beta
so that

b_{1}
and
\beta
satisfy

\displaystyle\beta(\varphi,x):=(\partial_{x})^{-1}\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{a}_{1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}(-b_{1}(\varphi))^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}-1\right),\quad b_{1}(\varphi):=-\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{a}_{1}(\varphi,x)\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}dx\right)^{-\alpha}.

(10.25)

(2)

There exist
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}(\omega),\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}(\omega)
, and
\tilde{q}_{\alpha}
, such that

\displaystyle b_{1}(\varphi)=-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}+\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}+\tilde{q}_{\alpha}(\varphi),\quad\text{ with }\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\tilde{q}_{\alpha}(\omega,\varphi)d\varphi=0,

(10.26)

with the estimates,

\displaystyle|\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}1,

\displaystyle|\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon^{7-4b},\quad|d_{i}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|\leq_{\mathtt{pe}}\varepsilon\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}|_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{q}_{\alpha}\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},\quad\rVert d_{i}\tilde{q}_{\alpha}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(3)

We have
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
Furthermore, we have
b_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
and
b_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
where
b_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4},b_{3}
are as in Lemma
10.2
.

Proof.

Proof of (1).

Note that (
10.25
) can be proved in the same way as in
[
6
, Section 3]
. Indeed, taking
\left(\Psi_{1}^{T}\right)^{-1}
in (
10.24
) and recalling that
B^{\prime}=1+\beta_{x}
(see definition of
B
in (
10.8
)), we are led to find
b_{1}(\varphi)
and
\beta(\varphi,x)
such that

\displaystyle(1+\beta_{x}(\varphi,x))^{\alpha}\left(-\frac{1}{2}+\tilde{a_{1}}(\varphi,x)\right)=b_{1}(\varphi),

which is equivalent to

(1+\beta_{x})^{\alpha}=\left(\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{a}_{1}\right)^{-1}\left(-b_{1}\right)\iff\beta_{x}=\left(\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{a}_{1}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}\left(-b_{1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}}-1.

To find
\beta
, we have to take
\partial_{x}^{-1}
, which forces us to choose
b_{1}
so that the very right-hand side has zero-average. This gives us a unique choice of
b_{1}
and
\beta
as given in (
10.25
).

Proof of (2)

To see
(2)
, recall that
a_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
thanks to
(1)
of Proposition
8.18
. Recalling the definition of
\tilde{a}_{1}
in (
10.11
), it easily follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
in Definition
8.2
that
\tilde{a}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
. Now, using the Taylor expansion of
x\mapsto\left(\frac{1}{2}-x\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}
near
x=0
, it is easy to see from
b_{1}
in (
10.25
) that

(-b_{1}(\varphi))^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\tilde{a}_{1}(\varphi,x)\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}dx=\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}+M_{x}(f),\text{ for some $f\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})$}.

Again, using the Taylor expansion of
x\mapsto\left(\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\alpha}}+x\right)^{-\alpha}
and Lemma
8.4
, we obtain (
10.26
) with the desired estimates described in the lemma.

Proof of
(3)
This follows straightforwardly from the choice of
\beta
in (
10.25
), item
(2)
and the fact that
\tilde{a}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
.
∎

10.1.0.3
Analysis of the transformation
\Phi_{1}

Lemma 10.4
.

\Phi^{\pm 1}=\Phi_{1},\Phi_{1}^{-1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert(\Phi^{\pm}-I)h\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)

(10.27)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\Phi^{\pm}(i_{0})h[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}.

Proof.

Using that
\Phi_{1}=\Psi_{1}+R
on
H_{S^{\perp}}
for a finite dimensional operator
R\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, which follows from Lemma
9.5
, we see that the above estimates for
\Phi_{1}
follow from the definition of
\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
in Definition
8.11
and Lemma
9.4
. For
\Phi_{1}^{-1}
, we recall the following lemma:

Lemma 10.5
.

[
8
, Lemma 8.5]

It holds that

\displaystyle\rVert\Phi_{1}^{\pm}h\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\Phi_{1}^{\pm}(i_{0})h[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}.

With the above lemma, we differentiate (denoting
\Phi_{\tau},\Psi_{\tau}
be the time-
\tau
flows of the PDEs (
10.6
) and (
10.7
) respectively),
I=\Phi_{\tau}\circ\left(\Phi_{\tau}\right)^{-1}
in
\tau
to see that

\frac{d}{d\tau}\Phi_{\tau}^{-1}=-\Phi_{\tau}^{-1}\partial_{x}b(\tau)\Phi_{\tau}^{-1},\quad\Phi_{0}^{-1}=I.

Using the Taylor expansion near
\tau=0
, we have

\Phi_{\tau}^{-1}=I-\int_{0}^{\tau}\Phi_{s}^{-1}\partial_{x}b(\tau)\Phi_{s}^{-1}ds,

therefore,

\displaystyle\rVert\left(\Phi_{1}^{-1}-I\right)h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq\sup_{\tau\in[0,1]}\rVert\Phi_{\tau}^{-1}\partial_{x}b(\tau)\Phi_{\tau}^{-1}h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})_{s}}.

(10.28)

From
b(\tau)
in (
10.6
) with
\beta\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
(see
(3)
in Lemma
10.3
), it follows that (see
(1)
of Lemma
8.3
)

\rVert b(\tau)\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right).

Hence, Lemma
10.5
and (
10.28
) implies the first estimate in (
10.27
) for
\Phi_{1}^{-1}
(note that Lemma
10.5
is written for the time-
1
flow, however, one can always reparametrize the time variable
\tau
to apply the same estimates to
\Phi_{\tau}^{\pm}
in (
10.28
) for
\tau\in[0,1]
).
The second estimate in (
10.27
) for
d_{i}(\Phi^{\pm})
follows immediately from Lemma
10.5
.
∎

Lemma 10.6
.

\Phi_{1}
is reversibility preserving and
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},\beta
satisfy,

b_{1}(-\varphi)=b_{1}(\varphi),\quad b_{2}(-\varphi,-x)=b_{2}(\varphi,x),\quad b_{3}(\-\varphi,-x-y)=b_{3}(\varphi,x,y),\quad\beta(-\varphi,-x)=-\beta(\varphi,x).

Proof.

It is clear from
\beta,b_{1}
in (
10.25
) and the property of
a_{1}
in (
8.51
) that

b_{1}(-\varphi)=b_{1}(\varphi),\quad\beta(-\varphi,-x)=\beta(\varphi,x).

From Lemma
9.7
, it follows that
\Phi_{1}
is a reversibility preserving map. The results for
b_{2},b_{3}
follow straightforwardly from their expressions in Lemma
10.2
with the definitions of
B,B_{2}
and
\Psi_{1}^{T}
in (
10.10
) and (
10.11
) with the property of
a_{i}
in (
8.51
).
∎

Lemma 10.7
.

\Phi_{1}
is a real operator and
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
are real-valued.

Proof.

Using that
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
in Proposition
8.18
are real-valued, it follows from (
10.25
) that
\beta
is real. This clearly implies
\Phi_{1}
is a real operator. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is real (see (
(3)
) of Proposition
8.18
),
\mathcal{L}^{1}
is also real operator and the coefficients
b_{2},b_{3}
are real-valued as well.
∎

Lemma 10.8
.

\Phi_{1}
is a symplectic transformation, therefore,
\mathcal{L}^{1}
is a Hamiltonian operator.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\Phi_{1}
, defined in (
10.6
), is a time-
1
flow of the Hamiltonian flow in
H_{S^{\perp}}
and Lemma
2.41
.
∎

10.1.0.4
Analysis of the remainder
R_{1}

Lemma 10.9
.

R_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

From (
10.14
) (also see (
10.1.0.1
), and (
10.1.0.1
)), it follows that each operator in
R_{1}
involves a finite dimensional operator (see Lemma
9.5
). Among all the terms, we only show that
\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}\Phi_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
only, since the other terms can be done in the same way. But this follows from
\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
, Lemma
10.36
and Lemma
8.12
, that
\Phi_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{R}\Phi_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.
∎

10.2
Reparametrization of time

In this subsection, we consider the transformation of type (3):

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}[h](\varphi,x):=h(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi),x),\quad\Phi_{2}^{-1}[h](\varphi,x)=h(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x)

(10.29)

for some
p_{1}:\mathbb{T}^{\nu}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\mapsto\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)
, the inverse of
\varphi\mapsto\omega p_{1}(\varphi)
. We will find
p_{1}
so that the coefficient of
\Lambda^{\alpha-1}
of the operator
\left(\Phi^{2}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{1}_{\omega}\Phi^{2}
is constant. More precisely, we summarize the main result in the following proposition:

Proposition 10.10
.

There exist a transformation
\Phi_{2}:H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
and
\rho
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}[h]

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\rho}(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{1}\Phi_{2}[h]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{2}[h]+R_{2}[h],

(10.30)

\displaystyle M_{2}[h]

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}(\omega)\Lambda^{\alpha-1}h+d_{2}h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}}h,

satisfy the following:

(1)

\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}+{\mathtt{m}}_{\alpha,2}
, where
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1},{\mathtt{m}}_{\alpha,2}
are as in
(1)
in Proposition
10.1

(2)

d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0})
and
d_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
(see Definition
10.16
for the definitions of
\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}},\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}
), and

d_{2}(-\varphi,-x)=d_{2}(\varphi,x),\quad d_{3}(-\varphi,-x,-y)=d_{3}(\varphi,x,y).

(3)

R_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

(4)

\Phi_{2}
is real and reversibility preserving, hence
\mathcal{L}^{2}
is real and reversible. Furthermore, it is Hamiltonian.

(5)

\mathcal{L}^{2}
and
\Phi_{2}
are
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving and

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(d_{2})=d_{2},\quad\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(d_{3})=d_{3}.

(10.31)

Proof.

The proposition is proved using the results obtained in the rest of this subsection.
The expression of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
is obtained in Lemma
10.32
with
d_{1}=\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
in Lemma
10.12
.
(1)
is given in (
10.46
).
(2)
is proved in (
10.17
) and Lemma
10.19
.
(3)
follows from Lemma
10.22
.
(4)
follows from Lemma
10.19
, Lemma
10.20
and Lemma
10.21
. Lastly,
(5)
follows trivially since the transformation
\Phi_{2}
in (
10.29
) does not change the variable
x
and therefore, (
10.31
) follows from (
10.35
) and (
10.5
).
∎

Recall from
[
8
, Subsection 8.2]
that

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{2}=\rho(\varphi)\mathcal{D}_{\omega},\quad\rho(\varphi):=\Phi_{2}^{-1}(1+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(\varphi)).

(10.32)

As Proposition
10.1
, we start with computing the conjugation of the linear operator.

10.2.0.1
Computing the conjugation

Lemma 10.11
.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{1}\Phi_{2}[h]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{2}[h]+R_{2}[h],

(10.33)

\displaystyle M_{2}[h]=d_{1}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+d_{2}h+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}}h,

where

\displaystyle d_{1}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}b_{1}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)),\quad d_{2}(\varphi,x)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}{b}_{2}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x),\quad d_{3}(\varphi,x,y)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}b_{3}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x,y),

(10.34)

\displaystyle R_{2}[h]=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}\Phi_{2}^{-1}R_{1}\Phi_{2}[h].

(10.35)

Proof.

From Proposition
10.1
, we have that for all
h(\varphi,x)\in C^{\infty}_{\varphi,x}
, such that
h(\varphi,\cdot)\in H_{S^{\perp}}
,

\displaystyle(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{1}\Phi_{2}[h]=(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Phi_{2}[h]-(\Phi_{2})^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}\Phi_{2}[h]+(\Phi_{2})^{-1}R_{1}\Phi_{2}[h]

(10.36)

Let us compute the conjugation of
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}
. Since
\Phi_{2}
in (
10.29
) commutes with
\Pi_{S^{\perp}}
and
\partial_{x}
, it follows straightforwardly that (recalling
M_{1}
from (
10.1
))

\displaystyle\rho(\varphi)\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{2}:=\Phi_{2}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{1}\Phi_{2}=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\Phi_{2}^{-1}M_{1}\Phi_{2}=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}\left(\Phi_{2}^{-1}[b_{1}]\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+\Phi_{2}^{-1}[b_{2}]+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{\Phi_{2}^{-1}[b_{3}]}\right).

(10.37)

Plugging this into (
10.36
) and using (
10.32
), the result follows immediately.
∎

10.2.0.2
Analysis of the new coefficients
d_{1},d_{2},d_{3}

Lemma 10.12
.

Letting

\displaystyle p_{1}(\varphi):=-\mathcal{D}_{\omega}^{-1}\left(1-\frac{{b}_{1}(\varphi)}{\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}}\right),\quad\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\nu}}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}{b}_{1}(\varphi)d\varphi,

(10.38)

we have that
d_{1}=\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
, which does not depend on
\varphi
.

Proof.

From Lemma
10.11
, we have that
d_{1}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}b_{1}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi))
. Hence, we need to choose
p_{1}
and
\rho
so that

\displaystyle b_{1}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi))=\rho(\varphi)\mathtt{m}_{\alpha},

(10.39)

for some
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
that does not depend on
\varphi
. From (
10.32
), we see that this can be achieved by choosing
p_{1}
and
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
so that

\displaystyle b_{1}(\varphi)=(1+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(\varphi))\mathtt{m}_{\alpha},

(10.40)

which gives us (
10.39
) with

\displaystyle\rho(\varphi):=1+\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)).

(10.41)

Integrating (
10.40
) in
\varphi
and using that
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(\varphi)d\varphi=0
, it is clear that
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
and
p_{1}
in (
10.38
) satisfy (
10.40
).
∎

For
p_{1},\rho
and
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
chosen in Lemma
10.38
, we have the following estimates:

Lemma 10.13
.

We have that

\displaystyle\rVert\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},

(10.42)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\left(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}\right)(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

(10.43)

\displaystyle\rVert p_{1}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),

(10.44)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}p_{1}(i)[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right),

(10.45)

Proof.

Estimates (
10.42
) and (
10.43
).

It follows from (
10.40
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}=\frac{1}{\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}-{b}_{1}\right),

(10.46)

while
(1)
in Proposition
10.1
tells us that

\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
and
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}-{b}_{1}
are

\displaystyle\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,1}+\mathtt{m}_{\alpha,2},\quad\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}-b_{1}=\tilde{q}_{\alpha}.

(10.47)

Then the estimates (
10.42
) and (
10.43
) follow from the estimates in (
10.3
) and (
10.46
).

Estimates (
10.44
) and (
10.45
) .

The estimates (
10.44
) and (
10.45
) follow from (
6.6
) and (
6.7
).
∎

With the estimates for
p_{1}
in Lemma
10.13
, the estimates for
\tilde{p}_{1}
determined by (
10.29
) are given in the next lemma:

Lemma 10.14
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\rVert\tilde{p}_{1}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\gamma^{-1}\left(\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),

(10.48)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\tilde{p}_{1}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(10.49)

Proof.

The invertibility of the map
\varphi\mapsto\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi)
follows simply from the inverse function theorem. With the estimates for
p_{1}
in Lemma
10.13
, the estimates (

) and (

) follow from the same proof as in Lemma
