, using the Taylor expansion of
\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)=\omega p_{1}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)).

∎

Lemma 10.15
.

It holds that

\displaystyle\rVert\rho-1\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}},

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\rho(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

Proof.

It follows from the definition of
\rho
in (
10.41
) and the estimates for
p_{1}
and
\tilde{p}_{1}
given in Lemma
10.13
(especially (
10.42
) and (
10.43
)) and Lemma
10.14
.
∎

Recalling that
\gamma=\varepsilon^{2b}
from (
6.3
),
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
in (
10.34
) are not in
\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
and
\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
. For this reason, we denote by
\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
, the classes of the coefficients to include
d_{2},d_{3}
.

Definition 10.16
.

We say a function
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x)\in\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0})
, if
a(\omega,\varphi,x)=\mathtt{a}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p_{1}}(\omega,\varphi),x)
for some
\mathtt{a}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
where
\tilde{p}
is in Lemma
10.14
. Similarly, we say
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
, if
a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)=\mathtt{a}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x,y)
for some
\mathtt{a}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
.

Lemma 10.17
.

We have that
d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0})
and
d_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

We prove
d_{2}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0})
only, since
d_{3}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
can be proved in the same way.

Note that since
\varphi\mapsto\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi)
is the inverse map of
\varphi\mapsto\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi)
, we can write
d_{2}
in (
10.34
) as

\displaystyle d_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x)={a}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x),\text{ where }{a}(\omega,\varphi,x)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi+\omega p_{1})}b_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x).

(10.50)

In view of item
(2)
of Proposition
10.1
, which tells us
b_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
, we can rewrite
a(\omega,\varphi,x)
as

a(\omega,\varphi,x)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi+\omega p_{1})}\left(b_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x)-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi))}-1\right)\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}.

Plugging this into (
10.50
), we have

\displaystyle d_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x)-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{a}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x),

(10.51)

\displaystyle\text{ where }\mathtt{a}(\omega,\varphi,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi))}\left(b_{2}(\omega,\varphi,x)-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\left(\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi))}-1\right).

Recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0})
from Definition
10.16
, it suffices to show that
\mathtt{a}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
. However, this follows straightforwardly from the fact that
b_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\in\mathfrak{C}_{1}(i_{0})
(see
(2)
in Proposition
10.1
) and the estimates for
\rho
and
p_{1}
in Lemma
10.13
and Lemma
10.15
.
∎

10.2.0.3
Analysis of the transformation
\Phi_{2}

Lemma 10.18
.

\Phi^{\pm 1}=\Phi_{2},\Phi_{2}^{-1}
satisfies

\displaystyle\rVert(\Phi^{\pm}-I)h\rVert_{s}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\varepsilon\left(\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)

(10.52)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\Phi^{\pm}(i_{0})h[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{s}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s}\left(\rVert h\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right)\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}+\rVert h\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}

Proof.

The estimates for
\Phi^{\pm 1}
follow from their definitions in (
10.29
) and the estimates for
p_{1},\tilde{p}_{1}
in Lemma
10.13
and Lemma
10.14
.
∎

Lemma 10.19
.

\Phi_{2}
is reversibility preserving. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,-\varphi)=-\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),\quad\rho(-\varphi)=\rho(\varphi),\quad d_{2}(-\varphi,-x)=d_{2}(\varphi,x),\quad d_{3}(-\varphi,-x,-y)=d_{3}(\varphi,x,y).

(10.53)

Proof.

First, we see from (
10.4
) and our choice of
p_{1}
in (
10.38
) that
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(-\varphi)=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}p_{1}(\varphi)
, therefore,

\displaystyle p_{1}(-\varphi)=-p_{1}(\varphi).

(10.54)

Recalling the definition of the reversibility preserving operators in Definition
2.46
, we have (recall the spaces
X,Y
from (
2.138
))

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}h(-\varphi,-x)

\displaystyle=h(-\varphi+\omega p_{1}(-\varphi),-x)=h(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi),x)=\Phi_{2}h(\varphi,x),\text{ for $h\in X$,}

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}h(-\varphi,-x)

\displaystyle=h(-\varphi+\omega p_{1}(-\varphi),-x)=-h(\varphi+\omega p_{1}(\varphi),x)=-\Phi_{2}h(\varphi,x),\text{ for $h\in Y$}.

Therefore,
\Phi_{2}
is reversibility preserving. Using the definition of
\tilde{p}_{1}
in (
10.29
), (
10.54
) implies that
\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,-\varphi)=-\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi).
Therefore, it follows from (
10.41
) that
\rho(-\varphi)=\rho(\varphi)
. With these symmetry of
\tilde{p}_{1}
and
\rho
, (
10.53
) follows from the definitions of
d_{2},d_{3}
in (
10.34
) and (
10.4
).
∎

Lemma 10.20
.

\Phi_{2}
is real and
\rho,p_{1},\tilde{p}_{1}
are real-valued functions.

Proof.

Since
b_{1}
is real-valued (see Lemma
10.7
), it follows from (
10.2
) and (
10.47
) that
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
is real-valued. Therefore, it follows from (
10.40
) and (
10.41
) that
p_{1}
and
\rho
are real-valued. Using the definition of
\tilde{p}_{1}
, it is clear that
\tilde{p}_{1}
is real-valued as well.
∎

Lemma 10.21
.

\mathcal{L}^{2}
is a Hamiltonian operator.

Proof.

Recalling the definition of Hamiltonian operators from Definition
2.40
, we only need to show that (from
\mathcal{L}^{2}
in (
10.33
))
M_{2}
and
\partial_{x}^{-1}{R}_{2}
are symmetric operators in
H_{S^{\perp}}
for each fixed
\varphi
. We show this for
M_{2}
only since
R_{2}
can be managed in the same way. From the definition of
M_{2}
in (
10.37
), it suffices to show that
M_{2}(\varphi)=\frac{1}{\rho(\varphi)}\Phi_{2}^{-1}M_{1}\Phi_{2}

is a symmetric operator. Clearly,
\Phi_{2}
reparametrizes the variable
\varphi
only therefore,
\Phi_{2},\Phi_{2}^{-1}
are symmetric operators on
H_{S^{\perp}}
for each fixed
\varphi
. Since
M_{1}
is also symmetric (see
(4)
in Proposition
10.1
) and
\rho
is real-valued (Lemma
10.20
),
M_{2}
is symmetric.
∎

10.2.0.4
Analysis of the remainder
R_{2}

Lemma 10.22
.

R_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

Recalling that
R_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}_{1}(i_{0})
from (
(3)
) of Proposition
10.1
, it follows from Lemma
8.12
and Lemma
10.18
that
\rho R_{2}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
(see (
10.35
) for the definition of
R_{2}
). Thanks to the estimates of
\rho
in Lemma
10.15
, the result follows straightforwardly from the definition of
\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.
∎

10.3
Egorov method

As a result of Proposition
10.29
, we obtained a conjugated linear operator
\mathcal{L}^{2}
whose top order term has a constant coefficient,
\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}
, which does not depend on
\varphi,x
. In this subsection, we aim to make the coefficients of the lower order terms constant, by means of Egorov method. We organize this subsection into three parts. In the first part, we express the linear operator
\mathcal{L}^{2}
in terms of symbols. In the second and the third parts, we will remove the dependence on the variables
x
and
\varphi
respectively.

10.3.1
Symbolic expression for the linear operator

We recall that the non-constant coefficients
d_{2},d_{3}
of
\mathcal{L}^{2}
in (
10.30
) are in
\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
respectively. We first define a class of symbols that arise from the coefficients in
\mathfrak{C}_{1,p_{1}}(i_{0}),\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
.

Definition 10.23
.

We say that a symbol
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
, if
\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi)=\mathfrak{b}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x,\xi)
for some
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}(i_{0})
(see Definition
8.5
for
\mathfrak{S}^{m}(i_{0})
), for
\tilde{p}_{1}
in (
10.14
).

We collect useful properties of the symbols in
\mathfrak{S}^{m}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
.

Lemma 10.24
.

Let
a=a(\omega,\varphi,x,y)\in\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
. Then there exists
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
such that
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{2,p_{1}}(i_{0})
, there exists a function
\mathtt{a}\in\mathfrak{C}_{2}(i_{0})
such that

{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,y)=\mathtt{a}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x,y).

Thanks to Lemma
8.7
, there exists a symbol
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{\alpha-3}(i_{0})
such that

\displaystyle Op^{W}(\mathfrak{b})=\Upsilon_{\mathtt{a}}^{\alpha-3}.

(10.55)

We set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}(\omega,\varphi,x,\xi):=\mathfrak{b}(\omega,\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\omega,\varphi),x,\xi).

(10.56)

From the definition of
\mathfrak{S}_{p_{1}}^{m}(i_{0})
, it is clear that
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}_{p_{1}}^{\alpha-3}(i_{0})
.
Then
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{a}
follows trivially by reparametrizing
\varphi
in (
10.55
).
∎

As in Lemma
8.4
, we can estimate the averaged symbol:

Lemma 10.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
. Then,
{M}_{x}(\mathfrak{a})
has a decomposition:

\displaystyle{M}_{x}(\mathfrak{a})=\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{m}(\omega,\xi)+\mathfrak{r}(\omega,\xi)+\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}(\omega,\varphi,\xi),\quad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{\nu}}\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}(\omega,\varphi,\xi)d\varphi=0,

(10.57)

such that the symbols
\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{r}
are independent of
\varphi,x
and
\mathfrak{m}
does not depend on
i_{0}
. Furthermore, we have the estimates:

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{m}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,0,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},\eta_{0}}1,

(10.58)

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{r}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,0,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{7-4b},\quad|d_{i}\mathfrak{r}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,0,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},\eta_{0}}\varepsilon\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}},,

(10.59)

\displaystyle|\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{4}+\varepsilon^{2}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})},\quad|d_{i}(\tilde{\mathfrak{q}})(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{2}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(10.60)

Proof.

By Definition
10.23
and Definition
8.5
, one can find
\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2},\mathfrak{a}_{3},\mathfrak{q}_{1}
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}(\varphi,x,\xi)=\varepsilon\mathfrak{a}_{1}(\varphi,x,\xi)+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{a}_{2}(\varphi,x,\xi)+\varepsilon^{3}\mathfrak{a}_{3}(\varphi,x,\xi)+\mathfrak{q}_{1}(\varphi,x,\xi),

(10.61)

such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{i}(\varphi,x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}\in S}C_{j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{i}}}(\xi)\sqrt{j_{k_{1}}\zeta_{k_{1}}}\cdots\sqrt{j_{k_{i}}\zeta_{k_{i}}}e^{\text{i}((\mathtt{l}(j_{k_{1}})+\cdots+\mathtt{l}(j_{k_{i}}))\cdot\left(\varphi+\omega\tilde{p}_{1}(\varphi)\right)+\text{i}j_{k}x)},

(10.62)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{1}(\varphi,x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{q}(\varphi+\tilde{p}_{1}(\varphi),x,\xi),

where
\mathfrak{q}(\varphi,x,\xi)
satisfies the estimates in (
8.22
) and (
8.23
). As in Lemma
8.4
, we decompose
M_{x}(\mathfrak{a})
as (using (
10.61
))

\displaystyle M_{x}(\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{1})+\varepsilon^{2}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{2})+\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{3})+M_{x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{2})+\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{3})+M_{x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{2}\underbrace{M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{2})}_{=:\mathfrak{m}(\omega,\xi)}+\underbrace{\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{3})+M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})}_{=:\mathfrak{r}(\omega,\xi)}+\underbrace{M_{x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})-M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})}_{=:\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}}.

Indeed, recalling the definition of
\mathtt{l}(j)
from (
8.5
), we see that the zero-th Fourier mode of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
in (
10.62
) does not depend on
\varphi
, hence

\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{r}
are independent of
\varphi,x
. Since
C_{j_{1},j_{2}}(\xi)
is independent of
i_{0}
,
\mathfrak{m}
does not depend on
i_{0}
either. Recalling the dependence between
\zeta
and
\omega
from (
5.5
), we have (
10.58
).

To see (
10.59
), it is clear from
\mathfrak{a}_{3}
in (
10.62
) that
\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{3})
does not depend on
\varphi,x
and satisfies (
10.59
) (indeed, it satisfies better estimates:
|\varepsilon^{3}M_{x}(\mathfrak{a}_{3})|_{m,0,\eta_{0}}^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}=O(\varepsilon^{3})
and
d_{i}\mathfrak{a}_{3}(i_{0})=0
). Furthermore, it follows from the estimates of
\tilde{p}_{1}
in Lemma
10.14
and
\mathfrak{q}
, satisfying (
8.22
), (
8.23
) that
M_{\varphi,x}(\mathfrak{q}_{1})
satisfies (
10.59
) and
\tilde{\mathfrak{q}}
satisfies (
10.60
).
∎

Lemma 10.26
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
, and
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m^{\prime}}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
. Then,
\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m+m^{\prime}}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}(i_{0})
and
\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m^{\prime}}(i_{0})
, then
\mathfrak{a}\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{a}\star\mathfrak{b}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m+m^{\prime}}(i_{0})
. This follows straightforwardly from the definition of
\mathfrak{S}^{m}(i_{0})
in Definition
8.5
, Lemma
2.11
and Lemma
2.15
.
∎

Lemma 10.27
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{m}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{a}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon(1+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}),

(10.63)

\displaystyle\rVert d_{i}\mathfrak{a}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]\rVert_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{3}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

(10.64)

Furthermore,
\mathfrak{a}
admits an expansion

\mathfrak{a}=\varepsilon\mathfrak{b}_{1}+\varepsilon^{2}\mathfrak{b}_{2}+\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{a}},

for some
\mathfrak{b}_{1}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{1},\mathfrak{b}_{2}\in\mathfrak{B}^{m}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{a}}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{a}}|^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\gamma^{-1}\left(\varepsilon^{5}+\varepsilon^{3}\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert^{\text{Lip}(\gamma,\Omega_{1})}_{s+\mu_{0}}\right),

(10.65)

\displaystyle|d_{i}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathfrak{a}}(i_{0})[\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}]|_{m,s,\eta_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathtt{pe},s,\eta_{0}}\varepsilon^{3}\gamma^{-1}\left(\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}+\rVert\mathfrak{I}_{\delta}\rVert_{s+\mu_{0}}\rVert\hat{\textbf{\char 16\relax}}\rVert_{s_{0}+\mu_{0}}\right).

Proof.

The estimates follow straightforwardly from Definition
10.23
and the estimates for
\tilde{p}_{1}
in Lemma
10.14
.
∎

With the symbols in
\mathfrak{S}_{p_{1}}^{m}(i_{0})
, we can rewrite
\mathcal{L}^{2}
as follows:

Lemma 10.28
.

For
\mathcal{L}^{2}
in Proposition
10.10
, there exists a symbol
\mathfrak{d}_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{2}[h]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{2}[h]+R_{2}[h],

(10.66)

\displaystyle M_{2}

\displaystyle=Op^{W}(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{d}_{0}(\varphi,x,\xi))=:Op^{W}(\mathfrak{p}_{M_{2}}(\varphi,x,\xi)),

such that

(1)

\mathfrak{d}_{0}
is a real-valued symbol.

(2)

\mathfrak{d}_{0}
is a reversible symbol.

(3)

Op^{W}(\mathfrak{d}_{0})
is a real operator.

(4)

\mathfrak{d}_{0}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant, that is,

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(\mathfrak{d}_{0})(\varphi,x,\xi):=\mathfrak{d}_{0}(\varphi,x+\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}},\xi)=\mathfrak{d}_{0}(\varphi,x,\xi).

(10.67)

Proof.

Recalling
M_{2}
from (
10.10
), we need choose
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
so that

\displaystyle M_{2}=\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}\Lambda^{\alpha-1}+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\left(d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}}=Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\mathfrak{d}_{0}\right).

(10.68)

Thanks to the definition of
m_{1,\alpha}
in Proposition
2.34
, it suffices to choose
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\left(d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}\right)+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}}=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{d}_{0}).

(10.69)

It follows from Lemma
10.24
and
(2)
of Proposition
10.10
that such a symbol
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
exists and
\mathfrak{d}_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}^{0}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})
. Since
M_{2}
is a symmetric operator, (
2.16
) tells us that
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
is real-valued. To prove item
(2)
, we see from the symmetry of
d_{2},d_{3}
in
(2)
of Proposition
10.10
that
\partial_{x}\left(d_{2}-\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\Upsilon^{\alpha-3}_{d_{3}}\right)
is a reversible operator. Hence,
\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{d}_{0})
is a reversible operator (see Definition
2.47
).
To see item
(3)
, it follows from
(4)
of Proposition
10.10
that
Op^{W}(\mathfrak{d}_{0})
is a real operator. Lastly,
(4)
follows straightforwardly from the properties of
d_{2}
and
d_{3}
in (
10.31
).
∎

In the rest of this section, we will introduce two natural numbers
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
and
\mathtt{b}_{0}
, defined to be the smallest integers such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}_{0}>6(\tau+1),\quad\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}>\max\left\{\frac{3-\alpha}{\alpha-1},\frac{(s_{0}+\mathtt{b}_{0}+2)(2-\alpha)+2}{\alpha-1},3\right\}.

(10.70)

Remark 10.29
.

In the rest of the paper, the loss of derivatives
\mu_{0}
will depend on
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
and
\mathtt{b}_{0}
. Since
\mathtt{b}_{0}
is completely determined by
\tau
, which is fixed in (
6.3
), we still have
\mu_{0}
depend on only
\mathtt{p}
, see Remark
7.4
.

10.3.2
Elimination of the dependence on
x

The goal of this section is to make the symbol
\mathfrak{d}_{0}
in (
10.66
) independent of the variable
x
, conjugating
\mathcal{L}^{2}
with a flow map of type (2) defined in Definition
9.1
. The result of this section is summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 10.30
.

There exists a symplectic transformation
\Phi_{3}:H_{S^{\perp}}\mapsto H_{S^{\perp}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{3}[h]

\displaystyle:=(\Phi_{3})^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Phi_{3}[h]=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}h-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{3}[h]+\partial_{x}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}W_{0}+R_{3}[h],

(10.71)

\displaystyle M_{3}[h]

\displaystyle:=Op^{W}\left(\mathtt{m}_{\alpha}m_{1,\alpha}(\xi)+\frac{T_{\alpha}}{4}+\sum_{k=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}M_{x}(\mathfrak{d}_{k})+\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\right),

satisfies the following:

(1)

\mathfrak{d}_{k}\in\mathfrak{S}^{k(1-\alpha)}_{p_{1}}
for
k=0,\ldots,\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{-2}\in\mathfrak{S}^{-2}_{p_{1}}
.

(2)

\mathfrak{d}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{-2}
are real-valued, reversible symbols (see Definition
2.47
)

(3)

W_{0}
is defined in (
10.114
) and
R_{3}\in\mathfrak{R}(i_{0})
.

(4)

\Phi_{3}
is real, reversibility preserving and symplectic. Therefore,
\mathcal{L}^{3}
is a real, reversible and Hamiltonian operator.

(5)

\mathcal{L}^{3}
and
\Phi_{3}
are
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving and

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathtt{M}}(\mathfrak{r}_{-2})=\mathfrak{r}_{-2}.

(10.72)

Proof.

The proof will be presented throughout the subsection. The expression of
\mathcal{L}^{3}
in (
10.71
) is given in (
10.89
), where the symbol of
M_{3}
is given in (
10.90
) and (
10.107
). Items
(1)
and
(2)
are the results of Proposition
10.35
. Item
(3)
is in Lemma
10.40
and item
(4)
is in Lemma
10.37
and
(4)
of Proposition
10.35
. Lastly, for item
(5)
, it follows from Lemma
10.38
that
\mathfrak{a}
, determined by (
10.92
) is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariant symbol. Hence the map
\Phi_{3}
, determined by a Hamiltonian PDE in (
10.73
), gives us a
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving transformation. Therefore
\mathcal{L}^{3}
is
\frac{2\pi}{\mathtt{M}}
-translation invariance preserving. (
10.72
) is already explicitly stated in Lemma
10.38
.
∎

In order to find a symplectic transformation in Proposition
10.30
, we use a symplectic transformation of type (2) in Definition
9.1
. We denote by
\Phi_{3}(\tau)
, the flow map of the PDE:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})u,\text{ for some real-valued symbol $\mathfrak{a}\in\mathfrak{S}^{1-\alpha}_{p_{1}}(i_{0})$.}

(10.73)

As in Subsection
10.1
, we denote by
\Psi_{3}(\tau)
, the flow map of the PDE:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}u=\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})u.

(10.74)

When
\tau=1
, we simply denote

\Phi_{3}:=\Phi_{3}(1),\quad\Psi_{3}:=\Psi_{3}(1).

From (
10.74
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Psi_{3}(\tau)=\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Psi_{3}(\tau),\quad\frac{d}{d\tau}\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}=-\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a}),\quad\Psi_{3}(0)=\Psi_{3}(0)^{-1}=I.

(10.75)

Since the equation (
10.74
) is autonomous, we have that

\Psi_{3}(\tau)\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})=\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\Psi_{3}(\tau).

Our analysis in this section is based on the properties of
\Phi_{3}(\tau),\Psi_{3}(\tau)
, studied in Subsection
9.2
.

10.3.2.1
Computing the conjugation

Lemma 10.31
.

We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{3}

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Phi_{3}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3})-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+R_{3},

(10.76)

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle:=R_{3,1}+R_{3,2}

where
R_{3,1},R_{3,2}
are defined in (
10.78
) and (
10.80
).

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Phi_{3}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}-\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{s}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}-\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+(\Phi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}})\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{s}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle\ -\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+(\Phi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}})\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{s}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle\ -\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right)

\displaystyle=:\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+R_{3,1},

(10.77)

where

\displaystyle R_{3,1}

\displaystyle:=-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+(\Phi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}})\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{s}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}

(10.78)

\displaystyle-\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Pi_{S}\Psi_{3}+\Phi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\left(\Phi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\right).

In (
10.3.2.1
), we further decompose
\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}
as (using (
10.30
)),

\displaystyle\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{L}^{2}\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Psi_{3}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}R_{2}\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Psi_{3}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}R_{2}\Psi_{3}

\displaystyle=:\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Psi_{3}-\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+R_{3,2},

(10.79)

where

\displaystyle R_{3,2}:=\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\Pi_{S}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}+\Pi_{S^{\perp}}\Psi_{3}^{-1}R_{2}\Psi_{3}.

(10.80)

Therefore, writing
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\Psi_{3}=\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3})+\Psi_{3}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
in (
10.79
) and plugging it into (
10.3.2.1
), the result follows.
∎

Now, we analyze the operators
\Psi_{3}^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3})
. We first observe that each operator can be seen as a solution to a Heisenberg equation.

10.3.2.2
Analysis of
\Psi_{3}^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}
.

We denote

\displaystyle M_{3}(\tau):=\partial_{x}^{-1}\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}(\tau),

(10.81)

so that

\partial_{x}M_{3}(\tau)=\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\partial_{x}M_{2}\Psi_{3}(\tau).

Using (
10.75
), we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}M_{3}(\tau)=M_{3}(\tau)\partial_{x}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})-Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})\partial_{x}M_{3}(\tau),\quad M_{3}(0)=M_{2}.

(10.82)

Therefore, we have (recalling the definitions of
\mathfrak{p}_{M_{2}}
from (
10.66
) and the notation
[\cdot,\cdot]_{x}
from (
2.31
)) that
M_{3}(\tau)
solves the Heisenberg equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d}{d\tau}M_{3}(\tau)=[M_{3}(\tau),Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})]_{x},\\
M_{3}(0)=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{p}_{M_{2}}).\end{cases}

(10.83)

Hence, we see from Proposition
9.11
that for the fixed
\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}
in (
10.70
),

\displaystyle M_{3}(1)

\displaystyle=Q_{M_{2}}+W_{M_{2}},

(10.84)

\displaystyle Q_{M_{2}}

\displaystyle=Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}),\quad\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},\leq\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{n!}\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},n},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},0}

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{p}_{M_{2}},\quad\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},n}:=\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},n-1}\star\mathfrak{a},\text{ for $n=1,\ldots,\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}$},

\displaystyle W_{M_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}!}\int_{0}^{1}\Psi_{3}(1-t)^{T}Op^{W}(\mathfrak{q}_{M_{2},\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}\star\mathfrak{a})\Psi_{3}(1-t)t^{\mathtt{N}_{\alpha}}dt.

10.3.2.3
Analysis of
\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3})
.

Lemma 10.32
.

(Conjugation with
\mathcal{D}_{\omega}
) We have that

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{-1}\Psi_{3}^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3})=\int_{0}^{1}S(\tau)d\tau,

(10.85)

where
S(\tau)
is a solution to the Heisenberg equation:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{d}{d\tau}S(\tau)=[S(\tau),Op^{W}(\mathfrak{a})]_{x},\\
S(0)=Op^{W}(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}\mathfrak{a}).\end{cases}

(10.86)

Proof.

Following the computations in
[
16
, Lemma 6.17]
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3}(\tau))\right)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\Psi_{3}(\tau))+\Psi_{3}(\tau)^{-1}\mathcal{D}_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\mathcal{A}\Psi_{3}(\tau))
